# GLOSSARY

❖ B Corp Certification is a designation awarded to companies that meet high standards of social and environmental performance, transparency, and accountability. It verifies that a business is committed to balancing profit with purpose. ❖ Blockchain: Blockchain is a shared, immutable digital ledger, enabling the recording of transactions and the tracking of assets within a network and providing a single source of truth. It supports transparency and traceability across the textiles value chain. ❖ Chemical recycling is the process of converting textile waste materials back into their basic building blocks, i.e. to the polymer or monomer level, using processes such as glycolysis, hydrolysis and methanolysis. This can also involve purification by removing dyes and contaminants. These building blocks can then be re-polymerised back into new material. ❖ Closed-loop recycling process involves recycling textiles and clothing into new products of the same type and quality as the original. ❖ Closed-loop system is a system where materials and products are continuously reused, repaired, and recycled, minimising waste and reducing the need for virgin resources ❖ Cradle to Cradle Certified is a globally recognized standard that helps businesses design and produce products that are safe, circular, and responsibly made. ❖ Digital Product Passport: digital profiles attached to products, providing detailed information about their origin, materials, usage and end-of life handling. ❖ Fast fashion: refers to a production model characterized by rapid turnover of styles, low costs, and mass production. ❖ Fibre to fibre recycling is a recycling process in which textile waste is processed into recycled textile fibres. ❖ Fourth industrial revolution (Industry 4.0): describes integration of digital technologies such as AI, IoT, and CPS into manufacturing, enhancing efficiency and sustainability. ❖ Global value chain refers to the full range of activities that economic actors engage in to bring a product to market. The global value chain does not only involve production processes, but preproduction (such as design) and postproduction processes (such as marketing and distribution). ❖ Internet-of-Things: refers to a system of interconnected sensors, instruments and other devices, creating an information network for the collection exchange and analysis of large amounts of real-time data. ❖ Life Cycle Assessment: is a process for evaluating the environmental impacts of a product or service over the course of its entire life. ❖ Mechanical recycling is a type of recycling, where materials are mechanically processed and converted into recycled fibres. This includes processes such as cutting and shredding. ❖ Open-loop recycling process: also known as down-cycling, in the fashion industry refers to the process of recycling textiles and garments into products that differ from their original form. This typically involves transforming used fabrics into materials for other sectors or into lower-value products within fashion. ❖ Original Design Manufacturer (ODM) is a company that designs and manufactures a product that is eventually rebranded and sold by another company. ODMs provide a fullservice solution: they handle the product design, engineering, and manufacturing, while the

purchasing company focuses on branding, marketing, and distribution. ODMs are common in industries such as apparel where companies want to minimize R&D costs and time-tomarket.

❖ Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM) refers to a company that produces parts and equipment that may be marketed by another manufacturer. OEMs typically manufacture components that are used in the final products of another company, known as the valueadded reseller or end-product manufacturer. ❖ Post-consumer textile waste: textiles that have been disposed of after consumption and use by the citizen or end-users of commercial and industrial activities. ❖ Recycling: any recovery operation by which waste materials are reprocessed into products, materials, or substances, whether for the original or other purposes. ❖ Re-use: any operation by which products or components that are not waste are used again for the same purpose for which they were conceived. ❖ Sorting is the process used to assess the suitability of textile waste for reuse or recycling. Items are sorted into different grades based on quality and end market, and can be sorted by product type, fibre type and colour. This is usually a manual process, but automated sorting processes are being developed. ❖ Sustainable fabrics: refer to clothing materials that are manufactured with reduced environmental impact, often utilizing organic cotton, recycled fibres, and eco-friendly dyeing processes. ❖ Take-back programs are initiatives where companies collect used products from customers for recycling, refurbishment, or remanufacturing, ensuring materials remain in circulation and reducing waste ❖ Technological Readiness Level: a 1 to 9 measure used to assess the maturity of a technology. TRL 9 indicates a high and mature level, while lower levels indicate emerging technologies. ❖ Textile recycling: refers to the process of recovering textiles and reprocessing them into usable materials or products, thereby diverting waste from landfills and reducing the demand for virgin resources. ❖ Textile reuse: refers to various means for prolonging the practical service life of textile products by transferring them to new owners with or without prior modification. ❖ Textile Value Chain: all activities including the design, manufacturing, distribution, retail, and consumption of a textile product, as well as the supply of raw materials and the end-oflife stage. ❖ Thermo-chemical recycling is a process using heat to break down materials into monomers or low-molar-mass components that can be used as feedstock. ❖ Thermomechanical recycling is a recycling method using heat and pressure to melt thermoplastic textiles, such as polyester and nylon. This allows the polymers to be recovered and re-spun into new fibres.

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Deliverable 1.5 is a component of Task 1.5 "Market and consumer trends", which according to the project's work plan, aims to present an in-depth analysis of current conditions and dynamics within the textile sector. Specifically, the purpose of D1.5 is to provide an analysis of market structures, consumer behaviours, and trends in the textile sector. By grounding TRUSTex in real-world market dynamics, the deliverable aspires to support evidence-based decision-making across subsequent work packages including the TRUSTex policy recommendations.

The textile industry is one of the world's oldest and most globally integrated manufacturing sectors. In the textile value chain, most production processes take place in low and middle-income countries, particularly in Asia, while consumption is concentrated in developed economies. Postconsumer textiles frequently travel back to developing countries, especially in Africa, fuelling a growing transboundary flow of textile waste. Increasing consumption in Western markets, rapid ecommerce expansion, and rising demand for fast fashion continue to intensify production volumes and waste generation. Overall, production costs, global demand, technological improvements, and evolving consumer behaviour are expected to continue driving the industry's growth in the coming decade.

The EU textile industry remains a vital part of Europe's industrial landscape and EU is the world's second-largest exporter of textiles and clothing after China. China remains the EU's largest import partner, followed by Bangladesh, Türkiye, India, and Vietnam, reflecting Europe's continued dependence on low-cost production countries. Key export destinations include the United Kingdom, Switzerland, China, and Türkiye.

Consumption trends shape the sector's dynamics. Average EU consumption has been rising until 2022 despite broader economic uncertainty. The industry has recently faced significant challenges. Weak domestic demand, high energy prices, supply chain disruptions, labour shortages, and increased regulatory burdens contributed to prolonged declines in textile production.

The EU textile market is projected in the long term to grow steadily, driven by rising demand for sustainable materials, technological innovation and shifting consumer trends. Growth is additionally supported by the EU sustainability regulations and advancements in digital production technologies. Technological advancements associated with Industry 4.0, including automation, AI, IoT, and smart textiles, are transforming the sector. These technologies also support sustainability objectives by reducing material waste and resource use.

As consumer preferences evolve toward durability, performance, and environmental responsibility, the sector is expected to continue transforming, with innovation and sustainability emerging as core competitive drivers.

Sustainability pressures are intensifying. Stricter regulations, rising consumer awareness, and climate targets require a shift from linear production to circular systems based on recycled materials, durability-oriented design, and closed-loop processes.

Although discussions about reshoring have intensified due to geopolitical instability and supply chain disruptions, large-scale manufacturing remains economically unviable in Europe because of higher labour costs and limited industrial capacity. Demographic and behavioural shifts are also reshaping demand.

The EU textile industry is globally recognised for exceptional production quality, especially in technical textiles and premium fashion. The sector demonstrates strong capabilities in design, creativity, and brand development. Intense competition from emerging economies and cross-sector players, such as e-commerce platforms, puts pressure on margins, particularly for SMEs.

Europe's energy prices remain significantly higher than those in other regions, affecting the competitiveness of domestic manufacturing.

Despite the globalised nature of the sector, the European textile sector remains economically significant. Most value added is generated within Europe, supporting domestic employment and GDP. However, the industry's overall value contribution has declined since 2000.

Overall, the sector stands at a crossroads. Rich in strengths and creative potential but confronted with structural vulnerabilities, international competition, and the need for major investments in skills, transparency, and sustainability to remain globally competitive.

Companies face mounting pressure to enhance transparency, reduce environmental impacts, and modernize business models, while consumers increasingly demand ethical sourcing, durable products, and credible sustainability claims.

The Waste Framework Directive (WFD) and the broader regulatory landscape are reshaping the core functions of textile firms. The Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD) obliges large enterprises to disclose detailed ESG metrics. The Eco-design for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces binding requirements on durability, reparability, recyclability, and material efficiency. Digital Product Passports (DPPs) further accelerate this shift. Complementing these measures, the Green Claims Directive requires that all environmental marketing claims be scientifically substantiated.

Ultimately, the industry is adopting circular models that prioritise repair, reuse, resale, and closedloop recycling to reduce waste and dependency on virgin resources. Overall, is progressively transitioning into a more digital, circular, and high-value sector.

Consumer behaviour plays a decisive role in shaping the environmental and social impacts of the sector, especially as the EU advances its Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles. Understanding consumers' motives, barriers, and behavioural patterns is essential for designing effective policies that support the transition to a sustainable and circular textile economy. Since the 1990s, the dominant model influencing consumer habits has been fast fashion, characterised by cheap, rapidly produced, trend-driven, and mass-manufactured clothing. This model encourages accelerated consumption cycles, low garment utilisation, and significant waste generation. Its affordability and constant novelty fuel widespread appeal, yet they also rely on environmentally damaging processes and exploitative labour conditions. As a result, fast fashion sits at the centre of unsustainable consumer patterns.

Digitalisation has strengthened consumer decision-making through easier access to information, reviews, and price comparisons, while e-commerce and smartphones have transformed shopping into an on-demand activity. Social media platforms intensify trend cycles and influence purchasing decisions through micro-trends and "influencer" culture. Demand for personalisation and

convenience further shapes expectations, as do demographic variables, with younger consumers more driven by aesthetics and older consumers by functionality. Technological innovations such as augmented reality fitting rooms and smart textiles also shift expectations, while rising sustainability awareness increasingly affects choices, although, very often, secondarily to price, quality and style.

Evidence shows that although consumers express positive attitudes toward sustainable fashion, an attitude / behaviour gap persists. High prices, distrust of sustainability claims, limited knowledge, stereotypes about sustainable products, low availability, and entrenched habits act as barriers. In contrast, drivers include perceived value for money, high product quality and comfort, alignment with ethical values, positive social influence, strong brand credibility, and enjoyable purchasing experiences. In the use phase, behaviours that extend garment life, such as repair, careful laundering, and emotional attachment, are motivated by cost-saving, environmental concern, knowledge, and social identity. During the post-use phase, sustainable disposal practices like donating, reselling, recycling, or repurposing are shaped by moral responsibility, social norms, convenience, trust in collection systems, and the perceived residual value of garments. Emerging practices such as slow fashion, second-hand shopping, clothing rental, swapping, and repair culture reflect a growing shift toward circularity. Yet, huge differences are recorded from country to country.

The global picture of textile circularity reveals a linear industry still dominated by virgin materials, low recycling rates, and growing waste streams, with fast fashion accelerating overproduction and shortening product lifecycles. Textile-to-textile circularity remains limited. The EU stands out as the most ambitious driver of circular transformation, leveraging regulatory frameworks, innovation funding, and high public awareness to push the industry toward durability, recyclability, and responsible production. The EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles, alongside emerging EPR schemes and design requirements, signals a fundamental shift from voluntary sustainability efforts toward mandatory circular practices across the value chain. Yet challenges persist. Namely, despite comparatively high collection rates, less than 1% of EU textiles are currently recycled back into new textiles, reflecting technological and infrastructure gaps that still constrain progress. Nonetheless, positive momentum is emerging across Member States, which are adopting advanced mechanical and chemical recycling solutions, expanding reuse markets, and integrating biodegradable and bio-based materials.

The evolving body of EU legislation on sustainable and circular textiles represents a decisive shift toward reshaping the textile ecosystem into one that is environmentally responsible, transparent, and resilient. Together, the Competitiveness Compass for the EU, the Green Deal, the Clean Industrial Deal the Circular Economy Action Plan, and the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles set a unified vision in which products are designed to last longer, be repaired, reused, and effectively recycled, while environmental and social impacts are reduced at every stage of the value chain. The growing regulatory framework, spanning the ESPR, the Digital Product Passport, consumer-protection directives, strengthened waste-shipment rules, and EPR obligations, forms a policy framework that aligns market incentives, consumer expectations, and producer responsibilities.

Particularly, the revised Waste Framework Directive establishes a regulatory shift for the EU textile sector, placing producers at the centre of responsibility for circularity and waste management. The Directive creates a framework in which producers must finance and support the entire end-of-life

chain, from collection and sorting to reuse, recycling, and final disposal. Eco-modulated fees further incentivise brands to prioritise durability, recyclability, and sustainable design from the outset, ensuring that environmental performance becomes a core component of business strategy rather than an optional extra. If effectively designed and implemented, EPR has the potential to realise the "polluter pays principle, to correct the economic imbalance that has long undermined separate collection and sorting, improve transparency in global material flows, and unlock investment in highquality recycling capacity. Ultimately, the Directive represents a decisive move away from a linear, low-accountability textile system toward a coordinated European approach in which producers, producer responsibility organisations, and policymakers together build the foundations for durable, repairable, and fully circular textile value chains.

Innovations such as blockchain and DPPs are laying the foundation for unprecedented levels of traceability, enabling brands, regulators, and consumers to access trusted information on material origins, manufacturing conditions, and product lifecycles. The expansion of eco-designed fibres, fabrics, and garments signals a decisive move away from resource-intensive, linear production models toward renewable, recyclable, and low-impact alternatives. Moreover, advances in automated, semi-automated, and AI-driven sorting, along with next-generation thermomechanical recycling technologies, are addressing long-standing bottlenecks in textile-to-textile recycling, offering the potential for high-quality secondary feedstock and scaled circularity. Complementary developments in big data, AI, and IoT are transforming product design, manufacturing processes, and consumer interaction. Together, these technologies form a comprehensive ecosystem that not only modernises the textile industry but also underpins the systemic change required to meet future sustainability and circular economy goals.

Both the global and European textile industry are undergoing a radical transformation shaped by political volatility, economic pressures, evolving social expectations, rapid technological innovation, tightening legal frameworks, and accelerating environmental challenges. The interplay of these factors is pushing the sector toward a new paradigm in which sustainability, transparency, resilience, and technological integration are no longer optional but essential for long-term competitiveness. In other words, the textile industry is entering a critical stage in which regulatory pressure, technological innovation, and shifting consumer expectations intersect to reshape how products are designed, produced, tracked, consumed, and recovered. Together, these forces are accelerating the transition from a linear model toward a more circular, transparent, and digitally enabled ecosystem. Despite persistent challenges such as limited recycling infrastructure, uneven consumer willingness to pay for sustainability, and cost pressures that still favour conventional production, the momentum toward circularity is strengthening, particularly as EPR schemes, DPPs, and eco-design requirements become central pillars of EU policy. At the same time, advancements in AI, IoT, automation, and new recycling technologies are unlocking efficiencies across the supply chain and enabling higher-quality reuse and textile-to-textile recycling. Yet, the full realisation of this transition will depend on coordinated action across brands, recyclers, policymakers, and technology providers, supported by significant investment in collection, sorting, and pre-processing systems. The TRUSTex envisaged technological solutions are in line with the needs and trends in the sector. Their market prospects are positive, but they need to be validated at industrial scale.

# 1. INTRODUCTION

The TRUSTex project is designed to strengthen the EPR scheme as a driving force behind circular business models across the EU, reinforcing Europe's leadership in sustainable innovation. By drawing on industry experience and expert guidance, TRUSTex tests practical approaches, identifies key challenges, and proposes tailored solutions to support the implementation of effective EPR systems. The project takes a holistic view, focusing on essential EPR elements such as textile collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling. It also incorporates forward-thinking practices like ecodesign, design for disassembly, and digital tools including the DPP. Continuous engagement with stakeholders ensures that TRUSTex recommendations remain relevant, feasible, and aligned with real-world needs.

Deliverable 1.5 is a component of Task 1.5 "Market and consumer trends", which according to the project's work plan, aims to present an in-depth analysis of current situation and dynamics within the textile sector, combining quantitative and qualitative research to assess economic factors and consumer behaviours both within the EU and in third countries. The methodology used was a comprehensive screening of scientific papers, reports and studies, as well as various news sources (during a "hot" period with relevant news), plus three targeted surveys that were conducted the last few months after concluding most of the desk research.

The report explores emerging trends across the value chain, critical drivers, gaps, and opportunities. Moreover, the relevant European legislative framework is presented, followed by technological specificities and the policy recommendations expected to derive from the project in WP7/Task 7.4. The TRUSTex KERs outlined, contribute to shaping the context in which project partners can position themselves. The deliverable also includes a SWOT and a PESTLE analysis to identify strategic focus areas and contextualize key drivers and barriers in different national markets. Lastly, changes influencing the commercial prospects in the post-project period are highlighted.

## 1.1 Purpose of the document

The purpose of Deliverable 1.5 is to provide a focused analysis of market structures, consumer behaviours, and emerging trends that directly influence the feasibility and design of EPR schemes in the textile sector. By grounding TRUSTex policy and exploitation activities in real-world market dynamics, the deliverable supports evidence-based decision-making across subsequent work packages and tasks.

## 1.2 Structure of the document

A market analysis can have different formats, length and depth. For this report, the project objectives and Task 1.5 description were the generators of the chapter definitions leading to a TRUSTex custom content specification.

## 1. Introduction

This introductory chapter sets the context of the deliverable by outlining its scope and structure.

cases. Emphasis is placed on the project's envisaged contribution to innovative EPR schemes and circular textile systems.

## 3. The Textile Industry

This section presents a multi-level analysis of the textile industry: globally, and within the EU. It includes market size, trends, legal frameworks, consumer behaviours, sustainability practices and other.

## 4. Textile Waste

In this part of the deliverable, particular focus is placed on the generation and management of textile waste. It highlights reuse practices, export dynamics, and traceability issues, especially in relation to third-country markets.

## 5. Textile Circularity

This chapter explores the current state of circularity in the textile sector at the global and EU levels and identifies challenges and best practices for closing the loop across the value chain.

## 6. Modern Trends and Drivers

Chapter 6 analyses emerging trends adopted by industry actors, such as sustainable fibres, ecolabelling, and digitalisation along with the influence of global initiatives.

## 7. EU Legislation

It summarizes key EU regulations affecting the textile sector, particularly those tied to sustainability, waste reduction, and producer responsibility obligations.

## 8. Blockchain, DPP and other TRUSTex relevant technologies specificities

Throughout this section, the technological specificities of several technologies are highlighted.

## 9. The Policy Recommendations Expectations from TRUSTex

This section outlines the expected contributions of TRUSTex to EU and national policymaking that are planned in later stages of the project.

## 10. The KERs of TRUSTex

Within this chapter, the project's Key Exploitable Results (KERs) are listed and described, highlighting their innovation potential and market orientation.

Presents a SWOT analysis focused on several important aspects relevant to current and future conditions.

Provides a PESTLE analysis at EU and national levels to contextualize market dynamics and geographical specificities.

This chapter addresses critical changes expected to influence the commercial prospects beyond the project's end.

The last chapter of the report is the deliverable's conclusions.

It should be noted that this report took into account other TRUSTex deliverables (e.g. D4.1 "Report on EPR scheme analysis") and activities, particularly of WP5, that took place (Online Hotspot event 9 October 2025, Rotterdam event 2-4 December 2025).

Also, adding on the desk research that resulted numerous sources and valuable input in a period "boiling" with regulatory changes and news concerning the sector, 3 focused surveys on a European level and 2 surveys on a national level (Greece), were conducted to further support with a recent, as much as possible (16 October – 16 December), evidence. In total 629 responses were collected resulting in useful contributions. Out of them, 335 responses were from across Europe and 294 from Greece.

# 2. ABOUT TRUSTEX

## 2.1 TRUSTex objectives

The main goal of TRUSTex is to support the European Commission, Member States, and businesses in making EPR schemes work in practice especially when it comes to collection, sorting, recycling, reuse, governance, and policy. At the same time, the project is contributing shape a more sustainable and circular textile industry by promoting eco-design and building transparent information systems. All efforts are closely aligned with the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles and the Waste Framework Directive. To get there, TRUSTex brings together partners to discuss, co-create, test, and recommend practical solutions, relying on innovation and strong stakeholder collaboration. To guide this process, the project is structured around eight clear objectives.

O1. Test and promote eco-design strategies for easy-to-sort/reuse/recycle fibres and garments

TRUSTex integrates upstream circularity solutions, including material composition, modular and disassemble garment design, recyclability, and supplier involvement, with a view to ESPR compliance and improved EPR performance.

O2. Demonstrate value chain data management practices, including traceability and labelling

The project pioneers the use of blockchain and IoT-based tracking, enabling product identification, sorting, reuse, and recycling, aligned with future DPP and labelling obligations.

O3. Study collection systems to increase capture of textile products for reuse and recycling TRUSTex evaluates high-quality collection systems, business models, and infrastructures for separate textile collections, identifying the most effective EPR-compatible collection solutions.

O4. Optimize material sorting and disassembly through technological implementation Advanced sorting and disassembly technologies will be piloted to increase identification accuracy, enable automatic disassembly, and improve EPR logistics.

O5. Reinforce the viability of the second-hand market against other options TRUSTex will assess economic and policy conditions (e.g. ownership rules, eco-modulation) that make reuse the most viable and preferred End-of-Life option under EPR schemes.

O6. Increase the legitimacy and effectiveness of EPR schemes through stakeholder dialogue and governance recommendations The project will engage stakeholders through structured dialogue and policy co-creation, to define legitimate, inclusive, and actionable governance models for EPR implementation.

O7. Robust assessment of current and future impacts of the textile sector, including thirdcountry externalities, circularity, and climate change and how to reflect it in EPR governance and policy measures

TRUSTex will assess material circularity, environmental impacts, economic externalities, and social justice issues in current and future textile value chains. Special emphasis will be placed on thirdcountry effects (e.g., Ghana and South Africa) and how EPR policies can address negative externalities and rebound effects. Findings will inform governance models and policy measures.

O8. Empower citizens for more sustainable choices and build the capacity of businesses to take action

TRUSTex will launch citizen engagement and awareness initiatives to promote responsible consumption, correct waste disposal, and support the uptake of second-hand textiles. In parallel, a capacity-building programme for businesses, particularly SMEs, will be deployed to facilitate the adoption of circular practices in compliance with EPR and sustainability regulations.

Figure 1. TRUSTex objectives

## 2.2 TRUSTex pilot cases

To demonstrate and validate key innovations across the textile value chain, TRUSTex is implementing a series of pilots focused on eco-design, digital infrastructure, circular logistics, and disassembly. These pilots engage industrial, digital, and policy partners in real-world settings to ensure technological feasibility, stakeholder relevance, and alignment with EPR principles. Below is a summary of the pilot cases, as presented in the TRUSTex Grant Agreement.

Pilot 1: Upscaled eco-designed fibres and garments

## WP: 3

Deliverables: D3.1, MS6

Description: Upscaled production of circular garments using eco-designed fibres for testing in sorting, reuse, and disassembly demonstrations. External contract manufacturers may support production.

Location: To be defined by manufacturer availability

Partners: CTB, RESORTECS, SANKO, RE&UP, CETIM, SCANTRUST, MADE2FLOW

Scale/Capacity: 200–300 kg per product

TRL: 6–8 (fibres/fabrics/garments), 6–7 (DPP)

Input: Eco-designed materials and DPP infrastructure

Output: Two product types – everyday wear and sportswear

Pilot 2: Disassembly of eco-designed products

## WP: 3

Deliverables: D3.4, MS7

Description: Demonstration of disassembly systems using heat-triggered fasteners and digital traceability tools to enable >95% material separation accuracy.

Location: RESORTECS (Belgium)

Partner: RESORTECS

Scale/Capacity: Pilot line

TRL: TRL4 to 6 (KER8) for the disassembly system

Input: DPP-tagged garments with removable components

Output: High purity feedstock of mono-material fabrics ready for recycling; trims that can be evaluated for reuse or recovered as well as RFID tags.

Pilot 3: Next-gen sorting technologies

## WP: 3

Deliverable: D3.5

Description: The "Next-gen sorting technologies" pilot is structured around two complementary implementations described below.

Pilot at RE&UP facility: Adaptation of RE&UP's advanced sorting systems using near-infrared and visual spectroscopy (NIR/VIS) linked to T3.1 & WP2 (eco-designed garments with data carrier tech linked to DPP, SCANTRUST), T3.4 (pre-assemble parts) and T3.5 RE&UP sorting techs. RE&UP's textile sorting technologies (textile-specific material handling, image recognition, sorting

of cut-parts, advanced machine learning) and facilities will be leveraged to tackle challenges such as efficient material identification and enhancing operational efficiency in sorting facilities. New sorting algorithms and classifiers (ejection parameters, NIR/VIS spectra, conveyor speed, metal detection, etc.) meeting the needs of the project will be developed and tested.

Location: RE&UP facility (textile specific line in Turkey)

Partners: RE&UP, IRIS

Scale/Capacity: Min 100 kg per batch (ideally more), 0,2 t/h is a typical throughput for initial trials, 1-4 t/h typical throughput for pilot. Actual volumes will depend also on the amounts provided by the previous WP3 tasks. Input and output volumes will be identical, as no material is lost during sorting. Up to 10 tons/day are covered by the current permit.

TRL: TRL5 to 7 (KER7)

Inputs/Outputs: Mixed "textile waste" of articles provided from T3.1 (everyday wear, sportswear), pre-sorted disassembled textile parts (T3.4); Other textile waste. / Materials separated based on composition, colour, etc.

Pilot at the RE&UP and IRIS facilities: Upgrade of IRIS multilayers advanced classifier system based on imaging technologies (NIR-HSI and/or RGB) linked to T3.5. IRIS sorting system, which will be based on Machine Learning (ML) will be capable of discriminating multilayer textile garments according to their composition, mainly focusing on the identification of cotton and PET rich textiles. New optical identification algorithms will be developed and tested, fulfilling the project needs.

Location: RE&UP facility and IRIS facility (Spain)

Partners: RE&UP, IRIS

Scale/Capacity: The prototype system at IRIS premises is designed for the recycling of multilayer garments, operating at an initial throughput approximately 30-48 kg per hour, based on an average garment weight of 500-800 grams. In an industrial environment, having access to bigger volumes, over a continuous manual feed of 8-hour operational shift, the system could be capable of handling up to 1 ton of textile material per day. This throughput is appropriate for early-stage trials and validation, enabling detailed monitoring, process optimisation, and classifier training under controlled pilot conditions.

TRL: TRL5 to 7 (KER7)

Inputs/Outputs: Mixed "textile waste" of articles provided from T3.1 (especially sportswear) Other textile waste; / Cotton and PET rich multilayer textiles.

### Pilot 4: Semiautomated sorting for reusing

## WP: 3

Deliverable: D3.3

Description: Study the mechanisms in which digital automated technologies can aid the process of manual sorting for reuse: The DPP and data carrier system (WP2) will be tested by entering a substantial number of garments marked with RFID/QR to a used clothing feeding line for one of the expert reuse sorters to test their separation based on defined criteria (e.g. brand) and possible improvement of the process both in speed and effectiveness, and in increasing reusability.

Location: Humana facility (Spain)

Partners: HUMANA /SCANTRUST

Scale/Capacity: Pre-pilot testing - Test will be done in one manual sorting line (daily capacity around 3,000 garments); Test will take place in an existing facility that has the needed permits.

TRL: 5–7

Inputs/Outputs: Feed of cloths including approximately several hundreds of pieces with RFID or QR (see above) / Garments with RFID/QR separated from the stream of used cloths.

The combination of the TRUSTex objectives and pilot cases forms the strategic and operational backbone of the project, enabling the development and validation of real-world solutions for circularity and EPR implementation. These activities are designed to respond directly to the practical needs and regulatory pressures of the textile sector. The following chapter provides a necessary context by examining the structure, trends, and challenges of the textile industry setting the stage for understanding where and how TRUSTex can make the greatest impact.

# 3. THE TEXTILE INDUSTRY

## 3.1 The textile industry globally

## 3.1.1 Market size and segmentation

## 3.1.1.1 Market size

Textile and apparel manufacturing is among the world's oldest industries. Because there is a relatively low barrier to entry, the principal requirement is low-cost labour. Τhe sector has traditionally served as a strategy for developing countries to establish and leverage an industrial knowledge base to systematically modernize and grow their economies.

In recent years, investments in logistics, telecommunications, predictive technologies, and manufacturing processes, all of which were prohibitively expensive in emerging markets and developing economies until recently, have helped countries integrate into more profitable value chains and improve the lives of millions of people (Manchanda, S. et al, 2020).

Global fibre production increased to a record of 124 million tonnes in 2023, up from 116 million tonnes in 2022. Since 2000, when production was 58 million tonnes, global fibre production has more than doubled and is expected to grow to 160 million tonnes in 2030. Polyester has contributed significantly to this growth, replacing cotton as the most widely used fibre in the world since the mid to late 2000s. Global fibre production per person has also increased significantly, growing from 8.3 kilograms per person in 1975 to 15.5 kilograms per person in 2023. This figure is expected to rise to 18.8 kilograms per person by 2030 (Textile Exchange, 2024).

The textile and apparel industry occupies a prominent position in global manufacturing. It was valued at \$1.3 trillion in 2023, 1.8 trillion in 2024 and is projected to increase from \$ 2,1 trillion in 2025 to approximately \$ 4 trillion by 2034, expanding at a CAGR of 7.35% from 2025 to 2034 (Precedence Research, 2025).

The rising demand for natural fibres globally is driving the growth of the textile market. The labourintensive sector provides substantial employment opportunities. Although the apparel and textile industries are usually considered separately, their interdependence and interrelated sectors make it worthwhile to treat them together. The number of people employed in the sector is over 430 million, representing around 12.6% of the global workforce (Szigethy-Ambrus, 2025) with women constituting around 75% of the global workforce.

## TEXTILE PRODUCTION

## Global textile fibre production has almost doubled:

![](_page_26_Picture_4.jpeg)

Figure 2. Projected EU Textile production. Source: ETC-CE Report 2023/5

The Textile & Clothing (T&C) value chain is extremely long and complex, spanning the whole world and having its various stages of production taking place in different countries, and also including the distribution of intermediate and final items and retail sale to final consumers. Most industrial production processes are located in developing countries (mainly in Asia), and the produced textiles and garments are transported to developed countries. After use, some of the textile waste is transported to developing countries (mainly to Africa). Textile waste streams are increasing in all countries, but especially in Western countries, in which the fashion consumers are mostly located (Niinimäki et al.,2024)

The textile market size has grown strongly in recent years. The growth can be attributed to growth in world population, increased demand for man-made fibres, government initiatives for the textile industry and strong economic growth in emerging markets.

The ever-increasing apparel demand from the fashion industry and the meteoric growth of ecommerce platforms are expected to drive market growth.

## 3.1.1.2 Market size by region

The global textile industry is a cornerstone of the world economy, encompassing a vast network of enterprises and a substantial workforce. The industry is expected to comprise around 278,600 enterprises worldwide by the end of 2025 and employ approximately 7.38 million individuals globally by 2025, growing at a CAGR of 2.54% between 2025 and 2029 (Statista, 2025).

Regarding a regional segmentation, the textile industry could be divided for the analysis in the following regions:

- North America
- Europe
- Asia-Pacific
- Latin America
- Middle East & Africa

North America held the largest market share in 2023, accounting for approximately 35% of the market. The region's growth is driven by factors such as increasing disposable income, growing demand for home textiles, and rising consumer spending on apparel and accessories (Jaiswal, 2025).

Europe is the second largest market for textiles, with a market share of around 28% in 2023.The region's growth is supported by a strong fashion industry, a large consumer base, and technological advancements in textile manufacturing (Jaiswal, 2025).

![](_page_27_Figure_9.jpeg)

Figure 3. Global textile industry market size by region. Source: Jaiswal, C., 2025

Asia-Pacific is projected to be the fastest-growing region in the textile market, with a CAGR of over 5% from 2023 to 2032. The region's growth is primarily attributed to the increasing population, rising urbanization, and expanding middle class. The growth is also driven by the easy availability of raw silk along with rising demand for fashionable clothes and home furnishing commodities. Moreover, the rising use of e-commerce for buying apparel, along with the rising young age population that tends to buy designer and fashionable clothes, is driving the growth of the textile market. Also, the rising interest of people in modelling and fashion designing, coupled with the increasing trend of wearing imported clothes, further accelerates market growth positively. Additionally, increased investments from the governments of countries such as India, China, Bangladesh, and others, have also boosted the market growth. Furthermore, prominent market players such as Arvind Limited, Vardhman Textiles Ltd., Welspun India Ltd., Reliance Industries Limited, Aditya Birla Group, Raymond Ltd, and others, are adopting strategies to strengthen their customer base and maintain their dominance in the industry (Precedence Research, 2025).

South America and the Middle East are relatively smaller markets for textiles, but they are expected to witness steady growth in the coming years due to factors such as population growth, economic development, and increasing demand for textiles in various industries (Precedence Research, 2025).

Some of the major players operating in the textile market are BSL Limited, INVISTA S.R.L., Lu Thai Textile Co., Ltd., Heytex Bramsche GmbH, B.D. Textile Mills Pvt. Ltd., DBL Group, IBENA Inc., Levis, Lakhmi Woollen Mills, Wilh. Wülfing GmbH & Co. KG, Wuxi Xiexin Group Co., Ltd., O'Formula Co., Ltd., The Bombay Dyeing & Mfg. Co., Ltd, Adidas AG, Mayur Fabrics, Huafu Top Dyed Melange Yarn Co., Ltd., and Nike Inc. (Grand View Research, 2024).

## 3.1.1.3 Segmentation

Textile and apparel products can be categorized based on the following features:

## (1) Raw material

- Natural fibres
- Synthetic fibers

Natural fibers are fibers obtained from nature, primarily extracted from plants or animals. These fibers are biodegradable, possess eco-friendly properties and can be broadly classified into plantbased (cotton, derived from cotton plants, or linen, obtained from the flax plant), or animal-based (wool, obtained from sheep, or silk, obtained from silkworm cocoons). (Eun Hyup Kim 2025) Synthetic fibers refer to human-made fibers derived from petrochemical products. These fibers are not biodegradable but their use grew exponentially in the last century, and today they play a significant role in clothing, household products, and industrial applications. Key synthetic fibers include polyester, nylon, and acrylic. (Anneke Smelik 2023).

## (2) By product

- Silk
- Cotton
- Wood
- Polyesters
- Nylon
- Others

The global textile industry market is subdivided by products into silk, wool, chemical fibres (polyesters, nylon, etc.), cotton, and others. Among these, cotton is the most dominant fibre due to its versatility, softness, and ubiquity. It is largely applied in garments, home textile, and industry. Other than cotton, synthetic fabrics such as polyester and other chemical-textile goods have gained much market share on the basis of their durability and low cost. Silk and wool occupy niche positions, particularly in luxury and specialty applications, but cotton and synthetics are the industry's pillars.

![](_page_29_Figure_4.jpeg)

Figure 4. Global fibre production (million tonnes). Source: Textile Exchange 2024

In 2024, cotton was dominant among natural fibres because of increasing demand across different industries, including clothing, medical dressings, and apparel. The universal supply of cotton in major producing countries like China, India, and the U.S. has also contributed to its market growth. In addition, the increase in cotton exports for uses such as diapers and home decor, along with continued research and development of methods to improve the production of cotton, has driven the growth of the market.

On the other hand, the wool sector is expected to see the highest growth in the future. The growth is driven by its higher utilization in the production of insulation materials, carpets, and upholstery. (Precedence Research, 2025).

Polyester is the most dominant fibre due to its versatility, durability, and cost-effectiveness. It is widely used in apparel, home textiles, and industrial applications. Polyester's resistance to wrinkling, shrinking, and fading makes it a preferred choice for manufacturers. While nylon and natural fibres like cotton and wool also hold significant market shares, polyester's adaptability and the rise of synthetic blends contribute to its leading position in the textile industry.

This segment is generally driven by factors such as the rising use of apparel and fashion industry and increasing awareness regarding sustainable clothing. Also, the growing demand for natural fibres for designing the interiors of cars and the rising application of natural fibres for medical usage has also accelerated the growth of the textile market. Moreover, the increasing application of natural fibres for making ropes and paper production further drives the growth of the textile industry. The polyester segment is expected to grow at a significant rate. The growing demand for polymers due to their cost-effectiveness, resilience, and durability has driven the growth of the market. Also, the rising use of polyester for the production of jackets, sportswear, and gym wear, along with its

usage in the production of tires and safety belts for cars, has further boosted the growth of the textile market (Precedence Research 2025).

### (3) Final application

- Household (bedding, kitchen, upholstery, towel)
- Technical (construction, transport, medical, protective)
- Fashion & Clothing (apparel, ties and clothing accessories, handbags, others)

The fashion and clothing segment is the most dominant category. This sector encompasses a wide range of products, including apparel, accessories, and footwear driven by changing consumer preferences and fashion trends. The demand for fast fashion and sustainable clothing further propels growth in this segment. While technical textiles and household textiles also contribute significantly to the market, the fashion and clothing sector remains the leading driver due to its substantial consumer base and continuous innovation.

## 3.1.1.4 Geographical poles

The global textile and apparel sector is one of the most geographically dispersed industries, with production processes fragmented and allocated across diverse regions. These geographical poles reflect varied stages of the global value chain where countries specialize in different functions, from raw material processing and mass manufacturing to branding, design, and retail. The spatial organization of this industry is not static but evolves in response to capitalist crises, trade liberalization, labour market changes, and technological developments (Montero Bressán, 2022; Rodil-Marzábal et al., 2022).

## East and South Asia: the global manufacturing core

Asia, particularly China, Bangladesh, Vietnam, Cambodia, and India, remains the principal manufacturing centre for textiles and garments. These countries have gained a competitive edge due to:

❖ Low labour costs, access to large labour pools, and industrial infrastructure ❖ Significant foreign direct investment and export-oriented industrial policies ❖ The rise of full-package suppliers capable of OEM/ODM services (Montero Bressán, 2022; Rodil-Marzábal et al., 2022).

China, though experiencing rising labour costs and transitioning toward higher-value segments, still accounted for around one-third of global apparel exports by the mid-2010s (WTO, 2023). However, production has started to shift toward lower-cost countries such as Bangladesh and Cambodia in response to wage pressures and trade tensions (Montero Bressán, 2022).

As of December 2024, the ready-made garments industry in Bangladesh earned \$50 billion from exports, marking an 8.3% increase from the previous year. The sector employs over 5 million workers, with women constituting 55% of the workforce (Mondal et al., 2025).

India holds a significant position in global textile manufacturing. It is the world's second-largest producer of textiles and garments. It is also the fifth-largest exporter of textiles spanning apparel, home, and technical products. The textiles and apparel industry contributes 2.3% to the country's GDP, 13% to industrial production and 10.5% to exports. The textile industry in India is predicted to double its contribution to the GDP, rising from 2.3% to approximately 5% by the end of this

decade. India has a 4.6% share of the global trade in textiles and apparel. Moreover, India is the world's third largest exporter of textiles and apparel. India ranks among the top five global exporters in several textile categories. In April-June 2025, exports of readymade garments including accessories stood at US\$ 2,244 million. The textile sector is expected to play a significant role, with a target of US\$ 100 billion in exports by 2030, up from US\$ 34.43 billion in 2024. In April- September 2025 the total exports of textiles stood at US\$ 21.35 billion. Around 45 million people are working in the textile business, including 3.5 million people who work on handlooms. (Indian Brand Equity Foundation, 2025).

## Europe: High-value production and regional nearshoring

Western Europe plays a leading role in design, innovation, advanced manufacturing, and branding, especially in high-end and technical textiles. Countries such as Italy, France, Germany, and Portugal have maintained strong positions through quality production, technological advancement, and integration with fashion hubs (Copenhagen Economics, 2025). Western Europe is also a centre for sustainable materials, circular business models, and eco-certification systems, with strong participation from Germany and the Nordic countries in developing sustainable solutions (Textile Exchange, 2024). Simultaneously, there has been a shift toward nearshoring in Eastern Europe and the Mediterranean (e.g., Turkey, Tunisia, Romania) (Montero Bressán, 2022). This regionalization is aligned with broader efforts to reduce dependence on distant Asian suppliers and to respond faster to changing consumer preferences.

## North America: Market leadership and assembly integration

The United States and Mexico constitute a North American pole characterized by asymmetric integration. The U.S. leads in retail, branding, and logistics, while Mexico functions as a key assembly location within global value chains, especially under the USMCA (United States–Mexico– Canada Agreement). Mexico's high backward global value chain participation reflects its reliance on imported inputs (e.g., fabrics, trims) for garment assembly, which are then re-exported to the U.S. (Rodil-Marzábal et al., 2022). This production model supports a just-in-time supply structure but also exposes Mexico to vulnerabilities tied to U.S. demand and trade policy fluctuations. The textile and apparel industry in Latin American and Caribbean (LAC) countries plays a strategic role in regional economies, especially in terms of employment, exports, and industrial development. While the region represents a relatively small share of global apparel exports (around 4%), it is highly significant at the national level, particularly in countries like Honduras, El Salvador, Guatemala, and Nicaragua, where the sector accounts for a substantial share of total exports and manufacturing employment. The industry benefits from geographical proximity to the U.S. market, especially under trade agreements like Dominican Republic-Central America FTA (CAFTA-DR), which offer tariff-free access under certain rules of origin. This has helped sustain competitiveness in basic apparel production, especially cotton-based items. However, limited diversification, high labour costs, and weak backward linkages in synthetic textiles have constrained the region's ability to move up the value chain (Fernández-Stark et al.,2022)

## Sub-Saharan Africa: Emerging low-cost manufacturing pole

In recent years, Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA), notably Ethiopia and Kenya, has emerged as a lowcost textile and apparel production hub (Lu,2025), stimulated by:

❖ Rising labour costs in Asia ❖ U.S. trade preferences under the African Growth and Opportunity Act (AGOA<sup>1</sup> )

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

<sup>1</sup> expired in September 2025

❖ Investments in industrial parks and public infrastructure.

Despite these advantages, the region faces challenges such as inadequate infrastructure, skills gaps, and limited integration into high-value functions of the global value chain. Relevant studies (Lu,2025) identified significant challenges for Sub Saharan African countries serving as immediate alternatives to sourcing for example from Asia for U.S. fashion companies. While SSA countries could offer relatively low sourcing costs, the range of apparel products available for U.S. fashion companies to source from the SSA region remained significantly more limited than those from Asia.

## South America: Regional integration and vertical models

Countries like Brazil, Colombia, and Peru have retained vertically integrated textile industries that primarily serve domestic and regional markets. Brazil, in particular, maintains a comprehensive supply chain from fibre to final product, supported by a large consumer base and protective industrial policies. However, limited export competitiveness and high production costs have constrained these economies' roles in global textile trade. Instead, their focus lies in niche markets and regional supply, rather than global value chain-based expansion (Rodil-Marzábal et al., 2022).

The spatial organization of the textile industry reflects broader processes of capital accumulation. Following the 2008 financial crisis, there was both a deepening of offshoring to low-wage Asian economies and a partial trend toward nearshoring in proximity to Western markets, especially in response to fast fashion dynamics and logistics volatility (Montero Bressán, 2022). Moreover, multinational firms continually reconfigure their production geographies in search of cost efficiency, political stability, and market responsiveness. This ongoing process results in dynamic but also uneven development patterns that define the spatial poles of the global textile economy.

## 3.1.2 Economic conditions

The textile industry is deeply globalized, operating within intricate global value chains that encompass raw material sourcing, manufacturing, and distribution across various regions. Key economic factors shaping the sector include:

## Global demand and export dynamics

Rising global population levels and increasing disposable incomes, particularly in emerging markets, have driven up demand for textiles and apparel. Countries like China, India, Bangladesh, and Vietnam have emerged as major exporters, significantly contributing to their economies through foreign exchange earnings and job creation. The Asia-Pacific region dominates the global textile market, holding the dominant market share. This strong position is largely due to the concentration of large-scale manufacturing facilities in countries such as China, India, and Bangladesh. The region's advantages include low labour costs, supportive government policies, and well-established supply chains (G. Peter Tessa, 2024).

The European textile industry functions within a globally dispersed value chain. While product design and development are typically concentrated in Europe, the bulk of manufacturing takes place in Asia, with notable contributions from Turkey and North Africa (Copenhagen Economics, 2025). This structure capitalizes on comparative advantage. Labour-intensive tasks are assigned to low-wage regions, while Europe focuses on high-value-added activities such as technical textiles and fashion design. However, the globalization of supply chains also introduces complexity and risks. Firms face growing uncertainty due to geopolitical instability and supply chain disruptions.

These challenges are motivating diversification strategies and sparking debate over reshoring production to Europe although such moves are frequently deemed economically unviable due to high labour costs and structural limitations in manufacturing capacity (Copenhagen Economics, 2025).

## Rising global population and urbanisation in the developing countries

In 1950, the global population stood at approximately 2.5 billion. By 2021, the global population has surged past 7.7 billion, and current projections estimate it will reach around 9.7 billion by 2050 (UN, 2024). This rapid population growth has inevitably led to increased demand for essential goods, with textiles being among the most significant. Urbanization is also accelerating, as individuals migrate from rural areas to cities in search of better economic opportunities and improved living conditions. Much of this growth originates from expanding middle classes in regions of Asia and Africa. These demographic groups, gaining purchasing power, are transforming from merely survival-oriented consumers to trend-conscious shoppers. This shift places further emphasis on the production capabilities of textile manufacturers globally. As a result, consumer behaviour is shifting. Items once considered luxuries, such as fashionable clothing, are now seen as everyday necessities. With rising disposable incomes, particularly in developing economies, people are more inclined to invest in garments that express their identity and social status (Gold Fortune Textile, 2025, Soni, 2025, Biradar, 2025).

## Technological advancements and "Industry 4.0"

The emergence of textile "Industry 4.0" is driving transformative change. Automation, artificial intelligence (AI), and the Internet of Things (IoT) are improving manufacturing precision, enabling predictive analytics, and reducing material waste (Soni, 2025). These innovations are not only enhancing productivity but also aligning with sustainability objectives by minimizing resource consumption.

A significant trend is the emergence of smart textiles, fabrics embedded with electronic components, that are gaining popularity in healthcare, sportswear, and fashion. These materials offer enhanced features such as temperature regulation, health monitoring, and interactive functionalities (Harshita, 2024).

Developed markets, particularly in North America and Western Europe, are leveraging these technologies to revitalize domestic production and develop high-value products such as smart textiles for sports, healthcare and defence applications (Fact MR, 2025, Saniyat & Ravi, 2025, Market Research Future. (n.d.), EDA, 2024).

## Trade policies and market access

The textile sector has benefited from international trade agreements that lower tariffs and enable access to the European market, such as the EU's Generalised Scheme of Preferences (GSP) and Everything But Arms (EBA) initiative. These policies have facilitated the growth of textile manufacturing in developing countries, thereby integrating them into global value chains (Copenhagen Economics, 2025). However, tariff differences such as 0% for Turkey versus 12% for China continue to influence sourcing and supply chain decisions. At the same time, the fragmentation of EU legislation creates administrative burdens for companies, increasing compliance costs and limiting the sector's agility. The need for harmonized international regulations is critical to improving the industry's operational efficiency and global competitiveness (Business Europe,2025).

Sustainability is a central concern within the textile industry, stimulated by increased consumer awareness and tighter environmental regulations. The use of eco-friendly materials, including organic cotton and recycled fibres, is growing steadily. Moreover, sustainable production practices, such as water-efficient dyeing and improved waste management, are becoming more widespread. The environmental impact of the textile industry, especially its reliance on virgin fossil-based materials, has triggered widespread concern and regulatory scrutiny. According to Textile Exchange (2024), the industry must reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 45% from raw material sourcing by 2030 to meet climate targets. To achieve this, the sector must move from a linear "takemake-waste" model to a circular economy. Key strategies include using recycled or renewable materials, designing for durability, and investing in closed-loop systems. The industry is also urged to redefine value, reduce production volumes and by all means promote environmental sustainability and social equity (Textile Exchange, 2024).

## Regional specialisation in production

The textile industry development is heavily influenced by regional specialization. Europe focuses on high-value segments such as design, advanced manufacturing, and marketing. In contrast, Asia and North Africa concentrate on labour-intensive processes, including raw material preparation and garment assembly. Labour cost variations play a crucial role in determining production locations, with Asia maintaining a competitive edge due to its lower wages and mature industrial infrastructure (Copenhagen Economics, 2025).

## Labour market considerations

As a labour-intensive sector, textiles are a major employer in developing economies. In Turkey, for example, the region of İzmir plays a key role in national textile production and exports. However, low wages, precarious work conditions, and labour rights concerns persist in many sourcing countries (UNDP, 2021). Ensuring a just transition toward sustainability, including decent work, fair wages, and social inclusion is essential for the sector's resilience and legitimacy in the face of growing public interest and legislative pressure (Textile Exchange, 2024).

## Crisis resilience and demand volatility

The COVID-19 pandemic exposed the vulnerability of the textile sector to external shocks. Companies reported production halts, raw material shortages, logistics delays, and declining demand. For example, in Turkey's textile hubs, turnover and exports fell significantly during the peak of the pandemic (UNDP, 2021). These disruptions have accelerated efforts to build resilience, including supply chain diversification, enhanced digitalization, and risk management strategies. Moving forward, resilience will be key to maintaining continuity in an increasingly unstable global environment.

## Cost competitiveness and reshoring dilemmas

Although reshoring is seen as a means to improve supply chain transparency and environmental performance, it poses economic and structural challenges. European countries generally lack cost competitiveness for mass textile production due to higher labour costs, labour shortages, and limited manufacturing infrastructure. Moreover, reshoring may undermine the developmental role of the sector in lower-income countries by reducing employment and trade opportunities (Copenhagen Economics, 2025).

## 3.1.3 Legal conditions

The global textile sector is subject to a complex and evolving landscape of legal regulations that influence production, trade, environmental performance, and labour conditions. These regulations are shaped by national laws, international agreements, and regional policies, particularly from major markets like the European Union, the United States and China. Here is described the international legal framework. EU legislation is described in chapter 3.2.

## Environmental and circular economy regulations

Environmental regulations are increasingly pivotal as the textile industry is one of the most resource-intensive and polluting sectors. Key instruments include:

⮚ US Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) and California's Proposition 65: These regulate chemical safety in textile imports, with California imposing warning labels for exposure to harmful substances. ⮚ The New York Fashion Act (Bill S4746B) or the Fashion Sustainability and Social Accountability Act is the first legislation of its kind worldwide to propose sustainability requirements for the fashion industry and retail sellers. The law mandates fashion brands generating over \$100 million and selling in New York to disclose impacts like greenhouse gas emissions and chemical use by mapping at least 50% of their supply chains. ⮚ French AGEC Regulative. The Anti-Waste Law for a Circular Economy is designed to shift production and consumption models to reduce waste and conserve natural resources, biodiversity, and climate. Law number 2020-105, enacted on February 10, 2020, represents a major advance in the ecological transition. It particularly impacts the textile sector (clothing, linen, and footwear), which is increasingly focused on sustainable development. This legislation seeks to transform the traditional linear economy of "produce, consume, dispose" into a circular economy.

## Labour and human rights legislation

Textile production often occurs in regions with complex labour dynamics. Key regulations include:

⮚ International Labour Organization (ILO) Core Conventions: Binding for member states, these protect workers' rights, including freedom of association, collective bargaining, and prohibition of forced or child labour [ILO, 2024]. ⮚ Uyghur Forced Labour Prevention Act (UFLPA) in the US (2022): Bans imports of goods, including textiles, suspected to be linked to forced labour in China's Xinjiang region [US CBP, 2023]. ⮚ German Supply Chain Due Diligence Act: In 2021, the German Parliament enacted the Act on Corporate Due Diligence Obligations in Supply Chains (Gesetz über die unternehmerischen Sorgfaltspflichten in Lieferketten, LkSG), legally mandating German enterprises to uphold human rights in their global supply chains. The Act obliges enterprises in Germany to implement specified due diligence measures to respect human rights. It applies to enterprises with their central administration, principal place of business, administrative headquarters, Statutory seat, or Branch office in Germany. The Act initially affected enterprises in Germany with at least 3,000 employees starting in 2023 and will extend to those with at least 1,000 employees starting in 2024. The Act on Corporate Due Diligence Obligations in Supply Chains (LkSG) significantly impacts the textile industry by mandating German companies to ensure human rights compliance throughout their global supply chains. This requires textile businesses to enhance transparency, conduct supplier audits, and implement robust due diligence processes, thereby mitigating legal, financial, and reputational risks associated with

non-compliance.

⮚ UK and Australia's Modern Slavery Acts: The Modern Slavery Act is a leading piece of global legislation addressing modern slavery and human trafficking in the UK. While not all sections are directly relevant to businesses, section 54, titled 'Transparency in Supply Chains' has a significant impact on the corporate sector. Effective from 29th October 2015, the Act requires many businesses to disclose a slavery and human trafficking statement. ⮚ Dutch Child Labour Due Diligence Act. The Dutch Child Labour Due Diligence Act was passed by the Dutch Senate in 2019. It became effective in mid-2022 and mandates that companies examine their supply chains for child labour, and if found, take steps to mitigate and prevent it. ⮚ The Fashioning Accountability and Building Real Institutional Change (FABRIC) Act is a proposed federal legislation aimed at revitalizing the US domestic garment industry and establishing the US as a global leader in responsible apparel production through new workplace protections and manufacturing incentives. This bill would take the protections for workers' wages now in effect as of January 2022 with California's Garment Worker Protection Act nationwide. The Act attempts to create a \$50 million annual support program through the Department of Labour, which will provide grants and technical assistance to manufacturers while holding brands accountable for their labour practices. ⮚ Bangladesh's RMG (readymade garments) Compliance Framework is a multi-layered system designed to ensure labour rights, workplace safety, and environmental standards within the country's vital garment sector. Grounded in the Bangladesh Labour Act (2006) and monitored by government bodies like the Department of Inspection for Factories and Establishments (DIFE), the framework is supported by key industry stakeholders such as the Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA) and international initiatives like the Accord on Fire and Building Safety and its successor, the RMG Sustainability Council (RSC). Since the Rana Plaza tragedy<sup>2</sup> in 2013, the framework has evolved to include factory inspections, fire and building safety audits, worker training, and remediation plans. Despite progress, challenges remain in enforcement, worker protection, and environmental compliance, calling for continued collaboration between the government, industry and global partners.

## Trade and customs regulations

Trade policies and origin rules significantly shape global textile flows:

⮚ Agreement on Textiles and Clothing (ATC) of the World Trade Organization (WTO) ended quotas in 2005 but left market access contingent on compliance with other rules. ⮚ Technical Barriers to Trade (TBT) Agreement – Ensures that technical regulations (like labelling or safety standards) do not become unfair trade barriers (WTO). ⮚ Trade Facilitation Agreement (TFA) – Simplifies customs procedures, helping textile exports/imports flow more efficiently (WTO). ⮚ Rules of Origin and Customs Codes: Vary across jurisdictions and affect eligibility for preferential treatment, often requiring verification of sustainable or ethical sourcing. ⮚ Trade Preference Programs – The U.S. offers duty-free access under the African Growth and Opportunity Act (AGOA), Caribbean Basin Trade Partnership Act (CBTPA), and Generalized System of Preferences (GSP).
