⮚ USMCA (United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement) – Includes yarn-forward rules of origin affecting textile content sourcing. ⮚ ASEAN Free Trade Area (AFTA) – Reduces duties across Southeast Asian textile exporters. ⮚ RCEP (Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership) – Covers 15 Asia-Pacific countries, harmonizing tariffs and rules of origin.

### Consumer protection

Textile products are increasingly regulated in terms of transparency and consumer information:

⮚ Consumer Protection from Unfair Trading Regulations (UK) and similar instruments globally require that product claims, especially related to sustainability, are clear, truthful, and verifiable. ⮚ United Nations Guidelines for Consumer Protection (UNGCP). ⮚ ISO Standards (International Organization for Standardization). ISO standards are often referenced in consumer protection laws, such as ISO 3758:2012 – Care labelling code using symbols for textiles, ISO 20471:2013 – High-visibility clothing for safety, ISO 26000 – Guidance on social responsibility, including consumer rights. ⮚ OECD Guidelines for Consumer Product Safety. ⮚ U.S. Consumer Product Safety Improvement Act (CPSIA, 2008). ⮚ Canada's Consumer Product Safety Act (CCPSA). ⮚ ASEAN Guidelines on Consumer Protection. Adopted by 10 Southeast Asian countries to harmonize consumer rights and product safety. Encourages enforcement of standards for labelling, product information, and dispute resolution, relevant to textiles and garments. ⮚ Voluntary Sustainability and Ethical Labels. While not legislative, these international labels influence consumer protection by ensuring transparency and safe product claims: OEKO-TEX Standard 100 – Tests for harmful substances in textiles. Fair Trade, GOTS (Global Organic Textile Standard) – Cover social, environmental, and safety aspects. Some national laws recognize these certifications as part of compliance (e.g., in Japan, South Korea, and Switzerland).

## 3.1.4 Trends and dynamics

The world of manufacturing is changing. Digitalization is transforming the manufacturing and textile industry. Fourth Industrial Revolution (Industry 4.0) is a neologism describing rapid technological advancement in the 21st century. In essence, the Fourth Industrial Revolution is the trend towards automation and data exchange in manufacturing technologies and processes which include cyberphysical systems (CPS), Internet of Things (IoT), cloud computing, cognitive computing, and artificial intelligence (Ross, 2021).

The rise of Industry 4.0 is also changing the game in the textile sector. New approaches are making textile manufacturing more efficient and sustainable than ever before. AI is helping brands predict fashion trends, streamline supply chains, and create more personalized shopping experiences. Automation is boosting production efficiency, reducing human errors, and maintaining consistent quality. IoT is transforming manufacturing, allowing real-time tracking of machinery, materials, and inventory. These advancements are not just making production faster. They are also pushing the textile sector toward a more sustainable and customer-centric future. Moreover, textile companies investing in smart manufacturing techniques are gaining a competitive edge by reducing waste and improving production timelines (Vaish, 2025).

Furthermore, there has been an increasing trend in the use of smart textiles in the market that use optical fibres, metals, and various conductive polymers to interact with the environment. The rapidly growing consumer preference towards sustainable products is forcing major textile companies to focus on restructuring their business and investing in manufacturing practices that target sustainable products (Grand View Research, 2024). Moreover, fashion is gaining increasingly higher importance in consumers' lifestyles. This can be attributed to the constant exposure to advertisements that results in impulsive buying. In addition, social media is another major factor adding to the rising demand for fashionable wear.

The textile industry will focus on sustainability, traceable supply chains, circular fashion, and advanced technologies. Gen Z's ethical priorities will boost slow fashion and premium quality. AI, automation, and wellness fabrics will drive innovation, while trade policies and microfinance will reshape global supply chains and sourcing (Casden, 2024).

Trends and drivers are also highlighted in other parts of the present document.

## 3.1.5 Consumer behaviour towards textile consumption

Textile consumption habits significantly contribute to environmental degradation, mainly through waste production but also through the consumption of energy and natural resources. Creating policies that are impactful, inclusive, and effective, requires an understanding of consumer motives, habits, and barriers.

## 3.1.5.1 Dominant trends in consumer behaviour

The dominant trend in the textile and clothing market since 1990 and onwards is the "fast fashion model". Fast fashion is considered a "fashion market system that dictates almost daily new trends, seasonal fashions and produces new collections that feed unbridled consumption" (Silva de Gregori & Prestes Maier, 2023). Since the end of the 1990s, the most dynamic fashion industry has been the so-called fast fashion industry (Miranda, J., & Roldan, A., 2023). This practice, despite having started in 1970, was only denominated as such later, in 1990. This model has grown faster than the fashion industry as a whole and its market share has increased, driven by the international expansion of companies such as Spain's Inditex (the parent company of Zara), Sweden's H&M, Japan's Fast Retailing (Uniqlo), America's Gap, Ireland's Primark, and Britain's Topshop (Miranda, J., & Roldan, A., 2023). According to Girelli et al.,2020, consumers wear the pieces of clothing considered fast fashion less than five times, generating 400% more carbon emissions. Several huge retail chains use fast fashion as a business model, including Zara and H&M.

Furthermore, the fast fashion model is defined by four key characteristics that set it apart from other fashion models: cheapness, quickness, trendiness and mass produced (Commonwealth Human Ecology Council, 2022). These elements are deeply interconnected and work together to sustain the fast fashion business model.

Cheap: Fast fashion clothing is designed to be produced at minimal cost. The lower the production cost, the greater the profit margin. To achieve this, brands often rely on low-wage labour and inferior-quality materials. While this reduces expenses, it is directly associated with human rights violations and environmental degradation, making cheapness one of the most ethically

troubling aspects of the model.

Quick: The faster an item is manufactured, the quicker it can reach store shelves and consumers. Time equates to money. To accelerate production, companies often sacrifice labour standards and material quality. This constant release of new products encourages consumers to buy the latest items before even wearing what they previously purchased, fostering a culture of overconsumption.

Trendy: Fast fashion thrives on replicating the latest trends. Garments are designed to mirror current styles seen on runways, influencers, and celebrities, making them more appealing to consumers. The industry fuels social pressure and insecurity, pushing people to stay fashionable by frequently updating their wardrobes. This manipulation of consumer psychology ensures consistent demand.

Mass-Produced: Mass production is at the core of fast fashion's profitability. Clothes are manufactured in high volumes to reduce costs and meet rapid trend cycles. However, this leads to excess stock and waste, as many items remain unsold and are either heavily discounted or discarded.

These four elements are interdependent. Speed supports trend responsiveness, mass production keeps costs down, and low prices drive continuous consumer purchases. While this model is effective in maximizing profit, it also exposes the systemic issues of fast fashion: environmental harm, labour exploitation, and unsustainable consumption.

## 3.1.5.2 Key factors influencing consumer behaviour

It is essential to thoroughly understand the factors that play an important role in purchasing decisions. These factors can be grouped as follows.

## Strengthened decision-making consumer abilities

In the digital age, consumers have unprecedented access to extensive information. They can readily research products, compare prices, consult reviews, and obtain insights prior to making purchasing decisions. This accessibility has enabled consumers to make more informed and autonomous choices. Rather than depending exclusively on advertisements or sales strategies, consumers increasingly rely on authentic user reviews and peer recommendations. Consequently, businesses must prioritise the cultivation of a positive online reputation, the provision of transparent and accurate information, and the consistent delivery of high-quality products or services (Patil S.

## G., 2024).

## Dominance of e-commerce and the decisive role of mobile phones

The digital era has precipitated a fundamental transformation in consumer purchasing behaviour, marked by a substantial migration towards online shopping. eCommerce platforms have redefined convenience by enabling consumers to engage in product exploration and transactions remotely, thus obviating the need for physical store visits. These platforms not only provide an extensive array of product options and competitive pricing but also leverage consumer data to deliver personalized recommendations based on individual preferences and historical purchasing patterns. This paradigm shift has exerted considerable pressure on traditional retailers, compelling them to either establish a robust digital presence or implement integrated strategies to maintain relevance and competitiveness within an increasingly dynamic market environment. In this totally different from previous decades shopping environment, decisive roles played smartphones and their increasingly upgraded technical capabilities. Smartphones have become integral to consumers' lives, transforming how they interact with brands. Mobile phones offer privacy, convenience, quick

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

access to online commerce allowing potential buyers to search, shop and even interact in real time anytime and anywhere they desire. Therefore, businesses optimize their websites and social media accounts for mobile devices in order to ensure continuous navigation, fast loading times, and a user-friendly interface. Gradually shopping applications for mobiles are gaining ground because they provide personalized experiences for the customer and customer loyalty for the supplier.

## Social media influence and micro-trends

Social media platforms like Instagram, TikTok, and Pinterest have drastically shortened fashion trend cycles, encouraging fast turnover and impulsive buying behaviours. Consumers now expect near-instant access to emerging trends, leading to a significant shift in how they perceive and purchase textiles (Goetting, A. et al., 2022). Consumers often seek recommendations and opinions from their peers on social media before purchasing. Influencers with a significant following and expertise in specific niches can also sway consumer opinions and preferences. Businesses have recognized the impact of social media and are actively leveraging these platforms to engage with their target audience, build brand awareness, and drive sales. With micro-trends reshaping consumer behaviour at unprecedented speeds, traditional demand forecasting is no longer sufficient. Businesses choose to shift toward flexible merchandising and agile marketing to stay competitive (Huber, G., 2022).

## Demand for personalization and customization

The digital era has led to an era of personalised experiences. Modern consumers seek products that reflect their individuality. Customized textile products, whether in sizing, design, or styling, significantly enhance consumer engagement and satisfaction. Brands leverage artificial intelligence and big data analytics to deliver personalized experiences and product recommendations. Brands utilize artificial intelligence and data analytics to create personalized recommendations and curated shopping experiences (Ahmed, M. et al., 2024). Personalisation extends beyond the mere use of customers' names; it encompasses the curation of product recommendations, the delivery of relevant content, and the provision of tailored promotions informed by prior interactions and purchasing behaviour. By prioritising personalisation, businesses can improve customer satisfaction, strengthen relational bonds, and cultivate loyalty within an increasingly competitive marketplace.

## Demographic variables

According to the literature review, many studies on marketing and consumer behaviour indicate that age and gender are the two most important demographic variables (Rahman et al.,2020). Clothing plays a very important role in the self-formation and socialization process, especially among the youth. According to previous studies younger consumers tended to pay more attention to the aesthetic aspects of a product (want-based/hedonic attributes), whereas older ones were more concerned with their functional values (need-based attributes) (Rahman et al.,2020). Regarding gender, research of female apparel consumers indicated that garment fit, style, comfort, and quality were often considered to be important determinants, but not as highly regarded as those related to aesthetics (Stuart,2019).

## Technological advancements

Technological advancements have emerged as a pivotal driver influencing consumer behaviour in the textile industry. Innovations such as smart textiles, e-commerce platforms, 3D body scanning, augmented reality (AR) fitting rooms, and sustainable production technologies, have significantly reshaped consumer expectations, purchasing habits, and brand loyalty.

### Sustainability concerns

Last but not least, purchase decisions are influenced by growing environmental consciousness, which forces companies to match their products with sustainable methods (Kemi & Zilahy, 2025). This is further elaborated in the next chapter.

## 3.1.5.3 Drivers and barriers of consumer behaviour towards sustainable practices

As it was mentioned previously, textile/garment production is associated with a vast number of sustainability challenges in areas such as pollution, use of resources, and exploitation (Ahmadi, S. 2020). The environmental impact of the fashion industry can be noticed in the many steps of its supply chains, from the environmental impacts seen in the agricultural and petrochemical production steps to the manufacturing, logistics, and retail. This impact is especially visible in fast fashion, as it tends to have social and environmental impacts to support the rapid increase in material throughput, having grown to twice the amount of clothing produced in the year 2000 (Ribeiro, P. R. et al., 2023).

![](_page_41_Diagram_7.jpeg)

Figure 5. Environmental influences of the clothing industry on the product life cycle. Source: M.Popowska, A.Sinkiewicz, 2021(https://www.undp.org/sustainable-development-goals).

The increasing awareness of the environmental impacts of textile production has given rise to the urgent need for sustainable practices in the fashion industry. Textile sustainability encompasses a wide range of measures aimed at reducing the ecological footprint of textiles while ensuring the social and economic well-being of the people involved in their production. By adopting sustainable practices, the fashion industry can work towards mitigating climate change, conserving natural resources, and promoting fair labour practices (Padmanaban, 2024).

## Sustainability in the textile sector is based in 3 pillars (Mallick, 2021):

The environmental pillar. Companies of the textile sector are increasingly adopting production methods that lead to the reduction of carbon emissions, packaging waste, water consumption, and overall environmental impact.

The social pillar. Companies of the textile sector are focusing on the work force sustainability. Employee sustainability is the current and future ability of workers to remain in the workforce and is determined by a healthy organizational culture that supports and values employees. A sustainable employee culture keeps employees engaged to the level needed to perform their jobs capably (Van Dam et al, 2016).

The economic pillar. Economic sustainability refers to practices that promote long-term economic growth and well-being while safeguarding the social, environmental, and cultural dimensions of the community. This pillar emphasises activities such as regulatory compliance, sound governance, effective risk management, and the responsible allocation of resources.

These pillars of sustainability within the textile sector are reflected in a range of practices, methods, and strategies that have been shaping and transforming the industry over the past decade. Specifically, as noted by Padmanaban (2024):

## 1. Sustainable raw materials

The textile industry is undergoing a significant transformation towards sustainability, marked by an increasing emphasis on environmentally responsible fibres that reflect both ecological and ethical concerns. A range of sustainable alternatives is emerging as prominent options, underscoring the sector's dedication to minimising its environmental impact. Organic cotton, hemp, jute, bamboo, tencel (derived from sustainably sourced wood pulp), and recycled fibres are increasingly recognised for their reduced environmental impact. Their cultivation and production typically require fewer pesticides and less water, while also contributing to a lower carbon footprint, thereby representing a more responsible choice for both manufacturers and consumers.

## 2. Recycling and circular fashion economy

The concept of the circular economy has emerged as a transformative approach to enhancing sustainability within the textile industry. Departing from the traditional linear model of 'take, make, dispose,' the circular economy seeks to establish a closed-loop system in which textiles are systematically recycled, refurbished, reused, upcycled, or biodegraded at the end of their lifecycle. Innovative technologies made possible the recycling of used fragments and the production of new fibres, reducing the demand for resources and decreasing the production of waste. This strategy reduces the environmental impact and creates business opportunities and creates jobs in recycling and upcycling industries. Waste recycling technologies have reached a mature stage at both preconsumer and post-consumer stages. Recycling of used garments is becoming an increasingly profitable and widely adopted practice within the textile industry.

## 3. Environmentally friendly production procedures

Textile production is characterised by high levels of water and energy consumption. Addressing this challenge necessitates the adoption of cleaner and more sustainable processing methods aimed at minimising water use and promoting energy-efficient technologies. Innovative techniques such as waterless dyeing, exemplified by air dyeing and digital printing, as well as the utilisation of non-toxic chemicals, are increasingly implemented. These approaches hold substantial potential

for reducing the environmental footprint of textile manufacturing.

### 4. Ethical labour practices

Ethical labour practices refer to the fair and respectful treatment of workers across all levels of the supply chain. This includes ensuring safe working conditions for textile workers, fair wages, reasonable working hours, and the right to unionise. It also involves the prohibition of child labour, forced labour, and any form of discrimination. Upholding these standards not only protects workers' rights but also strengthens corporate accountability and social responsibility within the industry.

## 5. Economic dimension of sustainability

The economic dimension of sustainability is often where businesses perceive the greatest stability, as financial viability is fundamental to long-term sustainability. However, profitability must be balanced with environmental and social considerations. Profit alone cannot override broader sustainability commitments. This pillar encompasses critical functions such as regulatory compliance, effective governance, risk management, and transparent financial practices. Often referred to as the "governance pillar," it involves aligning the interests of boards and management not only with shareholders but also with stakeholders across the value chain, including communities and customers. Stakeholders, including investors, typically seek confidence in a company's use of transparent accounting methods, ethical governance practices, and avoidance of conflicts of interest, undue political influence, or unlawful conduct. The economic pillar plays a vital role in enabling companies to adopt realistic and scalable sustainability strategies. Its inclusion ensures that sustainability efforts remain economically feasible and balanced, avoiding abrupt transitions such as the immediate elimination of fossil fuels or chemical inputs without proper phasing.

The factor "consumer" is essential in this transformation of the textile sector towards a more sustainable operation at all stages of the production and distribution chain. Consumers hold significant power in shaping market trends. Their purchasing decisions influence product offerings, innovation, and business strategies. When consumers demand sustainable, ethical, or high-quality products, companies respond by adapting their practices, sourcing methods, and value propositions accordingly. This consumer-led pressure can lead to broader industry shifts, for example, the rise of organic textiles. On the other hand, consumer preferences are not static. They can be influenced or guided, through information campaigns, marketing, labelling, and public policy, to adopt more sustainable behaviours. For instance, awareness initiatives about fast fashion's environmental toll can lead to a shift in preference toward slow fashion. This educational component is crucial in sectors where environmental or ethical impacts are not immediately visible or well-understood.

Having highlighted the framework of sustainability in the textile sector, the goal of the current section is to underline the elements that influence consumer behaviour towards sustainable practices.

Across multiple studies, consumers express positive attitudes toward sustainable and eco-friendly textile products. These attitudes are largely driven by environmental concern (e.g., resource use, pollution, textile waste); ethical values (fair labour, animal welfare); health and self-image considerations (comfort, natural fibres, personal identity).

However, there remains a notable gap between attitudes and behaviour. Consumers may intend to buy sustainably but often do not follow through due to external constraints like price and availability.

The aspect of ethical and eco-issues and sustainable behaviours reflects the extent to which consumers' values, moral beliefs, and environmental concerns influence their fashion choices. According to Schiaroli et al. (2024), while these factors are often cited as motivators, they primarily

In the literature several theories have been utilized to explain consumer engagement with sustainability in the fashion industry and the adoption of sustainable practices such as, Theory of Planned Behaviour, Value-belief-norm theory, Norm Activation Model, social identity theory etc (Ribeiro P., et al. 2023). Their implementation has demonstrated both advantages and limitations.

Factors, drivers and barriers of consumers' behaviour per phase of involvement Several researchers utilize a fragmented analysis of the consumers' behaviour according to the phases of involvement, namely:

- (i) Selection/acquisition phase
- (ii) Utilization/use phase
- (iii) Post use/discard phase.

The majority of the available research and studies focus on factors influencing consumer decisions in the first phase, the selection/pre-purchase phase. This indicates a predominant research emphasis on the initial stage of consumer behaviour within the sustainable fashion chain. There is relatively lack of studies concerning consumer behaviour in the product use phase and certainly less studies have been conducted to investigate behaviours related to the post-use phase (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## Selection/acquisition phase

Regarding the factors that influence sustainable fashion choices, the reviewed literature highlights several pathways for encouraging more sustainable consumption. Findings suggest that environmental attributes of garments often play a secondary role in shaping consumer behaviour. Instead, the primary drivers behind sustainable fashion purchasing decisions can be grouped into six key categories:

## (1) Price-value perception

Consumers are more willing to buy sustainable textile products when the product is perceived as fairly priced relative to its quality and longevity (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024). In most studies contacted on the topic there is a common finding. When asked for the most influential factors in product acquisition, price was identified as the most prominent factor affecting decision (Ribeiro, P. R. et al., 2023). Price relative aspects are perceived behavioural control (consumers are more likely to purchase when they believe they can afford sustainable fashion) and financial capability. Consumers with more disposable income or belief in financial manageability are more likely to purchase (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## (2) Product quality, functionality, comfort and aesthetic appeal

High-quality and comfortable garments are strong motivators for sustainable purchases. Consumers are more likely to choose sustainable clothing when it is perceived to be long-lasting, well-made, and pleasant to wear. They prioritize these tangible and personal benefits over abstract environmental benefits. In other words, most consumers would not choose sustainable garments if they do not provide enough benefits to satisfy their needs and aspirations (Rausch et al., 2021).

## (3) Ethical and eco-issue and sustainable behaviours

drive behaviour among already sustainability-committed consumers. Consumers who possess strong environmental awareness, feel a moral obligation to act responsibly, or hold altruistic and idealistic values are more likely to engage in sustainable fashion consumption. These individuals are often mindful of the social and environmental impacts of their choices and may prioritise fair trade, cruelty-free, or low-impact garments, even at higher costs. However, evidence shows that for the broader consumer base, ethical and environmental concerns tend to play a secondary role compared to functional or aesthetic product attributes. As such, while eco-consciousness is important, it is not sufficient alone to drive widespread behavioural change unless supported by other appealing product characteristics (e.g. quality, style) and social reinforcement.

## (4) Social influences, demographics, contextual factors

Social and situational influences are key drivers of consumer engagement with sustainable fashion, shaping behaviour through peer expectations, digital communities, identity expression, and shopping contexts. Consumers are more likely to adopt sustainable practices when these are perceived as socially desirable, endorsed by influencers, or embedded in cultural norms. Social media play a crucial role in spreading awareness and normalising behaviours, while fashion choices often serve as expressions of personal and group identity. Additionally, public visibility and positive online discourse can reinforce sustainable decisions, making these influences vital for closing the gap between awareness and action (Kautish and Khare, 2022; Johnstone and Lindh, 2022). Moreover, according to Mathew & Spinelli (2025) demographics play a significant role as a factor. Millennials and women, who tend to be more aware of sustainability issues, are leading adopters of eco-fashion, whereas male consumers and older age groups often show lower levels of engagement despite expressing ethical concerns, suggesting either a lack of awareness or less exposure to sustainable fashion initiatives.

## (5) Corporate image

Corporate reputation, trust, and brand equity drive consumers toward sustainable fashion choices. Grazzini et al. (2021) highlighted that incorporating sustainable features into fashion products, such as the use of recycled fibres, enhances consumers' perceptions of warmth, meaning they view the company as sincere, kind, and morally driven. This perception, in turn, positively affects their buying decisions. Researchers also pointed out that marketing communications which emphasize a company's sustainable practices, such as corporate social responsibility environmental initiatives, and ethical branding, play a crucial role in reducing consumer scepticism and encouraging sustainable fashion purchases.

## (6) Purchasing experience

The purchasing experience is a significant but often underestimated driver of sustainable fashion consumption. According to Schiaroli et al., 2024, consumers are more likely to choose sustainable garments when the overall shopping process is enjoyable, convenient, and emotionally rewarding. Positive experiences, such as engaging in-store environments, user-friendly online platforms, fast and ethical delivery, and responsive customer service, enhance satisfaction and reinforce sustainable behaviours. Additionally, hedonic values like the joy of discovering unique pieces, the excitement of second-hand treasure hunting, or the pleasure of supporting ethical brands contribute to shaping consumers. When sustainable fashion is associated with aesthetic pleasure and ease, it becomes more attractive, even to consumers who are not primarily motivated by ethical concerns. Therefore, improving the purchasing journey is crucial for mainstreaming sustainability in fashion.

discourage consumers from adopting sustainable behaviours across the phases of purchase, use, and disposal. These barriers may stem from individual perceptions, social norms, structural limitations, or market-related challenges. Schiaroli et al. (2024) identify several recurrent and interrelated types of barriers, which can be grouped into 5 main categories.

Consumers generally express positive attitudes toward sustainable textiles, driven by environmental and ethical concerns. However, their actual behaviour does not always align with these attitudes due to barriers like:

## (a) High price perception

High price is perceived as an important barrier when making sustainable fashion choices. The literature underlines that sustainable garments are commonly associated with higher prices compared to traditional garments and consumers might choose the cheapest choice due to scarce economic resources (Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## (b) Scepticism and distrust

The literature indicates that consumers often exhibit low levels of trust in sustainability claims due to a perceived lack of transparency, scepticism about the true environmental benefits of sustainable garments, doubts about the effectiveness of eco-apparel in addressing environmental issues, and a belief that companies are not genuinely committed to environmental and social responsibility (Carranza et al., 2022). When consumers sense that brands are engaging in greenwashing or making unreliable environmental claims, their confidence is undermined, discouraging them from purchasing sustainable clothing (Carranza et al., 2022; Apaolaza et al., 2022). Moreover, lack of clear, simple eco-labelling that can be easily understood by consumers further induces scepticism about the reliability (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## (c) Knowledge and stereotypes

Numerous studies have shown that limited consumer knowledge about sustainable fashion is closely linked to reduced purchasing behaviour. In particular, misconceptions and stereotypes such as associating sustainable clothing with outdated styles, poor design, lack of functionality, or poor fit, can negatively influence perceptions. To encourage more sustainable purchasing decisions, consumers require clearer, more accessible information on product labels that effectively communicate the unique qualities of sustainable garments, build trust in brands, and counteract these misconceptions, especially given the often vague or confusing terminology used by fashion companies (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## (d) Limited availability and accessibility

Consumers might perceive high effort in the process of purchasing sustainable garments, due to the difficulty in finding them in traditional stores, scarce knowledge of sustainable brands, and limited availability of apparel with desired attributes (Algahni et al. 2020, Vladimirova et al.2023).

## (e) Consumer habits and inertia

Consumers' tendency to repeatedly shop at the same stores without actively seeking alternatives that offer sustainable clothing, combined with the belief that altering their own shopping habits is insignificant if others continue to consume unsustainably, is negatively linked to sustainable fashion choices. Research indicates that individuals are more inclined to adopt sustainable purchasing behaviours when they believe their actions can meaningfully contribute to addressing environmental issues that is when they perceive their consumption as effective (Apaolaza et al., 2022; V.Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## Utilization/use phase

Sustainable behaviours during the use phase of fashion textiles are driven by emotional bonds, economic rationale, environmental concern, and social identity, supported by knowledge, perceived ease, and satisfaction. Strategies to encourage these behaviours must address both motivational and practical enablers, such as repair services, consumer education, and visible social norms.

## Α. Product attachment and emotional value

Consumers are more likely to reuse and maintain garments they are emotionally attached to (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024). Emotional bonds formed through memories or personalization encourage prolonged use and resistance to fast-fashion turnover. This emotional durability fosters mindful consumption (Zahid, et al.2022).

## Β. Prudence and cost-saving motivation

Economic considerations such as saving money drive behaviours like repairing instead of replacing and wearing items longer. This is particularly strong among low-income groups of peoples, for whom maximizing value and reducing spending are crucial incentives (V.Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## C. Environmental awareness and responsibility

While not always a dominant driver in pre-purchase decisions, environmental concern motivates sustainable use behaviours like cold washing, air drying, avoiding over-washing, and repairs. Consumers who are environmentally conscious often adopt laundry practices that reduce energy, water, and microfibre pollution (Ryding et al. 2022, Abrar et al. 2021).

## D. Knowledge and skills

Having the skills to repair or maintain garments (e.g., sewing, stain removal) enables consumers to extend products' lifespan (Niinimaki et al., 2021). Those who lack this knowledge are less likely to engage in repair or reuse, even if they are motivated.

## E. Social norms and identity

Consumers who identify with sustainability-minded communities or value circular lifestyles (e.g., zero waste, minimalism) are more likely to adopt conscious use-phase behaviours. Peer influence and a sense of belonging to eco-conscious circles can encourage long-term garment use. Indeed, consumer behaviour is shaped by societal and group dynamics (Yoon et al., 2020). Social norms, peer influence, and shared awareness significantly guide individuals toward making sustainable choices. Viewing sustainability as a key driver of consumer behaviour highlights its complex and wide-reaching influence, where both individuals and businesses play vital roles in the collective transition to a more sustainable future (V. Schiaroli et al, 2024).

## F. Perceived behavioural control

When consumers perceive themselves as capable of engaging in sustainable practices, such as eco-friendly laundering or accessing repair services, they are more likely to adopt such behaviours. This sense of self-efficacy plays a critical role in converting sustainable intentions into concrete actions. Cayaban et al. (2023) highlighted the positive influence of social media on shaping attitudes, subjective norms, and perceived behavioural control, identifying these factors as potential mediators in addressing the attitude–behaviour gap. Future research could further investigate the impact of emotional drivers (e.g., pride, guilt, or trust) as well as the effectiveness of advocacy campaigns and social media in promoting enduring behavioural change (Mathew & Spinelli,

### 2025).

## G. Consumer satisfaction and product utility

Satisfaction with the quality, comfort, and performance of garments is a critical driver of sustainable consumer behaviour during the use phase of textile products. High levels of satisfaction can lead to prolonged garment use, reduce premature disposal, and diminish the frequency of new purchases all of which are essential to extending product lifespans and supporting circular economy principles. Quality is often associated with durability, resistance to wear and tear, and maintained appearance over time. Consumers who perceive a garment as high-quality are more likely to engage in long-term use, viewing the product as a reliable investment rather than a disposable item (Schiaroli et al., 2024). Garments that retain their shape, colour, and texture through multiple wears and washes reinforce a sense of value, making consumers less inclined to seek replacements. Comfort is another essential attribute that significantly influences garment retention. Clothing that feels good on the body is more likely to become part of regular use cycles, contributing to a habit of re-wearing rather than discarding. Comfort is also tied to emotional satisfaction, as it can enhance the user's experience and strengthen personal attachment to the garment (Kemi & Zilahy, 2025). Performance, which includes aspects such as breathability, ease of maintenance, and suitability for different occasions, similarly affects continued use. Products that function well across various settings or climates are seen as versatile, increasing their usability and reducing the need to purchase additional items to fulfil similar roles (Ribeiro et al., 2023). In summary, garments that satisfy consumers in terms of quality, comfort, and performance are more likely to be worn longer and maintained better, reducing the overall environmental footprint of clothing consumption. Enhancing satisfaction through thoughtful design and materials selection thus represents a strategic entry point for brands seeking to align product offerings with sustainable consumption goals.

## Post use / discard phase

The post-use or discard phase of textile consumption plays a crucial role in advancing circular economy objectives, particularly through consumer practices such as reuse, resale, donation, recycling, and proper disposal. The reviewed literature highlights several interrelated drivers that influence sustainable consumer behaviour during this phase.

Environmental awareness and moral responsibility are consistently identified as key motivational factors for sustainable post-use behaviour. Consumers with strong ecological values are more likely to avoid landfilling garments and instead seek alternatives that contribute to environmental protection, such as recycling or donating (Schiaroli et al., 2024; Ribeiro et al., 2023). This behaviour reflects the influence of personal norms and perceived ethical obligations toward reducing textile waste and conserving resources.

Moreover, social norms and emotional motivations play a critical role in encouraging responsible disposal. Kemi and Zilahy (2025) observe that garments with sentimental value such as children's clothing are often reused within families or passed along to others as a form of symbolic exchange, reinforcing both environmental and social bonds. Similarly, practices such as donating or gifting used clothes contribute to feelings of community engagement and solidarity, supporting behaviour aligned with pro-social values.

Another significant driver is perceived convenience and accessibility, which significantly affects whether consumers engage in sustainable disposal practices. Access to infrastructure such as

textile collection bins, second-hand markets, and clear recycling instructions facilitates proper action. In contrast, lack of convenient options can act as a barrier even when intentions are positive (Ribeiro et al., 2023). Trust in the efficacy of these channels, such as belief that donated clothes will reach those in need, also enhances participation. Moreover, economic incentives and perceived utility influence post-use decisions. Consumers are more inclined to sell, swap, or repurpose garments if they perceive residual economic value. This is particularly relevant in the growing popularity of second-hand platforms and peer-to-peer resale applications, which combine sustainability with personal benefit (Schiaroli et al., 2024). Finally, product attributes, such as durability, brand reputation, and timeless design, also drive consumers to discard items responsibly. High-quality garments are more likely to be resold or donated due to their retained value, while low-quality items are often deemed unsuitable for reuse and are more frequently discarded in unsustainable ways (Kemi & Zilahy, 2025). In summary, the post-use phase is shaped by a combination of moral, social, logistical, economic, and product-related factors. Understanding and leveraging these drivers can support the design of more effective interventions and policies aimed at reducing textile waste and fostering circular consumption behaviours.

## Emerging consumer practices in fashion

Consumers are increasingly engaging in practices that support sustainability and circularity. Slow fashion represents a significant shift, with consumers prioritizing quality, durability, and ethical production. Many consumers demonstrate willingness to pay more for sustainable products and perceive them as higher value (Castro-Lopez et al., 2021; Gazzola et al., 2020; Musová et al., 2021).

Growing participation in second-hand markets and alternative models such as rental and swapping reduces demand for new products (even though this is not always the case<sup>3</sup> ) and extends garment lifecycles, which is key for circularity (D'Adamo et al., 2022; Brydges, 2021; Musová et al., 2021; Papamichael et al., 2024). Alongside these alternative consumption models, increased interest in maintaining, repairing, and upcycling clothing reflects a broader commitment to extending product use. This interest is supported by both traditional skills and digital resources that help keep products in use longer (Schiaroli et al., 2024; Dragomir & Dumitru, 2022).

Demand for transparency has become particularly pronounced, especially among younger generations who seek more information about sourcing, materials, and production processes. This consumer pressure is pushing brands to adopt circular and sustainable practices (Gazzola et al., 2020; Brydges, 2021; Jimenez-Fernandez et al., 2023).

While there is momentum toward sufficiency, meaning consuming less and focusing on need rather than novelty, the progress is gradual. Sufficiency strategies such as buying less, choosing versatile garments, and supporting sharing or rental models are gaining attention but are not yet mainstream, as most companies and consumers still prioritize less disruptive sustainability actions (García-Ortega et al., 2023; Freudenreich & Schaltegger, 2020).

Barriers to wider adoption include cultural norms around fashionability, lack of awareness, and the persistence of fast fashion's appeal (Schiaroli et al., 2024; García-Ortega et al., 2023; Musová et al., 2021). However, education and awareness are strongly correlated with willingness to adopt circular and sufficiency-oriented behaviours (Papamichael et al., 2024).

Exploring factors shaping consumer behaviour towards circular fashion: a focus on Generations Y<sup>4</sup> and Z<sup>5</sup> (11 August 2025) Abdelmeguid A, Afy-Shararah M and Salonitis K (2025) Exploring factors shaping consumer behaviour towards

"…The fashion industry faces significant challenges due to its linear systems and environmental impact. As sustainability gains priority among consumers, especially Generations Y and Z, industry is urged to transition towards a circular economy. Despite this, the "attitude-behaviour gap" persists, indicating minimal impact on consumer behaviour.

…The study explores factors influencing consumer behaviour towards circular fashion, focusing on Generations Y and Z.

Nine hypotheses were developed, exploring relationships among environmental awareness, circular fashion awareness, willingness to change, willingness to pay a premium, and circular behaviour.

Online surveys yielded 408 responses from participants from developing and developed countries. Results show consumer behaviour is influenced by environmental awareness and circular fashion awareness, and willingness to change. Additionally, purchasing decisions are driven by product quality and durability.

## …Hypotheses development

The variables included in the study, including environmental awareness, circular fashion awareness, willingness to change, willingness to pay a premium, and circular behaviour, were selected based on their demonstrated significance in prior research examining sustainable consumption and circular fashion practices.

H1: Environmental awareness influences consumers' circular fashion awareness

H2: Environmental awareness influences consumers' circular behaviour H3: Circular fashion awareness influences consumer circular behaviour

H4: Consumers with higher levels of environmental awareness are more willing to pay a premium for

circular fashion items

H5: Consumers' circular fashion awareness positively correlates with their willingness to pay a

premium for circular fashion items

H6: Willingness to change positively correlates with willingness to pay a premium for circular fashion

<sup>4</sup> Millennials; demographic cohort born roughly between 1981-1996

<sup>5</sup> demographic cohort born roughly between 1997 and 2012

### items

H7: Consumers with higher levels of environmental awareness are more willing to change their behaviour

H8: Consumers with higher levels of circular fashion awareness are more willing to change their behaviour

H9: Consumers' willingness to change positively correlates with their circular behaviour

….A path analysis was performed to test the hypotheses, and considering a 95% confidence level, the results were found to support 5 out of the 9 hypotheses.

H1 was supported indicating that higher environmental awareness is positively associated with increased circular fashion awareness among consumers. This finding agrees with prior research suggesting that environmental consciousness plays a crucial role in promoting an understanding of circular fashion (Abdelmeguid et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2023).

H2 was also supported indicating that higher environmental awareness positively influences the adoption of circular behaviour among consumers. Consistent with existing research, the results supported this hypothesis, indicating that individuals with higher environmental awareness are more likely to engage in circular fashion practices (Ertz et al., 2016; Zhang, 2021). This emphasises the critical role of environmental consciousness as a driving factor for consumers to embrace circular behaviours within the fashion industry.

Likewise, H3 was supported confirming that higher circular fashion awareness is positively associated with increased adoption of circular behaviour among consumers, such as purchasing second-hand fashion items (Aramendia-Muneta et al., 2022). This presents an opportunity for education and communication efforts and initiatives to bridge this awareness gap and promote the relevance of circular fashion principles among consumers, as suggested by Dissanayake and Perera (2016).

Furthermore, H4 was not supported, indicating no statistically significant relationship between environmental awareness and willingness to pay a premium. This finding suggests that while environmental awareness may drive awareness and adoption of circular fashion practices among consumers from Generations Y and Z, it may not necessarily translate into a willingness to allocate financial resources to purchase circular fashion items. This aligns with contingency theory, which suggests that businesses need to adapt their strategies, such as pricing models, to meet the demands and limitations of the current consumer market, including price sensitivity and economic factors (Kumar P. et al., 2021).

Similarly, H5 was not supported, indicating no statistically significant relationship between awareness of circular fashion practices and the willingness to pay a premium for circular fashion items. This implies that familiarity with circular principles does not necessarily translate into a desire to pay a premium for such items. This highlights the challenge for fashion businesses to balance consumer demand for sustainable or circular fashion with price consciousness, as addressed by contingency theory and stakeholder theory, which emphasise the need to adapt to economic conditions and consumer preferences (Jabbour et al., 2018; Meherishi et al., 2019). As highlighted in existing literature, this challenge requires fashion businesses and brands to assess whether they are prepared to assume the risk of losing some of their current consumers in exchange for the possibility of attracting future customers who are willing to pay a circular premium (D'adamo and Lupi, 2021). Alternatively, it underlines the need for fashion businesses and brands in the fashion industry to find ways to make their products more accessible and affordable to a broader consumer

Moreover, H6 was supported confirming the positive relationship between willingness to change and paying a premium. Therefore, as suggested in existing literature, individuals more open to changing their behaviour are willing to invest in circular fashion products (Francis and Hoefel, 2018; Gazzola et al., 2020). This highlights the importance of consumer attitudes and their willingness to shape consumers' purchasing decisions, reinforcing the connection with stakeholder theory.

Controversially, H7 was not supported, indicating that there is no statistically significant relationship between environmental awareness and the willingness to change behaviour among consumers. This suggests that while environmental awareness may influence awareness and adoption of circular fashion, it may not directly impact consumers' readiness to change their behaviour. One possible reason for this non-significant result is that environmental awareness alone may not be sufficient to overcome established habits or practical barriers to behaviour change (Blas Riesgo et al., 2023). Factors such as perceived inconvenience, lack of alternatives, or social and cultural norms may play a stronger role in shaping willingness to change (Musova et al., 2021).

 Additionally, previous research indicates that the translation from awareness to action is often mediated by other variables, such as perceived value and risk, social influence, behaviour control, or emotional engagement, which were not directly measured in this study (Lin and Chen, 2022; Blas Riesgo et al., 2023; Liu et al., 2023). It is also possible that the frequent exposure to environmental messages among Generation Y and Z has led to greater scepticism, particularly in light of concerns about greenwashing or a perceived lack of real impact. At the same time, other practical and personal priorities may take priority, making it less likely that awareness alone will drive meaningful behavioural change.

Interestingly, H8 was not supported, revealing no statistically significant relationship between circular fashion awareness and the willingness to change behaviour among consumers. This result suggests that being aware of circular fashion concepts does not automatically translate into a personal motivation or readiness to change established consumption patterns. Practical constraints limited perceived impact, or scepticism about the effectiveness of individual action could be contributing factors. It may also reflect that awareness of principles like recycling or repair is widespread, but a willingness to make the required effort or adopt new routines depends on additional drivers, such as rewards and incentives, peer behaviour, or supportive infrastructure (Cruz and Rosado da Cruz, 2023; Liu et al., 2023). These findings emphasise the importance of moving beyond informational campaigns to address practical, social, and psychological barriers to change in future

Finally, H9 was supported confirming that a higher willingness to change behaviour is positively associated with increased adoption of circular behaviour among consumers. Hence, individuals who are more willing to change their purchasing, usage, and disposal behaviours are more likely to engage in circular practices, such as reducing their new clothing purchases or repairing clothing items (Kumar P. et al., 2021). This aligns with institutional theory, as the increasing consumer willingness to change behaviours reflects broader societal pressures that encourage sustainability (Bag et al., 2021a).

## Practical implications

This study has a few practical contributions to the field of circular fashion.

Firstly, it can be a guideline for fashion businesses, brands, and policymakers to understand the factors that positively influence consumers' circular behaviour among Generation Y and Generation Z.

For example, our findings underscore the significant impact of environmental and circular fashion awareness on consumer behaviour. As a result, businesses and brands are urged to ensure widespread access to relevant information, promoting awareness that encourages sustainable development and aligns with the natural consumers' lifestyles (SDG12). This is consistent with stakeholder theory, which stresses the importance of addressing the interests of key groups, including consumers, in strategic decision-making. Moreover, the study advocates actions aligned with Sustainable Development Goal 13 (SDG13), emphasising the necessity to enhance education and raise awareness of climate change and early warnings.

Secondly, providing insights into the ranking of factors driving consumers' purchasing decisions can be highly valuable to fashion industry players looking to align their strategies with consumer preferences amongst Generation Y and Generation Z. Additionally, reducing waste generation through various options, such as prevention, reusing, and recycling, aligns with SDG12. These recommendations reflect institutional theory, highlighting the role of regulatory and societal pressures in shaping industry behaviour. Our findings demonstrate that fashion businesses and brands actively incorporating these practices positively impact consumers' readiness for change. This willingness to change, in turn, exhibits a positive correlation with positively influencing consumer behaviours towards sustainability and circularity, as well as a higher willingness to pay premium prices. However, strategically considering pricing to retain existing customers without losing them to circular premiums (SDG12) and exploring alternative revenue streams to offset pricing impacts is equally important.

Therefore, our findings demonstrate the importance of specific variables to bridge the attitude-behaviour gap and align industry practices with consumer perceptions and preferences. These insights will be invaluable in guiding stakeholders towards facilitating the implementation of circular fashion practices and positively influencing individuals' behaviour in the context of circular fashion. Consequently, adapting industries for sustainability and clean and environmentally friendly industrial processes to enhance the efficiency of using resources aligns with SDG9, encouraging meaningful and impactful change actions in the fashion industry in all countries, per the respective capabilities of each country.

For example, there is no statistically significant relationship between environmental awareness and the consumers' willingness to change their behaviour (H7) or their willingness to pay a premium for circular fashion items (H4).

Similarly, there is no statistically significant relationship between circular fashion awareness and consumers' willingness to change their behaviour (H8) or their willingness to pay a premium for circular fashion items

In addition to testing these hypotheses, our study revealed critical insights into the awareness and behaviours

of the participants. Notably, the majority of participants exhibited an awareness of the fashion industry's high pollution levels. However, it is intriguing to note that most of them were not yet familiar with the circular fashion concept, although they demonstrated awareness of the different principles of circularity.

Furthermore, the findings showed that most participants rarely purchased circular or sustainable clothing items. This emphasises the existing gap between awareness and buying behaviour in this domain. However, a very high percentage of the participants expressed a willingness to change their behaviour to be more supportive of circularity, suggesting the growing interest in circular fashion practices. Nevertheless, the

majority of participants are not willing to pay a circular premium.

Finally, when participants were asked to rank the factors that matter most in their purchasing decisions, some

clear trends emerged. At the top of their list were factors like product quality and durability, followed closely by price and affordability. Personal interests, brand reputation, and the latest fashion trends also played significant roles in their choices. Interestingly, environmental sustainability and eco-friendliness were important but fell a bit lower on the list, indicating a potential area for growth in consumer awareness and preferences.

## Despite the significant contributions of this study, it features a few limitations.

Firstly, the study uses convenience sampling, which is a non-probability sampling method, due to the availability and accessibility restrictions encountered. This sampling method does not provide a random representation of the entire population, making it challenging to generalise findings on the broader population. Since individuals are not selected randomly, a selection bias may occur, where certain groups within the population are underrepresented or overrepresented in the sample. Thus, to ensure an unbiased and representative sample, future research should explore a wider range of sampling techniques.

Secondly, this study collected data from consumers using surveys with closed-ended questions. Closedended questions, while efficient, limit participants' responses to predefined choices and may not capture the full spectrum of participant perspectives. Future research could consider collecting data using qualitative methods or surveys with open-ended questions to provide a more comprehensive understanding of consumer

Moreover, while our survey offers valuable insights into the perspectives of Generation Y and Generation Z globally, it is important to note that the data represents both developing and developed countries without further analysis. Given that this wasn't the primary focus of our research, a recommendation for future studies is to conduct a more detailed examination to determine trends specific to different socio-economic contexts, enhancing the depth of our understanding.

Finally, although this study focused on Generations Y and Z, it did not conduct a direct comparison of behavioural differences between these cohorts. Future research is encouraged to analyse and compare the specific behavioural patterns and attitudes of Generation Y and Generation Z, which could provide further insight into generational influences on circular fashion adoption".

## 3.1.6 Recycle and reuse practises

Across the different segments of the textile industry, from fibre production to the manufacturing of clothing and other products, there is substantial consumption of water, chemicals, and energy, alongside the generation of waste. Figure 6 illustrates how these four factors are linked to each key stage of the clothing lifecycle. The textile industry's most significant environmental impacts include emissions of carbon dioxide, as well as water, chemical, and fibre pollution. (Abrishami et al.2024).

![](_page_54_Diagram_4.jpeg)

Figure 6. Schematic illustration of different stages of the process from production to consumption of clothing and its environmental effects at each stage. Source: Abrishami et al.2024

Textile reuse and recycling have the potential to lower environmental impacts by reducing the demand for virgin fibre production and minimizing subsequent processes within the textile product life cycle. Compared to incineration or landfilling, both practices offer more sustainable alternatives. Among the two, reuse is typically regarded as more advantageous than recycling, particularly when it effectively extends the lifespan of textile products (Labayen et al 2022).

Various terms have been coined by different parts of the textile sector to describe resources, used products, and waste within the textiles lifecycle. However, inconsistent usage of these terms is complicating the reuse and recycling process for textiles. The use of terminology within the textile sector can, without intention, convey implicit assumptions regarding the utility or value of materials, thereby influencing their subsequent treatment and perception. The current lack of standardisation, clarity and consistency among stakeholders presents a significant barrier to effective collaboration, impedes coherent policy formulation, and ultimately constrains the advancement of a circular economy within the textile industry (WRAP UK Sustainable Textiles Action Plan).

"Textile recycling refers to the process of recovering textiles and reprocessing them into usable materials or products, thereby diverting waste from landfills and reducing the demand for virgin resources. This includes both pre-consumer waste (e.g. production scraps) and post-consumer waste (e.g. discarded garments) that are sorted, processed, and either reused, mechanically or chemically recycled, or used for energy recovery (Textile Exchange, 2021; Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2017).

and access-based consumption" (G.Sandin & GM Peters, 2018).

![](_page_55_Diagram_4.jpeg)

Figure 7. Classification of textile reuse and recycling routes. Source: Abrishami et al.2024

Recycling can be applied in many fields of the textiles as textile-to-textile (closed-loop) recycling or textile-to-nontextile (open-loop) recycling (Celep et al., 2023). It is now well documented that reuse and recycling of textiles is advantageous to the environment.

The Future of a Closed-Loop Industry

## WHY THIS MATTERS

| <ul> <li>• Massive reduction in virgin resource usage</li> <li>• Lower CO<sub>2</sub> footprint</li> </ul> | <ul> <li>• Keeps textiles in circulation far longer</li> <li>• Supports a fully circular fashion ecosystem</li> </ul> |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|

Reuse typically results in lower environmental impacts compared to recycling. In general, textiles made from recycled fibres have a smaller ecological footprint than those made from virgin materials except in cases involving cotton/polyester blends (Abagnato, Rigamonti, Grosso, 2024). A recent life-cycle assessment (LCA), commissioned by the European textile reuse and recycling sector, confirms the substantial environmental benefits of textile reuse. The findings highlight that the environmental impact of reusing textiles is 70 times lower than producing new garments, even when factoring in global transportation for reuse. Specifically, the study shows that reusing a single high-or medium-quality garment saves approximately 3 kg of CO₂, while water consumption is reduced to just 0.01% of what is needed to manufacture new clothing (EuRIC and Trzepacz et al., 2023).

In order to effectively determine the possibilities and limitations associated with textile reuse and recycling, it is essential to examine these concepts within the context of the textile lifecycle.

## A textile in general demonstrates four (4) lifecycle stages, mapping the commonly used terms. (WRAP UK Sustainable Textiles Action Plan, 2024)

Source: WRAP UK Sustainable Textiles Action Plan, 2024

 Post-industrial surplus materials: These refer to fibres, yarns, fabrics, and trims that have been manufactured but not utilised in the production of final consumer products. Such materials remain unused due to factors such as changes or cancellations in orders, or overproduction. Notably, this category excludes materials that are defective or damaged. Post-industrial surplus products: These are finished products that, although manufactured, have not been distributed to retail channels. The causes may include alterations in order specifications, cancellations, or production exceeding demand. Products with defects or damage are not included in this category. Post-industrial by-products: This term encompasses textile materials and products generated as residuals during the manufacturing process that are no longer suitable for their original purpose. Examples include fibre and yarn waste, fabric off-cuts, and items with defects or damage. In alignment with the waste hierarchy, such by-products should ideally be reprocessed for fibre production or recycled, with disposal considered as a last resort. Pre-consumer surplus products are separated into surplus stock, unsold stock and returned products. Surplus stock is excess stock that does not make it to market and includes products remaining in a warehouse. Unsold stock occurs when products are put up for sale for a certain period of time and not sold. Returned products refer to products sold to consumers that have been returned in reusable or non-reuseable current state for a variety of reasons. Pre-consumer by-products are separated into damaged/faulty stock and returned products.
