Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

Damaged/faulty stock are products which are not addressing the quality standards of the product. This may happen during the production process and not detected by the quality control or may happen during transportation or storing the products from the production site to the retailers.

 Post-consumer reusable textiles. Textiles that have been used by a consumer and ready to be discarded but are still reusable in their current form or with minimal repair. Post-consumer non-reusable textiles. Textiles that are not reusable for their intended purpose. These textiles need to be collected, sorted, and treated in line with the waste hierarchy, prioritising remanufacture and recycling over energy recovery or landfill and incineration.

The potential future use of the textile waste can be divided in two categories: the non-reusable textiles, and the reusable textiles.

Non-reusable textiles can follow one of three specific treatment pathways, namely:

- Remanufacture: It refers to the process of taking used textile products that still contain valuable materials or components and transforming them into new items, either of the same kind or for a different purpose or industry, through disassembly and repurposing. Closed-loop recycling process. Closed-loop recycling in the textile sector involves recycling textiles and clothing into new products of the same type and quality as the original. This system aims to create a continuous cycle of reuse, maintaining the integrity and properties of the materials. For example, recycling polyester garments into new polyester fibres that are used to create new clothing; or turning used cotton fabrics back into cotton fibres for producing new garments. The main challenges of the closed-loop recycling process are:
  - (a) Technical complexities in material recovery: One of the foremost challenges lies in the technical intricacies of material recovery and recycling. Many products are designed for linear use, not for disassembly or recycling. Modern textile products often contain a mix of diverse materials, including plastics, metals, dyes and composites. Even when materials are sorted, effective recycling technologies may not exist for all of them, especially for certain types of plastics or composite materials.
  - (b) Quality retention: Each recycling loop can degrade material properties. Ensuring that recycled materials retain sufficient quality to be used in new products, sometimes multiple times, requires advanced processing techniques and careful material management. This is particularly crucial for plastics and metals where degradation can limit the number of recycling cycles.
  - (d) Economic factors: Beyond the technical aspects, economic factors play a crucial role in the feasibility of closed-loop solutions. Some of these challenges are the high initial investment cost, the virgin material cost advantage (in some cases virgin material is cheaper than the recycled ones) and the need to create large scale economies in order for the project to be profitable.
  - (e) Logistical challenges. The massive amount of textile waste produced by fast fashion creates a major logistical challenge. Developing efficient and cost-effective systems for waste collection and sorting remains difficult. Textile waste sorting is labour and time intensive, and the complexity is further heightened by the wide variety of fibre blends, which complicates the separation and processing processes. Automation for sorting and innovations in textile recycling are growing interests (Labayen et al, 2022).
  - (f) Environmental impact. Textiles contaminated with dirt, chemicals, or mixed materials (e.g., buttons, zippers, coatings) can reduce the quality of the recycled output or make recycling unfeasible.

(g) Consumer behaviour. The success of closed-loop systems depends not only on technological and economic factors but also on significant social and behavioural shifts. Active engagement of individuals and communities is vital, though it comes with its own challenges. The effectiveness of closed-loop systems hinges on consumers actively participating in collection and return programs. Achieving consistent and broad participation calls for increased awareness, behavioural change, and ensuring these practices are convenient and accessible. Recycled materials may face scepticism due to concerns about quality or cleanliness. Addressing these perceptions and building public confidence in recycled products is essential to drive acceptance and demand. Closedloop systems require coordinated action among multiple stakeholders, producers, consumers, recyclers, and policymakers. Establishing strong partnerships and aligning motivations across this diverse landscape can be both challenging and time intensive. Tackling these social and behavioural barriers demands strategic communication, education, and the promotion of social norms that embrace circularity and responsible

consumption. Open-loop recycling process. Open-loop recycling, also known as down-cycling, in the fashion industry refers to the process of recycling textiles and garments into products that differ from their original form. This typically involves transforming used fabrics into materials for other sectors or into lower-value products within fashion. For instance, discarded clothing might be turned into industrial cleaning cloths, insulation materials, or stuffing for furniture and car interiors. Open-loop recycling can involve both mechanical and chemical methods.

## 3.1.6.1 Methods of textile recycling

Textile fibre recycling employs three primary technologies: mechanical, thermal, and chemical (F.Charnley et al.,2024).

 Mechanical recycling is known as the most straightforward and efficient method of textile recycling (Abrishami et al., 2024). In mechanical recycling, there are activities such as shredding and respinning of the fibres and the formation of new yarns and fabrics (Guru et al.2025). Mechanical recycling, characterized by maturity and cost-effectiveness, offers the advantage of processing diverse textile waste streams at variable scales (F. Charnley et al.,2024). Thermo-mechanical recycling is a type of mechanical recycling, where heat and pressure are used to melt synthetic textiles to recover the polymers, which can be re-spun into new fibres. It is mainly used for synthetic materials such as polyester and polyamide, involves melting and then extruding the material to create new fibres. As with mechanical recycling, this technique also preserves the original appearance (Lanz et al 2024) Thermo-mechanical technologies are a promising approach for textile recycling of thermoplastic materials such as polyester and nylon. (Abrishami et al., 2024). The most common method from thermo-chemical recycling is pyrolysis that is effective for the treatment of heterogeneous and complex waste materials. Pyrolysis process has been suggested as a waste-to-energy method to transform textile waste into combustible gas (Hee Sue et al., 2022) Chemical recycling enables a circular polymer economy by converting waste polymers into virgin monomers, fuels, or other valuable chemicals. In theory, this kind of recycling could produce monomers in this manner allows to produce polymer fibers of equal or superior quality compared to virgin materials. However, chemical recycling currently struggles with the

presence of blends/multi, as well as chemical and additives such as dyes and anti-wrinkle agents, which obstruct the process (Ndagano et al. 2025)

 Biochemical recycling is a process that uses enzymes to depolymerize end-of-life textiles into monomers, which can subsequently be used in the production of value-added products such as glucose and bioethanol (Ndagano et al. 2025)

Methods such as pyrolysis and chemical or biochemical recycling are examined to demonstrate the viability of textile recovery. However, their implementation is often constrained by high operational costs and technical complexities (Guru et al.2025).

![](_page_60_Diagram_6.jpeg)

Figure 8. Mechanical recycling process for textile waste. Source: (Guru et al.2025)

![](_page_61_Diagram_1.jpeg)

Figure 9. Chemical recycling process for textile waste. Source: (Guru et al.2025)

The IoT is being considered for use in the sorting and identification of textile waste for further improvement of recycling (Damayanti et al., 2021). End-of-life textiles can be depolymerized using enzymes in a bio-chemical recycling process, breaking them down into their monomeric building blocks to serve as substrates for the production of value-added products like sugars (glucose) and bioethanol. The biological processes have the advantage of being low in energy demand, benign solvents and chemicals, and renewal instead of fossil carbon-based. Along with this, the process is extremely complex, which makes those perfect for separating combined textile waste. Chemical conversion of postconsumer mixed textile waste using microwave-assisted glycolysis over a ZnO catalyst followed by solvent dissolution also has been investigated with promising results. A simple solvent dissolution enabled the separation of cotton and nylon (Andini et al., 2024).

Finally, an innovative method called Bio Recycling has been presented by the Hong Kong Research Institute of Textiles and Apparel (HKRITA). Textile waste recycling can be achieved through a bioprocess incorporating enzymatic hydrolysis with ultrasound pre-treatment to transform composite materials into bio-products. The polyester component can be re-spun into reusable yarn, while the generated cellulosic powder can undergo further processing to produce soluble glucose. The Green Machine, developed by the Hong Kong Research Institute of Textiles and Apparel (HKRITA) is a polyester and cellulose recycling system by hydrothermal treatment and dissolution treatment on cotton and polyester blends. Through decomposing cotton into cellulose powders, it enables the separation of the polyester fibres from the blends. The quality of the polyester fibres is maintained, permitting fibre-to-fibre recycling. Recycled cotton cellulosic powder is used in respinning and agriculture. The project aims to develop the Green Machine into a complete treatment process and an industrial set-up. Through separation of the blended textile, Green Machine will recover PET fibre for re-spinning, while converting cotton fibre into cellulosic powder for a number of uses, including the re-producing of cellulose composite fibre, cellulosic water-retaining material for cotton farming (Absorboost), and PFC-free function durable water-repellent (DWR) surface finish, hence fulfilling the goal of "end-to-end recycling" where post-consumer textiles are recovered as the raw materials of the production cycle. As HKRITA claims Green Machine is a viable commercial solution to separate cotton and polyester blended textile (Ghosh, 2022).

Circ has developed an innovative hydrothermal process to recover polyester and cotton from blends to create new polyester and lyocell fibres. While many synthetic recycling startups say they accept textile blends, including polyester-cotton, to produce material for fresh polyester fibers, Circ distinguishes itself by recycling the cotton too.

Ambercycle is developing an enzymatic recycling feature that would allow a multi-fiber output, enabling brands a "one-stop shop" if they want specific blends of polyester, nylon or spandex.

![](_page_62_Picture_6.jpeg)

Dye Recycle is a UK-based company who have developed a system to remove dye, enabling both the textile and the dye to be recycled. Their technology enables separation of dye from synthetic textile waste, so both components can be recycled.

![](_page_62_Picture_8.jpeg)

Epoch Biodesign has developed a biorecycling system using AI-designed enzymes to recycle synthetic textiles (nylon and polyester) using a low heat process. The recycled polymers are then extruded into new fibres.

Worn Again has developed a method for recycling polyester/cotton blends using a closed loop chemistry process. It separates the polyester from the cotton, then the polyester can be re-extruded into new fibre, while the cotton is broken down into cellulose and spun using a wet spinning technique into cellulose fibre.

## 3.1.6.2 Textile reuse methods

Textile reuse can take various forms depending on the origin, type, and condition of the materials, as well as the intended purpose.

- Repairing refers to addressing minor damages or defects in textiles to prolong their usability and functionality.
- Upcycling involves creatively repurposing textiles into new, often higher-value items such as accessories, home furnishings, or artwork. Aus, Moora, Vihma, et al.2021, summarised the results of a more than 5-year practice-led study. They demonstrated that depending on the size of the factory the fabric leftovers and textile waste generated in garment production ranges from 25–40% of the total fabric used. Experiments showed that 50% of that material can be upcycled into new garments and for some types of leftovers, mainly spreading loss and excess fabric, it can even be up to 80%. Implementing upcycling on the industrial level requires transparency to understand the waste created in garment production and create designs that suit the production system (Aus, Moora, Vihma, et al.2021)
- In contrast, downcycling occurs when the quality and economic value of a product obtained from recycling processes is lower than that of the original product. The use of recycled clothing and home textile wastes in agriculture and gardening products, decoration materials, insulation materials, low-quality blankets, and upholstery fabrics are the examples of downcycling (Celep et al., 2023).

The textile industry employs a range of reuse practices aimed at extending the life cycle of textile products and reducing environmental impact. These practices encompass both pre-consumer and post-consumer strategies and align closely with circular economy principles.

## (I) Direct reuse and second-hand markets

Direct reuse involves the redistribution of garments in their original form. Globally, second-hand clothing markets are a major reuse channel, especially prevalent in Africa and South Asia, where imported used clothing is resold. In Europe, approximately 40% of collected post-consumer textiles are exported for reuse, primarily to developing countries (Labayen et al., 2022).

## (II) Industrial reuse and downcycling

Textiles that are no longer suitable for wear are often repurposed for industrial applications such as cleaning rags, insulation, and stuffing materials. This form of reuse allows for the extension of material life but usually results in products of lower economic value compared to the original (Tang, 2023).

## (III) Charitable reuse and donation programs

Many organizations facilitate reuse by collecting clothing donations and redistributing them to individuals in need or to second-hand retailers. This model has both social and environmental benefits and is widely practiced in North America and Europe (Labayen et al., 2022).

### (IV) Corporate take-back<sup>6</sup> schemes

Fashion brands have introduced take-back initiatives wherein consumers return used garments to the point of sale for reuse, recycling, or resale. These systems are part of EPR schemes (Tang,

H&M's Conscious Collection promotes sustainable fashion, using recycled materials to produce new garments. Their garment recycling initiatives encourage customers to return old clothing, which is then processed for reuse.

### 2023).

## (V) Design for reusability and longevity

An emerging reuse strategy involves the design of garments for extended use, ease of repair, and modular upgrading. This approach reduces the rate of garment disposal and encourages multiple use cycles before recycling is considered (Tang, 2023).

## (VI) Digital traceability for facilitating reuse

Innovations such as blockchain and IoT technologies are being tested to support reuse by enabling product traceability and improving the accuracy of textile sorting and logistics. These technologies support higher rates of reuse by providing verifiable data on material composition and use history (Tang, 2023).

Collectively, these practices play a critical role in diverting textiles from landfills and promoting resource efficiency in the fashion and textile industries.

## 3.1.7 Companies and sustainability performance across the value chain

As it has been described in the United Nations Environment Programme (2023), the textile value chain comprises all activities and stakeholders that provide or receive value from designing, developing, making, distributing, retailing, and consuming a textile product (or providing the service that a textile product renders), including the extraction and supply of raw materials, as well as activities involving the textile after its useful service life has ended. The value chain covers all stages in a textile product's life, from supply of raw materials through to disposal after use, and includes the activities linked to value creation such as business models, consumption patterns, investments and regulation. The value chain also comprises the actors undertaking the activities, and the stakeholders that can influence those activities. The textile value chain is thus considered as a whole system that goes beyond the supply chain and the life cycle of products (UN Environment Programme, 2023)

## 3.2 The EU textile industry

## 3.2.1 Market size

The EU textile and clothing (T&C) industry has historically played a pivotal role in European industrial development. While globalisation and delocalisation have altered its structure, the sector remains a cornerstone of the EU economy, particularly in southern and eastern Member States. According to EURATEX (2024), the EU textile and clothing industry comprises approximately 197,000 companies, generating an estimated turnover of €170 billion in 2023. Nearly all these enterprises (99.7%) are classified as micro, small, or medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), underscoring the sector's decentralized and artisanal nature. The industry is a significant employer, providing jobs to about 1.3 million individuals, with women representing more than 70% of the workforce (EURATEX, 2024).

The EU remains the second-largest global exporter of textiles and clothing, following China. In 2023, extra-EU exports amounted to €64 billion, while imports reached €115 billion. The export structure is heavily oriented towards high-value fashion products, with clothing accounting for 58% of the total export value, despite representing only 9% by volume (EURATEX, 2024). This reflects

the EU's competitive advantage in premium and luxury segments.

China remains the EU's most significant import partner, supplying nearly one-third of all T&C imports. Bangladesh, Türkiye, India, and Vietnam are also key suppliers. In contrast, Switzerland and the United Kingdom constitute the primary export destinations, followed by the United States. Notably, exports to China and Türkiye have increased in recent years, highlighting evolving trade dynamics (EURATEX, 2024).

![](_page_77_Figure_5.jpeg)

Figure 17. EU-27 Textile and Clothes Turnover. Source: EURATEX. Economic Update. Fourth quarter 2024 and prospects for 2025.

The EU is a major global player in textile exports, accounting for approximately 25% of world textile exports. Main producing countries include Italy, Germany, France, Spain, Portugal, Poland, Romania and Bulgaria.

The EU trade deficit reached over -50 billion EUR in 2023 (from -72 billion the previous year), a noticeable improvement resulting from a sharp decrease of clothing imports from China and Bangladesh. However, the share of clothing in the EU deficit has increased from 85% to 91%. The textile sector accounted for only 9% of the total trade deficit (against 15% the previous year). At the same time, the export coverage ratio (export over import) improved, rising from 48% in 2022 to 56% last year (EURATEX, 2024). Key export destinations include the United Kingdom, the United States, China, and Switzerland.

Imports, on the other hand, are dominated by low-cost producers such as China, Bangladesh, Turkey, and Vietnam, highlighting the EU's exposure to global supply chain dynamics.

Fashion and clothing (underwear, shirts, suits, dresses, coat etc) comprises 42% of the total value, furnishings and homeware 14%, and textile for industrial uses (ropes and nettings, parachutes, medical textiles, synthetic grass, sunblinds, smart textiles) 19% (EURATEX, 2024). The average consumption of clothing, footwear and household textiles per person in the EU increased most recently from 17kg per person in 2019, to 19kg per person in 2022. Between 2010 and 2019, consumption fluctuated between 14kg and 17kg. This amount is divided into approximately 8 kilograms of clothing, 4 kilograms of footwear, and 7 kilograms of household textiles ( ETC, 2025,

EEA, 2024/2, EEA. 2024/5, ETC/EEA, 2024). 99.7% of the textile and clothes industry in the EU enterprises are Micro and SMEs.

In the fourth quarter of 2024, the clothing sector showed a notable recovery, with production increasing by 6.3% quarter-on-quarter and sales rising by 2.7%. In contrast, textile production continued its downward trend for the eleventh consecutive quarter. Throughout 2024, the EU textile and clothing industry faced persistent difficulties, primarily driven by weak domestic demand. The annual decline in both production and turnover highlights external pressures such as supply chain disruptions, subdued consumer demand, and elevated operational costs. Heightened economic uncertainty has dampened consumer spending and made companies more cautious about investing. Additional challenges include Europe's comparatively high energy costs, complex bureaucratic processes, and the financial burden of complying with new regulatory requirements (EURATEX, 2024).

![](_page_78_Figure_5.jpeg)

Figure 18. Size and % share of T&C Textile Companies in the EU. Source: EURATEX, 2024.

Employment in the textile and clothing sector declined compared to both the last quarter of 2023 and the same period last year, reflecting growing difficulties in workforce retention. Throughout 2024, many T&C companies either downsized or froze hiring due to the uncertain economic environment and rising labour costs.

The EU's trade deficit narrowed quarter-on-quarter, driven by a slowdown in imports from non-EU countries, while exports demonstrated stronger momentum. However, the overall trade outlook remains challenging, with potential risks on the horizon, including the possible introduction of tariffs by the US and corresponding retaliatory measures (EURATEX, 2024).

Europe imported €176.9 billion of apparel in 2023 (up from €144.7 billion in 2018 but down from €193 billion in 2022), corresponding to 27.4 billion units of clothing (down from 30.2 billion). Of the total value of imports, 50.9% was trade in knitted or crocheted apparel (up from 49.7% in 2018) and 49.1% was in non-knitted/woven apparel. Between 2018 and 2023, the market grew by an average of 4.1% per year (CBI, 2025).

![](_page_79_Figure_4.jpeg)

Figure 19. EU Imports from 5 Top Suppliers. Source: EURATEX. Economic Update. Fourth quarter 2024 and prospects for 2025.

The EU is also a large exporter and re-exporter of apparel. According to WTO statistics, the EU was the second largest exporter of apparel in 2022 with a 27.1% share, second only to China. This is shown by Eurostat statistics for 2023, which indicate that the EU exported €140.3 billion worth of apparel (up from €107.3 billion in 2018) of which 73.3% was destined for other countries in the EU and 26.7% for the rest of the world (CBI, 2025). EU apparel exports grew at an average yearly rate of 5.5% between 2018 and 2023. The biggest EU exporters in 2023 were Germany (€25.9 billion), Italy (€25.7 billion), the Netherlands (€15.1 billion), France (€14 billion), Spain (€13.6 billion) and Poland (€10.9 billion). Together, these 6 countries represented 75.2% of the EU's apparel exports. Poland and the Netherlands show the strongest export growth within this group with an average yearly increase in export value of more than 8%. Poland has grown by as much as 14% each year during the same period (CBI, 2025).

![](_page_80_Figure_4.jpeg)

Figure 20. EU Exports to top 5 customers. Source: EURATEX. Economic Update. Fourth quarter 2024 and prospects for 2025.

The EU imports a higher share of its apparel from inside the EU than from outside, at 53.1% and 46.9% respectively. In 2023, developing countries were the origin of 44.4% of all EU clothing imports in terms of value (down from 48.4% in 2018 and from its peak of 49.2% in 2022). The value of EU apparel imports originating from developing countries corresponded to €78.5 billion in 2023 (up from €70.1 billion in 2018 but down from €94.9 billion in 2022) growing at an average yearly rate of 2.3% between 2018 and 2023.

Imports shares showed different patterns in value and in volume when looking at the more specific product breakdown. Women's clothing (26% in value, 12% in volume) and other garments knitted and woven (28% in value, 16% in volume) represented the main segments, both in value and volume, while other main segments were men's clothing (18% in value, 9% in volume) and technical textiles (8% in value, 16% in volume). Technical textiles recorded the sharpest contraction in value among textile and clothing imported products in 2023 (almost -23%), followed by woven and knitted fabrics (-20%), home textiles (-19%), yarns and natural fibres (-17%), men's and women's clothing (between -14% and -15%). Similarly, man-made fibres and carpets recorded large declines during the year (-13%). EU imports in volumes were also down with technical textiles, fabrics and clothing recording the deepest drops (between -15% and -20%).

## The latest data about EU industry textile industry performance reveal the following (Imarc, 2025):

➔ The European textile market is projected to reach a value of USD 409.4 billion by 2033, up from an estimated USD 261.7 billion in 2024, reflecting a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 4.6% over the period 2025–2033. ➔ Germany leads the market with a share exceeding 20.7% as of 2024. The sector is experiencing significant expansion, driven by increasing demand for sustainable textiles, continuous technological advancements in production processes, and the strong performance of the fashion and home furnishings industries. These developments are further supported by a culture of innovation and shifting consumer preferences throughout the region. ➔ Moreover, the EU regulations and policies for sustainable practices with reduced environmental impacts drive the demand for ecofriendly manufacturing practices in textiles. Advances in digital textile printing and automated production processes are further improving efficiency and quality of fabric production, attracting global investments in the sector. The fashion industry is growing in the region as well, especially with global fashion capitals such as Paris and Milan enjoying significant influence. ➔ Boosting interest in luxury home furnishing and smart textiles are also contributing to the market. Furthermore, demand is rising for technical textiles applied in various sectors, such as the automotive and health and construction industries. ➔ Technical textiles also provide functionalities, such as durability, fire resistance, and antibacterial properties, matching the evolving demands of advanced industries. The inclusion of smart textiles in clothing and medical applications that integrate sensors and responsive materials within them also increases the rate of demand.

## 3.2.2 Trends and dynamics

The textile industry in Europe is a dynamic and evolving sector that plays a crucial role in the global economy. With its long history and significant technological advancements, Europe continues to be a key player in both manufacturing and design. The European textile industry is experiencing major changes, propelled by growing sustainability demands, rapid technological innovation, and evolving consumer tastes. Notable trends include increased interest in sustainable textiles, the integration of advanced technologies, and expanding markets for both luxury and technical fabrics. Additionally, the strong push toward a circular economy is reshaping the industry's priorities around resource efficiency and waste minimisation.

Within the European textile industry, manufacturers and fashion retailers are under increasing pressure to comply with new Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) regulations while also responding to heightened consumer expectations for ethical and transparent practices. ESGrelated developments are expected to significantly reshape key business functions, including product design, production processes, corporate reporting, and marketing strategies. Firms that proactively adapt will gain a competitive advantage, whereas those that delay risk reputational damage, regulatory penalties, and financial underperformance.

## (I) Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD)

A cornerstone of the 2025 EU ESG regulatory framework is the full-scale implementation of the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD). This directive mandates that large European enterprises disclose comprehensive information on ESG performance, including climate-related risks, supply chain due diligence, environmental impacts, and social outcomes. Industry leaders are actively responding to this regulatory shift by strengthening their internal ESG infrastructures. For textile professionals, adapting to this new regulatory environment necessitates not only compliance, but also the cultivation of technical expertise in ESG reporting, cross-functional collaboration, and systems-level thinking.

## (II) Eco-design for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR)

The enactment of the Eco-design for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) constitutes a structural shift in the European textile sector, introducing binding sustainability criteria at the product design phase. The regulation emphasizes parameters such as durability, reparability, and recyclability. Leading firms have begun aligning with ESPR principles. For industry stakeholders, the regulation not only imposes compliance obligations but also presents a platform for R&D-driven innovation, sustainable product differentiation, and enhanced consumer trust.

## (III) Digital Product Passports (DPPs)

DPPs represent a key digital innovation in the textile industry's ESG transformation. These tools digitally encode comprehensive lifecycle information, including origin of raw materials, processing impacts, and end-of-life pathways, thereby enhancing product-level transparency and traceability. Information accessibility through technologies such as QR codes empowers consumers and regulatory actors to verify claims related to environmental and social performance. For textile producers, DPPs offer not only a mechanism for compliance and transparency but also a datadriven foundation for improving circularity metrics and sustainability reporting.

This draft deliverable has not yet been validated by the granting authorities The European Commission's Green Claims Directive introduces a stringent framework for

validating environmental marketing claims. Under this directive, any public environmental assertion must be substantiated by methodologically robust, verifiable, and scientifically grounded evidence. Enforcement is anticipated to intensify from 2025 onward, particularly within the textile and apparel sectors. Consequently, cross-disciplinary coordination between legal, marketing, and sustainability professionals is essential to mitigate legal risk and safeguard corporate credibility.

## (V) Circular Economy Initiatives

The transition toward circular economy models is accelerating as firms seek to reduce material throughput and decouple growth from resource consumption. Textile manufacturers are scaling initiatives in reuse, resale, repair, and closed-loop recycling to reduce environmental externalities and enhance resource efficiency.

The confluence of evolving ESG regulatory frameworks, increasing consumer awareness, and rapid technological development is redefining the operating environment for European textile firms. Companies that proactively embed ESG considerations into governance structures, supply chain processes, and product innovation pipelines will be better positioned to achieve resilience and competitiveness. For professionals in the sector, future-readiness entails continuous knowledge acquisition, interdisciplinary collaboration, and strategic foresight in sustainability planning.

## (VI) Industry 4.0 Integration

The European textile and clothing industry is undergoing a significant transformation driven by the principles and technologies of Industry 4.0. This shift marks a move from traditional manufacturing models to digitally enabled, data-driven production systems that are more flexible, efficient, and sustainable. The adoption of Industry 4.0 is not only reshaping how textiles are produced but is also redefining business models and value chains across the sector.

The integration of Industry 4.0 in textiles involves a suite of advanced technologies:

- Internet of Things (IoT): Deployment of sensors and connected devices across machinery allows for real-time monitoring of production parameters, predictive maintenance, and optimization of resource use.
- Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning: These technologies enhance decision-making across the value chain from trend forecasting and inventory management to defect detection and process automation.
- Automation and Robotics: Increasingly applied in spinning, weaving, dyeing, and sewing operations, automation improves precision, reduces manual labour, and enhances workplace safety.
- Big Data and Advanced Analytics: Companies leverage data collected from production lines, consumer behaviour, and supply chains to identify inefficiencies and anticipate market demand.
- 3D Printing and Virtual Prototyping: These enable rapid design iterations and support the development of customized or on-demand fashion products.
- Blockchain and Digital Product Passports: Emerging tools to guarantee product traceability, authenticity, and sustainability credentials.

Despite its potential, Industry 4.0 adoption in textiles faces several obstacles (Forno et al., 2021; Singh et al., 2025):

- High capital expenditure: Investment in digital infrastructure and smart equipment is often unaffordable for SMEs, which form the backbone of the European textile sector.
- Skills gap: There is a notable shortage of workers with the interdisciplinary skills required to

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

operate, maintain, and innovate using Industry 4.0 technologies.

- Legacy systems: Existing machinery and IT systems are frequently incompatible with new digital tools.
- Cybersecurity concerns: The increasing digitalization of operations raises exposure to cyber risks and requires enhanced protection mechanisms.

## (VII) Demand for Technical Textiles

Technical Textiles are materials and products primarily manufactured for their technical performance and functional properties rather than aesthetic or decorative characteristics. These textiles are used across various industries including automotive, healthcare, construction, agriculture, and defence. They are engineered with specific functionalities such as high strength, durability, resistance to heat and chemicals, and improved safety and efficiency. This trend presents an opportunity for manufacturers focusing on innovation. The technical textiles market is at the forefront of industrial transformation, with rapid advancements in sustainable, smart, and high-performance fabrics. As global industries integrate advanced textile solutions, the market is poised for steady expansion over the next decade. With continued investments in nanotechnology, AI-driven manufacturing, and circular economy practices, the future of technical textiles is both dynamic and promising.

According to a report by Data Bridge Market Research, the Europe Technical Textile Market was valued at USD 42,150.1 million in 2024 and is projected to reach USD 60,267.8 million by 2032 (DBMR, 2021). This growth is driven by factors such as high demand for fuel-efficient and lightweight transportation vehicles across the globe. The increasing demand for high-performance materials in sectors such as automotive, construction, and healthcare has significantly boosted the adoption of technical textiles. These materials offer enhanced properties such as durability, flexibility, and resistance to environmental conditions, making them ideal for applications like geotextiles, protective clothing, and medical textiles.

Major market players in the EU are DuPont, Ahlstrom-Munksjö, Freudenberg Performance Materials, Mitsui Chemicals and Alexium International.

The latest facts about technological advancement on the EU Technical Textiles comprises the following (Claight, 2025):

- In April 2024, BASF's sustainable polyamide PA6 and PA6.6 product range, was certified as Recycled Claim Standard (RCS) for textile applications. This label enables manufacturers to market certified textiles that have been produced using recycled raw materials.
- In March 2024, the Sustainable Transition Fund granted USD 892,480 to support research that will help to shape the sustainable future of the UK fashion and textile industry. It plans to establish a data-driven baseline to ensure future strategy is aligned with environmental targets. This two-year project is led by the Leeds Institute of Textiles and Colour (LITAC) and is supported by the National Environment Research Council, the Arts and Humanities Research Council, and Innovate UK.
- In January 2024, Ohoskin, an Italian manufacturer of an alternative material to animal leather, and Recarbon, a company that has developed a one-stop solution for the circularity of carbon fibre, announced a collaboration to create a new material for the automotive, marine, and furniture industries. The new material will be produced by combining Ohoskin's leather alternative made from orange and cactus by-products with ReCarbon's recycled carbon fibre.
- In September 2023, MYGroup, a Yorkshire-based waste management firm, announced the UK's first green textiles factory in Hull. The facility constitutes a recycling and remanufacturing process for waste textiles, comprising clothing, accessories, and home furnishings.

(VIII) Innovative Fabric Technologies and Smart Textiles in Europe Smart textiles are becoming increasingly prevalent across various industrial sectors. Smart textiles are innovative products integrating sensors and actuators that enable interaction between the wearer and their environment. These advanced fabrics, which draw on innovations in materials science, electronics, and biotechnology, are crucial for monitoring physiological functions and facilitating data transmission via IoT (Muñoz Gonzalez, 2025). Another type of innovative textiles are nano-engineered fibres. Micro and nano-scale engineered fibres that offer improved strength, durability, and unique properties (Pereira et al. 2020).

Also, an emerging trend in the modern industry are the printable electronics. Integrating circuits, displays and other electronic components directly into textile structures through advanced printing techniques (Mahbub et al. 2024).

The Smart Textiles Market grew from USD 2.79 billion in 2024 to USD 3.27 billion in 2025. It is expected to continue growing at a CAGR of 18.37%, reaching USD 7.67 billion by 2030. Most applications are concentrated in 4 main fields: (a) Protection and defence, (b) Sports and fitness, (c) Industrial and technical applications (transport, architecture) and (d) Medicine and healthcare (Research And Markets, May 2025).

The armed forces and the military industry have been a primary driver in smart materials for wearables from a very early stage of development, providing clearly defined performance needs and a potentially large market segment. Recently the European Defence Agency began funding relevant projects (e.g. "smARt Multifunction tExtiles for integrated Soldier Systems").

Two European companies are included in the list of the five more important companies of specialty fibres, namely:

❖ Fibrecore (Humanetics Group) - USA ❖ Corning Incorporated - USA ❖ Ahlstrom - Finland ❖ Lenzing Group - Austria ❖ Toray Industries (Mitsui Group) - Japan

Another important European company producing high performance technical fabrics is Schoeller Textil AG in Switzerland. Fabrics of Schoeller AG are primarily used in gear, workwear, and sportswear and the company specializes in water-repellent, breathable, and abrasion-resistant textile solutions.

## 3.2.3 Geographical poles

The analysis of the geographical distribution of the textile industry in the European Union, based on data concerning turnover, employment, and exports, reveals the existence of distinct regional poles, each contributing uniquely to the sector's structure and performance (EURATEX, 2024).

Italy clearly stands as the dominant actor, holding the largest shares in turnover (36%), employment (24%), and exports (30%). This comprehensive leadership underscores the country's integrated capacity across the textile value chain, combining high-value manufacturing with extensive labour

engagement and a strong export orientation. Italy's traditional emphasis on luxury textiles, fashion, and design, coupled with advanced industrial capabilities, positions the country as the main pillar of the EU textile economy.

Germany follows as the second most influential pole, characterized by a substantial share in turnover (15%) and exports (18%), although it accounts for only 9% of employment. This configuration reflects Germany's positioning as a technological and industrial leader in the textile sector, with a focus on high productivity and value-added segments such as technical and functional textiles. The relatively lower employment share indicates a capital-intensive and innovation-driven textile economy.

France constitutes a third pole, particularly in high-end fashion and luxury textiles. With 9% of turnover and 15% of exports but only 7% of employment, France is indicative of a design-and brand-oriented model. The French textile industry prioritizes quality, creativity, and sustainability, leading to high export values despite its comparatively limited industrial base.

Portugal presents a contrasting profile, with a high employment share (10%) but modest contributions to turnover (5%) and exports (2%). This pattern identifies Portugal as a labourintensive production base, specializing in cost-competitive manufacturing for both domestic and international markets.

A similar trend is observed in Romania and Poland, which respectively contribute 8% and 10% to EU textile employment while generating relatively lower turnover and export values. These countries function primarily as subcontracting hubs within the broader European textile supply chain, often serving the needs of Western European brands.

Spain represents a balanced regional player, with shares of 7% in turnover, 6% in employment, and 8% in exports. The Spanish textile industry is diversified, with strengths in both fashion and home textiles, and maintains a steady presence in international markets. This distribution suggests a relatively self-sufficient and resilient sector.

Smaller yet strategically significant roles are played by countries such as Belgium, the Netherlands, and Austria. These Member States exhibit strong export performance relative to their overall size and are often specialized in technical textiles, textile machinery, or serve as key logistics and distribution hubs within Europe. For instance, the Netherlands, although not prominent in terms of turnover or employment, accounts for 6% of EU textile exports, indicating its function as a re-export and trade-oriented centre.

In conclusion, the EU textile industry is characterized by a multi-polar structure, where countries assume differentiated roles based on their industrial traditions, labour cost structures, technological capacity, and market positioning. Italy operates as a comprehensive leader, Germany as a hightech industrial hub, and France as a design-driven exporter. Meanwhile, countries like Portugal, Romania, and Poland serve as labour-intensive production centres, while others, such as Belgium and the Netherlands, contribute through specialized and export-intensive niches. This geographical differentiation supports the resilience and diversity of the European textile value chain, allowing for specialization, intra-European cooperation, and global competitiveness.

|-----------------------------|-------------------------------|--------------------------------------|

## 3.2.4 Economic, legal, and institutional factors and conditions

## 3.2.4.1 Economic factors and conditions

As a labour-intensive industry experiencing rapid technological advancement, the textile sector, particularly its SMEs, faces significant constraints due to a shortage of skilled workers. Emerging skills trends and evolving occupational profiles support the assumption of a gradual transition from low-skilled to medium- and high-skilled employment.

## Strengths of the EU Textile Industry

❖ Exceptional production quality, especially in technical textiles and premium fashion segments. ❖ Rapid adoption of innovative and sustainable materials. ❖ Strong capabilities in design, creativity, and brand development, particularly in high-end markets. ❖ Market leadership in high value-added niches where competitive advantages are hard to replicate. ❖ Agile, specialised firms leveraging emerging technologies and consumer trends.

Challenges of the EU Textile Industry

❖ Intensifying competition from emerging market players and cross-sector entrants (e.g. ecommerce platforms). ❖ High energy costs: Energy costs in Europe are significantly higher than in other regions, making production less competitive ❖ SMEs' specific challenges such as slim profit margins. ❖ Sustainability challenges across the value chain, including a high environmental footprint and costly compliance with EU environmental and chemical regulations. ❖ Strong international competition exacerbated by weaker environmental and social standards in third countries. ❖ Persistent skills gaps and high labour costs. ❖ Ageing workforce. ❖ Innovation largely concentrated in a limited number of EU Member States. ❖ Heavy reliance on imported materials and products. ❖ Complex global value chains. ❖ Geopolitical volatile conditions. ❖ Regulatory complexities and compliance costs.

Despite the availability of information nowadays it is difficult for the average European consumer to fully understand the complex processes behind the wide range of products offered by textile retailers and wholesalers (Copenhagen Economics, 2025). These businesses source their products from various global markets, each of which specialises in specific product types, raw materials, and production techniques.

At the beginning of the supply chain, different raw materials are produced. These include natural fibres like cotton and wool from agriculture, as well as synthetic fibres such as polyester and nylon, which are derived from petrochemicals. These raw materials then undergo several stages of processing, including yarn spinning, knitting or weaving, dyeing, printing, and trimming before

reaching the textile manufacturing stage. Then, materials are cut and sewn into finished products according to the retailers' or wholesalers' design specifications. Suppliers used by textile retailers and wholesalers can be based within or outside the EU and are typically organised into different tiers (Copenhagen Economics, 2025):

➢ Tier 0: Agents acting on behalf of retailers and wholesalers ➢ Tier 1: Final product manufacturers that interact directly with retailers/wholesalers or their agents ➢ Tier 2: Component producers (e.g., yarn or fabric manufacturers) ➢ Tier 3 and beyond: Raw material suppliers

The number of tiers in a supply chain affects the level of transparency. As the number of tiers increases, it becomes more difficult for companies to monitor and control production practices across the supply chain. Most retailers and wholesalers have direct relationships only with Tier 1 suppliers, meaning visibility and oversight significantly decrease in the upper tiers.

While some companies work with vertically integrated suppliers, those managing multiple steps of the production process, this approach is not always practical. Different textile products require distinct raw materials and specialised expertise, limiting the feasibility of vertical integration across the entire product range (Copenhagen Economics, 2025).

![](_page_91_Diagram_3.jpeg)

Figure 26. Overview of linkages between the circular business model, product design, consumer behaviour and policy. Source: EEA, 2024.

Main features of the current conditions of the European textile and garment industry comprise the following:

- (a) The sector has become increasingly international. Raw materials, components, and textile products are traded across borders before they reach the final consumers. The presence of European textile retailers and wholesalers in sourcing and production countries is a product of globalisation.
- (b) Most of the sector's value added is created within Europe. The European textile sector is economically significant, providing employment and contributing to the European GDP. One measure of economic significance is value added, which consists of wages and company profits, which are also the main components of GDP.
- (c) The European textile sector supports jobs and economic activity in Europe.
- (d) The value contribution from the sector has declined since 2000.

## 3.2.4.2 EU textile industry legal framework

The EU textile market as a part of the global textile, fabrics and clothes industry is not unaffected by the significant revisions taking place. Major transformation forces are the needs for enhancing sustainability, digitalization, and global competitiveness in the sector.

Ever more European buyers require facilities, products and/or materials to be independently certified for social responsibility, environmental impact and use of harmful substances. The certification process can be time-consuming and costly. However, getting certification shows a commitment to sustainable practices throughout the supply chain. ## Labour regulations

EU labour laws provide strong protections for workers, which directly affect textile manufacturers, retailers, and importers:

⮚ Working Time Directive (2003/88/EC): Governs working hours, rest periods, and paid leave. ⮚ Health and Safety at Work Framework Directive (89/391/EEC): Enforces occupational safety and health standards. ⮚ European Pillar of Social Rights: Promotes fair working conditions, gender equality, and social protection.

These regulations are further enforced at national level, ensuring high labour standards across member states.

## Environmental legislation

The EU has implemented an advanced environmental protection legal framework. Its laws and directives directly affect textile and fabric production and import. The main aspects of this legal framework that affect directly the textile industry are the following:

⮚ REACH Regulation (EC 1907/2006). REACH is the main EU law to protect human health and the environment from the risks that can be posed by chemicals, like the ones used in textile production. ⮚ EU Green Deal. The European Green Deal is the EU's growth strategy. Launched in 2019, it consists of a package of policy initiatives, which set the EU on the path to a green transition, with the ultimate goal of reaching climate neutrality by 2050. Under the Green Deal, the Council, together with the European Parliament as co-legislators, have adopted legislation, which has turned the strategy's vision into laws and rules, which are applied, in all EU member states. ⮚ CEAP - Circular Economy Action Plan. The European Commission adopted the new CEAP in March 2020. It is one of the main building blocks of the European Green Deal and. provides a future-oriented agenda for achieving a cleaner and more competitive Europe in co-creation with economic actors, consumers, citizens and civil society organisations. It aims at accelerating the transformational change required by the European Green Deal, while building on circular economy actions implemented since 2015. This plan ensures that the regulatory framework is streamlined and made fit for a sustainable future, that the new opportunities from the transition are maximised, while minimizing burdens on people and businesses. ⮚ ESPR - Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation entered into force on 18 July 2024. ESPR is part of a package of measures that are central to achieving the aims of the 2020 Circular Economy Action Plan and fostering the transition to a circular, sustainable, and competitive economy. It will contribute to helping the EU reach its environmental and climate goals, double its circularity rate of material use and achieve its energy efficiency targets by 2030. ⮚ Waste Framework Directive: The targeted revision of the Waste Framework Directive, which entered into force on 16 October 2025, focuses on textile waste, building upon these principles and helping to align standards across the EU with a common set of rules for all Member States. ⮚ Climate Law: The European Climate Law writes into law the goal set out in the European Green Deal for Europe's economy and society to become climate-neutral by 2050. The law also sets the intermediate target of reducing net greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by at least 55% by 2030, compared to 1990 levels. ⮚ Clean Industrial Deal: The Clean Industrial Deal launched on 26 February 2025 outlines concrete actions to turn decarbonisation into a driver of growth for European industries. The Commission will adopt a circular economy act in 2026 to accelerate the circular transition.

## Trade policies and tariffs

Common External Tariffs apply to non-EU imports. Many textile-producing countries benefit from the Generalised Scheme of Preferences Plus (GSP+) that give developing countries a special incentive to pursue sustainable development and good governance. Eligible countries have to implement 27 international conventions on: human rights, labour rights, the environment and good governance.

exports. Beneficiary countries include Bolivia, Cape Verde, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Pakistan, Philippines, Sri Lanka, Uzbekistan. The current GSP+ is valid until 2027. The part of the EU Generalized System of Preferences GSP is also the Everything but Arms (EBA) initiative under which all imports to the EU from the least developed countries are duty-free and quota-free, with the exception of armaments.

Another upcoming legislation concerning the textile industry is EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM), which will apply in its definitive regime from 2026, while the current transitional phase lasts between 2023 and 2025. CBAM is the EU's tool to put a fair price on the carbon emitted during the production of carbon intensive goods that are entering the EU, and to encourage cleaner industrial production in non-EU countries.

## Intellectual Property Rights (IPRs)

The textile industry in the EU implements a strict and well oriented legal framework concerning IPRs. Main control bodies that enforce EU IPR legislation are the EU Intellectual Property Office (EUIPO) which supervises design and trademark registration and the customs offices legally responsible to investigate and prosecute counterfeiting and IP infringement.

EURATEX provides tailored information and resources to companies (mainly SMEs) to manage their IPRs, as they do not have enough human resources to deal with and implement these measures. The use of IP systems is typically seen by SMEs as a challenge, as they find its procedures very costly and they lack the necessary expertise. EURATEX reports to the European Commission these challenges and those related to IPRs enforcement.

## Product Standards, labelling, safety regulations

Consumer safety and transparency are regulated by the Textile Labelling and Fibre Composition Regulation (EU No 1007/2011) which requires disclosure of fibre composition (silk, wool, nylon, polyester, cotton) and origin. According to this Regulation, products must be labelled with information concerning their fibre composition (e.g. 100% Polyester).

In general, textile products must carry a durable, legible, easily visible, and accessible label, either on the product or on its packaging. The regulation does not require the provision of product size or the provision of care instructions. Almost all clothes nevertheless include such information because it aids consumers in choosing products of the appropriate size and implements adequate usage of them. Voluntary standards that are used commonly are:

 Joint European standard for size labelling of clothes, formally known as the EN 13402 Size designation of clothes is a European standard for labelling clothes sizes. The standard is based on body dimensions measured in centimetres, and as such, and its aim is to make it easier for people to find clothes in sizes that fit them. ISO 3758:2023. Textiles — Care labelling code using symbols. The International Organization for Standardization (ISO) has issued the 3758:2023 standard for textiles, which replaced the previous one from 2012. The standard sets a system of graphics and symbols for the purpose of labelling textile products in order to provide the necessary information for their proper cleaning and protection from irreversible damage. ISO 3758:2023 applies to all textile products except non-removable furniture covers, non-removable mattress covers and carpets that require professional cleaning, as they are considered to require special procedures for their care and cleaning. The symbols are addressed to consumers and professional users and concern washing, bleaching, drying and ironing. For industrial users, the corresponding symbols are described by ISO 30023.

## EU textile industry institutional landscape

The EU enlisted numerous policy initiatives and funding mechanisms to support the textile industry. The flagship of these strategies is the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles (2022) which sets a 2030 vision for sustainable, reusable, and recyclable textiles.

Furthermore, Academic Institutions and textile companies, especially SMEs, can benefit from a wide range of EU programs, like Horizon Europe, COSME, LIFE, European Regional Development Fund (ERDF) and European Social Fund+ (ESF+). These funds support research and innovation actions or provide grants for a wide range of business plans from sustainable materials and circular models to digital production and skills training.

Regarding the business networks, the principal associations of European textile industry are: The European Apparel and Textile Confederation (EURATEX) and The European Technology Platform for the Future of Textiles and Clothing (Textile ETP).

EURATEX is a part of an extensive network representing European industries, which includes the Confederation of European Business (BUSINESSEUROPE) and the Alliance for a Competitive European Industry (ACEI). EURATEX is cooperating daily with the other branch federations linked to their sector, research organisations and universities active in the textile and clothing industry (AUTEX, Textranet), and the Textile and Clothing Information Centre (CITH) which provides tailormade reports on EU trade in textile and clothing. EURATEX is an observatory Member of CEN, the official European body for standardisation, and follows in particular the works of TC248 technical committee for textiles products. EURATEX is a founding member of the European Technology Platform for the Future of Textiles and Clothing and the International Apparel Federation. Since 1993, EURATEX is part of the regular Social Dialogue together with the Trade Unions, organised by the European Commission's DG Employment to promote the actions aimed to safeguard the respect of fundamental labour standards.

The European Technology Platform for the Future of Textiles and Clothing (Textile ETP) was created in 2004 to match research and innovation needs and priorities of the European industry (represented by companies, associations, and clusters) with the knowledge, scientific and technological capacities of universities, research organisations and technology developers to raise the sector's competitiveness through intensified collaborative research and innovation. Since 2013, the Textile ETP has been established as an international non-profit association under Belgian law with its permanent premises based in Brussels. According to the current strategy, adopted in 2018, the Textile ETP acts as Strategic Connector, Think Tank and Advocate, and Intelligence and Funding Access Provider to all our members across Europe.    

There are also a number of national federations that influence policy and sectoral training to various extent. Important national federations are:

⮚ Fedustria – Belgian Federation of textile, woodworking and furniture industries (Belgium) ⮚ Union des Industries Textiles – UIT (France) ⮚ Gesamtverband der deutschen Textil- und Modeindustrie – T M (Germany) ⮚ Confindustria Moda – Federazione tessile e moda (Italy) ⮚ Norsk Industri – The Federation of Norwegian Industries ⮚ Turkish Clothing Manufacturers' Association – TGSD

⮚ Turkish Textile & Raw Material Exporters' Association – ITHIB ⮚ Federation of Apparel and Textiles Industry Employers – PIOT ⮚ Finnish Textile & Fashion – STJM (Finland) ⮚ Associaçao Têxtil e Vestuàrio de Portugal – ATP (Portugal)

## EU education and research ecosystem

As the textile industry plays such a significant role in the EU economy it is easily understandable that a variety of education and technical institutions of different levels are offering training and knowledge building courses on the subject. The EU's textile sector is supported by a dense web of research institutions, technology centres, and innovation hubs, often working closely with industry partners. Vocational schools and technical institutes to top-tier universities contribute to equipping students, young professionals and experienced workers with the knowledge and skills needed to navigate to a rapidly evolving sector.

In a number of European countries like Germany, Austria, and the Netherlands, a robust dual education system has been constructed where apprentices combine hands-on work in the textile industry with formal training.

In higher education organizations (Universities, Colleges, Technical Institutes) across Europe a significant number of courses are offering a variety of diplomas and academic degrees.

The European Union also supports innovation and research solutions through technology platforms such as the European Textile Technology Platform (ETP). Other supporting platforms are operating at a regional level, such as "Techtextil" in Germany or "Made in Green" in Portugal which bring together businesses, research centres, and training providers to share knowledge and scale up new solutions.

## 3.2.5 European consumer behaviour towards textile consumption and sustainable practices

Consumer behaviour and attitudes toward textile consumption and sustainable practices in the EU are undergoing significant transformation. This shift is driven by increased environmental awareness, evolving regulatory frameworks, and changing consumer preferences and due to its significant environmental and social impacts, especially those associated with fast fashion and overconsumption. However, challenges persist, particularly in bridging the gap between sustainable intentions and actual purchasing behaviours (Cosma Α., 2024). The transition from a linear economic model, characterised by the "take-make-dispose" paradigm, to a circular economy model depends heavily on consumer attitudes and engagement throughout the entire product lifecycle (Koszewska, 2019).

Ribeiro et al. (2023) conducted a survey in Portugal with 1026 respondents to assess attitudes towards sustainable textile consumption across four phases: acquisition, use, maintenance, and disposal.

In Poland, Popowska and Sinkiewicz (2021) analysed fashion consumers' awareness of sustainability-related concepts such as fair trade, organic certification, and carbon emissions.

Nencková et al. (2020) studied 1046 Czech consumers, focusing specifically on post-consumer textile disposal and its relation to demographic variables.

Koszewska (2019) provided a European perspective through a conceptual analysis of the consumer's role in advancing a circular economy in textiles and apparel.

A study focusing on Slovenian consumers revealed a positive attitude toward eco-friendly textile products, with many perceiving them as higher quality and more sustainable. Certified labels play a crucial role in influencing purchasing decisions, as they serve as indicators of environmental friendliness and ethical production practices (Zver & Vukasović, 2021). Similarly, research involving Romanian consumers indicated that altruistic values, social norms, and sustainable attitudes positively influence the intention to purchase sustainable clothing. This suggests that both personal values and societal influences are pivotal in shaping consumer behaviour in the textile sector (Cosma Α., 2024).

Over half of the United Kingdom (Mintel, UK Fashion and Sustainability Market Report 2024) and German (Mintel, Germany Fashion and Sustainability Market Report 2024) consumers regard sustainability as an important purchase factor for fashion. In the US (Mintel, US Women's Clothing Market Report 2023), two in three consumers say they pay attention to eco-friendly claims when buying fashion items. While a significant portion of Germans believe that everyone is personally responsible for protecting the environment, the majority try to act in ways that aren't harmful to the planet. Consumers across the US, UK, and Germany generally believe that a sizable portion of the responsibility for sustainability in fashion lies with businesses.

It is often believed that fostering positive attitudes toward the environment through consumer education can drive sustainable consumption patterns. However, research conducted in Budapest, Hungary (Kemi & Zilahy, 2025) found that attitudes are the weakest predictors of repair and giveaway intentions. While other post-purchase activities, such as recycling, may be more influenced by attitudes, the findings of this study suggested that education alone may not be sufficient to address the challenges of sustainable fashion consumption. Instead, greater emphasis should be placed on improving the repairability of products and showcasing practical examples of sustainable practices. These approaches may have a more substantial impact on encouraging sustainable fashion consumption and reducing the environmental burden of the fashion industry. Moreover, research in Poland about consumer choice between sustainable and non-sustainable apparel revealed the importance of the product to provide enough aesthetic, functional and financial benefits, to satisfy their needs and aspirations besides being sustainable or "green" (Rahman & Koszewska, 2020).

Furthermore, findings (Banytė, Vaidelinskaitė & Šalčiuvienė, 2023) suggest that consumers assess both practical and ethical attributes of clothing before making their purchase. However, when making their final decision to purchase ethical clothing, practical attributes often become the key criteria for decision-making. It has been concluded that physical and external practical factors are the strongest influencers on consumer attitudes towards ethical clothing, while a sustainability attribute is among the ethical factors that has the strongest effect on positive-attitude-formation among consumers. Moreover, findings suggest that the lower the perceived ethical clothing value cost, the more positive attitude towards the brands exists. Neither the fauna nor environment had any significant effect on consumer attitudes towards ethical clothing. The same study has also confirmed that attitudes significantly and positively influence ethical clothing purchase intentions (Magnuson, Reimers, Chao, 2017).

It is common ground that despite growing awareness, a notable discrepancy exists between consumers' sustainable intentions and their actual purchasing behaviours, commonly referred to as the "value-action gap". This green gap between what consumers say and do is arguably one of the greatest challenges for marketers, policymakers, and nonprofit organizations working to

promote the United Nations' 2030 sustainable development goals (SDGs), in particular goal number 12 which concerns sustainable consumption and production. Factors contributing to this gap include price sensitivity, limited availability of sustainable options, and habitual purchasing patterns. For instance, while many consumers express a willingness to support sustainable fashion, the convenience and affordability of fast fashion often override these intentions. A study examining Italian consumers found that while attitudes toward sustainable clothing were generally positive, actual purchasing behaviour was significantly influenced by social norms and perceived behavioural control. This underscores the importance of societal influences and perceived ease or difficulty in performing sustainable behaviour (Kemi & Zilahy, 2025). Post-purchase behaviours, such as repairing, recycling, and purchasing second-hand clothing, are gaining traction among European consumers. Research conducted in Hungary identified distinct consumer segments based on their engagement in sustainable post-purchase activities. The study highlighted that social norms and perceived behavioural control significantly influence these behaviours, suggesting that societal expectations and perceived ease of action play critical roles (Kemi & Zilahy, 2025).

The rise of the second-hand clothing market further exemplifies this trend. A study exploring European consumer motivations behind second-hand clothing purchases found that economic, environmental, and hedonic factors drive engagement in this market. Consumers are increasingly valuing the uniqueness and sustainability associated with second-hand apparel (Halicki & Zaborek & Meylan, 2024).

People in Berlin, Grenoble, Prato, and Catalonia all show some interest in circular textiles, but their habits and attitudes differ depending on age, income, and local culture, among other demographic features. In all four places, price is the biggest factor influencing what people buy. Most consumers value affordable clothing, with price being more important to some demographics such as students and low-income families. Even though many say sustainability is important, it usually comes after cost, quality, and convenience when making choices.

Second-hand shopping is common but still faces challenges. Many people worry about hygiene, finding the right size, or the time it takes to search for good items. In Prato and Catalonia, there is also a strong stigma, especially when it comes to buying used clothes. Even in places like Berlin and Grenoble, second-hand clothes are not always seen as appealing or easy to access.

Repairing clothes is also not very popular. It's often seen as expensive or too much effort, and repair services are not always easy to find. Still, people like the idea of community events such as repair cafes or workshops, which could make fixing clothes more social and accessible.

Young people in Berlin, Prato, and Catalonia tend to be more influenced by external motivators such as social media and peer pressure. They are more likely to buy more and follow trends and are therefore less likely to choose circular options. Older consumers, especially in Grenoble and Catalonia, are more focused on quality and comfort and are more open to buying long-lasting items especially if these are linked to trusted brands or a sense of exclusivity.

Most people know about donating clothes or shopping second-hand, but fewer are aware of other circular options like renting, swapping, or upcycling. To get more people involved in circular fashion, several ideas were shared:

⮚ add second-hand sections to regular shopping malls ⮚ create community spaces for repair and education ⮚ run fun events like circular fashion festivals ⮚ promote campaigns to improve the image of second-hand clothing ⮚ show its value could also help change people's minds and habits.

## Key findings of European consumer surveys

During the acquisition phase, European consumers generally place greater emphasis on price, comfort, and style than on sustainability-related attributes. For instance, in Portugal, consumers reported low participation in clothing rental or second-hand markets, and most acquisitions were guided by personal budget and product quality rather than environmental concerns (Ribeiro et al., 2023). Similar patterns are evident in Poland and other EU countries, where fast fashion has been embraced widely due to its affordability and trendiness (Popowska & Sinkiewicz, 2021; Koszewska, 2019).

Regarding the usage and maintenance of clothing, there is some evidence of willingness to extend garment lifespans, particularly through practices like careful washing or infrequent laundering. However, deeper behavioural commitment, such as active repair or upcycling, remains limited.

In the Czech Republic, gender and education levels influenced sustainable maintenance practices, with women and those with higher education more likely to engage in environmentally responsible behaviours (Nencková et al., 2020).

The disposal phase is particularly crucial within a circular economy model. In the Czech context, disposal behaviours were significantly affected by demographic factors. Older consumers, women, and those with higher income or education were more likely to separate textile waste or donate used clothing. Preferred disposal methods included donations to charities, giving garments to family or friends, or using designated textile collection containers. However, a considerable

proportion of textiles still entered the general municipal waste stream, highlighting a disconnect between sustainable intentions and actual behaviour (Nencková et al., 2020).

Despite increased environmental concern, Polish consumers still show limited engagement with sustainability in fashion. Many express a general interest in environmental issues, yet sustainable development within the garment industry is not a significant driver in their purchasing decisions. Furthermore, Polish consumers tend to view their national performance in sustainable fashion negatively compared to other European nations (Popowska & Sinkiewicz, 2021).

Trust in sustainability-related claims and access to reliable information are key factors influencing consumer behaviour. Many consumers struggle to differentiate genuinely sustainable products from greenwashed alternatives. This lack of transparency, combined with complex or inaccessible information about textile sourcing and lifecycle impacts, contributes to consumer fatigue and inaction (Ribeiro et al., 2023; Popowska & Sinkiewicz, 2021).

## Barriers to sustainable behaviour

One of the most persistent barriers to sustainable fashion consumption is economic constraint. Many consumers perceive eco-friendly products as more expensive, making them less attractive to budget-conscious shoppers. Infrastructure gaps also limit sustainable action, particularly in areas lacking textile recycling points or repair services. Furthermore, mistrust of sustainability claims, due to rampant greenwashing and inconsistent labelling, erodes consumer confidence and reduces engagement (Koszewska, 2019; Popowska & Sinkiewicz, 2021).

## Enablers and opportunities

Several opportunities exist to support sustainable behaviours among European textile consumers. Clearer labelling, such as the forthcoming Digital Product Passport, may help consumers make informed choices. Policy measures, such as eco-design regulations, tax incentives for sustainable consumption, and EPR schemes, could also shift the market. Social marketing campaigns targeted at specific psychological drivers, including perceived efficacy, social norms, and emotional engagement, are necessary to promote lasting behavioural change. Additionally, alternative business models, such as renting, swapping, and resale platforms, provide consumers with more accessible and affordable paths to sustainability (Ribeiro et al., 2023; Koszewska, 2019).

In summary, European consumers are increasingly aware of the environmental and social implications of textile consumption, yet their behaviours often fail to align with these values. The persistent intention–action gap is driven by economic, informational, and systemic barriers. Addressing these requires a coordinated approach involving governments, industry stakeholders, non-governmental organisations, and consumers themselves.
