 A 2025 Report on analysis of consumer research, carried out in the context of the SOLSTICE Horizon Europe project<sup>7</sup>

"Key findings reveal that both for consumer behaviour related to textile consumption as well as for circular textile solutions, price is considered the most important motivator across all territories. Therefore, making circular options affordable must be a key priority.

At the same time, different consumer groups have various perceptions and understanding of

circular solutions, so targeted communication for different population segments is essential.

Certain circular solutions like donation and second-hand shopping are well recognised, while rental

and upcycling need more visibility. Still, for all of them, affordability is the biggest barrier: circular options are often perceived as too expensive compared to fast fashion.

At the same time, availability and accessibility matter: location, infrastructure, and convenience determine engagement with circular solutions".

A 2025 Report on analysis of Consumer circular behaviour understanding and guidelines to support behavioural change via interventions, carried out in the context of the CISUTAC Horizon Europe project<sup>8</sup>

"Qualitative exploration of consumer perspectives: For second-hand acquisition, consumers are motivated by value, uniqueness, and environmental care, but are deterred by poor store presentation and hygiene.

For repair, motivations include emotional attachment and waste reduction, while barriers include a lack of skills, time, and cost.

Finally, for disposal, consumers are motivated by a desire to "do good" and reduce waste, but are often hindered by emotional attachment to garments and a lack of knowledge about proper disposal

Quantitative study on circular behaviour:

Egocentric motives (e.g., cost savings) tend to dominate behaviours related to acquisition and use, while prosocial motives (e.g., environmental concern) are more prominent for disposal behaviours. Most consumers are in the early stages of change for acquisition behaviours but are more advanced for disposal.

Interventions to foster circular behaviours must be carefully designed. A one-size-fits-all approach is not effective, and strategies must be tailored to different elements of the textile system and the respective role of consumers in the textile system.

The results also underscore that consumer segmentation (e.g., by age, motivation, or stage of change) is essential to maximising impact.

Motivation is the strongest driver of behaviour and fostering emotional attachment to garments can be a powerful lever.

Furthermore, findings suggest that repair and donation behaviours can be complementary, with some consumers engaging in both".

On 9 October 2025, Prospex Institute (TRUSTex partner 11) hosted the TRUSTex online workshop "Uncovering Social and Environmental Hotspots: A textile value chain workshop". The purpose was to introduce the project and its initial research findings to key stakeholders in the textile sector. Eight TRUSTEX researchers gave brief presentations and invited 65 participants to provide their feedback on the insights and research directions shared, via an online survey tool.

In the poll, participants were generally pessimistic about the extent to which consumers' purchasing decisions are influenced by environmental consciousness, seeing affordability as the biggest contributing factor. Participants saw policy and governance gaps as a critical barrier to effective EPR, with many preferring full EU EPR harmonisation to partial harmonisation. Improving sorting, recycling and reuse infrastructure and ecomodulation and fee structures were seen as key to improving EPR implementation.

During the session "Threads of Change: Market Dynamics and Consumer Attitudes" participants were asked questions aimed at understanding different perspectives about consumer attitudes. Namely,

- 1. How much do you think consumers' purchasing decisions on textile products are influenced by environmental consciousness?
- 2. What's the biggest barrier for consumers to buying sustainable textile products? and
- 3. Regarding the factors that influence the purchase of sustainable textile products and affect consumers' sustainable decisions which, in your opinion, are the three primary ones? Many participants saw limited budgets and high prices as a key barrier to purchase. Transparency was seen by several as another key barrier, with consumers left unsure as to whether brands or products actually are sustainable and if their claims can be trusted.

## Regarding the factors that influence the purchase of sustainable textile products and affect consumers' sustainable decisions, what are the three primary ones?

Given the possibilities of ENA, the same survey was conducted in Greece. Specifically, the survey on consumers' purchasing decisions resulted 158 responses from across the country with quite similar numerical results, and most importantly showcased that, other factors than environmental consciousness, are influencing more the consumers' purchasing decisions.

Again, in the (open) question "From your perspective, what's the biggest barrier for consumers in buying sustainable/circular textile products?" showcased as barriers, mostly their price and the perception about their quality.

In the next question the respondents in Greece ranked affordability as the first factor and environmental awareness and eco-consciousness as the second factor influencing the purchasing of sustainable textile products. This finding is differed from the result on a European level.

On the 15th of December 2025, the JRC published the 3rd milestone of the Preparatory Study on Textiles<sup>12</sup>, where in its summary is reported that "The main criteria applied by people when buying textile apparel are price and the perceived quality. Sustainability principles rank lower in importance. Approximately 33% of 27,498 EU survey respondents express agreement with the notion that they are not concerned about the environmentally-friendliness of their apparel, while 64% disagree or tend to disagree. 11,483 consumers from 10 European countries prioritize price (68%), perceived quality (61%) and fit when they buy new clothes (56%). Other aspects considered while to a lesser extent include product longevity (30%), the type of fibre (organic, synthetic, upcycled) (24%) and environmental impact of the apparel item (15%)".

![](_page_114_Picture_6.jpeg)

The next more detailed paragraphs come from the main working document.

Regarding the last finding mentioned above that "Those incorporating second-hand apparel tend to purchase fewer new items", it is worth noting that a paper of October 2025<sup>13</sup> in Nature, the "Secondhand fashion consumers exhibit fast fashion behaviors despite sustainability narratives", has raised a different finding (necessary to tell, coming beyond the Atlantic).

This study addresses that gap using a nationally representative survey of 1,009 U.S. consumers. We find that secondhand consumption is positively correlated with new clothing purchases, particularly among younger consumers and frequent shoppers. Cluster and principal component analyses reveal that highly engaged secondhand consumers also exhibit high overall consumption and short garment retention. "…The relationship between primary and secondary fashion markets reveals a complex dynamic that challenges conventional assumptions about sustainable consumption. While secondary markets are often celebrated for extending garment lifespans and reducing waste, our findings highlight a rebound effect that warrants a critical re-evaluation of the belief that secondhand consumption inherently mitigates the environmental impact of fashion. Instead of displacing consumption in the primary market, the growth of secondary markets appears to complement it. This suggests that both markets collectively sustain high levels of overall consumption, undermining the environmental benefits typically attributed to secondhand practices".

## 3.2.6 Reuse practices in the value chain

The reuse of textiles has gained increasing attention in the EU as part of broader efforts to transition toward a circular economy and reduce the environmental footprint of the fashion and textile industries. With the European Commission's Circular Economy Action Plan and the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles (European Commission, 2022), textile reuse is prioritized over recycling and incineration. The Waste Framework Directive (WFD) mandates that from 2025, EU Member States must establish separate collection systems for used textiles. Promoting an enhanced diversion of textile waste from landfills demands optimized reuse and recycling technologies. As it was mentioned before, reuse is a more environmentally sustainable option than recycling. Moreover, it offers socioeconomic benefits; for example, establishing small businesses centred around upcycling, repurposing, or selling second-hand textiles, thereby creating employment opportunities. Additionally, it provides access to clothing for disadvantaged individuals (EEA, 2024).

Available literature today provides ample suggestions on how to improve collection of textiles, albeit with the reservation that data is not comparable and success depends highly on local circumstances. Sometimes the conclusions of different authors are even contradictory. Just because an item is put on sale, it does not mean that it is actually resold, as the sector struggles with steep competition from cheap and trendy fast fashion clothing.

Additionally, the textiles collection, sorting for reuse, and recycling sector in Europe is facing an unprecedented crisis with rising sorting costs, dropping sales of second-hand clothes, and a lack of demand for recycled textile content. The rise of the ultra-fast fashion sold online has further increased those challenges, with a serious impact on the environment (EURIC, 2025).

In 2021, a total of 451,268 tonnes of textiles were reused in the EU27 (excluding Bulgaria and Cyprus and including Norway). This corresponds to an average of 2.3 kilograms of textiles per person. In the same data year, the average consumption of new textiles was 15.9 kilograms per person in the EU27 (ETC CE, 2025) which means approximately 12.6% of the total consumption of textiles consists of reused textiles.

![](_page_118_Figure_0.jpeg)

Figure 28. Reuse of textiles in EU 2021. Source ETC, 2025 & EEA, 2024/2.

## 3.2.6.1 Collection systems

Textile reuse starts with well-organised collection systems, including municipal collection points, donation bins managed by non-profit organisations, and in-store take-back schemes offered by retailers. In Europe, most textile waste is currently gathered via street containers (bring points). These are often supplemented by civic amenity sites, which allow residents to dispose of household waste and recyclables not covered by regular curbside collection. Each collection method presents its own benefits and limitations. Indoor collection, for instance, is particularly effective for acquiring clean, high-quality textiles suitable for reuse (Wagner, 2022; van Duijn et al., 2022). Nonetheless, its effectiveness in collecting large volumes is often constrained by limited opening hours and a relatively small number of accessible sites (Mörsen, 2023). Bring points are effective for collecting large volumes of textiles but carry a higher risk of contamination compared to indoor collection (van Duijn et al., 2022). Exposure to moisture, such as rain or damp clothing, can lead to mould growth, rendering the textiles unusable. Given the slim profit margins in the sector, additional processing like cleaning, washing, or drying is often not economically viable. Door-to-door collection, while convenient, is more costly and comes with risks such as theft (van Duijn et al., 2022). Overall, bring points are generally regarded as the most efficient method for collecting substantial amounts of used textiles of reasonably good quality (Wagner, 2022). However, several other factors, such as the placement of collection sites, frequency of collection, type and condition of containers, proper labelling, and clear communication, play a crucial role in enhancing both the volume and quality of collected textiles.

## 3.2.6.2 Second-hand retail and digital resale

The second-hand market in the EU is expanding rapidly, both through physical second-hand shops and digital resale platforms. Physical stores such as those operated by charities (e.g., Oxfam) play a significant role in promoting reuse. In parallel, online resale platforms such as Vinted, Vestiaire Collective, and Depop allow consumers to buy and sell used clothing peer-to-peer (Thomson et al., 2020). These platforms have become particularly popular among younger, sustainabilityconscious consumers. Reuse actors have developed many creative ways to better market their second-hand products, like outlet stores, swapping and upcycling initiatives, and e-commerce platforms. To boost local reuse, some collection and sorting actors have teamed up with private companies to sell their sorted second-hand clothes in their stores (Mörsen, 2023). Zeeman and the social enterprise Het Goed, for example, cooperate on that level in the Netherlands (Mörsen, 2023).

An illustrative case is MUD Jeans, a Dutch denim brand recognized with numerous industry awards for its innovative use of recycled materials and commitment to a fully circular model. It was among the first consumer brands to implement a leasing system for jeans, retrieve them after use, and ensure their recycling at specialized facilities. MUD Jeans also led the way in adopting design features that facilitate recycling, such as replacing traditional leather patches with eco-friendly printed alternatives. Similarly, the Swedish outdoor brand Houdini offers another strong example. In its Fall/Winter 2023 collection, the company used only materials that were recycled, recyclable, renewable, biodegradable, or certified by Bluesign. Among these is PrimaLoft Bio, a highperformance fabric that is both 100% recycled and biodegradable. Houdini further supports sustainability by providing rental, repair, and second-hand sales services to its customers.

## 3.2.6.3 Charitable and social economy models

Charity organisations are key actors in the reuse ecosystem, collecting textiles for redistribution or resale to support social programs. Many of these operate under social enterprise models, combining environmental and social objectives. For example, Belgian "De Kringwinkel" and French "Emmaüs" engage in textile reuse while providing employment for marginalized populations (EEA, 2025).

Charity organisations have historically been the main actors involved in the collection of used textiles, and they still are. They collect used textiles via bring banks (containers), or over the counter in the charity organisations shops. Containers can be located in civic amenity sites (also named recycling stations in some countries) or on the streets. In some cases, e.g. in The Netherlands, charities are asked to pay a fee to the municipalities for the textiles they collect via containers in public space. Following collection via bring banks, textiles are then collected and transported to central sorting or bagging locations for either local second hand or exported to sorting companies. Textiles are sorted into different qualities which are usually directed to different markets. This kind of collection gives an average quality of collected textiles, with less quality than in-store collection, but likely more efficiency in terms of quantity of textiles collected. Charities' collection can also happen over the counter at second-hand shops, which makes the quality of textiles for re-use higher than collection via containers, since the collection is carefully monitored by the shop's personnel. In this case, used clothes are sold directly in the charity shop, without the need for transport to a sorting facility. Kerbside collection by charities is very rare, although some charities offer on-demand pick-up services, usually in return for a fee.

The main aim of charitable organisations is normally to sell used textiles for direct re-use, without any type of repair or mending. Therefore, charitable collectors in most countries tend to make it

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

clear on their bring banks that they only wish to receive clean and reusable textiles (and footwear), often due to the negative impact that collecting non-reusable textiles have on their economies (Köhler et al., 2021). Some organisations then carry out sorting into different streams completely "in-house"; others sort a part of the collected textiles internally (usually differentiating between textile for domestic re-use vs textiles for treatment in other EU or non-EU countries), and sell another part unsorted (Palm et al., 2014). However, the collection system by charity organisations usually does not address textile material for recycling outside the portion of collected textiles that is not suitable for re-use and that is sold as unsorted stream to other countries/actors.

Charity organisations entities are by far the main actors for the collection of used textiles in many EU countries, especially the Nordic countries (Denmark, Norway, Sweden, Finland and Iceland), but also globally. In fact, charity organisations have often preferential treatment in that they are the only ones allowed by the municipalities to set up collection containers.

However, these organisations have been joined by a number of other actors, such as second-hand shops, social economy entities and on-line platforms for the sale and exchange of clothes. For example, in the Netherlands the collection share of used textiles managed by charities has dropped from nearly 100% in 2000 to 55% in 2013. In some countries like the Netherlands, charitable organisations create dedicated private companies to carry out the textile collection, sorting and sales operations with the revenues returned to the charitable part of the organisation (Watson et al., 2020a). In the Baltic countries, separate collection is relatively evenly split between charitable organisations, commercial/private collectors and municipalities/contracted waste companies (Watson et al., 2020b). In Italy, where collection of textiles via bring banks is considered as waste, there is a complex interaction of companies and cooperatives active in the sector of separate collection of textile waste, managed at national level by a large association (Unicircular, 2018). The sole country where charity organisations are less involved is France, where household textile waste is managed through the EPR scheme, and the collection is managed by an accredited Producer Responsibility Organisation (PRO). However, charities can register as official collectors under the EPR scheme. Some countries collect both reusable and non-reusable textiles, building thus a centralised system for all textiles. Large charity organisations active in the collection of used textiles are Oxfam and Caritas, and are characterised by having at least a share of the workers not receiving a full salary when supporting the organization (Huygens et al., 2023).

Social enterprises involved in the collection of textiles partly differ from charities in that their employees are not volunteers. They normally have a social or environmental goal and can be forprofit or non-profit. Social enterprises can organise textile collection via kerbside, drop-off or instore systems. In the EU there are many examples of social entities that collect used textiles or textile waste. In the Netherlands, Flanders and Austria, socio-economic re-use organisations under the HERWIN and RepaNet networks specialise in receiving used goods including textiles across the counter for processing and repair in the shop. These national networks are all part of the European RRE-USE network, an international non-profit network of social enterprises. In 2021, RRE-USE had 31 members across 29 countries. As of 2021, 341,000 tonnes of textiles were collected by RRE-USE members, of which 39,000 were re-used locally (RRE-USE, 2022). Other social enterprises include the Red Cross in the Nordic Countries, Humana in several countries such as Lithuania, Spain, Italy and Sweden, and Komosie in Belgium (Huygens et al., 2023).

## 3.2.6.4 Retailer take-back and reuse schemes

Commercial fashion retailers have increasingly adopted take-back schemes, often incentivised through vouchers or discounts. A take-back program is a brand's initiative to manage the end-oflife of its products and, sometimes, its packaging. Customers send products through the program, often receiving points or discounts from the brand. The brand gets these products and processes them internally or sends them to a partner. Some brands offer dedicated second-hand collections. Many European brands have implemented take-back programs on some scale. Below a number of successful take-back programs is being presented:

## (i) H&M Group (Sweden)

➢ Scheme: Garment Collecting Program (since 2013) ➢ Details: Customers drop off any brand of old textiles in stores. Items are sorted into three categories: re-wear (second-hand), reuse (e.g., cleaning cloths), or recycle (fibres) ➢ Incentive: Voucher/discount on next purchase ➢ Partnerships: I:CO, Loop Industries.

## (ii) ZARA / Inditex (Spain)

➢ Scheme: Clothing Collection Program ➢ Details: Available in stores in over 20 countries. Collected items are donated or recycled. Inditex collaborates with NGOs and recycling firms ➢ Reuse focus: Second-hand resale under trial in select markets (e.g., UK 2022) ➢ Ambition: All garments from sustainable sources by 2025.

## (iii) Decathlon (France)

➢ Scheme: Second Life & Buy Back Programs ➢ Details: Customers return used sports gear and apparel for repair and resale. Pilot resale platforms launched in multiple EU countries ➢ Incentive: Store credit for returned items ➢ Circular ambition: Reduce material waste and encourage longevity in product use.

## (iv) C&A (Netherlands, Germany)

➢ Scheme: We Take It Back ➢ Details: Partnered with I:CO for in-store collection of used clothes. Focus on recycling and reusing textiles ➢ New initiative: Trialling circular collections made from collected post-consumer fibres.

## (v) BESTSELLER (Vero Moda, Jack & Jones, etc.) (Denmark)

➢ Scheme: Take-back trials & Circular Design Guidelines ➢ Details: Pilot take-back schemes and focus on designing for circularity ➢ Collaborates with Fashion for Good and Circular Fashion.

## (vi) MUD Jeans (Netherlands)

➢ Scheme: Lease a Jeans / Return for Recycling ➢ Details: Circular business model. Customers lease jeans and return them for recycling into new denim ➢ Key point: 40% of each new jean is made from old jeans

➢ Innovative: 100% cotton and mono-material approach for easy recycling.

### (vii) Marks & Spencer – Shopping (Great Britain)

➢ Scheme: Shopping Partnership with Oxfam ➢ Details: Donations of unwanted clothes (any brand) in M&S stores. Items resold, reused, or recycled by Oxfam ➢ Impact: Millions of garments reused or recycled ➢ Added value: Strong social benefit (Oxfam fundraising).

### (viii) Benetton (Italy)

➢ Scheme: Limited reuse/recycling pilots ➢ Details: Starting circular collections and engaging in material innovation. No extensive pan-European take-back yet, but direction is shifting.

## (ix) Swedish Stockings (Sweden)

While Swedish Stockings is aiming to "close the loop" and turn old stockings into new ones, the technology isn't there yet on a commercial level. Until then, they are collecting thousands of pairs of tights, grinding them up, and turning them into stylish moulded fibreglass tables.

## (x) Underprotection (Denmark)

The Danish brand combines ethics and aesthetics to create underwear, loungewear, and swimwear from lower-impact materials like organic cotton. Through its Take-back Program, when you drop off or send in your pieces to it, you'll receive a 30% off reward on supporting circularity.

## (xi) O My Bag (Netherlands)

Consumers who have an old O My Bag can bring it back to the brand in exchange for a 20% discount or to swap with another vintage item.

## 3.2.6.5 Export of used textiles

Between 2005 and 2023, the export of used textiles from EU countries more than doubled to around 1.37 million tonnes in 2023 or 3.1 kg per person, and EEA publications have shown that it nearly tripled since 2000. The volume has been relatively stable since 2015, with Africa and Asia as the main receiving continents. In 2020, there was a drop in exports due to the Covid-19 pandemic EU exports of used textiles (Lingås et al., 2023).

The fate of used textiles exported from the EU is highly uncertain. The perception of used clothing donations as generous gifts to people in need does not fully match reality. Used clothing is increasingly part of a specialised and traded global commodity value chain (Lingås et al., 2023).

The exports mainly include worn clothes and other worn textile articles and only a minor number of rags and textile scraps. There is currently very limited information on the quality of used textiles due to the absence of specific reporting requirements for textiles that are not considered as waste. Between 2005 and 2023 the main recipients of exported used textiles were Africa and Asia, with Asia steadily catching up with Africa to equal levels of 44,6% and 43,2% respectively in 2023. A profound shift towards circular textile consumption in the EU, such as lower consumption, longer use, increased reuse and increased textile-to-textile recycling in the EU would likely decrease the volume of used textiles exported (Lingås et al., 2023).

## 3.2.7 Main fabrics, garments and fibres in the EU and the reliance on third countries

According to EURATEX 42% of the total production of fabrics in the EU concern clothes and fashion products such as underwear, shirts, suits, dresses and coats, 14% concern furnishing and home related products, such as curtains, upholstery, carpets and bed linen and 19% concern industrial/technical related fabrics such as ropes and nettings, parachutes, medical textiles, synthetic grass, sunblinds and smart textiles. It is worth mentioning that the latest category demonstrates steady growth the last five years.

## Cotton

The European Union is a key player in the cotton sector and is among the world's top 10 exporters. Approximately 250-350 thousand tonnes of cotton are produced in Europe, representing 2% of world production, which is grown on around 200,000 hectares in Greece and more than 60,000 hectares in Spain.

Table 6. Cotton Production in 1000 tons

|        | 2021   | 2022   |
|--------|--------|--------|
| EU 27  | 353,84 | 341,78 |
| Greece | 294,07 | 299,78 |
| Spain  | 58,31  | 40,91  |

Source: Eurostat

Europe ranks among the largest consumers of cotton globally, following the Asia-Pacific and North American regions. Despite its significant consumption levels, domestic cotton production within Europe remains relatively limited. Greece stands out as the leading cotton producer and exporter in the European Union, with over 45,000 active cotton farmers and approximately 200,000 hectares under cotton cultivation (EUROSTAT).

Cotton maintains high popularity in the European market due to its favourable properties-namely durability, softness, breathability, biodegradability, and ease of dyeing and blending with other fibres. These characteristics make it particularly suitable for the apparel and home textiles sectors. Consequently, Europe serves as a substantial market for cotton-based products, with major consumer markets located in Italy, Germany, Portugal, Spain, France, and Romania (Research and Markets, 2024).

EU production levels are insufficient to meet internal demand. As a result, Europe relies heavily on cotton imports to bridge the gap. According to the ITC Trade Map, Italy was the largest European importer of cotton in 2021, with import values reaching approximately USD 1.13 billion, followed by Germany (USD 805.6 million). Other significant importers include Portugal, and Spain. The increasing demand for cotton-based home textiles, particularly bed linen, has contributed to this trend.

## Wool

Europe produces over 200,000 tonnes of coarse wool annually, primarily as a by-product of livestock farming. The landscape of raw wool production is marked by regional contrasts, with Romania emerging as the clear leader, followed by Norway and the United Kingdom. Eastern European nations such as Romania and Lithuania are witnessing notable growth, reporting

increases of 4.78% and 6.44% respectively. In contrast, countries like Spain, Italy, and the UK are facing declines, with Spain registering a significant drop of 18.54%. Substantial reductions are also seen in Portugal, Ireland, and Germany. France is producing 13.46 million kg of raw wool annually. These trends suggest a potential continued rise in Eastern European production, while traditional producers in Western Europe may face further declines. (Source: ReportLinker, 2023).

European Union imports of wool, animal hair, horsehair yarn and fabric was US\$1.49 billion during 2024, according to the United Nations COMTRADE database on international trade.

Looking ahead, the future of Europe's raw wool sector will likely be shaped by factors such as sustainable production practices, technological innovation, and evolving fashion trends. Moreover, climate change and shifts in trade regulations are expected to significantly influence production patterns across the region. Keeping track of these developments will be essential for anticipating market changes and identifying new opportunities. The EU's Product Environmental Footprint (PEF) methodology has raised concerns within the wool industry. Critics argue that it may undervalue natural fibres like wool compared to synthetics, potentially impacting consumer perceptions and demand.

## Synthetic fibres

Polyester is the most widely used fibre worldwide. With an annual production volume of around 71 million tonnes, up from around 63 million tonnes in 2022, polyester had a market share of approximately 57% of global fibre production in 2023 (Textile Exchange, 2024).

![](_page_126_Figure_3.jpeg)

Figure 30. World Production of Cotton, Wool & Man-Made Fibres. Source: https://www.cirfs.org/statistics/keystatistics

In 2022, 58% of fibres and yarns used by the EU industry were of synthetic origin. A total of 3.3 million tonnes of fibres and yarns were produced. In the same year, almost 2.3 million tonnes were imported, and 0.82 million tonnes exported. This implies that the total 2022 use of fibres and yarns by the EU industry was around 4.8 million tonnes, a decrease of 10% compared to 2010 with significant fluctuations in volumes consumed over this time period. These volumes have remained relatively constant, although synthetic fibres have also seen a 10% decrease compared to 2010, along with a drop in the volumes consumed by industry during the COVID-19 years. The outputs of this industrial use are intermediate and finished products, which are meant for internal EU use as well as for export (EEA,2024). These numbers support the hypothesis that a large share of our textile products is of synthetic, and therefore non-renewable, origin, and the reported share of synthetics is in the same order of magnitude as what Textile Exchange reports on a global scale in their Materials Market Report (Textile Exchange, 2024).

![](_page_127_Figure_3.jpeg)

Figure 31. Share of synthetic fibres and yarns in EU industrial textiles use. Source: Textile Exchange, 2024

## Manmade cellulosic fibres

Manmade cellulosic fibres (MMCFs) are regenerated fibres usually made from the dissolved wood pulp or "cellulose" of trees. Viscose, lyocell and modal are all kinds of manmade cellulosics. Because they are plant-based, MMCFs are renewable, and so have the potential to be a climatefriendly material if the wood is sourced sustainably and the processing chemicals are handled properly. With an annual production volume of around 7.9 million tonnes in 2023, MMCFs, including viscose (rayon), acetate, lyocell, modal and cupro, had a market share of around 6% of global fibre production. Global MMCF production has more than doubled since 1990, when annual production was around 3 million tonnes, and is expected to continue growing in the coming years (Textile Exchange, 2024).

## EU reliance from third countries

Since the early 2000s, both the value and volume of textile imports to the EU have shown a marked increase. Between 2002 and 2022, the value of textile imports rose by approximately 82%, from 124 billion to 226 billion euros. Similarly, the volume of imports increased by 78%, from 9.8 million tonnes in 2000 to 17.4 million tonnes in 2022. A significant share of these imports originates from Asian countries, underscoring the region's central role in the global textile supply chain.

The structure of EU import tariffs plays a crucial role in shaping trade flows and the textile value chain. These tariffs vary by country of origin. For instance, textiles imported from Turkey and Bangladesh benefit from zero import duties, whereas imports from China are subject to variable tariffs depending on the product type, ranging from 5% to 12% (Copenhagen Economics, 2025).

In the light of growing concerns about environmental sustainability and supply chain transparency, there is increasing institutional advocacy within the EU for reshoring or nearshoring textile

manufacturing i.e., relocating production to Europe or nearby regions. The principal objectives behind this strategic shift include enhancing traceability across the value chain and facilitating the enforcement of sustainable production standards.

However, while reshoring presents potential benefits, its effectiveness and economic viability in the textile sector remain contested. Several structural and economic limitations may undermine its feasibility (Copenhagen Economics, 2025):

- Capacity constraints: Europe currently lacks the manufacturing capacity to match that of Asia and North Africa, limiting its ability to absorb large-scale production.
- Cost differentials: Production costs in Europe are substantially higher than those in established textile-exporting regions, which would likely lead to increased consumer prices without corresponding improvements in product quality or sustainability.
- Labour intensity and shortages: Given the labour-intensive nature of textile manufacturing and the persistent labour shortages across many European countries (a trend projected to intensify) reshoring may prove impractical in the long term.
- Specialisation patterns: European firms predominantly operate in high value-added segments of the textile value chain. Redirecting resources toward low value-added activities may lead to economic inefficiencies and a misallocation of skills and capital.
- Global equity considerations: Relocating textile production to Europe could negatively impact lower-middle income countries that rely on textile exports for economic development. For example, approximately 60% of Bangladesh's textile exports are directed to the EU market. A reshoring strategy could therefore have adverse socio-economic consequences for such countries, potentially undermining broader EU commitments to inclusive global trade and development. Moreover, it may be perceived as a protectionist measure, conflicting with the EU's stated objective of reducing trade barriers.

Figure 32. Illustration of the competitive situation for the sector. Source: Copenhagen Economics, 2025.

## Specifically:

## Zara

❖ Country: Spain

![](_page_133_Picture_10.jpeg)

❖ ❖ Segment: Fast fashion ❖ Notable facts: Flagship brand of Inditext (Industria de Diseño Textil, S.A); global reach; known for speed-to-market.

Zara, as part of the Inditex Group, has developed a comprehensive sustainability strategy centered on climate action, circular economy practices, and sustainable sourcing. The company aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 50% across its entire value chain by 2030 and achieve netzero emissions by 2040. By 2030, Inditex plans for 90% of Zara's textiles to originate from more sustainable sources, 40% from conventional recycling, 25% from next-generation materials, and 25% from organic or regenerative farming. Supporting this transition, Zara has invested in startups such as Galy and Circ, which focus on lab-grown cotton and textile recycling technologies, respectively (Reuters, 2024; Climate Action, 2023).

Zara has introduced the Pre-Owned platform to foster circularity by enabling customers to resell, repair, or donate clothing. Complementary recycling initiatives in stores are managed in partnership

with non-profit organizations (Inditex, 2024). In 2023, the company eliminated single-use plastics, and in 2024, halved the consumption of new paper bags, redirecting proceeds toward environmental programs in collaboration with WWF and Water.org (Inditex, 2024). Further sustainability efforts include restoring five million hectares of land by 2030 and supporting over 300 startups through its Sustainability Innovation Hub (Climate Action, 2023; Cinco Días, 2025). Although Zara no longer publicly promotes its Join Life label, it continues to implement sustainability standards internally. It also participates in initiatives such as the Better Cotton Initiative and the Zero Discharge of Hazardous Chemicals (ZDHC) program (Reuters, 2024; Weavabel, 2024).

### H&M

❖ Country: Sweden

![](_page_134_Picture_5.jpeg)

❖ ❖ Segment: Fast fashion ❖ Notable facts: Among the largest clothing retailers worldwide

H&M, a major global fashion retailer, has developed an extensive sustainability strategy targeting climate neutrality, circularity, and ethical supply chain management. The company has committed to achieving net-zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2040, with an interim goal of reducing absolute emissions by 56% by 2030. As of 2024, 89% of the materials used by H&M were either recycled or sustainably sourced, and 29.5% were recycled materials. H&M aims to reach 100% recycled or sustainably sourced materials by 2030 and 30% recycled content by 2025 (H&M Group, 2024; Impakter, 2024). To advance sustainable innovation, H&M supports projects through its Global Change Awards, including materials made from grape leather and manure-based textiles (Vogue, 2022). The company also promotes consumer circularity through garment collection bins in stores and a men's suit rental programme (Reuters, 2024).

To validate its environmental and social claims, H&M uses certified 100% organic cotton and has received Cradle to Cradle Certified® Gold for its newborn collection, ensuring high standards in material health, circularity, and social fairness (About H&M, 2022). The company is also a member of the Better Cotton Initiative and has pledged to eliminate virgin down and feathers by the end of 2025, achieving 90% recycled use as of 2024 (Reuters, 2024). H&M's 2021 Transparency Index score of 68% placed it second among global fashion brands, underlining its reporting efforts (Impakter, 2024).

## Adidas

❖ Country: Germany

![](_page_134_Picture_12.jpeg)

❖ ❖ Segment: sportswear ❖ Notable facts: Second-largest sportswear brand globally (after Nike)

renewable energy integration, and the adoption of sustainable materials across its value chain. The company is committed to achieving net-zero emissions by 2050, with interim targets of reducing Scope 1 and 2 emissions by 70% and Scope 3 emissions by 42% by 2030, using 2022 as the baseline year. By 2024, Adidas had already achieved a 17% reduction in Scope 1 and 2 emissions and a 24% overall reduction in GHG emissions, including supply chain-related emissions (Adidas Group, 2024a).

To ensure the credibility of its environmental and social initiatives, Adidas has pursued multiple third-party certifications. As of 2023, the company had 70 sites certified under ISO 14001 for environmental management, 140 sites under ISO 45001 for occupational health and safety, and 324 sites under ISO 50001 for energy management (Adidas Group, 2024b). Additionally, its retail locations have earned Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design (LEED) certifications, including a Platinum certification for its Cape Town store (Adidas Group, 2024b). Adidas is also a signatory of the Science Based Targets initiative (SBTi), the Fashion Industry Charter for Climate Action, and the Fashion Pact, aligning its corporate strategy with international climate goals (Impakter, 2024).

## Louis Vuitton

❖ Country: France

![](_page_135_Picture_7.jpeg)

❖ ❖ Segment: Luxury fashion

❖ Notable facts: World's most valuable luxury brand (part of LVMH group)

Louis Vuitton, a flagship brand of the LVMH Group, follows a comprehensive sustainability strategy through its LIFE 360 (LVMH Initiatives for the Environment) program, which emphasizes circular creativity, biodiversity protection, climate action, and supply chain transparency. The brand has committed to reducing its greenhouse gas emissions by 55% by 2030 compared to 2018 levels and aims to achieve net-zero emissions across by 2050. In 2023, Louis Vuitton reported total greenhouse gas emissions of approximately 1.37 billion kg CO₂e, including 375.74 million kg CO₂e from Scope 1, 329.42 million kg CO₂e from Scope 2, and 1.14 billion kg CO₂e from Scope 3 (DitchCarbon, 2023). Additionally, the company is working toward sourcing 100% renewable electricity by 2026, a significant increase from 36% in 2019 (DitchCarbon, 2023).

To support its environmental and ethical objectives, Louis Vuitton has obtained several certifications. All its jewellery products have been certified by the Responsible Jewellery Council (RJC) since 2012, affirming the brand's adherence to rigorous standards in labour rights, environmental practices, and supply chain ethics (Louis Vuitton, n.d.). Furthermore, Louis Vuitton uses Forest Stewardship Council (FSC)-certified materials in its packaging and sources cotton through the Better Cotton Initiative (BCI), reflecting its commitment to sustainable sourcing and responsible production (Luxiders, 2020).

## Gucci

❖ Country: Italy

![](_page_136_Picture_0.jpeg)

❖ ❖ Segment: Luxury fashion ❖ Notable facts: High-end brand under the Kering Group

Gucci, operating under its sustainability platform Gucci Equilibrium, has embraced a strategic framework called the "Culture of Purpose," introduced in 2015 with targets set for 2025. This strategy focuses on environmental stewardship, social inclusivity, and innovation-driven transformation. Gucci has aligned its climate goals with the 1.5°C target of the Paris Agreement by committing to a 40% absolute reduction in greenhouse gas emissions across Scopes 1, 2, and 3 by 2035. The brand also employs an Environmental Profit & Loss (EP&L) accounting methodology to assess and manage environmental impacts across its entire supply chain (Gucci Equilibrium, 2023a).

To reinforce the credibility of its sustainability actions, Gucci has obtained multiple third-party certifications. In 2019, it was awarded ISO 20121 certification for the sustainable management of its fashion shows and ISO 45001 certification for occupational health and safety at its main Italian offices, production facilities, and stores (Gucci Equilibrium, 2023b).

## Prada

❖ Country: Italy

![](_page_136_Picture_6.jpeg)

❖ ❖ Segment: Luxury fashion ❖ Notable facts: Iconic Italian brand with a global influence

Prada Group's sustainability strategy, titled "IMPACT," is built upon three strategic pillars: Planet, People, and Culture. The company has committed to achieving net-zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050, with an interim target of reducing emissions by 29% by 2026, using 2019 as the baseline year (Prada Group, 2024a). The company is also increasing its reliance on renewable energy and has become a founding member of the Re.Crea Consortium, which focuses on recycling and the sustainable management of post-consumer textile waste (Prada Group, 2024a).

## C&A

❖ Country: Netherlands

![](_page_136_Picture_11.jpeg)

❖ Segment: Mass-market fashion ❖ Notable facts: Large European retailer with sustainability focus

C&A's 2028 Sustainability Strategy, titled "Renew & Restore, Innovate & Lead, Unite & Inspire," reflects the company's commitment to circular fashion, climate action, and responsible sourcing.

By 2022, C&A had reduced its greenhouse gas emissions by nearly 32% compared to its 2018 baseline and sourced 78% of its core materials—cotton, polyester, and man-made cellulosic fibres—more sustainably (C&A, 2023a). The company aims to achieve 100% sustainable sourcing of these materials by 2028. Supporting its circular economy goals, C&A launched jeans made from 100% recycled cotton and expanded second-hand resale pilot programs in Germany (C&A, 2023a). In terms of labour practices, C&A significantly improved supply chain transparency by increasing the number of its factories with verified Social & Labour Convergence Program (SLCP) assessments from 46% in 2021 to 87% in 2022 (C&A, 2023a).

C&A has also been a frontrunner in sustainable product certifications. It was the first global retailer to launch Cradle to Cradle Certified® Gold Level T-shirts in 2017 and followed with Gold Level jeans in 2018. As of 2023, the company had introduced over 4 million Cradle to Cradle Certified® items, including denim and babywear, and achieved the first-ever Platinum Level certification for denim fabric (C&A, 2023a). C&A is also one of the world's largest buyers of certified organic cotton, sourcing materials certified under both the Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS) and the Organic Content Standard (OCS) (C&A, n.d.-a). Moreover, the company supports sustainable agriculture through its participation in the Better Cotton Initiative (C&A, n.d.-b).

As part of this strategy, Mango aims to ensure that by 2030, 100% of the fibres used in its garments are either more sustainable or recycled. The company has set interim targets for 2025, including sourcing 100% sustainable cotton, 100% recycled polyester, and 100% traceable, controlled-origin cellulose fibres (Mango Fashion Group, 2023). Furthermore, it uses the Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Protocol and follows the Science-Based Targets initiative (SBTi) to guide its emissions reduction pathway, with a long-term goal of reaching netzero emissions by 2050 (Just Style, 2022). ## 3.3 Textile reuse practices countries outside the EU

## 3.3.1 Global overview of second-hand clothing reuse and traceability challenges

The increased consumption of low-cost garments produced by the fast fashion industry has contributed to the generation of vast quantities of post-consumer textile waste. While reuse is prioritized over recycling and disposal in the EU waste hierarchy (European Parliament and Council, 2008), the large-scale export of second-hand garments from developed countries to the third-world countries has raised significant concerns. Key issues include the environmental fate of garments that are not reused, the socio-economic dynamics of SHC markets in recipient countries, and the lack of traceability mechanisms to ensure responsible trade practices (UNECE & ECLAC, 2023).

The reuse of textiles, particularly SHC, constitutes a major component of textile waste management in many developing countries, with Africa serving as a key case study. SHC imports have become a cornerstone of clothing consumption in numerous African nations, where over 80% of the population relies on these garments due to their affordability, durability, and fashion relevance (Sumo, 2024). These garments are predominantly sourced from developed countries such as the United States, China, the United Kingdom, and Germany, and are often donated or exported under charitable schemes before entering local resale markets (Sumo, 2024; IVL Swedish Environmental Research Institute, 2025).

From a socio-economic perspective, the SHC trade contributes substantially to local livelihoods. In Kenya, for example, the trade supports employment not only in direct retail activities but also in associated operations such as sorting, repair, and upcycling. This sector is particularly important for marginalized groups and communities with limited access to formal employment opportunities. It facilitates income generation, skills development, and the accessibility of affordable clothing, especially for low-income households (IVL, 2025).

While the reuse of textiles offers clear benefits, significant environmental challenges arise from the current implementation of SHC systems. One critical issue is the low quality of a substantial share of the imported garments, exacerbated by the proliferation of fast fashion. Many garments, upon arrival in African markets, are deemed unsellable due to damage, obsolescence, or material composition. These low-grade bales often end up in open dumpsites, rivers, marketplaces, or are incinerated without proper controls, contributing to pollution and disrupting local waste systems (Sumo, 2024; IVL, 2025).

Life Cycle Assessments (LCAs) show that textile reuse is among the most environmentally beneficial waste management strategies, regardless of whether garments are reused in Europe or abroad. Reuse outperforms recycling and incineration in terms of climate impact, energy savings, and reduced eutrophication. However, these benefits depend heavily on two key factors: the replacement rate (how many new garments are avoided) and the loss rate (how many reused items end up as waste) (IVL, 2025). Similarly, the replacement rate, that is, the degree to which reused garments displace the purchase of new ones, is a critical variable. Lower replacement rates lessen the environmental justification for reuse practices, especially when set against the potential of high-value textile-to-textile recycling.

Despite the increasing policy focus on circular economy principles in Europe, the traceability of exported garments remains limited. There is currently a lack of systemic monitoring mechanisms for the post-export phase of SHC, leading to significant data gaps in understanding the actual use, disposal, and environmental fate of these garments in the third-world countries (IVL, 2025). The absence of standardized quality control measures and certification systems across the SHC supply chain further exacerbates this lack of transparency. Poorly sorted or contaminated garments not only pose environmental hazards but also erode the trust and profitability of the SHC trade in recipient markets (Sumo, 2024).

Despite the socio-economic benefits, the imports of large volumes of SHC may undermine local textile industries by saturating the market with inexpensive goods<sup>14</sup>. Countries such as Rwanda and Kenya have debated restrictions or bans on imports for this reason, although such measures often lead to unintended consequences, including increased informal trade and a shift to low-cost new imports from other countries (UNEP, 2023). In Kenya, the SHC market, locally known as mitumba, plays a crucial economic and social role. It provides employment opportunities, affordable clothing options, and skill development in sorting, repairing, and upcycling. The sector is particularly vital for marginalized communities and informal workers. However, it also presents risks, including financial instability, informality in employment arrangements, and exposure to low-quality imports, particularly from China, that reduce profitability and consumer satisfaction (Nellström et al., 2025). Similarly, Chile has become a major importer of second-hand textiles, with the Iquique Free Trade Zone (ZOFRI) in northern Chile functioning as a major sorting and redistribution hub. Here, women primarily perform the sorting work, categorizing garments into quality grades for resale or re-export. Despite these efforts, significant amount of imported SHC is either unsellable or exceeds local demand and is discarded in landfills or informal dumpsites in the Atacama Desert (UNECE & ECLAC, 2023). The accumulation of textile waste in desert regions has created severe environmental and health concerns, particularly due to the burning of synthetic fibres, which releases harmful pollutants.

Efforts to regulate and improve traceability in the SHC value chain have gained traction in policy debates. Within the European Union, discussions are ongoing regarding the possible restriction or reform of textile waste exports to third-world countries (EEA, 2024). Such regulatory shifts are aimed at increasing the proportion of textiles recycled within Europe and reducing dependency on export-based reuse systems. However, these developments must be balanced against the socioeconomic role of SHC in importing countries. Without coordinated international frameworks and investment in local reuse and recycling infrastructure, restricting exports could unintentionally harm local economies that depend heavily on SHC for income and affordable clothing (IVL, 2025).

Efforts to improve traceability include proposals for the development of DPPs, harmonized definitions of textile waste, and minimum export criteria. Enhanced sorting practices in Europe, supported by automated technologies and better labelling, are also necessary to ensure that only garments with actual reuse potential are exported. Without these measures, the current trade system risks externalizing the environmental burden of textile waste from the Global North to the

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

This draft deliverable has not yet been validated by the granting authorities

<sup>14</sup> There are arguments that the impact of China on the domestic textile industry is arguably more challenging than SHC. China's penetration into African apparel markets is underappreciated. Consequently, it is also the most underestimated hurdles to Africa's textile renaissance (Current Status of Mozambique's Second-Hand Clothing Market: Opportunities and Challenges, Consulting for Africa and Abalon Capital Limitada, p.44). The authors also stated that IMF's structural adjustment policies, the liberalisation of global trade, withdrawal of government subsidies throughout the national value chain as the explanation of this decline. While the erosion of local industries cannot be attributed solely to worn-clothing imports, its impact must be understood within a broader context of structural adjustment and trade liberalisation (The socioeconomic impact of second-hand clothes in Africa and the EU27+, Oxford Economics, 2024, Pag. 24).

third-world countries (UNECE & ECLAC, 2023; Nellström et al., 2025).

In conclusion, textile reuse in third-world countries offers both significant environmental and socioeconomic benefits, but these are highly contingent on the quality of imported garments, local waste management capacities, and the structure of SHC trade systems. Addressing the current limitations in traceability and quality assurance, while fostering regulatory harmonization and cross-border collaboration, will be critical for enhancing the sustainability and fairness of global textile reuse practices.

## 3.3.2 Country case studies: textile reuse practices in Africa and Latin America

## 3.3.2.1 Textile reuse practices in Africa

The global trade of second-hand textiles has significant environmental, social, and economic implications, particularly for developing countries that receive large volumes of used garments. While reuse practices contribute to climate mitigation and livelihood generation, concerns over traceability and the inflow of textile waste under the claim of reuse challenge the sustainability of this model.

Launched in 2023, the UNEP Circularity and Used Textile Trade Project aims to develop global guidelines that differentiate between textile waste and reusable textiles, promoting responsible trade. It works with stakeholders in countries such as Ghana, Kenya, and Tunisia to identify policy, regulatory, and investment priorities to support circularity (UNEP, 2023). This initiative is part of the broader One UNEP Textile Initiative, which integrates efforts to eliminate hazardous chemicals, reduce overproduction, and scale circular business models in the textile sector. By aligning with the UN's goals on sustainable consumption and production, the initiative seeks to embed traceability and accountability across the textile value chain (UNEP, 2023).

Ghana's SHC sector, around Accra's Kantamanto Market, is both a critical economic engine and a growing environmental concern. In Ghana, the used clothing market serves as an example of how global overproduction and consumption patterns translate into complex socio-environmental realities. Ghana is one of the largest global importers of second-hand clothing. It is estimated that the recent years (2022) is receiving approximately 15 million garments weekly or 111,329 tonnes annually (A.Khan, 2025). Kantamanto Market in Accra, spanning over 24 acres, hosts around 30,000 vendors and traders who rely on this inflow for income generation (Vintage Clothing Guides, 2023). The specific Market has an undisputable contribution to employment, and it is estimated to support 2.5 million livelihoods (Financial Times, 2024). However, the market also functions as a site where excess from the Global North is dumped under the guise of reuse. Key facts about this huge used textile market are the following (Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2024):

 Textiles transported to Kantamanto market (Relative to the quantity of textiles imported into Ghana in 2022): 63% Textiles sent onwards to other markets in Ghana (Relative to the quantity of textiles transported to Kantamanto market): 30% Textiles exported to neighbouring countries (Relative to the quantity of textiles transported to Kantamanto market): 50% Textiles remaining for sale on Kantamanto Market (Relative to the quantity of textiles transported to Kantamanto market): 20% of which approximately 60% are considered

quality 1 and 2 grades, while 40% are considered low value and therefore likely to end up in final disposal.

The used textiles trade plays a vital role in Ghana's informal economy. Following the liberalization policies of the 1980s and 1990s, second-hand clothing became an affordable and accessible commodity for local consumers while offering economic opportunities to women, youth, and lowincome traders (Tetteh, 2023). Tailors, recyclers, and informal resellers form a decentralized value chain, allowing for low-cost repairs, restyling, and redistribution.

However, this system bears a high environmental cost. Up to 40% of imported textiles are unusable<sup>15</sup> due to poor quality or damage, turning Ghana into a dumping ground for textile waste (A.Khan, 2025). This waste ends up in open landfills, coastal wetlands, and urban drainage systems, contributing to microplastic pollution and toxic chemical leaching (Phys.org, 2024). A recent investigation found discarded garments from major UK brands in protected wetland areas near Accra, exposing a failure of international supply chain accountability (The Guardian, 2025). In response to these challenges, Ghanaian creatives and NGOs have turned to upcycling and design innovation as mechanisms for local empowerment and environmental action. Organizations such as The Or Foundation and The Revival partner with communities to transform waste garments into new fashion, creating income while reducing landfill pressure (AP News, 2023). Events like Obroni Wawu October, an annual celebration of thrift and reuse culture, amplify these efforts by integrating fashion shows, community dialogues, and policy debates (Vogue Business, 2023). Calls for the Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) legislation have gained momentum, advocating that fashion brands in the Global North contribute financially to the end-oflife management of their products in the third-world countries (Financial Times, 2024).

Textile reuse in Mozambique<sup>16</sup> presents a complex interplay between informal economic practices, cultural adaptation, and environmental sustainability. Despite lacking a structured textile recycling industry, Mozambique exhibits widespread reuse behaviours, driven primarily by affordability and availability. Mozambique imported around 40,000 tonnes of used clothing in 2023 and is the second largest importer in the Southern African Development Community (SADC), after Tanzania (E&M Magazine,2025). Second-hand garments, locally referred to as "calamidades", are commonly sold in open-air markets and informal retail zones, such as Maputo's Feira de Xipamanine. This trade supports a significant number of micro-entrepreneurs, especially women, who rely on the resale of used clothing for livelihood (Nixon, 2019). Imported clothing is often redistributed across regions, generating a supply chain rooted in informality. These markets fill the gap left by Mozambique's underdeveloped domestic textile production and limited access to affordable new clothing (Mambo & Archer, 2022). Beyond resale, textile reuse at the household level is common. Clothing is frequently passed down within families or repurposed for alternative uses, such as cleaning rags, baby nappies, or shading material in agricultural settings. Tailors also play a vital role in repairing and creatively altering garments, often blending Western styles with traditional capulana fabrics. This form of upcycling, although largely informal, reflects a culturally embedded circular practice

The trade in second-hand clothes ensures jobs for over 200,000 people, sustains more than 1 million livelihoods, and generates millions of dollars in taxes for Mozambique.

and has gained traction among youth who seek personalized or unique fashion statements (Fischer et al., 2021). Several non-governmental organizations and community enterprises have initiated textile upcycling and tailoring training programs aimed at women and youth. These initiatives aim to increase income-generating opportunities and reduce textile waste through skillbuilding and sustainable entrepreneurship. For example, the Mozambican organization MozEco supports workshops focused on turning textile waste into marketable products, aligning with sustainable development goals and climate resilience strategies (UNDP Mozambique, 2020).

## 3.3.2.2 Textile reuse practices in Latin America

Latin America has emerged as a significant player in the global SHC trade, with countries like Chile, Guatemala, Mexico, Nicaragua, Honduras, and El Salvador serving as major import hubs for what the industry terms "credential clothing" (Bank & Vogue, 2025). Among Latin American countries, Chile ranks as one of the top global importers of credential clothing. In 2021, the country imported over 126,000 metric tons of used garments, with approximately 40% entering through the Iquique Free Trade Zone located in the Atacama Desert, where clothes are manually sorted, and most of the leftovers that can't be resold end up illegally dumped in the desert, which serves as a central logistical hub for re-export and local distribution. Central America also represents a vital region for the SHC trade. Guatemala receives around 130,000 tons annually, followed by Honduras with 66,000 tons, Nicaragua with 52,500 tons, and El Salvador with 35,000 tons. (UNECE & ECLAC, 2023).

![](_page_144_Figure_5.jpeg)

Figure 35. Exports of used clothing in selected countries and regions, 2001–2021, in million tons. Source: UNECE & ECLAC, 2023

Mexico presents a more complex regulatory environment. Despite being a major importer, the country imposes additional bureaucratic requirements, such as mandatory importer registration with the Secretaría de Hacienda y Crédito Público (SHCP), which can deter small-scale or new entrants into the market. Nevertheless, Mexico remains a significant player in the trade due to its large domestic consumer base and strategic proximity to the United States.

To enhance sustainability and accountability in textile reuse practices, the establishment of international standards that clearly define reusable garments versus waste is essential. Digital platforms such as blockchain could facilitate traceability, ensuring that garments shipped abroad are genuinely reusable. EPR schemes should also be implemented to make exporters accountable for the post-use phase of garments. Finally, capacity building in recipient countries is necessary to strengthen waste management and textile sorting infrastructure.

Multiple factors have contributed to the growing demand for credential clothing in Latin America. Economic affordability is a central driver. Rising inflation in the apparel sector, especially in countries like Argentina, has made new clothing unaffordable for large segments of the population. As a result, second-hand imports offer a critical alternative for low- and middle-income consumers.

Cultural perceptions around used clothing have also shifted. Whereas second-hand garments were once stigmatized as symbols of poverty or foreign surplus, they are now increasingly embraced by younger, urban populations as expressions of sustainable fashion or vintage style. This transformation reflects global trends in consumer awareness, where environmental and ethical considerations are influencing fashion choices.

Additionally, credential clothing complements the structure of local markets. Many Latin American economies lack strong domestic fashion brands with diverse offerings at affordable prices. Credential imports fill this gap by providing access to a wide variety of styles and international labels. These garments often cater to niches that are underserved by the region's mainstream fashion industry, including plus-size clothing, outerwear, and Western fashion. Credential clothing enters Latin America through formal trade channels, typically accompanied by all necessary documentation, such as bills of lading, certificates of origin, and fumigation certificates. In Chile, for example, all imports must undergo sanitary inspections and meet labelling standards. In Mexico, additional customs registrations are required, including proof of importer authorization and proper tariff classification. Despite its economic benefits, the credential clothing trade poses significant environmental challenges. In Chile, for instance, garments that cannot be resold, often low-value, synthetic, or soiled textiles, are illegally dumped in the Atacama Desert. These textile accumulations, often visible from satellite imagery, reflect the lack of proper waste management infrastructure and the unsustainable disposal practices tied to overconsumption. The governance of credential clothing imports varies widely across the region. While some governments promote the trade as a source of affordable goods and tax revenue, others raise concerns about consumer safety and environmental risk. Inconsistent regulatory enforcement further complicates the situation. In some countries, mislabelling or illegal dumping persists due to limited institutional capacity (Bank & Vogue, 2025).

Nonetheless, efforts are underway to address these issues. Regional trade bodies and NGOs have begun advocating for harmonized import standards and improved infrastructure for textile sorting and recycling. International organizations, including the United Nations and development agencies, are also encouraging greater transparency and circularity within the second-hand garment economy. Pilot programs in cities like Santiago and Mexico City aim to improve sorting processes and establish textile recovery centres to divert low-quality garments from landfills. On 22 August 2024, the Chilean Ministry of Environment proposed a Draft Circular Economy Strategy for Textiles. The strategy is part of a broader shift away from a linear economic model set out in the Roadmap for a Circular Chile by 2040. It aims to channel efforts to prevent the generation of waste, promote better quality garments and increase the reuse and recovery of textiles.

Socially, the trade supports job creation across multiple sectors. From logistics and customs brokerage to sorting, resale, and upcycling, credential clothing generates livelihoods for thousands of people in Latin America<sup>17</sup>. Informal street vendors, warehouse operators, and small retailers all depend on the consistent flow of imports. Moreover, the second-hand economy has catalysed innovation in sustainable fashion, with local designers and artisans incorporating used garments into upcycled collections or artisanal crafts.

Quality assurance is also an essential aspect of the trade. Bales of credential clothing are labelled according to grade, seasonality, or style, including categories such as "vintage," "tropical mix," or "premium." Importers and local distributors often rely on trusted suppliers to maintain consistency in quality, since variations can negatively impact consumer confidence and resale value. In recent years, some companies have begun implementing digital inventory tracking and traceability tools to improve oversight and transparency in the supply chain.

## 3.4 Extended Producer Responsibility

## In a nutshell

The revised Waste Framework Directive introduces 2 main sets of measures:

A. Mandatory EPR schemes for textile and footwear products: All Member States are required to establish their own EPR scheme for textiles and footwear, following common rules applicable across the EU. Under such schemes, textile and footwear producers will pay a fee for each product they place on the market. This fee will finance collection schemes and the management of the collected textiles, providing for their re-use, preparing for re-use, recycling and disposal.

EPR fees will be adjusted based on sustainability criteria, such as the ones developed under the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR), considering factors such as durability and recyclability. This approach, known as eco-modulation, links the cost producers pay for EPR to the sustainability of their textiles, thus encouraging them to design more circular and environmentally friendly products.

The EPR fees will also be used to inform consumers about sustainable textiles and footwear, and support research and development to improve product design, waste prevention and management operations.

B. New rules for the management of used textiles and textile waste: All separately collected textiles will be considered as waste, ensuring a uniform interpretation across Member States of what constitutes "waste" versus "used" textiles. This means Member States will have to ensure that separately collected textiles undergo sorting operations before their possible shipment, preventing waste from being falsely labelled and exported as reusable. If not sorted, textile waste will be subject to the Waste Shipment Regulation.

The concept of Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) was introduced with its origins in Nordic countries and Germany. EPR gained traction as a way to address the growing issue of product waste. In a work by Lindhqvist in the 1990s "Extended Producer Responsibility is an environmental protection strategy to reach an environmental objective of a decreased total environmental impact from a product, by making the manufacturer of the product responsible for the entire life cycle of the product and especially for the take-back, recycling and final disposal. Extended Producer Responsibility is implemented through administrative, economic, and informative instruments. The composition of these instruments determines the precise form of the Extended Producer Responsibility."

Despite variation across the world, EPR policies have a common goal of reducing waste, by increasing the recycling of materials and, in turn, reducing the dependency on them. This goal is based on the "polluter pays" principle, which is the concept that producers take responsibility for their products at their end-of-life stage. The concept encourages product design with reusability in mind and incentivizes product designs that boost recyclability and minimize environmental impact.

Any business that manufactures, imports, sells, or brand-owns any product in an area with an active EPR policy could be considered a producer, depending on the area's policy. The definitions of each of these types of businesses are listed below (Source: Source Intelligence 2024):

![](_page_148_Diagram_1.jpeg)

Figure 36. Definitions of types of businesses.: Source Intelligence 2024

Companies that meet any of the producer obligation criteria must check for EPR legislation in each country they operate in to assess their compliance. Countries may also have added thresholds for businesses, such as turnover and quantity of products placed on the market.

## 3.4.1 EPR in the textile sector

EPR, which holds producers accountable for the entire lifecycle of their products, is not a new concept in the textile industry. However, the regulatory landscape is undergoing significant change. While the EU leads in adopting such measures, similar initiatives are gaining traction worldwide, a trend that fashion and textile brands must be ready to navigate. Rather than placing the burden of textile waste on consumers and national governments, EPR shifts this responsibility to producers. Fashion brands are expected to take responsibility for their products from creation through to endof-life, including collection, recycling, and final disposal of textiles.

![](_page_148_Figure_6.jpeg)

Figure 37. Map of existing and emerging EPR systems for textiles. Source: Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2024

## 3.4.2 Textile EPR requirements

The specific EPR requirements can vary depending on the country, but they generally include several key elements such as (Lüttin, 2025):

 Reporting on the quantities or weight of clothes placed on the market, material type, endof-live management data and waste treatment processes. Participation in take-back schemes for waste collection. Fee payment per unit or weight of textiles placed on the market, which helps fund waste management and recycling systems. Eco-modulation mechanisms.

Eco-modulation refers to the practice of adjusting the fees that producers pay under EPR schemes based on the environmental impact of their products. In the textile industry, eco-modulation incentivizes companies to design, produce, and sell more sustainable textiles by modulating (increasing or decreasing) the fees they pay according to specific environmental criteria. According to these criteria, the "eco-friendlier" the textile product is, the less the producer pays.

The objective is to encourage apparel and footwear brands to account for the environmental impact of their products from the initial design phase. This entails the integration of sustainable practices across their operations, including the use of recyclable materials, the development of durable product designs, and the implementation of end-of-life garment recycling systems. Such measures contribute to the creation of products that are both environmentally sustainable and more suitable to recycling processes.

## 3.4.3 The role of Producer Responsibility Organisations (PROs)

In a collective EPR scheme, obligated businesses delegate their responsibility (fully or partially) to a third party. Typically, but not exclusively, the third party is a joint PRO, which fulfils obligations for the in-scope products on the businesses' behalf, and coordinates the activities identified as within the scope of such a body. The legally obligated businesses pay the PRO, in order to cover the necessary expenses for achieving the legally required outcomes and objectives. PROs are legally obliged to provide training and financial support to promote the inclusion of informal waste pickers and recyclers.

On 22 January 2025, leading PROs and industry associations announced the launch of the Textile PRO Forum, a voluntary European initiative coordinated by EURATEX. This Forum aims to harmonize the implementation of EPR schemes for textiles and footwear across EU member states. As Europe prepares for mandatory separate collection of textile waste under the updated Waste Framework Directive, the Textile PRO Forum seeks to address the fragmentation of up to 27 different national EPR models. By facilitating collaboration among PROs and business associations, such as Refashion, ATOK, Danish Fashion & Textile, Czech Cobat Tessile, and others, the Forum will share best practices and pursue strategic goals such as reducing administrative burdens, aligning eco‑modulation fee systems, supporting recycling efforts, and expanding PRO frameworks. Participants include 22 organizations spanning various European countries. Their joint efforts aim to streamline national EPR schemes, foster regulatory alignment, and advance textile circularity throughout Europe. Below is the list of the participating organizations:
