## 3.4.4 Benefits of EPR in the textile industry

If designed well, EPR policy significantly improves the cost-revenue dynamics for separate collection, sorting, reuse, repair and recycling of discarded textiles. EPR also delivers transparency and traceability on global material flows, and it helps to attract capital investments in the infrastructure needed to reuse and recycle at scale.

![](_page_150_Figure_8.jpeg)

Figure 38. EPR benefits for the textile sector. Source: Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2024

Around the world, the vast majority of textiles (more than 80%) leak out of the system when they are discarded. They are incinerated, landfilled, or end up in the environment. Separate, dedicated textile collection systems are underdeveloped and do not capture all textiles placed on the market.

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

In the current textile system, most business models are linear. They are based on high volumes of new products, made from virgin resources, which are often used for a short time and then thrown away. Also, products are not always designed to last and are often hard to recycle. Separate collection is the only way to keep textiles from ending up in the waste stream or worse, the environment. But today, the economics of separate collection and sorting do not stack up. Collecting and managing discarded textiles happens at a cost (beyond textiles with high reuse value), and sorters around the world experience challenging profitability. This is a key barrier to achieving a circular economy for textiles.

While sorters can generate a positive operating margin for reusable clothing, the non-reusable clothing fraction (sold to down cyclers or recyclers) is operating at loss, based on current market prices (Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2024).

![](_page_151_Figure_5.jpeg)

Figure 39. The business case for sorters in Europe, Kenya, and India. Source: Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2024

## 3.4.5 Waste collection

Collection systems currently in place

Apparel and footwear companies are required to manage waste collection while also ensuring compliance with country-specific reporting obligations. For waste collection, brands can become members of a PRO. Even if this isn't mandatory in a given individual EU-member state, it's advisable to become a member of a PRO, otherwise brands will have to organize their own waste end-of-life processes such as waste collection.

Separate collection of waste fractions diverts recyclable materials from the residual or mixed municipal solid waste and thereby provides opportunities to make more value out of valuable resources that would otherwise be landfilled and incinerated (Albizzati et al., 2023). Main collection strategies for dry recyclables and textiles typically consist of pick-up schemes and drop-off schemes (Huygens et al., 2023).

Post-industrial textile residues are generated during the manufacturing process (e.g. cut-off leftovers), and their management is controlled by the textile manufacturer. It may involve on-site recycling, posterior management by industrial waste management as a third-party (business-tobusiness), or disposal. Post-industrial waste is usually clean and undamaged and of a well-known chemical composition and can often be recycled without any additional cleaning or sorting. Therefore, it is a preferred source for mechanical recycling processes (Huygens et al., 2023)

In the EU, textile waste is primarily gathered through street containers, commonly known as bring points. These are often supplemented by civic amenity sites where residents can dispose of household waste and recyclables not covered by standard curbside services (ΕΕΑ, 2024). Municipalities, charities, social enterprises, second-hand shops and retail companies (via takeback schemes) play a varying role in the collection of post-consumer textile waste. The organisations involved in separate collection are typically also responsible for sorting the collected textile material (Huygens et al., 2023).

Each collection method offers distinct advantages and inherent limitations. Indoor collection is particularly effective for obtaining high quality, clean textiles suitable for reuse (Wagner, 2022; van Duijn et al., 2022). However, limited opening hours and the small number of available sites can constrain the total volume collected. Bring points, while capable of handling larger quantities of textiles, are more susceptible to contamination than indoor systems (van Duijn et al., 2022). Specifically, moisture or exposure to rain can cause mould, rendering the textiles unusable. Given the sector's slim profit margins, cleaning, washing, or drying such contaminated items is not economically viable. Conversely, door-to-door collection incurs higher operational costs and is more prone to theft (van Duijn et al., 2022).

Overall, bring points are generally considered the most effective method for collecting substantial amounts of used textiles of reasonable quality (Wagner, 2022). Key factors, such as the strategic placement of collection points, collection frequency, container design and condition, as well as clear labelling and communication, play a crucial role in enhancing both the quantity and the quality of the collected materials.

Across Europe, the average textile waste capture rate is approximately 12%, meaning most textiles end up in mixed municipal waste streams, ultimately being landfilled or incinerated (ΕΕΑ, 2024). This highlights a considerable potential for improving separate textile collection systems. Luxembourg and Belgium lead with a 50% separate collection rate, followed by the Netherlands (37%) and Austria (30%). These countries employ diverse and well-structured collection systems adapted to various urban contexts.

## 3.4.6 EU Waste Framework Directive

The WFD was adopted in 2021 with the objective of enhancing waste management practices across the European Union. A central element of the Directive is the introduction of EPR, a policy approach whereby Member States are required to establish national legal frameworks that ensure producers assume responsibility for the entire lifecycle of their products, including post-consumer waste management.

In accordance with the WFD, all EU Member States are mandated to implement separate textile waste collection systems by January 1, 2025. This requirement obliges municipalities to segregate textile waste from general municipal solid waste.

In 2023, the European Commission proposed a targeted revision of the WFD focusing on the textile sector. Given the current fragmentation with various Member States applying different EPR schemes, compliance and reporting obligations for producers have become increasingly complex. The primary aim of the revised legislation is to harmonize these frameworks across the EU, thereby facilitating more coherent regulatory compliance while promoting innovation, sustainability, and circular economy principles within the textile and footwear industries.

Under the revised WFD, each Member State is required to introduce an EPR scheme specific to textiles. Producers will be held accountable for the post-consumer phase of their products. The policy is designed to incentivize the development of products with extended durability and enhanced potential for reuse, repair, and recycling. Producer contributions under the EPR scheme will also be directed toward infrastructure development, including separate collection systems, sorting and reuse facilities, and textile recycling plants

Additionally, the revised Directive introduces stringent controls to prevent the illegal export of textile waste. A clear distinction is now drawn between "waste" and "reusable" textiles, mandating comprehensive sorting processes prior to the export of used garments. These provisions aim to ensure that textile waste exported outside the EU is managed under environmentally sound conditions.

A provisional political agreement on the revised Directive was reached on February 18, 2025, between the European Parliament and the Council. This was subsequently endorsed by the European Parliament's Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Food Safety (ENVI) on March 18, 2025, and validated by COREPER (Committee of Permanent Representatives) in March 2025.

The revision of the Waste Framework Directive, Directive 2025/1892, entered into force on 16 October 2025.

By June 2027 EPR systems must be embedded in domestic law, and by April 2028 EPR systems must be operational (the deadline for microenterprises to comply with EPR schemes has an additional 12-month transition time until April 2029).

The following product categories are included:

## Textile products

 Knitted or crocheted clothing and accessories Non-knitted clothing and accessories Blankets and travelling rugs (except electric blankets) Bed linen, table linen, toilet linen, kitchen linen Curtains, drapes, and interior blinds Curtain or bed valances Other furnishing articles (excluding mattresses) Worn clothing and other worn articles Hats and other headgear made from plaited strips Knitted or crocheted hats, lace or felt headgear, and hairnets Non-textile apparel & footwear

## Leather clothing and accessories

 Waterproof footwear made from rubber or plastics Footwear with rubber/plastic soles and uppers Footwear with leather soles and uppers Footwear with textile uppers Other types of footwear

### France

France has been a pioneer in the implementation of EPR, having introduced legally binding EPR obligations for the textile sector as early as 2008. The enactment of the Anti-Waste for a Circular Economy (AGEC) Law in 2020 significantly broadened the scope of this framework by redefining the concept of a "producer" and establishing a series of enhanced regulatory obligations.

Key regulatory provisions include:

 EPR expansion to textiles: Producers of apparel, footwear, and household linens are required to finance the collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling of post-consumer textile products. Ban on destruction of unsold goods: The destruction of unsold textiles and footwear is prohibited, with the aim of encouraging reuse, donation, and recycling. Product volume reporting: Producers must annually report the number and type of textile products placed on the French market, disaggregated by category. Recycled content and recyclability disclosure: Brands are obliged to declare the percentage of recycled fibres in their products and whether these products are designed for recyclability. End-of-life management data: Producers must report detailed data on product volumes collected, reused, and recycled, including final waste treatment routes (e.g., donation, incineration, material recovery). Eco-modulation reporting: Information must be provided on hazardous substances, repairability, and durability to support the eco-modulation of fees according to environmental performance.

The French EPR framework for textiles applies to all new clothing, household linen, and footwear (CHF) placed on the French market, including overseas territories. It encompasses products sold or donated to consumers, whether new or upcycled, as well as those distributed through rental systems. Exemptions apply to items made entirely from leather or natural fur, imported secondhand products, and upcycled products composed exclusively of materials that have already fulfilled EPR obligations. All other materials (e.g., cotton, polyester, silk, recycled or not) fall within the scope of regulation. Additionally, clothing and accessories for animals marketed to consumers are covered under this framework (ADEME, 2025).

The obligations apply to any entity marketing textile products in France. While some companies operate individual take-back systems, most participate in Refashion, the accredited PRO managing a national network of textile collection points. The scheme follows an eco-modulated fee structure, whereby EPR contributions are adjusted based on environmental attributes. Standard EPR fees range between €0.01 and €0.06 per item.

year, detailing the quantities of textiles, footwear, and household linens placed on the market in the preceding year. Products must also display the Triman logo and corresponding sorting instructions (Info-tri) to guide consumers in appropriate end-of-life disposal.

The French EPR scheme is governed by the decree of 23 November 2022, which sets out the specifications for the accredited eco-organization (Refashion). It establishes quantitative and qualitative targets for the period up to 2027–2028.

## Quantitative targets (Collection, Reuse, and Recycling)

- i. Separate Collection Rate: 60% of the volume (by mass) of textiles placed on the French market in 2028.
- ii. Reuse and Preparation for re-use: 120,000 tonnes of used textiles sorted must be prepared for reuse or re-use. 15% of the collected and sorted textiles must be reused or re-used within a range of 1,500 km from the collection point by 2027.
- iii. Recycling: 70% recycling rate (of the collected, sorted but non-reused textiles) by 2024. 80% recycling rate (of the collected, sorted but non-reused textiles) by 2027.
- iv. Specific Recycling Target for Synthetic Materials: 90% recycling rate by 2027 for used textiles that contain at least 90% synthetic plastic fibres and are not reused.
- v. Disposal (Landfill/Incineration): A maximum of 0.5% of the collected or supported textiles is to be eliminated (landfill or incineration without energy recovery).
- vi. Repair: An increase of +35% in the volume of repair operations supported by the scheme by 2028 compared to 2019.

The scheme incorporates qualitative mechanisms to support eco-design and circular economy objectives:

- i. Eco-Modulation of fees (Bonus/Malus System): The scheme uses variable contributions to incentivize eco-design. Producers receive a bonus (reduced fee) or a penalty (increased fee) based on criteria like: Durability of the products. Inclusion of recycled materials (especially from post-consumer TLC waste). Presence of recognized environmental certifications (labels). Recyclability issues (e.g., penalties for materials that hinder recycling).
- ii. Support for Circular Economy: The eco-organization must dedicate funds to Develop and finance a local reuse and repair sector. Support R&D into new recycling technologies, especially for fibre-to-fibre recycling. Finance the collection and sorting infrastructure.
- iii. Consumer Information: Obligation to display the Triman logo and specific sorting instructions (Info-tri) on the product or packaging to guide consumers on disposal.
- iv. Prevention and Eco-design: Producers must adopt an eco-design approach, promote product longevity, and contribute to the prevention and management of waste.

As of 2025, France's eco-modulation scheme includes the bonuses for product durability, based on product-type-specific benchmarks, recognized environmental certifications (e.g., GOTS, Oeko-Tex® Made in Green), use of recycled materials, with financial bonuses of €1,000/ton for closedloop recycled content and €500/ton for open-loop recycled inputs. Penalties are being described for low recyclability, particularly for products containing metallo-plastic fibres or electronic

### components that impede recycling.

All eco-modulation data are submitted via Refashion's digital platform using standardized templates and must be accompanied by a waste prevention and eco-design plan, available through Refashion's official documentation.

By decree of 30/6/2025, the French Minister for Ecological Transition, Biodiversity, Forests, the Sea, and Fisheries approved CITEO as an accredited eco-organization responsible for managing single-use sanitary textiles (including wipes) under the EPR framework established by the French Environmental Code. Producers of these items, manufacturers, importers, and distributors, are required to either operate an approved individual system or join an accredited eco-organization to ensure proper end-of-life management. CITEO's accreditation is valid from 1 July 2025 to 31 December 2030, in accordance with the decree of December 2024 governing eco-organizations and individual systems for single-use sanitary textiles.

France introduced an EPR scheme for textiles in 2008, covering clothing, footwear, and household linens. Over the years the scheme has evolved and new legislation has supported its intentions, such as a ban on the destruction of unsold non-food products. The core principles are very similar to those covered by the EU-wide EPR legislation, such as paying a fee to finance the collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling of waste textiles, the use of a PRO to facilitate the scheme, and the eco-modulation of fees. What is interesting is the level of reporting that producers are required to do. Like reporting on the number of textile items they place on the French market, producers must report end of life management data (such as the quantity of products collected and how the waste was processed) and whether products are designed for recyclability or contain recycled materials. Discounts on fees are given on products that demonstrate durability, use recycled materials and have recognised environmental certifications. The average EPR fee paid in 2021 was €0.0116 per garment, although fees can be as high as €0.063 per garment. (Source: Source Fashion).

## Netherlands

As of July 2023, the Netherlands enforced EPR requirements for producers and importers of clothing, workwear, and household textiles (excluding footwear, belts, headwear, bags, blankets, and curtains). The list of the products included in Decree of 14 April 2023 are (M. van A. Zaken.2025): Clothing: Consumer clothing and work clothing, such as safety outfits; Household Textiles: Table linen, bed linen, and household linen, including items like hand and tea towels.

Items such as shoes, belts, headgear, blankets, curtains, and cleaning cloths are not covered under the EPR obligations. Unsold stocks are also excluded if they have not been placed on the market (Lüttin, 2025).

Foreign producers selling into the Dutch market must appoint an Authorized Representative.

The principal PRO, Stichting UPV Textiel, manages compliance, including collective collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling operations. Producers must report product volumes, pay EPR fees, and contribute to achieving national targets.

|------------------------------|------------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| (Overall)                    | 50% of weight placed on the market | 75% of weight placed on the market |
| Prepared for Reuse (Minimum) | 20% of weight placed on the market | 25% of weight placed on the market |
| Reuse in the                 | Netherlands                        |                                    |
| (Minimum)                    | 10% of weight placed on the market | 15% of weight placed on the market |

| Target Category          | Goal for 2025                   | Goal for 2030                   |
|--------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| Fibre-to-Fibre Recycling | (High                          |                                 |
| quality)                 | 25% of the total recycled share | 33% of the total recycled share |

The Dutch EPR fee, effective from July 1, 2025, is set at €0.24/kg, prorated for the first year. Fashion brands must submit a provisional weight-based declaration before April 1 of each year and reconcile it the following year based on actual sales. Reporting is facilitated through the Textool system provided by Stichting UPV Textiel.

The Netherlands introduced EPR legislation for textiles in July 2023. This includes mandatory reuse and recycling targets for producers as of 2025 with reporting on those targets beginning from 2026. The EPR scheme covers clothing, workwear, and household textiles but not footwear, bags, blankets, or curtains. Fees are based on the estimated weight of textiles that producers plan to sell in the Netherlands in the upcoming year. They then submit actual sales for the calendar year and pay any differences in the fee. Currently, an average fee of €0.12 per kilogram is advised. The mandatory targets include one focused on fibre-to-fibre recycling. As of 2025, 50% of textiles sold in the Netherlands must be prepared for reuse or recycling, and 25% of recycled textiles must undergo fibre-to-fibre recycling. This rises to 33% by 2030 (Source: Source Fashion).

### Hungary

Hungary initiated its EPR scheme for textiles in July 2023. The scope includes a broad range of items classified by customs nomenclature codes (Process Solutions, 2023): Clothing, apparel, and accessories (knitted, crocheted, or not); Footwear; Household textiles (bed linen, table linen, toilet linen, kitchen linen); Furnishing articles (carpets, blankets, curtains, bed curtains).

The obligation falls on the entity that first places the product on the Hungarian market, which includes Hungarian manufacturers; Hungarian importers; Foreign companies engaged in distance selling (e-commerce) directly to end-consumers in Hungary.

Producers must fulfil their obligations by contracting with the state-designated concession company, MOHU MOL Hulladékgazdálkodási Zrt. (MOHU). The primary method is to join the collective system and pay the EPR fee, transferring the responsibility for waste management logistics to MOHU. Producers must register with both the relevant National Waste Management Authority and the concession company MOHU. Non-Hungarian companies selling directly to endconsumers in Hungary must appoint an Authorized Representative in Hungary to handle the EPR obligations.

Producers must pay an EPR fee (HUF 145/kg (€0.42/kg)) to MOHU on a quarterly basis. The fee is calculated based on the quantity (weight) of the circular products placed on the market during the quarter, multiplied by a fixed fee rate determined by the government for the textile product category. Producers must submit a mandatory report on the quantity of textile products placed on the market to the authorities and MOHU. The EPR fee generally replaces the previous Environmental Product Fee (or "Green Tax") for the products concerned, allowing producers to deduct the EPR fee when calculating the environmental product charge liability.

The Hungarian system generally does not impose individual reuse or recycling percentage targets on each producer, unlike France or the Netherlands. The EPR fee model is the main driver for

compliance. Hungary, as an EU member state, is still obligated to meet the EU-wide targets for waste collection and preparation for reuse/recycling (e.g., separate collection of textiles, and general recycling targets for municipal waste). The EPR fees fund the system (managed by MOHU) responsible for meeting these national targets. Unlike the more mature French and Dutch schemes, the Hungarian EPR fee structure does not widely use a bonus/malus (eco-modulation) system to incentivize environmentally friendly product design (durability, recycled content, etc.).

Hungary calculates its EPR fees based on textile weight. Its EPR legislation came into effect on July 1st, 2023 and requires producers to register with the National Waste Management Authority of the National Inspectorate for Environment and Nature (NWMD) and national PRO entity MOHU. It covers all textiles from clothing and accessories to blankets, curtains, carpets and home linens. Unlike other markets, Hungary requires its EPR fees to be paid quarterly, which means producers have to file a quarterly report on the amount of textiles placed on the market (Source: Source Fashion).

### Latvia

The EPR framework for textiles in Latvia, which became mandatory on January 1, 2024, is primarily a fee-based system that places the financial burden of waste management on producers. Unlike some other EU countries, Latvia's EPR system is strongly linked to its existing Natural Resource Tax (NRT) framework (State Environmental Service Republic of Latvia, 2025)

The EPR obligation applies to any entity that first places textile products on the Latvian market, which includes:

- Domestic manufacturers and importers.
- Foreign companies engaged in distance sales (e-commerce) directly to end-users in Latvia.
- There are no minimum thresholds based on company size or volume, meaning all businesses placing products in scope on the market are affected.

The scope is broad and generally covers products for consumer use, including:

- Clothing (apparel, garments, workwear).
- Footwear.
- Household Textiles (bed linen, table linen, towels, curtains, blinds).
- Accessories (even if textiles are not the main component).
- Bags.

Latvia's system primarily focuses on funding and infrastructure development to meet the overarching EU waste directives. The specific quantitative targets for reuse and recycling are set for the Producer Responsibility Organizations (PROs), not the individual producers, which must contribute financially to the system.

Producers must fulfil their obligations through one of two ways:

- Pay the Natural Resource Tax (NRT): The producer pays the NRT directly to the government on the volume (weight) of textiles placed on the market (the rate is reportedly €0.50 per kilogram of affected products).
- Join a PRO: The producer enters into a contract with an approved PRO (e.g., Latvijas Zaļais Punkts) to manage their obligations. By doing so, the producer is exempt from paying the NRT. Instead, they pay EPR fees to the PRO.

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them. The responsibility falls on producers, which includes (R. Mutilica,2025): Companies manufacturing textiles in Italy; Importers bringing textile goods into Italy; Online sellers/e-commerce platforms

The targets under the Latvian system are focused on ensuring the national waste management infrastructure can manage the textile waste stream.

The primary financial target is to generate sufficient funds (through NRT or PRO fees) to cover the costs of collection, processing, and recycling of textile waste across Latvia.

In essence, the Latvian EPR is designed to internalize the waste management cost into the textile product price, thereby funding the infrastructure necessary to divert textiles from landfill and incineration and move towards national reuse and recycling goals.

EPR legislation for textiles came into effect on January 1st 2024. Producers are charged a Natural Resource Tax (NRT) for their textiles of €0.5 per kilogram of applicable products to finance the collection, disposal and recycling of textiles. These include clothing, household textiles, accessories, bags and footwear. This amount can be reduced if the producer joins an EPR return scheme (Source: Source Fashion).

## 3.4.8 Forthcoming EPR legislation in additional EU Member States

### Belgium

A fully mandatory, national EPR scheme for textiles with specific legislative targets is currently in the preparatory stages. A broad range of textile and footwear products are included such as clothing and apparel, footwear, household textiles (e.g., bed linen, curtains, blankets, kitchen linen). A private voluntary platform Circletex vzw was established in 2022 by industry stakeholders, like the Belgian fashion federation Creamoda, to start early preparations and support future compliance with the mandatory EPR obligations, linking producers, collectors, and processors. Fedustria (Belgian non-profit federation of the textile, wood and furniture industry) announced at the first semester of 2025 a new circular economy initiative titled 'Retexbel' that will be set for launch in 2027. The Retexbel initiative aims to: Increase collection retention by 10%; Create more local reuse (targeting 10%); Improve traceability within the textile sector; Strengthen closed-loop recycling systems.

### Italy

Italy implemented the EU Directive through Legislative Decree No. 116/2020. The detailed regulatory and operational structure of the EPR scheme is being defined through a Draft Ministerial Decree prepared by the Italian Ministry of Environment and Energy Security (MASE), currently under public consultation. While the legal basis for separate collection is already in force, the full operational implementation of the EPR system, including the financial responsibilities of producers is expected to commence in 2026. From that point, producers will be required to register with the National Register of Producers, marking the formal start of their compliance obligations.

The system will rely on PROs, such as Erion Textiles, which will act on behalf of producers to coordinate the collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling of post-consumer textiles. Producers may either establish their own systems or participate in an accredited PRO to fulfil their legal duties.

The Italian EPR scheme is generally expected to cover a comprehensive range of finished textile products placed on the Italian market for the first time.

selling directly to Italian consumers.

The obligation applies to the entity that first places the finished product on the Italian market (manufacturer of components or semi-finished products are generally excluded). The draft decree's scope typically includes Clothing; Footwear; Accessories; Leather goods; Home textiles (blankets, bed linen, curtains); Mattresses (Products.Erion Textiles, 2025).

Producers will be required to finance and organize the end-of-life management of their products, ensuring that post-consumer textile waste is collected, sorted, reused, or recycled. To this end, producers must register with the National Register of Producers and contribute financially through eco-contributions paid to their designated PRO. These contributions will cover the operational costs associated with waste management and recycling (R. Mutilica,2025).

A central feature of the system is eco-modulation, the mechanism that adjusts producer fees based on the environmental performance of their products.

Producers will also be required to implement transparent reporting systems to document the quantities and types of textiles placed on the market. Clear labelling and consumer information, potentially integrated in the future through a DPP, will guide consumers on appropriate disposal and recycling practices, reinforcing the scheme's educational and behavioural change objectives (Producers.Erion Textiles, 2025).

Although specific national targets for recycling and reuse have not yet been finalized pending the publication of the final decree, they are expected to align closely with EU objectives and reflect an ambitious trajectory for improvement. Current estimates suggest that Italy collects approximately 20% of its textile waste, leaving substantial room for progress under the EPR framework (Assenza, 2025, Products.Erion Textiles, 2025).

The forthcoming targets are anticipated to focus on increasing the volume of post-consumer textiles collected for reuse and recycling, alongside establishing minimum thresholds for the proportion of collected textiles prepared for reuse or processed for recycling. Future policy measures may also introduce requirements or incentives for the integration of recycled fibres, particularly from closedloop processes into new textile products.

### Spain

The EPR framework for textiles in Spain is set by Law 7/2022 on waste and contaminated soil for a circular economy (Ley 7/2022, de 8 de abril, de residuos y suelos contaminados para una economía circular). While the law established the mandate, the specific operational details are being finalized through a subsequent Draft Royal Decree (Real Decreto). A pilot textile waste collection program commenced in April 2025, lasting one year. Full implementation of the national EPR system must occur within three years of the law's entry into force.

Producers must fulfil their obligations by joining or creating a Collective System for EPR (SCRAP - Sistema Colectivo de Responsabilidad Ampliada del Productor).

The Spanish EPR framework establishes comprehensive obligations for producers and applies to a notably wide range of textile and footwear products. Under this system, the term "producer" encompasses any entity that places textile or footwear products on the Spanish market for the first time on a professional basis. This definition includes domestic manufacturers, importers introducing such goods into Spain, as well as distance sellers and e-commerce platforms that sell directly to end users within the country, regardless of whether these users are households or businesses (Hurtado, 2025).

The regulation does not set a minimum compliance threshold. Consequently, all producers irrespective of size or production volume are required to register and comply with the EPR obligations. The scope of products covered by the draft decree is extensive and defined primarily through Combined Nomenclature (CN) codes. It includes textile-based apparel and clothing

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

accessories, footwear of any primary material composition, household textiles such as bed linen, blankets, and curtains, as well as leather goods and waterproof garments.

Certain exclusions apply to products intended exclusively for professional or industrial use, as well as to items already governed by other EPR streams, such as medical devices and specific categories of protective equipment. This broad and detailed approach ensures that the Spanish EPR framework encompasses the full lifecycle of textile and footwear products while avoiding regulatory overlap with existing waste management systems.

The targets set by the Spanish EPR are focused on collection and waste reduction and will align with the final EU targets (Glover.2025).

 All municipalities must implement separate collection for textiles. Producers must finance the full cost of waste management, including collection, sorting, preparation for reuse, and recycling operations.' The regulation encourages the involvement of local social economy enterprises in reuse and sorting schemes, with at least 50% of the public waste treatment contracts being reserved for insertion companies and social initiative Special Employment Centres.

Producers' financial contributions (eco-fees) to the SCRAPs will be eco-modulated based on product criteria to encourage eco-design. Higher fees will be applied to products that are more difficult to recycle or contain substances that hinder circularity, while lower fees or bonuses will reward durable, easily repairable, and highly recyclable products (RLG, 2025).

## Sweden

Sweden is one of the pioneering countries in the EU to establish an EPR framework for textiles, going beyond the basic requirements of the EU's Waste Framework Directive (WFD). The framework, based on a government inquiry (SOU 2020:72), aims to significantly increase the reuse and recycling of textiles and is characterized by ambitious reduction targets and clear producer obligations.

Separate collection of textile waste for reuse and recycling became mandatory in Sweden on January 1, 2025, aligning with the EU's WFD requirement. Producers must ensure their compliance obligations (licensing, financing, and participation in a collective system) are fully in place. Producers must enter into a contract with an approved PRO and notify the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency (Naturvårdsverket).

The scheme will cover clothing (consumer and workwear), footwear (shoes, accessories), and household textiles (e.g., bed linen, blankets, curtains, kitchen textiles). Products made from 80% or more textile waste (e.g., from fibre-to-fibre recycling) and 100% leather or natural fur garments are excluded.

The Swedish framework sets ambitious, long-term targets focused on waste reduction and increasing circularity (Schneider202, AxFoundation, 2025, Huygens et al, 2023):

 Waste Disposal Reduction: 70% reduction in the weight of discarded textiles by 2028 (compared to 2022 baseline), 80% reduction by 2032 and 90% reduction by 2036. Reuse and Recycling Rate: From 2028 onwards, at least 90% of the collected textile waste (by weight) must be prepared for reuse or sent for material recycling.

In line with the "Polluter Pays Principle", the financial responsibility in Sweden for the end-of-life management of textile products is transferred to producers.

The fee structure consists of two main components: administrative and inspection fees payable to the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency, and operational contributions directed to approved PROs to cover the costs of collection and treatment activities.

While the detailed parameters of the system will be defined in the final implementing set-up, it is anticipated that Sweden will adopt an eco-modulated fee structure.

## 3.4.9 EPR outside the EU

While the European Union was the first to implement EPR legislation, there are now active EPR policies on every continent. From North America to Australia, EPR policies continue to expand, impacting producers worldwide. However, despite its global presence, EPR compliance varies not only from country to country but also between regions, states, provinces, etc., from tariffs and fee structures to schedules and reporting requirements.

## Australia

 As of July 1, 2024, Australia has initiated a voluntary EPR framework for the textile sector, known as the Seamless Clothing Stewardship Scheme. This initiative was developed as part of the nation's broader circular economy strategy, aiming to divert approximately 200,000 tonnes of textile waste from landfills annually by 2030. The scheme encourages industry participation through the implementation of a voluntary levy of 0.04 Australian dollars per garment. The collected funds are allocated toward initiatives that promote the design of more durable and recyclable clothing, as well as the expansion of infrastructure for textile collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling. The Australian government has declared that should participation levels remain insufficient, legislative measures will be introduced to transition the scheme from voluntary to mandatory.

## Norway

Norway is in the process of establishing an EPR scheme for textiles, largely in alignment with EU developments. The Norwegian EPR scheme is expected to apply to producers (manufacturers, importers, and potentially online platforms) who first place textile products professionally on the Norwegian market. Products in scope are generally defined as any product, regardless of the mixing or assembly process employed, which consists mainly of textile fibres or leather. This typically includes, clothing (including workwear and uniforms), footwear, accessories and bags, household textiles (e.g., bed linen, towels, tablecloths, blankets, and non-attached carpets), healthcare textiles. Products expected to be out of scope (as they may be covered by other schemes) are those not mainly composed of textiles, such as furniture, mattresses, and certain types of carpets. According to published information (Systemiq, 2025), several pilot systems are now being evaluated by the Norwegian Fashion & Textile Agenda (NF&TA) with the aim of increasing the waste collection rate from the current 23% textile to 80% by the end of 2025.

## US California

In September 2024, California passed the Responsible Textile Recovery Act, making it the first American state to enact EPR legislation. The Act requires textile producers to join an approved PRO. These organizations will need to submit a plan for the collection, transportation, repair, sorting, and recycling of apparel and textiles in the state. The "producer" is defined as the person or company responsible for covered products. California has introduced a tiered approach:

- 1. The manufacturer of a product who owns or licenses the brand or trademark under which it is sold.
- 2. The brand owner or exclusive licensee if no such manufacturer exists in California.
- 3. The importer of a product if neither the manufacturer nor licensee are present.
- 4. If none of the above are present in the state, the distributor, retailer, or wholesaler selling the product, is responsible.

The sellers of second-hand products or who have less than \$1 million annual revenue are exempt. In terms of timeline, California's Responsible Textile Recovery Act requires all producers to join a PRO by July 1, 2026.

## US Washington

The Washington State bill HB 1420 (introduced on January 20, 2025) will establish a statewide EPR program for apparel and textiles.

 Covered products: apparel, footwear, accessories, and other items that contain cloth or fibre components (e.g. home textiles). Exclusions: Certain items such as PPE, military equipment, and personal hygiene products are typically excluded. Producers: Entities that introduce covered textile products into the Washington State market (manufacturers, importers, brand owners). Producers must either join or form a PRO by a set date.

The main features are:

 By January 1, 2027, producers must register with the state or join/form a PRO. Producers / PROs must submit a plan to the Department of Ecology for the management of covered products (collection, transportation, repair, recycling) by January 1, 2029. The plan must include convenient drop-off systems across local jurisdictions and tribes. Producers bear full operational cost of the collection, sorting, recycling, and management of post-consumer textiles. Annual reporting, record-keeping, and periodic review every five years of the plan. Civil penalties are proposed for noncompliance (e.g. \$1,000/day first offense; \$10,000/day subsequent).

### China

The system is driven by government guidance and market operation rather than specific PROs. While not an EPR scheme, this policy aims to accelerate the recycling of waste textiles by improving infrastructure, promoting green design, and enhancing producer and consumer responsibility. It focuses on creating a resource-circulating industrial system through coordinated efforts by multiple government ministries, stimulating market participation, and integrating technology and innovation. The policy includes targets for increasing recycling rates and mandates for improving recycling practices but does not enforce direct producer responsibility legislation.

## Colombia

Colombia was one of the first countries in the region to introduce EPR regulations, and while they cover a broad range of waste, the system has only become fully operational recently. The textile EPR system is voluntary and includes apparel. The program invites entrepreneurs in Colombia to learn about the importance of EPR in the fashion sector, as well as the relevance and current legislation on textile waste in Colombia and worldwide. The PRO in Colombia is Colombia Productiva, an autonomous Department affiliated with the Ministry of Commerce, Industry, and Tourism, responsible for supporting the business community to increase the productivity, quality, and added value of its processes, goods, and services.

### Chile

The EPR Law was introduced in Chile in 2016 and currently covers six product categories. The EPR model is very close to the approach used in Europe. It provides solid regulations making waste pickers a strategic stakeholder with rights and obligations. It is also one of the newest systems in the region that plans are underway to add textiles to the product scope a key strategy to promote sustainability and circularity. This means that textile producers will become financially responsible for the management of their products after they are discarded by consumers. The Chilean Ministry of Environment plans to begin the regulatory process for EPR for textiles before the end of 2025, with the aim of having targets in place by 2029. This initiative is part of a broader effort to transition towards a circular economy in the textile industry, reducing waste and promoting reuse and recycling (Ellen MacArthur Foundation, 2024).

### Ghana

In collaboration with the Government of Ghana, through the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation (MESTI), and the European Union Delegation in Ghana a national Circular Economy Action Plan (CEAP) has been developed. Currently, they are developing an EPR scheme for plastics, which will likely be expanded to textiles and other material streams over time. The forthcoming law will replace the existing voluntary system and make EPR a mandatory practice in the country.

### Kenya

Kenya has established a national EPR framework through the Sustainable Waste Management Act (2022) and the Sustainable Waste Management (Extended Producer Responsibility) Regulations of 2024. These instruments introduce EPR obligations for textiles and leather goods. The regulations operationalize producer responsibilities under the 2022 Act and required compliance by May 2025.

Textiles are included in the list of non-packaging products covered under the EPR Regulations. Producers, including manufacturers, importers, and distributors, must register with the National Environment Management Authority (NEMA) and submit an EPR plan outlining their waste management, collection, and recycling measures. Producers may comply individually or collectively via PROs, which are also regulated and certified by NEMA. Producers are mandated to design products to minimize waste generation, facilitate reuse and recycling, and promote the use of secondary raw materials. The regulations also provide for eco-modulated fees that reward sustainable design features.

Importers of textile products must pay the prescribed EPR fee at the point of importation and compliance is monitored through annual reporting and independent audits. Failure to comply with EPR obligations can result in penalties, revocation of licenses, and prosecution.

 Given the importance of the topic, what follows is the latest (10 October 2025) "Mapping of Global EPR for Textiles", by Global Fashion Agenda, summarising the most recent available information, including PROs by country.

**Netherlands**![](_page_167_Picture_89.jpeg)

**Status**[Active](#) since 2022.

**Obligated producer**Applies to manufacturers, producers, and importers. More specifically to ‘the party who is the first to offer the textile product professionally in the Netherlands.’

**Products in scope**Apparel, workwear & home textiles.

Unsold stock that has not been placed on the market is not covered whereas [returned products](#) that have been placed on the market are.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**PROs: [Stichting UPV Textile](#); [Collectief Circulair Textile](#); [European Recycling Platform](#).

Key actors: [Inretail](#); [Modint](#).

**Other**In December 2024, the Dutch Government published its [Circular Textile Policy Programme 2025-2030](#), establishing its roadmap for a circular textile economy by 2050. Specific targets will need to be met for textiles: by 2025, at least 50% of textiles must be prepared for reuse or recycling, of which at least 20% must be for reuse, at least 10% for reuse within the Netherlands, and at least 25% of the recycling must be fibre-to-fibre recycling; by 2030, the targets increase to a minimum of 75% prepared for reuse or recycling, including at least 25% for reuse, at least 15% for reuse within the Netherlands, and at least 33% of recycling through fibre-to-fibre processes.

**Norway**![](_page_168_Picture_46.jpeg)

**Status**Planned. On 29 September 2022, the Ministry of Climate and Environment set up a working group with the task of preparing proposals for a producer responsibility scheme. The working group handed over its final report to the Minister for Climate and Environment on 10 October 2023.

**Obligated producer**Any company that brings textiles into circulation on the Norwegian market for the first time. Foreign companies that place products on the Norwegian market via online platforms will also have obligations.

**Products in scope**Apparel, footwear, accessories, home textiles & healthcare textiles.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**Undefined.

**Other**N/A

**Portugal**![](_page_168_Picture_58.jpeg)

**Status**Upcoming. Pending the adoption of the WFD.

**Obligated producer**Undefined.

**Products in scope**Undefined.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**Electrão.

**Other**There are already initiatives for the selective collection of textiles in many regions of Portugal. These operations are carried out by private actors, such as Humana Portugal.

**Spain**![](_page_168_Picture_70.jpeg)

**Status**Upcoming. On 23 June 2025, Spain published a Draft Royal Decree introducing EPR for textiles and footwear. The draft decree was open for public consultation from 1 July to 4 September 2025.

**Obligated producer**Any manufacturer, importer, distributor, or distance seller placing textile or footwear products on the Spanish market, including domestic and foreign companies of any size or sales model.

**Products in scope**Textile and footwear products for household or similar use.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**Gerescal & Re-viste.

**Other**Spain has launched Re-viste, former SCRAP initiative (Collective System for Extended Producer Responsibility), a pilot program for large-scale textile waste management. Major brands like H&M, Inditex, and Mango are involved. In April 2025, the program began testing collection methods across six municipalities, focusing on recycling and reuse. Collected textiles will be sorted for resale or recycling into new fabrics. Collaboration between councils, producers, and consumers is key to creating a sustainable waste management model for the sector, which accounts for 3% of Spain's GDP.

**Sweden**![](_page_168_Picture_82.jpeg)

**Status**Planned.

**Obligated producer**Companies based in Sweden placing textiles on the market in Sweden, and foreign companies that sell textiles to customers in Sweden.

**Exemption:** Producers that produce textile products from >80% textile waste (fiber-to-fiber).

**Products in scope**Undefined.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**Naturvårdsverket, TEKO.

**Other**From 1 October 2025, Sweden has made the decision to no longer require separate collection of textiles that are torn, stained, or otherwise unsuitable for reuse or recycling. Such items should instead be disposed of with residual household waste, a measure intended to reduce sorting costs and ease pressure on recycling systems following the EU's separate collection requirement. Targets for textile waste reduction include a 70% reduction by 2028, 80% by 2032, and 90% by 2036.

**United Kingdom**![](_page_168_Picture_95.jpeg)

**Status**Undefined.

**Obligated producer**Undefined.

**Products in scope**Undefined.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**WRAP, Chartered Institution of Waste Management; Circular Economy Taskforce; Valpak; Textile Recycling Association; British Retail Consortium; UK Fashion and Textile Association.

**Other**The UK's Waste/Resources Strategy (updated in 2023) stated that by the end of 2025, the UK Government will have reviewed and consulted on EPR measures for five new waste streams, including textiles, bulky waste (e.g., mattresses, furniture & carpets), construction & demolition materials, vehicle tyres, and fishing equipment. In 2023, the UK Government announced an expected delay, stating that progress first needs to be made implementing and assessing the new EPR system for packaging before EPR for other waste streams can be considered.

 Under development. Textiles were added as priority products under Chile's EPR Law in June 2025.

Any producer or importer of textiles into the Chilean market.

Clothing, household textiles, footwear, fabrics, and yarns.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**

In October 2025, the Circular Economy Strategy for Textiles by 2040 was officially launched. The strategy aims to prevent textile waste generation of waste, promote higher-quality textiles and boost reuse and recovery across the sector. EPR targets for textiles are expected by 2029.

**California**

![](_page_169_Picture_79.jpeg)

**Status**

Passed and signed in September 2024. Legal basis found in  SB-707 Responsible Textile Recovery Act of 2024.

**Obligated producer**

Any fashion company that makes or sells products in scope in the state of California.

**Products in scope**

Apparel, workwear, footwear, accessories (handbags and backpacks), home textiles.

The Act establishes stewardship programs for various products, including, among others, carpet, mattresses, and pharmaceutical and sharps waste.

**Key actors/known PRO(s)**

Undefined. In July 2025, industry associations signed an MoU to establish a PRO under California's EPR Law. The Department of Resources Recycling and Recovery's (CalRecycle) must approve the PRO by 1 March 2026, and apparel and textile producers must join by 1 July 2026. By 1 July 2030 or upon CalRecycle's approval, the upcoming PRO must submit a comprehensive plan detailing the collection, transportation, repair, sorting, recycling, and proper management of post-consumer apparel and textile articles in California.

**Other**

CalRecycle is tasked with adopting regulations to implement the program, with an effective date no earlier than July 1, 2028.

Starting 1 July 2030, or upon plan approval (whichever is earlier), producers must be in compliance with the program requirements. Non-compliant producers may face civil penalties up to \$10,000 per day, or up to \$50,000 per day for intentional or knowing violations.

- • Either individually or cooperatively submit a plan to the Department of Environmental Conservation (DEC) for the establishment of a collection & treatment program by 31 December 2024, for approval by the Department.
- • Implement and cover the costs of the collection program (which must be free of charge to users) by July 1 2025.
- • Cover the costs of scheme administration and enforcement by the DEC, and consumer communications.
- • Submit annual reports on the functioning collection & treatment systems.

# 4. TEXTILE WASTE

Textile waste constitutes a significant environmental concern, with the fashion industry being a major source of solid waste sent to landfills or incinerated. Textile waste and garments production and disposal have increased over the last decades, driven by prevailing consumer behaviour, price reductions and rising living standards. It is estimated that the textile industry produces a substantial amount of waste every year and currently most of the waste ends up as part of municipal solid waste. Therefore, textile waste reduction is one of the main goals linked to sustainable waste management, while its innovative management is important to further enhance sustainability and promote circularity.

In 2024, the world discarded enough clothing to fill more than 200 Olympic stadiums, highlighting a significant crisis of textile waste (R.Sajdeh et al., 2025). Every year, the fashion industry produces over 100–150 billion garments, using approximately 93 billion cubic meters of water and generating 1.2 billion tons of CO₂ emissions, while also releasing around 500,000 tons of microplastic fibers into the oceans. Globally, an estimated 92 million tons of textile waste accumulate throughout the various stages of the products' life cycle, with half of it generated after consumer use. Despite this, only 15–20% of discarded textiles are collected for reuse or recycling, while more than 80% end up in landfills or are incinerated (Shamsuzzaman et al., 2025).

Europeans throw away some 16 kilograms of textiles every year. Together, this accounts for 6.95 million tonnes for the whole of Europe. Of all discarded textiles, only 4.4 kilograms are selectively collected. The vast majority still ends up in residual waste and 85% of this waste is made up of clothes and domestic textile items (ΕΕΑ, 2024).

A summary of textile waste generation in European countries in 2020 is presented in Figure 40, regarding the amounts of textile waste collected separately from households, those collected for economic activities, and the household textile waste that ends up in mixed municipal waste. The data on the amounts of separately collected textile waste show substantial variation between countries, from as low as 1 kilogram per capita in Sweden, Finland, Hungary, Latvia, Greece and Ireland, to as high as 15.5 kilograms per capita in Belgium (EEA, 2024).

Impending legislation such as the WFD, that requires mandatory separate textile collection from households throughout Europe by the current year 2025, will drive the scaling of collection, sorting and recycling infrastructure across the EU. Inevitably, the mandatory collection of textile waste will result in increased collection of low-value, non-reusable textiles. It is also noted that from the total of 1.95 Mt of textile waste separately collected, only 38% collected for sorting, and 10% for reuse and recycling (Textile Exchange. Preferred Fibre & Materials, Market Report, 2020).

Last, 40% of this textile waste in Europe is reused or recycled, whereas the remaining 60% is exported to third countries (46% to Africa and 41% to Asia). The traceability is lost, with a large fraction still disposed of in these countries. These facts underscore the urgent need for a transformative shift (EEA, 2023).

![](_page_176_Figure_4.jpeg)

Figure 40. Textile waste generation in kilograms per capita in 2020. Source: ΕΕΑ, 2024.

The results from the separate collection of textile waste showed that the main source of textile waste is post-consumer in most countries, which refers to discarded clothing and household textiles after consumer use (clothes, towels, bedding etc.). On average, post-consumer waste accounts for 82% of all textile waste generated (household and non-household sources), followed by post-industrial and pre-consumer waste, accounting for 17% and 1%, respectively (EEA, 2024). The notable portion of post-consumer waste underscores the significant impact of consumer behaviour on textile waste generation, highlighting the need to encourage consumers to adopt sustainable textile reuse practices and reduce environmental impact.

Moreover, regarding the environmental impact, the clothing and textile sectors significantly contribute to plastic waste generation, as synthetic fibres are composed of polymers. More specifically, in 2020 textile consumption in the EU was responsible for a carbon footprint of 270 kg CO2 per capita (EEA, 2023), as Figure 41 depicts.

Global fibre production reached a record of 124 million tonnes in 2023, rising from 116 million tonnes in 2022. Since 2000, when production stood at 58 million tonnes, global output has more than doubled and is projected to increase to 160 million tonnes by 2030. Polyester has been the main driver of this expansion, overtaking cotton as the most widely used fibre worldwide in the mid to late 2000s. Synthetic fibres have dominated the market since the mid-1990s, accounting for about 84 million tonnes, or roughly 67%, of total fibre production in 2023. Polyester alone represented around 57% of global output, with production reaching approximately 71 million tonnes. The second most common synthetic fibre, polyamide (nylon), contributed 6.7 million tonnes, corresponding to about 5% of the global market. Plantbased fibres held a market share of around 25% in 2023, with total production reaching 31.4 million tonnes. Cotton remains the most significant natural fibre and the second most used fibre overall, with around 24.7 million tonnes produced in 2023, representing roughly 20% of global fibre production (Textile Exchange,2024a).

## THE ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT OF TEXTILES

## 4.1 Reuse practices in textile waste

Consumption of textiles has increased substantially during the past few decades. In this context, the innovative management of textile waste is important to enhance sustainability and circularity. Both reuse and recycling have environmental and economic benefits, such as saving energy, water, land and emissions, creating employment opportunities, supporting charities and reducing waste disposal volumes. The interest in increased textile reuse and recycling is consistent with the growing attention being given to the circular economy concept in international and national policy.

The need to recover fabric waste stems mainly from the viewpoint of resource recovery and the harmful effects of disposal and landfilling on the environment. Generally, the environmental benefits of textile reuse and recycling include the potential to reduce virgin textile fibre production and avoid processes further downstream in the textile product life cycle. Moreover, clothing reuse can significantly reduce textile waste in landfills, lower demand for raw materials, and thus support the transition towards a sustainable textile industry and a more circular fashion system. These practices help reduce environmental impact, conserve resources, and promote circular economy principles. However, reuse is widely recognised as more beneficial than recycling and includes various means for extending the useful service life of textile products from first owner to another (Lorena et al., 2017).

## 4.1.1 Textile waste converted into second-hand textile products

Reusing textile waste and converting it into second-hand textile products involves repurposing used or discarded fabrics into wearable or functional items, thereby extending their lifecycle. This is a key strategy within the circular economy model adopted by the EU and many other regions for promoting sustainable fashion and reducing environmental impact.

In general, the most common textile waste reuse practices are as follows:

 Collection and sorting Upcycling Donation and resale Clothing swaps and rental Recommerce and return / turn-back schemes

## Collection and sorting

Collection and sorting of textile waste is crucial for ensuring that textile reuse and conversion into second-hand products function efficiently. This system forms the foundation for the circular textile economy, allowing materials to be reused, upcycled, or recycled effectively.

There are multiple pathways to meeting the EU's circular economy goals, but the textile sector faces two key challenges. Enhancing sorting and recycling processes and decreasing overall consumption. The industry places significant focus on improving sorting and recycling, as the two are closely linked. High-quality collection and sorting are essential for efficient recycling, while progress in recycling technologies can make sorting more cost-effective (Valtere, M et al., 2025).

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them. Donating used clothes supports charities that distribute them to low-income families as well as disaster relief efforts or sell them to fund charitable activities. As for the environmental

Textile waste gathered mainly from post-consumer household sources and pre-consumer waste (industry leftovers) is collected through public collection bins (often run by municipalities or NGOs), retail take-back schemes (e.g., H&M, Zara, Levi's in-store collection) or municipal collection (Chen et al., 2021).

Next, items are sorted manually or automated based on the type, colour, size, fabric and quality. Quality assessment is an important step, which determines the reuse or refurbishment process. Textile grading describes quality categories, ranging from highquality items which can be directly resold in second-hand markets to low-quality (nonreusable) items which can be recycled or downcycled into new products.

## Upcycling

Another widely adopted reuse practice is upcycling, which is an innovative, creative, lowenergy, and socially inclusive method for transforming textile waste into new value-added products. It enhances the sustainability of the fashion industry by extending the life of materials and supporting local economies. However, its full potential relies on systemic support, better infrastructure, and consumer education.

Upcycling is a growing trend among fashion designers, helping to save resources and keep large quantities of textile waste out of the disposal pathway, while adding value to discarded items and encouraging creativity and sustainability (Ghosh and Subarna, 2025). Unlike recycling, upcycling retains much of the original fabric's structure, saving energy and preserving textile quality. The purpose of the upcycling process is to deconstruct textiles, plan new items based on available materials and reconstruct them, often using patchwork or collage-like methods. Examples include the upcycling of T-shirts into bags or wall arts, jeans into other types of denim products, curtains into dresses or tablecloths, etc.

Fibrelab is a company that takes waste post-consumer textiles and creates new products including upcycled garments, paper products e.g. tags and notebook covers, cushion inserts and bespoke design projects.

More and more brands or organizations are adopting the method and applying it as they seek solutions to the industry's environmental impact and offer socially and environmentally conscious choices to their customers. However, upcycled products depend on available waste materials and may face limitations due to the uniqueness of each item.

## Donation

Donation plays an important role by supporting social causes while supplying the secondhand market. However, charities are increasingly burdened by the volume and poor quality of donations. For textile donation systems to remain sustainable, they need to be paired with public awareness, sorting infrastructure, and policy alignment.

aspect, donating garments extends their lifespan, reducing the volume of textiles sent to landfills. Moreover, keeping clothes in circulation, donations decrease the demand for new clothing production, conserving water, energy, and raw materials.

Regarding clothing donation behaviour, studies indicate that consumers often do not associate giving clothes to charity with environmental benefits, but primarily for philanthropic reasons (Fenitra et al., 2021). Younger individuals, particularly university students, may be more inclined to donate due to heightened environmental awareness and exposure to sustainability education (Klucarova et al., 2022). The convenience of the donation channel is an important factor in determining where the clothes would be donated (Fenitra et al., 2021). Through the overall act of donation, consumers gain both hedonistic (i.e., good feeling) and utilitarian benefits (i.e., freeing up closet space) from donating clothes. However, despite positive intentions, several barriers may hinder clothing donation. These include the lack of community awareness regarding where or how to donate can prevent action. In this context, limited access to donation centres or inconvenient locations can deter individuals. Last, the time and effort required to sort and deliver items may discourage donations.

## Repair and refurbish

The repair and refurbish activities focus on extending the useful life of textile products. These actions recover the functionality and value of garments without transforming them into new materials, allowing them to be directly reintroduced into the use cycle. The process may include repairing damaged components, minor aesthetic improvements, or replacing specific elements, ensuring that garments retain their performance and quality. This approach represents a key reuse strategy, reducing waste generation and avoiding the need to produce new products, thereby saving resources and minimising environmental impact. (Ramesh et al 2022). Various factors influence the decision to repair a product, particularly emphasizing the interaction between emotions and personal, economic and social variables (Bettiga et al 2025).

## Clothing swaps and rental

Clothing swapping or rental is a growing movement among young consumers, especially Gen Z and Millennials, providing a cost-effective way to refresh wardrobes. They can acquire new-to-them clothing without spending significant amounts, making fashion more accessible (Armouch et al., 2024). This practice also challenges the fast-fashion model by promoting reuse over constant purchasing. Clothing swaps contribute to reducing textile waste by extending the life of garments. Each item swapped represents one less garment potentially ending up in landfills, thereby conserving resources like water and energy required in producing new clothing.

Across Europe, many initiatives promote clothing-swapping behaviour, such as events where individuals exchange garments, large-scale facilities and neighbourhood-based swap networks (Eco Styles, 2023). As for clothes rental, peer-to-peer and brand rental marketplaces operate, offering high-end and trendy fashion brands for rent, with various rental durations and services (Commons, 2024).

While clothing swaps offer numerous benefits, challenges such as ensuring quality control, managing logistics, and reaching broader audiences persist. Addressing these issues is crucial for the scalability and effectiveness of swapping initiatives.

## Recommerce and return-back schemes

Online marketplaces facilitate the buying and selling of pre-owned clothing, making it easier for consumers to participate in textile reuse. These platforms often provide user-friendly interfaces and wide reach, encouraging more people to engage in sustainable fashion practices (Practical Ecommerce, 2024). In return or take-back schemes, many brands collect used clothes for resale, recycling or repurposing. Another initiative is corporate uniform reuse programs where old uniforms are refurbished or repurposed.

## 4.1.2 Second-hand clothing global markets and consumers

Purchasing second-hand clothing online is growing rapidly, as second-hand shopping has become more popular and socially acceptable over the last few decades, due to economic and environmental drivers. Purchasing pre-owned garments can significantly reduce environmental impacts, including reduced carbon emissions and water usage, as the need for new garments declines.

The global second-hand market is experiencing significant growth, driven by increasing consumer demand for affordable, sustainable and unique fashion options. Garments in good condition are resold through thrift stores, flea markets, consignment shops, and online platforms like Vinted, ThredUp, Poshmark and Depop. This practice supports a circular economy by keeping clothing in use longer and reducing demand for new production. Moreover, second-hand culture is encouraged by regulatory measures as well as innovative retail strategies. Across Europe, various programs demonstrate the multifaceted approaches undertaken to promote textile reuse, including social initiatives, community-driven movements, and policy frameworks. For instance, the EU WFD requires that by the current year 2025 all EU Member States shall establish separate collection systems for used textiles. The directive stimulates various national initiatives to develop infrastructures for efficient textile waste management. In this context, the EU has proposed regulations holding producers accountable for the entire lifecycle of textile products, including financial contributions to support separate collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling, thereby encouraging sustainable practices within the textile industry.

Moreover, non-profit programs participate in reuse practices, such as RREUSE Network, which is an international non-profit network linking social enterprises active in reuse, repair, and recycling across Europe. RREUSE advocates for policies favouring end-of-life product reuse and repair, contributing significantly to the EU's circular economy. Major retailers are integrating second-hand offerings into their business models, reflecting the mainstreaming of pre-owned fashion. Last, fashion brands across the EU are increasingly adopting upcycling practices, showcasing the fusion of classic materials with modern aesthetics.

In the EU, textile reuse practices vary across member states, influenced by national policies and waste management infrastructures. Countries such as Luxembourg, Belgium, the Netherlands, and Austria have implemented effective collection systems, resulting in higher rates of textile reuse and recycling (OECD, 2024). A significant portion of Europeans participate in the second-hand market, with 87% reporting engagement in buying or selling used goods, considering a variety of motivations. The primary motivator is lower price when purchasing second-hand clothing across EU countries. Moreover, sustainability and

environmental impact is a key reason to buy second-hand clothes (between 40% and 50% of consumers in countries like France, Germany, Italy, and the UK cite eco-friendly concerns). Finally, the desire for distinctive and vintage items also drives consumers toward second-hand markets. Consequently, the second-hand market has expanded rapidly over recent decades. It is estimated that in 2025, the second-hand apparel global market will be valued at 264 billion dollars, while the projections for 2028 indicate growth to 350 billion dollars. Furthermore, the EU's second-hand apparel market is valued at approximately 17.3 billion dollars in 2024, while projections indicate that it will grow at a CAGR of 7.7%, reaching around 36.4 billion dollars by 2034 (Future Market Insights, 2024).

## Second-hand market in third-world countries

In many developing countries, the reuse of textiles plays a pivotal role in both economic and cultural processes, influenced by external waste streams and growing local initiatives. A significant volume of used clothing from Western nations is exported to developing countries, including Ghana, Vietnam, and Turkey. While this practice provides affordable apparel, it also poses environmental and economic challenges, especially in several African countries, primarily due to the massive flow of low-quality second-hand clothing and inadequate waste management infrastructure.

Africa's engagement with textile recycling and reuse practices is multifaceted, reflecting both the continent's rich traditions and contemporary challenges. While certain regions have embraced innovative recycling initiatives, others grapple with the environmental and economic impacts of imported second-hand clothing. The massive flow of imported second-hand clothes often overwhelms local waste management systems, resulting in improper disposal methods like open burning and unregulated landfilling, leading to environmental pollution and health issues. In Ghana, for instance, markets are inundated with low-quality garments, leading to substantial waste increase (My Joy Online, 2025).

It is also mentioned that only South Africa generates 680,000 tons of textile waste annually. Local designers and organizations are addressing this issue by upcycling discarded materials into new fashion items, promoting sustainability and economic activity. Last, the social and economic impacts of textile recycling and reuse are notable in these countries, especially the employment opportunities being created especially for women (Page and Truter, 2024). However, challenges persist, including inadequate infrastructure, limited access to advanced recycling technologies, and competition with inexpensive imported textiles. In response to these challenges, innovative recycling and upcycling initiatives are emerging, while NGOs, and the private sector establish sustainable waste management systems and support local recycling enterprises. Addressing the abovementioned, Africa's participation in textile waste management is multifaceted, combining community-driven initiatives, policy reforms, and international support to build sustainable and textile industries.

The second-hand apparel market in Africa is experiencing significant growth. In the Middle East and Africa, it is projected that the second-hand market will grow from 1.6 billion dollars (2023) to approximately 3.34 billion dollars (2032), reflecting a CAGR of 8.43% from 2024 to 2032 (Credence Research, 2024).

Textile waste conversion into second-hand products is a significant practice in many African countries, such as Ghana, Kenya, Nigeria, Tanzania, South Africa, and Uganda, where used clothes and other textile waste are repurposed to contribute to the local economy, provide affordable clothing, and reduce environmental impact. Second-hand clothing is preferred by most consumers because it is more affordable compared to locally made garments that are more expensive. A portion of the population favours thrifted clothes because they offer better variety than locally manufactured apparel. Then there is a small percentage that opts for thrifted clothes to promote environmental sustainability (Greenpeace, 2023). However, some argue that the quality of second-hand clothes has been gradually declining, with a part of the imports ending up in landfills either because
