they are too damaged or outdated to be resold (the unsellable garments are being disposed leading to environmental pollution). Still, there are organisations and reports saying the opposite and that a very low percentage ends up as waste<sup>19</sup> .

Thus, Africa's textile reuse landscape is evolving with innovative solutions. In response to the abovementioned, many organizations in Africa have pioneered in textile recycling and upcycling projects, in order to promote environmental sustainability. The action of various African countries in textile reuse is summarized in Table 13.

Regarding Asian countries, textile waste management presents a complex landscape, influenced by rapid industrialization, fast fashion trends, and varying levels of infrastructure and regulation. Some countries have initiated recycling programs and international collaborations, significant efforts are needed to improve recycling rates, implement effective policies, and mitigate environmental impacts. India is one of the largest importers and recyclers of second-hand clothing in Asia, while China is one of the world's largest textile producers, generating substantial amounts of both pre- and post-consumer textile waste amounting 26 Mt annually. However, recycling and upcycling are the most common methods in Asia, as presented in Table 13. Especially in India, there is the largest textile recycling hub, converting rejected items from around the world into recycled yarns and fabrics. Last, the Asian second-hand apparel market is projected to grow from 16.23 billion dollars in 2023 to approximately 47.39 billion dollars by 2032, reflecting a CAGR of 12.56% (Credence Research, 2024). Factors such as technological advancements, increased consumer awareness, and supportive policies are likely to further drive this expansion.

## 4.1.3 Traceability of exported garments

Improving transparency and traceability of garments has become a priority for the textile industry to more effectively manage value chains, identify and address labour and human rights violations as well as environmental impacts, combat counterfeits, and handle reputational risks, while embracing more sustainable production and consumption patterns.

Traceability and transparency of information along the value chain is key to helping circular business models close the loop. Traceability is important as it enables eco-design and resource efficiency, facilitates recycling and repairing, supports remanufacturing and waste management, and finally allows impact measurement of water, chemicals, energy, climate change mitigation and adaptation (UNECE, 2021).

The garments supply chain (from raw textile material to finished product) involves five networks that are distinct but interconnected, contributing to the traceability process (Textile Exchange and Fashion for Good, 2022):

- 1. Raw Material Network (Tier 4): This stage establishes the initial point of traceability, where documentation of material origin and environmental impact begins.
- 2. Components Network (Tier 3 and 2): Accurate traceability records at this stage ensure that the origin, processing techniques, and fibre types are well documented and compliant with regulatory or voluntary standards (e.g., GOTS, OEKO-TEX).
- 3. Production Network (Tier 1): Manufacturers transform fabric into finished garments using lead firms or contract manufacturers. This stage is critical for social compliance and ethical traceability, as it involves labour practices, workplace safety, and quality control systems.
- 4. Export Network: Trade firms facilitate the international movement and commercial distribution of garments. Maintaining traceability through this network ensures proper documentation of shipping, regulatory certifications, and chain-of-custody records
- 5. Marketing Network: The final step in the supply chain involves consumer-facing retail and distribution. At this point, traceability data may be presented directly to consumers via product labels, QR codes, or digital product passports, promoting informed purchasing decisions and brand transparency.

Each of the abovementioned networks in the value chain involves a series of industrial processes performed by different industry types and different companies and which affect the environment in different ways. Raw material production and product manufacturing are generally located in developing countries due to the availability of low-paid workers. On the other hand, most retailers and customers are in developed countries, which means that products (raw materials or final products) may travel large distances from raw material extraction to the final consumer. Moreover, the fabric itself is sometimes made up of different types of yarn, for example, cotton, wool, polyester, nylon, etc. This increases the number of companies involved in the creation of a piece of textile, leading to a more complex value chain. All these complexities make supply chain integration extremely difficult and result in an unclear network with limited information exchange between partners (Textile Today, 2023).

The key trends of garments traceability are as follows:

## 1. Regulatory Initiatives:

- EU Textile Strategy: The EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles published in 2022 ensures that all textile products placed on the EU market are durable, repairable, recyclable, and increasingly made of recycled fibres. Traceability is seen as a crucial enabler for achieving these goals (European Commission, 2022).
- ESPR: The EU proposed in 2022 the ESPR as part of the EU Circular Economy Action Plan, in order to introduce mandatory Digital Product Passports in textiles and garments. The adoption of the ESPR is expected to lead to more transparent, verified and responsible exports.
- Digital Product Passport (DPP): The EU's ESPR, which came into force in July 2024, introduces the Digital Product Passport for textiles. This digital record will provide comprehensive information about a product's environmental footprint, material composition, and end-of-life instructions throughout its lifecycle.
  - Each garment will have a unique identifier (e.g., a QR code) linking to the DPP.
  - The DPP aims to make it easier for consumers to make informed choices, and for recyclers to handle products at their end-of-life.
  - The legislative release period for the textile DPP is expected to conclude by 2030, with the first product groups potentially seeing implementation by 2026.
- Eco-design requirements: New eco-design requirements for textiles are expected to be finalized by mid-2025, which will likely include aspects related to traceability and information disclosure.
- Starting July 19, 2026, large companies must publicly report the quantity and weight of unsold consumer goods, along with the reasons for their destruction, on an annual basis. Medium-sized companies have until July 19, 2030, to comply with this requirement. Smaller companies are exempt but are encouraged to adopt sustainable practices.
- Waste Framework Directive: Amendments to this directive in 2023 aimed to foster a circular economy and make producers responsible for the full lifecycle of textile products, including textile waste management (Directorate-General for Environment, 2023).
- EU Green Deal and Due Diligence Law: Several EU countries already have or are implementing national laws on supply chain due diligence, requiring companies to identify and address human rights and environmental risks in their supply chains. The EU has also been working on a broader Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive.

2. Industry initiatives and collaborations: Various initiatives and collaborations are

- emerging to advance traceability in the European garment industry.
- European Fashion Alliance (EFA): This alliance focuses on driving sustainable transformation in the European fashion industry, with traceability and transparency being key priorities.

- Alliance for European Flax-Linen and Hemp: This organization offers certifications that guarantee the origin of the fibre and its traceability along the value chain. They are launching a digital traceability platform for certified European Flax-Linen in 2025.
- EUCOTTON Initiative: This initiative by the European Cotton Alliance focuses on the traceability of European-grown cotton, from farm to fashion, through a dedicated platform.
- UNECE: UNECE is actively promoting traceability and transparency in the garment and footwear sector, developing standards and tools to support these efforts (UNECE, 2022).
- Monitor for Circular Fashion: Monitor for Circular Fashion is a multi-stakeholder project involving leading companies in the fashion industry and players in its supply chain. SDA Bocconi's Sustainability Lab is the project's scientific partner. They intend to "enhance traceability and transparency for circularity, through the development of sustainability claims at b2b and b2c level". Monitor for Circular Fashion also takes part in some of the calls to action on sustainability and circularity at a global level, starting from the UNECE Project "Enhancing Transparency and Traceability of Sustainable Value Chains in the Garment and Footwear Sector".
- Fashion for Good: This organization promotes transparency and traceability as essential for a more sustainable industry, exploring technologies like additive tracers and blockchain.
- Aura Blockchain Consortium: While global, this consortium, with members including European luxury brands, focuses on using blockchain to enhance transparency and traceability in the fashion supply chain.
- Project digital label: Initiative for the development of a digital traceability system for fashion products, with digital tools, smart labels and QR codes.

## 3. Technological advancements:

○ Physical tracers: They are verified using specialized tools or laboratory analysis, embedded directly into the textile material at various stages of the supply chain.

- 1. Forensic tracers: Provide geographic verification of plant- and animalbased fibres.
  - DNA markers: can be used to verify the origin of raw materials.
  - Isotopic tracers: measures unique environmental isotope signatures to verify geographic origin.
- 2. Addictive tracers: Facilitate better end-to-end traceability, covering more fibre types.
  - Micro taggants: tiny, colour-coded particles mixed into textiles.
  - Synthetic / artificial DNA: use of DNA codes and molecular identifiers that can be applied to fibre or materials.
- Digital tracers: Various software solutions and platforms are being developed to manage and share traceability data.
  - 1. RFID / NFC tags: Allow for the tracking of individual items.
  - 2. QR codes and barcodes: allow consumers to scan for the detailed product history.

- 3. DPP: Structured traceability data.
- Blockchain-based tracers: Offers a secure and transparent ledger for tracking materials and products throughout the supply chain.
- GS1 Standards: The adoption of standards like the GTIN and GLN is seen as crucial for establishing unique product and location identities, facilitating better traceability.
- 4. Consumer demand: Growing consumer awareness of the social and environmental impact of fashion is driving demand for more transparent and traceable products. Consumers want to know where their clothes come from, how they were made, and their environmental footprint.

## The effectiveness of traceability

With rising global attention on sustainability and waste management, the traceability of exported garments has become essential for circular economy compliance and ethical trade. EU customs authorities play a critical role in ensuring exported garments are reusable, legally traded, and properly documented. Moreover, the global second-hand clothing trade is growing rapidly, driven by overproduction and fast fashion. However, this trade often lacks transparency, leading to illegal waste exports, unsustainable practices, and labour exploitation. For customs authorities, enforcing traceability regulations is crucial for distinguishing legitimate reuse exports from disguised waste dumping.

The benefits of garments' traceability are notable for customs authorities. First, the identification of true origin, quality, and ownership of garments is strengthened, along with the assurance of compliance with the EU Waste Framework Directive and Basel Convention. Furthermore, traceability helps to track EPR obligations for exported textiles and enables real-time monitoring of cross-border flows. Finally, it supports the enforcement of Circular Economy Action Plan objectives. However, many challenges are being faced, such as customs lacking access to DPP data that trace a garment's lifecycle, the variation in quality and availability of shipping records across Member States (non-standard documentation) and the untracked second-hand market, where once garments are exported, they often vanish into informal resale markets.

In order to address the abovementioned challenges and barriers, and enhance the effectiveness of traceability, customs authorities must improve their control over used textile imports and exports. Considering this, they must focus on improving information systems, training, and collaboration, integrate customs systems with partners, utilize electronic documentation, and implement risk-based inspections. Moreover, they must strengthen training programs, streamline documentation processes, and build strong relationships with stakeholders including authorities and clients (International Monetary Fund, 2022):

## 1. Enhance information systems and technology

- Integrate systems: Connect customs' information systems with partners, including neighbouring countries, to track transit cargo and facilitate data crosschecking.
- Electronic documentation: Embrace electronic submission and processing of customs information to streamline processes and reduce errors.

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

- Digital technologies: Explore the use of digital technologies to monitor the supply chain beyond customs territories, from origin to destination.

## 2. Improve training and procedures

- Targeted training: Develop and refine training materials for customs officers, focusing on identifying and addressing potential issues related to used textile imports and exports.
- Documentation: Create checklists and standardize documentation processes to minimize errors and ensure compliance.
- Risk-based inspections: Implement risk assessment strategies to prioritize inspections and focus resources on high-risk shipments.

## 3. Foster collaboration and communication

- Stakeholder engagement: Build strong relationships with customs brokers, clients, and other relevant stakeholders to facilitate smooth operations.
- Clear communication: Maintain open communication with clients regarding potential delays, issues, and solutions related to textile shipments.
- Supply chain collaboration: Work with logistics partners to ensure alignment with export procedures and address potential challenges.

## 4. Address specific issues related to used textiles

- Traceability: Enhance traceability of used textiles, including potentially using blockchain technology.
- Compliance: Ensure shipments comply with regulations regarding textile labelling, fibre composition, and other relevant standards.
- Circular economy: Promote circular economy principles by encouraging the reuse, repair, and recycling of textiles.
- Continuous Monitoring and Improvement
- Audits: Regularly audit export operations to identify areas for improvement.
- Technology: Utilize technology to track shipments, monitor compliance, and automate documentation.
- Regulatory updates: Stay informed about and adapt to any changes in textile regulations and standards.

The DPP can be a powerful tool for extending the life of garments by making relevant product data available throughout the entire lifecycle, especially during the post-consumer phase. To make it effective, it must deliver interoperable and durable data in a way that fits the practical realities of collection, sorting, resale, and recycling. More specifically, the DPP facilitates traceability by capturing and storing data related to the garment's journey, from raw materials to the final product, including details about its manufacturing processes, the origin of materials, and the use of chemicals and dyes and by making this information accessible through a digital platform. It allows consumers, retailers, and other stakeholders to verify the authenticity and sustainability of the garment. Moreover, DPPs streamline supply chain management by providing a centralized source of information, enabling better tracking of products, identifying potential bottlenecks, and improving overall efficiency. Also, by providing information about the product's composition, recyclability, and end-oflife options, DPPs support circular economy initiatives, promoting reuse, refurbishment, and recycling. DPPs often utilize unique product identifiers, such as GTINs, to ensure accurate tracking and traceability across different systems and throughout the product's lifecycle.

In order for the DPP to be effective in second-hand clothes traceability, the key information for the post-consumer phase includes care instructions, repair options, and disposal guidelines, all accessible through a unique identifier, such as a QR code. To ensure this information remains accessible and compatible with handling processes, it needs to be standardized, easily scannable and equipped with durable identifiers (permanent QR, RFIDs), and stored in a secure, accessible cloud-based system.

The DPP must encompass the following information (Directorate-General for Environment, 2023):

- Material Data Fibre composition (e.g. 65% polyester, 35% cotton), dye type, chemical treatments, recyclability
- Manufacturing Information Date & place of manufacture, stitching method, care instructions, supplier ID
- Durability Metrics Wash cycles tested, tensile strength, abrasion resistance
- Repairability Information Modular parts (zippers, buttons), stitching type, original spare part availability
- Resale Data Brand, original price, SKU, fashion season, images, authenticity verification
- End-of-Life Recycling pathway suggestions, compatibility with known sorting/recycling systems (e.g. Fibresort), disposal guidance
- Usage Data (optional) Wear history, number of washes, ownership history (if privacy allows).

## Traceability in the textile sector globally

With the rise of global supply chains and concerns over ethical labour, environmental impact, and circularity, the EU and its member states are taking decisive steps to enforce greater transparency in the textile sector. The garment's traceability readiness of the EU countries can be measured using a scale from 1 (low) to 5 (high), as Figure 42 illustrates, with the overall EU traceability readiness score equal to 5. Beginning with France, Germany, Sweden and Denmark, that rank among the top European countries in traceability readiness (5.0), driven by strict laws and high consumer expectations. Specifically, all products in France must be labelled with manufacturing and production stages and geographical details. Next, Germany is primarily driven by strong human rights and environmental due diligence law, which mandates that companies ensure respect for human rights and environmental standards throughout their global supply chains. On the other hand, countries like the Netherlands and Spain, are characterized by strong environmental regulations and EPR for textiles laws (traceability readiness scale 3.5). Unlike France and Germany, Italy has no standalone mandatory traceability laws for garments (traceability readiness scale 3.0). Last, Romania, Croatia and Bulgaria are characterized by no national traceability law for garments and insufficient digitalization of the textile sector (traceability readiness score 1.0).

![](_page_200_Figure_4.jpeg)

Figure 42. Garment Traceability Readiness Scores across EU Member States in 2025. Source: Directorate-General for Environment, 2023; European Commission 2024

What follows is real-world examples of traceability systems implementation across different regions, each tackling the challenge of transparency and accountability in textile reuse and export:

## The QR Foundation – Ghana (Dead White Man's Clothes, 2025)

- Project: Dead White Man's Clothes & Open Apparel Registry for Kantamanto
- Focus: Tracing second-hand clothes imports, resale patterns, and environmental burden.
- Key Features:
  - Mapping exporters and shipping routes from Global North.
  - Surveys and interviews with Kayayei (head porters), resellers, and waste pickers.
  - Estimating % of second-hand clothes imports that become immediate waste (est. 40%).
  - Advocating for EPR contributions from EU/UK.

## Reverse Resources – Bangladesh, Sri Lanka, globally (Reverse Resources, 2025) ● Project: Textile Waste Traceability & Circular Supply Matching

- Focus: post-industrial waste, expanding into post-consumer and second-hand clothes.
- Key features:
  - Digital platform matches waste suppliers (including SHC) to recyclers.
  - Tracks fibre type, condition, and quantities across nodes.
  - Now integrated into several brand EPR and circularity pilots (e.g. H&M).
  - Can be adapted to trace SHC flows from sorting to recyclers or reuse markets in Global South.

## Fibresort – Netherlands (Fibresort, 2025)

- Project: Automated Sorting + Traceability System
- Focus: Mechanically sorts post-consumer textiles by fibre composition.
- Key Features:
  - Uses near-infrared (NIR) technology to scan garments.
  - Digitally logs each sorted category (e.g., 100% cotton, polyester blends).
  - Can integrate with DPP systems to track SHC batches by material.
  - When used at SHC sorting hubs, can help link material info to final reuse or recycling path.

## Trace4Value – DPP for second-hand clothes – Sweden and EU (Trace4Value, 2025)

- Focus: Piloting Digital Product Passports for garments, including second-hand.
- Key Features:
  - QR codes on garments linking to supply chain data, care, resale, and recyclability.
  - Public-private platform involving retailers, recyclers, and sorters.
  - Designed for scalability across EU traceability schemes.
  - Makes second-hand clothing traceable at product level even after multiple use cycles.

## EON Circular ID (EON, 2025)

- Focus: Assigning persistent digital identity to garments
- Key Features:
  - Digital identity (QR/NFC) linked to product metadata: fibre, washing, resale, repair.
  - Used by brands to facilitate resale and take-back programs.
  - Ensures data continuity through reuse cycles, enabling traceability even for exported second-hand items.

# 5. TEXTILE CIRCULARITY

Fast fashion concepts in the textile industry have significantly contributed to the overconsumption of clothing and overproduction of waste. Meanwhile, public awareness of the environmental pollution associated with textile production and disposal has increased substantially. For these reasons, greener and cleaner solutions are constantly being sought to mitigate the negative environmental impact of textile waste in a sustainable manner.

Circularity refers to the adoption of sustainable methods that promote a closed-loop system by reducing waste generation and maximizing the value of resources. In other words, it is about keeping materials and resources in use for as long as possible (Schumacher et al., 2022). Relevant key principles are described below:

Typically, circularity in the textile industry is measured by looking at materials flowing into the economy. It is measured by analysing what materials flow through the global value chain, and how. This gives value chain actors an understanding of the current state, providing a jumping-off point for informed decision-making. Boosting circularity has two objectives, which can be achieved through four key strategies that are presented below.

![](_page_204_Diagram_5.jpeg)

Figure 44. Objectives and key strategies to achieve textile industry circularity. Source: Circle Economy, 2024.

## 5.1 Circularity in the textile industry globally

The textile industry stands as one of the leading manufacturing sectors globally, contributing to the production of approximately 100 billion clothing items annually (Bour et al., 2023). Every year, over 92 Mt of clothing waste is generated, with only 14% undergoing recycling while the rest is deposited in landfills. It is estimated that by 2030, this amount will be increased to 134 Mt (John et al., 2023). Additionally, the textile industry contributes nearly 10% of global carbon emissions, recognized as a leading driver of global warming. Moreover, textiles take a long time to disintegrate and produce greenhouse gases (GHG) as they do so, and disposing of textile waste in landfills worsens the environment. Finally, the societal effects of the textile industries cannot be disregarded.

The global textile industry is 0.3% circular: of the 3.25 billion tons of materials it consumes each year, over 99% come from pure sources (Circle Economy, 2024). Despite increasing sustainability efforts, the industry remains heavily dependent on virgin materials. Synthetic fibres like polyester, derived from fossil fuels, make up 63% of the raw materials used in textile production. At the same time, garment quality is being decreased, as brands are opting for synthetic materials and focusing on maximizing production volumes. This clearly contrasts with the principles of circularity, which aims to keep materials in use at their highest value for as long as possible. The above highlight the urgent need for sustainable practices and a shift towards circularity in the textile industry.

Asia is the largest textile and garment producer in the world, including key countries like China, India, Bangladesh, Vietnam, Pakistan, and Indonesia. Circular methods are being driven by a mix of export market demand, corporate sustainability commitments, and governmental regulation (especially in China and India). More specifically, post-industrial recycling is common in China, India, and Bangladesh, where waste from garment factories is recycled into bags, cloths or insulating materials. Chemical recycling is emerging in China and Japan for synthetic materials. Moreover, textile-to-textile recycling<sup>20</sup> is still limited. Last, natural, biodegradable fibres (e.g., cotton, hemp, banana fibre, jute) are widely used, while India and China are major producers of organic cotton. Furthermore, agro-waste fibres such as banana fibre, coconut coir and water hyacinth are being used in India and Indonesia.

In Africa, upcycling is common in urban hubs like Lagos and Nairobi, while Kenya and South Africa are leading in textile waste-to-value startups. Natural fibres are widely used in many regions, such as organic cotton, jute and hemp, as local brands explore low-impact textile production using natural dyes, plant-based fibres, and handcrafting techniques.

<sup>20</sup> e.g., Ambercycle's Cycora process transforms polyester-rich textile waste into yarns indistinguishable from virgin polyester. Circ uses hydrothermal technology to separate cotton and polyester from mixed fabrics, recovering both at high quality. Industrial-scale chemical recycling from Eastman and Loop can break down even contaminated polyester textiles into pristine feedstock (virgin-quality material). Unifi's flagship brand, REPREVE, is focused on turning post-consumer textile waste into performance recycled polyester yarns for global apparel companies.

## 5.2 Circularity in the EU textile industry

Europe is at the forefront of driving circularity in the textile industry, thanks to progressive policies, strong public awareness, and investments in innovation. The EU aims to make textiles repairable, recyclable, and sustainable by design.

The current management of textile waste is considered suboptimal with respect of the waste management hierarchy, as landfilling and incineration are the predominant treatment methods.

In 2022, the European Commission presented the "Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles" with the main goal that by 2030, all textile products on the EU market should be recyclable, made as much as possible from recycled fibres, free of hazardous substances and produced in an environmentally and socially sustainable manner (European Commission, 2022). More specifically, key objectives include:

⮚ Designing textiles for durability and recyclability: ensuring that products are longlasting and can be easily recycled. ⮚ Reducing the environmental footprint: minimizing the use of hazardous substances and promoting sustainable production processes. ⮚ Promoting circular business models: encouraging practices such as reuse, repair, and recycling. ⮚ Implementing EPR: making producers responsible for the entire lifecycle of their products, including end-of-life management.

Today, in the EU countries, 35–40% of discarded textiles are collected, while less than 1% are recycled into new textiles. According to Eurostat, the countries with the lowest circularity rates are Romania (1.3%) and Ireland (1.6%), followed by Portugal and Bulgaria with a rate of 2.3%. The EU average is 12.5%, and the countries that have taken effective measures to improve the circular use of materials are Belgium with the maximum European rate of 23.5% and France with 20% (Nistorac and Loghin, 2023). An overview of circularity status in the European textile industry is presented in the next Table.

In 2022, the average capture rate for textiles and shoes waste from households in EU27 was just under 15%, which means that most of the textiles and shoes waste are not separately collected and end up in the mixed household waste (EEA, 2025).

Between 2005 and 2023, the export of used textiles from EU countries more than doubled to around 1.37 million tonnes in 2023 (EEA, 2025).

An analysis of the most popular clothing items sold online in May 2024 on five different Zalando web shops throughout Europe, shows that on average 8% of these items contain recycled content (EEA, 2025).

The European Environment Agency, after analysing 14 metrics on textiles in its Circular Metrics Lab, has made the following observations:

 Increased circularity of textiles can reduce negative impacts on the environment, climate and human health. Enhancing circularity in the EU textile value chain by extending product lifespans through longer use, increased reuse, repair and more efficient recycling, can reduce the demand for new raw materials. The COVID-19 pandemic temporarily reduced pressures across all measured categories, but these pressures rose again post-pandemic. This highlights the importance of addressing overconsumption and fast fashion. It also underscores the need to promote greater textile circularity. Effective separate collection systems are needed to divert used and waste textiles from incineration, landfill and exports. Europe needs to strengthen its sorting and treatment capacities. This will help limit the amount of textile waste sent to landfills or incineration in Europe and elsewhere. It is also vital to ensure that the newly established European collection systems are efficient. Data, information and knowledge gaps limit effective monitoring and analysis of the circular economy of textiles in the EU. Reliable and publicly accessible information, knowledge, indicators and data are essential for measuring the circularity of textiles in Europe and also for supporting informed decisionmaking. The EU strategy for sustainable and circular textiles prioritises textile reuse and recycling. In a fully circular economy, none of the separately collected textiles should go to incineration or landfill. Instead, non-reusable textiles should be directed to recycling.

# 6. MODERN TRENDS AND DRIVERS

## 6.1 Across the global and European textile sectors

The global textile industry is undergoing significant transformations, influenced by technological advancements, sustainability initiatives, and evolving consumer preferences. What follows is an overview of modern trends and drivers shaping both the global and European textile sectors, underscoring a global shift towards sustainability, technological innovation and strategic realignment in the industry.

## Sustainability and eco-friendly practices

There is a high emphasis on reducing environmental footprints using recycled fabrics, energy-efficient manufacturing, and certifications, such as the Global Recycled Standard (GRS) and OEKO-TEX. These certifications ensure ethical sourcing and minimal environmental impact. Also, consumers are increasingly demanding sustainable and ethical textile products.

## Eco-friendly materials

As the demands of environmentally conscious consumers grow, the industry is witnessing an accelerated shift toward eco-friendly materials. Sustainable textiles are becoming more prevalent due to advancements in the sourcing and production of natural fibers, such as organic cotton, hemp, and bamboo. These materials offer the dual benefits of reducing the environmental footprint and providing biodegradability. Moreover, manufacturers are exploring bio-based polymers that mimic traditional fabric properties while being compostable, therefore addressing concerns regarding microplastic pollution.

## Regulatory measures

The EU has introduced regulations for the industry and the circular economy, as well as regulations requiring textile actors to fund waste management, including collection, sorting, and recycling. For instance, the new Product Environmental Footprint Category Rules (PEFCR) for apparel and footwear introduce a science-based and impartial method to assess the environmental impact of clothing and shoes across their entire lifecycle, from raw material extraction, production and logistics to use and end-of-life.

## Automation and advanced manufacturing

Manufacturers are increasingly integrating automation technologies, such as robotics and intelligent fabric handling systems, to enhance precision and efficiency and reduce labour costs. They foresee to enhance production efficiency, drive cost reductions, reduce defects, and ensure sustainability, improve quality, and optimize the production process yielding productivity.

## Recycling technologies innovations

Traditional recycling methods are being enhanced to increase efficiency and output. Chemical recycling which breaks down textiles to their basic molecules for reuse, is gaining traction, allowing high-quality fibers to be regenerated repeatedly without degrading their properties. Mechanical processes are also seeing improvements, with advancements in

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

fiber sorting and separation that allow for mixed-material garments to be recycled more effectively.

## Digital transformation and AI integration

The adoption of AI, blockchain, IoT, big data analytics and other relevant digital technologies is revolutionizing design processes and supply chain optimization, leading to enhanced efficiency, traceability, customer experience and reduced waste.

## Adoption of next-generation materials

New materials are under the spotlight and brands are partnering with innovators to incorporate sustainable ones, such as recycled textiles and bio-based fibres into their products aligning with environmental goals.

## Smart textiles

The integration of technology into fabrics has led to the development of smart textiles with applications in healthcare, sports, fashion and other sectors, offering functionalities such as health monitoring and adaptive responses to environmental changes.

## Circular economy initiatives

The EU's Green Deal and Circular Economy Action Plan for example, are driving brands to adopt eco-friendly materials, recycling programs, and sustainable production processes. Brands are increasingly adopting circular economy models, focusing on recycling and repurposing textiles to minimize waste and promote sustainability. The shift toward recycling and reusing textiles to reduce waste and improve sustainability is gaining momentum.

## Nearshoring and localization

In response to global supply chain disruptions, companies are moving production closer to end markets to reduce shipping costs, ensure faster delivery and enhance supply chain transparency. This is aligned with sustainability goals by minimizing the carbon footprint associated with long-distance shipping.

## Trade policies

Recent tariffs and trade tensions have prompted textile companies to reassess their sourcing and manufacturing strategies, influencing decisions on sustainability and supply chain configurations.

## Standardized reporting

The proliferation of certifications, standards, and regulations has led to complexities in supply chain reporting. The G7 Agenda on Circular Textiles and Fashion (G7 ACT) aims to promote greater circularity in the sector and has tasked the United Nations Economic Commission for Europe (UNECE) with standardizing requirements for brands and suppliers. This initiative seeks to simplify the reporting ecosystem, making it more efficient and less burdensome for suppliers.

## Consumer demand for transparency

Modern consumers are increasingly seeking information about the origins and

manufacturing processes of textile products. Transparency in sourcing, labour practices, and environmental impact has become a key factor in purchasing decisions. Brands that provide clear and honest communication about their processes are gaining consumer trust and loyalty.

## Direct-to-consumer models

Brands are increasingly leveraging online platforms to sell directly to consumers, offering personalized products and enhancing customer engagement while bypassing traditional retail channels.

## Reskilling and upskilling personnel

The growing complexity of technologies requires retraining workers to address skills shortages, improve quality, and enhance productivity. Mastering a wide range of new software products, solutions and production methods is impossible without systematic retraining and skill enhancement for employees.

Adoption of novel sustainable fibres in the European textiles sector The adoption of novel sustainable fibres is growing but remains partial and uneven. Policy push (EU textiles strategy, EPR rules), consumer demand and supply-side innovation are accelerating uptake, while cost, supply constraints, technical performance and insufficient recycling infrastructure limit large-scale replacement of conventional fibres (DG ENV). Regenerated cellulosics and recycled polyester are recognised as commercial winners. Adoption is highest among mid-to-large brands and technical textiles where performance and traceability matter. Regarding novel bio-materials and mycelium/seaweed fibres, commercial pilots and designer collections exist, but volumes are small and often niche. Circular-technology startups address feedstock and processing challenges but have not yet displaced commodity fibres at scale (UNEP). The EU's textiles strategy and circularity agenda are raising minimum standards and creating economic incentives to use recyclable/sustainable inputs. This creates near-term market pull for fibres that enable circular business models. Large retailers and brands increasingly demand recycled/certified inputs for risk management and brand value and procurement scale helps early movers secure supply. Improvements in solvent spinning, chemical recycling and sorting technologies are reducing environmental trade-offs and improving fibre performance. On the other hand, novel sustainable fibres typically carry cost premiums and limited production capacity vs. polyester/cotton. Some bio-based/niche fibres lack long-term durability data or are incompatible with existing processing lines (dyeing, finishing), increasing operational friction. Low textile collection/recycling rates and weak sorting technology constrain feedstock quality for recycled fibres, keeping most textiles out of circular loops. Multiple sustainability claims and inconsistent standards raise verification Market experts (IMARC) expect steady growth in regenerative cellulosics and recycled polyester market share, rising regulatory pressure to use recyclable inputs and continued niche growth of bio-materials. Full commodity displacement of cotton/polyester is unlikely soon without major cost declines and system investments, but policy and industry coordination can shift the sector decisively toward circular fibre mixes.

### Eco-labelling initiatives in the European textiles sector

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

Eco-labelling initiatives play a crucial role in driving the sustainability transition of Europe's textile industry. They provide transparent information about environmental and social impacts, support green consumer choices, and incentivise producers to improve supply chain practices. Europe leads globally in textile eco-labelling, with several schemes integrated into EU sustainability policy frameworks (EU Ecolabel, OEKO-TEX Standard 100 / MADE IN GREEN, Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS), Bluesign and Nordic

## New manufacturing and recycling technologies of textiles

New manufacturing and recycling technologies are reshaping fibre production, fabric processing and end-of-life management. Digital knitting, weaving, and 3D printing enable on-demand, zero-waste garment creation and localised microfactories, cutting material waste by up to 30%. Waterless and low-chemical dyeing reduce water use and effluent pollution, meeting stricter EU REACH and wastewater directives. AI and digital twins support predictive design and process optimisation, minimising overproduction and improving energy efficiency. Advances in biomanufacturing produce fibres from renewable sources (cellulose, algae, agricultural waste, mycelium). Improved fibre-sorting technologies using NIR (Near-Infrared) scanning and AI vision systems enhance feedstock purity, improving fibre quality in recyclates. Chemical recycling is emerging as a breakthrough technology for mixed or blended textiles. Polyester depolymerisation enables monomer recovery for virgin-quality fibres. Cellulose regeneration converts cotton waste into new cellulosic fibres suitable for large-scale fibre spinning.

## 6.2 Trends adopted by companies

The modern trends and drivers in the global and European textile industry are also important with respect to sustainability trends being adopted by companies across the value chain. These trends highlight an effort to embrace sustainability, innovate with new materials and technologies and adopt eco-friendly practices.

## Adoption of novel sustainable fibres

 Econyl: Developed by Aquafil, Econyl for instance is a regenerated nylon made entirely from waste materials such as industrial discards, fabric scraps, old carpets, and fishing nets. This innovative fibre has been embraced by brands like Stella McCartney, Adidas, and Levi's, offering a sustainable alternative to traditional nylon. Spinnova: This Finnish company has developed a patented technology to produce textile fibre from wood pulp and waste without harmful chemicals. The process is energy-efficient and results in fibres that are recyclable and biodegradable. Mycelium-based textiles: Utilizing the root structure of fungi, mycelium-based textiles are emerging as sustainable alternatives to leather. These materials are biodegradable and can be engineered for desired properties like flexibility and durability.

### USA

Aquafil has won an important share of the textile market thanks to Econyl. Econyl regenerated

nylon is a sustainable solution. Econyl yarn offers the same quality and performance as traditional Nylon 6, but with outstanding environmental benefits, as it is produced from nylon waste and is 100% regenerated.

### Eco-labelling initiatives

 OEKO-TEX Standard 100: This certification ensures that textiles are tested for harmful substances, promoting consumer safety and environmental responsibility. The OEKO-TEX Association has expanded its certifications to include sustainable textile production and organic cotton standards. Made in Green by OEKO-TEX: This label certifies that a textile product has been tested for harmful substances and manufactured using sustainable processes under socially responsible working conditions.

## Environmental footprint of polyester

 Many brands are exploring partnerships with startups to test new ways to reduce the environmental footprint of polyester production by replacing virgin fossil fuel-based inputs with recycled fibres sourced from post-consumer textiles. H&M has invested in Syre, a textile-to-textile polyester recycling startup and Syre has announced a strategic partnership with Gap, Houdini Sportswear and Target to accelerate the adoption of circular polyester in the fashion sector. Simplifyber is a startup that creates fabrics from fibres such as wood pulp or recycled fabrics. Because these can be 3D moulded into shape like plastics they can be used to make items such as trainers with no cutting, sewing or wastage. Suzano, the Brazilian wood pulp producer invested in Simplifyber to replace fossil materials with a new biobased material that contains eucalyptus fibres. Another approach is to improve the process of recycling polyester so that more of it can be recovered. Toyota Ventures invested in recycled textiles startup Tereform. Tereform is able to chemically process mixed textiles with minimal prior separation of the materials into individual fabric types, or removal of coatings, dyes and other contaminants, removing a highly labourintensive step. Tereform's solution is especially effective for recycling some of the more challenging textile substrates, such as polyester/spandex blends.

## Sweden

Syre Impact AB is a textile impact company on a mission to decarbonize and dewaste the textile industry through textile-to-textile recycling at hyperscale, starting with polyester. Syre aims at driving the green shift of the textile industry to a future where every textile fibre sees a new day. The company's textile-to-textile recycling solution provides circular polyester with quality on par with virgin polyester, but with a superior sustainability performance. Founded in 2023, Syre is initiated by Vargas and H&M Group.

### AI-powered sorting & automated collection

 AI is accelerating textile sorting. HKRITA the Hong Kong Research Institute of Textiles and Apparel Limited, Refibred, NewRetex A/S, and Valvan use hyperspectral imaging, machine learning, and automation to classify fibres, detect contaminants, and refine material recognition. These innovations improve recycling accuracy, making large-scale

textile circularity possible.

### Belgium

Valvan is a leading provider of automated sorting and baling systems designed for the secondhand textile market and textile recyclers. Their cutting-edge solutions integrate machine vision, and AI-driven smart technology to optimize sorting efficiency and maximize material recovery. Whether enhancing a specific step in the sorting process, expanding a machine park, or implementing a fully customized turnkey installation, Valvan delivers future-proof technology to help businesses scale sustainably. Headquartered in Belgium, Valvan operates worldwide, transforming textile processing through intelligent automation.

## New manufacturing and recycling technologies

 Textile recycling innovations: Companies are investing in technologies to recycle textile waste into new fibres. For example, Infinited Fibre Company produces a fibre similar to cotton from textile waste and Recover Textile Systems focuses on mechanically recycled cotton. Chemical recycling: Innovative processes are being developed to break down textile waste into raw materials chemically, allowing for the creation of new fibres without degrading quality.

![Logo of the Nintendo Firm featuring a circular emblem with the words 'NINTENDO FIRM' in a circular pattern.]()

## Finland

Infinna, by Infinited Fibre Company, is a virgin-quality circular fiber made 100% from cottonrich textile waste, such as worn-out clothes, otherwise destined to landfills or incinerators. Infinna has a natural and soft look and feel of cotton, and it can be used either on its own for 100% recycled garments or blended with other fibers, such as organic cotton or viscose.

## Consumer demand and market shifts

 Transparency and traceability: Consumers are increasingly seeking information about the origins and manufacturing processes of textile products, driving brands to adopt transparent and traceable supply chains. Second-hand and rental markets: The popularity of second-hand clothing and fashion rental services is growing, reflecting a shift towards sustainable consumption patterns.

## Expansion of resale and repair programs

 Take-back programs are evolving beyond collection. Resale and repair extend product lifecycles, reduce waste, and embed circularity into business models. EILEEN FISHER, INC (Renew), Zalando (Pre-Owned), and The North Face (Remade) lead this transformation. Startups like LOSANJE upcycle garments into new collections, while Reskinned accepts clothing from any brand for resale or recycling. Odlo's ReWEAR initiative refurbishes used apparel collected in stores. Patagonia offers a product repair service, along with instructional videos. Similarly, Levi's Tailor Shops allow customers to refresh denim pieces with patches and precision tailoring. Mulberry's

restoration centre aims to lengthen usability of their heritage accessories.

## Online sales and social media

 Online sales of clothing, home and professional textiles are a significant and rapidly rising share of the total market. Omnichannel (both physical and digital channel) capacities are becoming the norm, which allow the customer to engage with the product both on and offline and where the experience of touching and trying the product can be separated from the process of purchasing and delivering it. Social media platforms and mobile applications are further important tools to reach and engage with the customer, to collect feedback on the user experience and to create customer loyalty.

# 7. EU LEGISLATION

The Green Deal is the European Union's overarching policy framework to tackle climate change and drive sustainability across all sectors. It sets out an ambitious roadmap of 35 key actions aimed at fundamentally transforming the EU economy and society, with the ultimate goal of achieving climate neutrality by 2050.

One of the key initiatives under the Green Deal is the Circular Economy Action Plan (CEAP). This plan aims to accelerate the EU's transition to a circular economy by introducing measures to promote sustainable product design, extend product lifecycles, and improve waste management across various sectors.

To translate these ambitions into actions within the textile sector, the European Commission introduced the EU Strategy for sustainable and circular textiles. This strategy outlines targeted measures that address environmental and social impacts across the entire textile value chain, from production and consumption to reuse, recycling, and final disposal. It calls for textiles to become longer-lasting, easier to recycle, and more transparent in terms of materials and environmental footprint, ultimately supporting a more circular and resource-efficient economy.

Indeed, the strategy aims to support the development of a greener, more competitive, and resilient textile sector that can better withstand global disruptions. The vision for 2030 is that: all textile products placed on the EU market should be durable, repairable, and recyclable, largely made from recycled fibres, free from hazardous substances, and produced in ways that respect both environmental and social standards.

## The Commission's 2030 Vision for Textiles

![](_page_218_Picture_10.jpeg)

The strategy also calls for a shift in consumer behaviour, moving away from fast fashion and toward longer-lasting, high-quality and affordable textiles. To support this, reuse and repair services should become widely accessible and economically viable. At the same time, the sector is expected to become more innovative and responsible, with producers

taking ownership of their products throughout the value chain, supported by strong recycling infrastructure and a significant reduction in incineration and landfilling.

The strategy lays out a forward-looking set of actions:

- Set design requirements for textiles to make them last longer, easier to repair and recycle, as well as rules on minimum recycled content.
- Introduce clearer information and a Digital Product Passport.
- Reverse overproduction and overconsumption and discourage the destruction of unsold or returned textiles.
- Address the unintentional release of microplastics from synthetic textiles.
- Tackle greenwashing to empower consumers and raise awareness about sustainable fashion.
- Introduce mandatory and harmonised EPR rules for textiles in all member states and incentivise producers to design products that are more sustainable.
- Restrict the export of textile waste and promote sustainable textiles globally.
- Incentivise circular business models, including reuse and repair sectors.
- Encourage companies and member states to support the objectives of the strategy.

The EU strategy for sustainable and circular textiles is being operationalised through a series of legislative initiatives, stakeholder platforms, awareness campaigns, and funding instruments. These actions create the legal and practical framework needed to support a circular and sustainable transformation of the textile sector.

The Eco-design for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) establishes a framework for setting eco-design requirements addressing a wide range of products, including textiles. It aims to improve durability, reparability, and recyclability while reducing environmental impacts across product lifecycles. It expands the scope of the current Eco-design Directive beyond energy-related products to include a wide range of product groups, including textiles. Under the ESPR, textile products need to meet performance and sustainability requirements such as durability, reusability, repairability, recyclability, and resource efficiency. A core feature of the ESPR for textiles is the introduction of the DPP. The DPP contains standardised, accessible information on a product's composition, circularity characteristics, use of recycled content, and potential presence of hazardous substances.

The Directive on Empowering Consumers for the Green Transition and the Green Claims Directive, proposed in 2022 and 2023 respectively, seek to combat greenwashing and ensure that environmental claims made by textile brands are substantiated, transparent, and comparable.

The Directive on Empowering Consumers for the Green Transition, was adopted in early 2024 to be implemented by member states by September 2026. It focuses on amending consumer protection laws (specifically the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive and the Consumer Rights Directive). It aims to equip consumers with better information and protect them from misleading practices related to the environmental and

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

social characteristics of products, including textiles. The directive prohibits vague or unsubstantiated environmental claims, such as generic terms like "eco-friendly" or "green" without clear evidence of verifiable environmental performance. It also targets misleading claims about a product's durability, repairability, and the use of carbon offsetting to suggest a neutral or positive environmental impact without proper substantiation. Furthermore, it regulates sustainability labels, ensuring they are based on credible certification schemes or established by public authorities and are transparent and independently verified.

The Green Claims Directive is more specifically focused on the substantiation and communication of explicit voluntary environmental claims. It sets out detailed criteria for how companies should prove their environmental claims before making them. These claims must be based on scientific evidence, consider the entire lifecycle of the product, and be specific rather than generic. The directive also introduces a verification system where an independent and accredited third party must verify environmental claims before they can be used in marketing. Moreover, it establishes rules for environmental labels to ensure they are trustworthy and transparent, prohibiting self-made labels without thirdparty verification. While the timeline for its full implementation is still under development, it is expected to bring significant changes to how textile companies communicate their environmental performance to consumers across the EU.

The revision of the Waste Shipment Regulation aims to restrict the export of textile waste, ensuring that waste treatment aligns with EU environmental standards and avoids negative impacts in third countries. The new regulation entered into force on 20 May 2024 aims to ensure that the EU does not export its waste to third countries and contributes to environmentally sound management of waste. Furthermore, it strengthens enforcement to prevent illegal shipments of waste occurring within the EU, as well as from the EU to third countries. Finally, it aims to increase traceability of waste shipments within the EU and facilitate recycling and reuse. With the new regulation, procedures will move away from a paper-based approach to an electronic one. A central EU system will ensure the smooth operation of this exchange from May 2026.

In 2023, the Commission proposed a revision of the Waste Framework Directive to introduce harmonised EPR schemes for textiles across all EU Member States, making producers financially and operationally responsible for the full lifecycle of textile products. The Directive sets the basic concepts and definitions related to waste management, including definitions of waste, recycling and recovery. It requires that waste be managed without endangering human health and harming the environment; without risk to water, air, soil, plants or animals; without causing a nuisance through noise or odours; and without adversely affecting the countryside or places of special interest.

Moreover, it clarifies the point at which discarded materials transition from being classified as waste to becoming valuable secondary raw materials, and it provides criteria for differentiating between waste and unintentional by-products of production processes. In addition, the Directive promotes two fundamental principles: the "polluter pays principle," which holds those who produce waste responsible for its treatment, and the "extended producer responsibility," which extends this responsibility to producers for the entire lifecycle of their products, including their end-of-life management. Central to the directive is the five-step "waste hierarchy," which establishes a preferred order for waste

management actions, prioritizing prevention, followed by preparing for re-use, then recycling, other recovery (e.g., energy recovery), and finally, disposal as the least desirable option.

The Waste Framework Directive revision was finally approved in September 2025, introducing targeted measures to reduce waste and promote circularity in the textile and footwear sectors. The updated rules aim to hold producers accountable for the end-of-life management of their products, covering a wide range of items, including clothing, accessories, hats, footwear, blankets, bed and kitchen linen and curtains. All producers, including those based outside the EU and those operating through e-commerce platforms, will be responsible for the costs associated with the collection, sorting and recycling of textile and footwear waste placed on the EU market.

Finally, a revision of the Textile Labelling Regulation was launched in 2023 and adopted in 2025, with the aim of modernising labelling rules to better reflect circular and environmental criteria, thereby supporting informed consumer choices and supply chain transparency. The current Regulation, which primarily focuses on fibre composition, has led to a fragmented landscape of diverse national labelling requirements across the EU for other textile characteristics. This creates complexity and confusion for consumers due to overlapping information, while simultaneously burdening businesses with increased compliance costs and hindering the smooth operation of the internal market. Furthermore, advancements in new fibres and recycling technologies necessitate more sophisticated fibre identification methods, and the existing regulation's exclusive reliance on physical labels overlooks the potential benefits of readily available digital labelling solutions. The Commission has also carried out a stakeholder public consultation from 19 December 2023 to 15 April 2024, with a view to collecting evidence in relation to the functioning of the current Regulation regarding pre-identified problems and to seek opinions on envisaged solutions. However, the revision is still under assessment and did not make it into the Commission Work Programme for 2025, meaning it likely will be adopted by the second quarter of 2026.

In response to these challenges and in line with the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles, the EC is undertaking a review under its REFIT program to establish a harmonized set of labelling rules for all textile and related products, encompassing environmentally relevant information. This initiative aims to ensure consumers have access to clear, accurate, and comparable information, thereby preventing misleading claims. Simultaneously, it seeks to reduce compliance costs for businesses through EUwide standardization, fostering regulatory clarity and consistency with current and upcoming legislation. This process involves a thorough evaluation and impact assessment, incorporating feedback gathered through consultations.

Together, these measures reflect the EU's commitment to building a circular, resilient, and transparent textile economy. TRUSTex contributes directly to this implementation process by generating evidence, piloting solutions, and supporting stakeholder alignment across the textile value chain.

# 8.BLOCKCHAIN, DPP AND OTHER TRUSTEX RELEVANT TECHNOLOGIES' SPECIFICITIES

The textile industry has witnessed a significant progress with the infusion of modern technologies which are now instrumental in accelerating its transition towards sustainability, circularity, and regulatory compliance.

## Blockchain technology

Among the key prerequisites to achieve sustainability in textiles, the key element is full traceability of supply chains to ensure sustainable supply chain management. Reliable, transparent, and efficient data is one of the crucial requirements in today's advanced industrial context and this is possible by blockchain technology. The core advantages of blockchain technology namely transparency, data auditability, privacy, value transfer, process efficiency, and automation are essential for achieving the multiple goals of textiles sustainability (S.S.Muthu, 2022). Blockchain creates a shared, tamper-proof record of every transaction and interaction, giving brands and consumers a clear view of a product's origin, materials, and manufacturing processes. By recording a product's entire life cycle on the blockchain, brands can verify genuine items and combat economic losses. Blockchain also allows brands to provide proof of ethical sourcing, sustainable material origins, and fair labour practices, building consumer trust and supporting responsible production. Furthermore, blockchain enables real-time data sharing improving inventory management, and by streamlining processes, reducing manual data handling, and eliminating discrepancies in transactions, helps lower operational costs across the supply chain.

## Digital Product Passport (DPP)

A DPP is designed to provide comprehensive and easily accessible digital information about a product throughout its lifecycle. Similar to a physical passport that contains essential details about an individual, a digital product passport contains crucial information about a specific product. Technically DPP's are nothing new and are already widely used, but just not called that way. Most consumer products today, in addition to the printed information, also carry a bar code which represents a unique identifier for the product that can be read with an appropriate scanning device and if linked with the right database reveals additional information about this uniquely identified product such as its price when used at the checkout of a shop. They are also commonly used in manufacturing supply chains to identify specific lots of materials, components in an assembly or items in a warehouse. Also, nothing prevents companies from equipping their products with QR codes or other smartphone readable tags to provide extra information about the product, the company that made it, its origin or other details that consumers may need. The difference between such voluntary digital product tagging and the DPP for textile products is the fact that both the technical solution and the information to be provided will be defined by law and non-compliance will likely have uncomfortable consequences (L. Walter, 2023).

## Eco-designed fibres

Eco-designed fibres are sustainable materials made from renewable sources, like plants

e.g., organic cotton, hemp, bamboo, flax or recycled synthetic materials like recycled polyester and are manufactured using processes that minimize environmental harm, requiring less water and energy and avoiding harmful chemicals.

## Eco-designed fabrics

They are materials that minimize environmental impact using renewable resources, recycled materials, and eco-friendly production methods that conserve water and energy while reduce harmful chemicals. Examples include linen, lyocell, and fabrics made from recycled plastics, as well as innovations derived from by-products like pineapples and coffee grounds.

## Eco-designed garments

Eco-designed garments are clothing produced with a low environmental impact, achieved through the use of sustainable materials like recycled fibres, eco-conscious manufacturing processes, and practices like zero-waste design, design-for-disassembly, upcycling, and ethical production. These garments are designed to be durable and recyclable, minimizing waste and pollution across their entire lifecycle, from raw material to disposal<sup>21</sup> .

## Sorting

Sorting is the process used to assess the suitability of textile waste for reuse or recycling. Items are sorted into different grades based on quality and end market, and can be sorted by product type, fibre type and colour. This is usually a manual process, but automated sorting technologies are being developed.

## Next-generation textile sorting technologies

Next-generation textile sorting technologies heavily rely on AI and Near-Infrared (NIR) spectroscopy to automate the process of distinguishing fibres, identifying contaminants, and assessing garment re-wearability. Other innovations include AI-powered imaging systems for enhanced fibre identification, development of automated pre-processing methods for recycling and the creation of integrated, closed-loop systems that leverage data to optimize recycling and reuse.

## Semi-automated sorting

Semi-automated sorting refers to the use of technology to analyse and separate discarded textiles by fibre composition and colour with partial human intervention. This process is crucial for creating consistent, high-quality feedstock for high-value recycling, transforming post-consumer waste into reusable materials for manufacturing new products.

### Thermomechanical recycling

Thermomechanical recycling uses a combination of heat and mechanical action to melt and recover polymers from synthetic textiles like polyester and polyamide, transforming them into reusable pellets for creating new textile products. This technology is more energy-efficient than conventional mechanical recycling and produces quality materials. However, it requires feedstock with at least 99% mono-material or compatible polymers, making it more suitable for post-industrial waste or monochromatic post-consumer

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners.
