products.

## Big data, AI, IoT

The possible combinations of fibres, structuring, processing, shaping, colouring, patterning etc. are practically infinite. For this reason, many product development and manufacturing processes in the industry are guided by experiential shortcuts or simple trial-and-error. The emergent ability to capture massive datasets and simulate, analyse and optimise rapid iterations through high performance computing and AI models can revolutionise many traditional paradigms in the design, production, distribution and customer interaction.

Textile-materials due to their lightweight, flexibility, and softness are a carrier medium for smart components and micro-systems such as sensors, actuators, conductors, energy harvesters and storage media, light emitters etc. on irregular, moving, shape-changing and large-area surfaces including the human body. Thanks to rapid technology advances in micro and nanoelectronics, miniaturisation, power efficiency, flexible integration technologies, computing and data processing the potential of smart or e-textiles and wearables is expected to rapidly advance and offer a multitude of market opportunities for smart textile-based systems in healthcare, sports, personal protection, smart interiors, soft robotics, and consumer electronics.

## Authority dashboard tool

An Authority dashboard refers to a dashboard designed to monitor and display data relevant to a specific domain, often using data visualization to present indicators, trends, and metrics in an understandable way. It provides a high-level overview of performance to support decision-making. The TRUSTEX web-based authority dashboard will visualise aggregated information on material flows and uses, and their impact as they become newly available via the EPR schemes, DPPs, etc. for granular monitoring to guide decision making by authorities.

# 9. THE POLICY RECOMMENDATIONS EXPECTATIONS FROM TRUSTEX

A key ambition of TRUSTex is to support the development and implementation of effective EPR schemes by providing robust, evidence-based policy and industry recommendations. These recommendations will focus on improving the viability and impact of EPR in the textile sector, targeting areas such as collection, sorting, reuse, recycling, governance, and supporting policy tools. They will also contribute to broader goals, such as promoting eco-design, traceability, and the use of digital solutions, in line with the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textiles and the WFD.

The recommendations will be based on a combination of technological demonstrations, market and regulatory analysis, and structured engagement with stakeholders across the value chain. Task 7.3, running from M30 to M36, will play a central role in collecting and consolidating these insights. It will translate findings from WP2–WP4 and stakeholder feedback from WP5–WP6 into targeted guidance for policymakers, industry actors, and regulatory bodies. Key themes to be addressed include the use of DPPs for traceability and sustainability in the textile value chain, eco-design for recyclability, strategies to enhance recycling, next generation sorting technologies, re-use solutions and the structure and governance of EPR systems.

The recommendations will also consider how EPR schemes can support climate goals, including GHG reductions, by promoting better material use, reducing waste, and supporting the market uptake of low-impact, circular textile products. Tools such as ecomodulated fee structures, harmonised reporting criteria, and policy mechanisms to manage impacts associated with extra-EU value chain flows will be examined in detail. Barriers to innovation will also be addressed, with specific attention to identifying regulatory gaps and outlining future research and development needs.

Deliverables D7.4 (Policy and Industry Recommendations) and D7.6 (Final Policy Brief) will formalise these outputs, providing both a comprehensive set of recommendations and a concise policy summary. These will also update and expand on earlier results captured in D4.3 "Policy brief on regulatory landscape, EPR policy and governance". Key elements include single eco-modulation of fees concept, governance best practice elements of EPR schemes, handling of non-EU parts of the value chain and fostering domestic re-use and recycling. Moreover, opportunities for an integrated policy approach, the reduction of regulatory barriers, skills needs and capacity building will be addressed. To ensure relevance and uptake, the recommendations will be developed in dialogue with policymakers, industry representatives, and other stakeholders, including through the Advisory Board and relevant EU and international standardisation bodies. This process will be supported by TRUSTex collaboration with innovation clusters, enabling synergies and alignment with related initiatives.

In addition, TRUSTex will carry out a thorough analysis of the regulatory landscape, covering both EU-level and member state legislation, as well as legal frameworks in key third countries. This will ensure that the policy recommendations take into account real-

world legal constraints and opportunities and provide a clear basis for harmonised and effective EPR implementation. The project will also apply gender-sensitive approaches in the development of EPR recommendations, helping to ensure inclusivity in future policy frameworks.

By the end of the project, TRUSTex legislative and policy recommendations are expected to become a cornerstone of its overall impact, and they will be disseminated to guide sectoral efforts while aligning with evolving EU requirements.

TRUSTex aims to influence at least three EU policies (including the proposal for harmonisation of EPR rules), seven Member State policies (particularly those establishing public EPR schemes), and over 25 industries across the value chain. Project knowledge and results will be documented and stored in open-access repositories and platforms, ensuring broad accessibility for stakeholders, policymakers, and the academic community. This open science approach will enhance transparency and visibility, enabling further impact-oriented work within the textile and circular economy communities. TRUSTex partners will continue participating in policy fora, collaborating with industry and regulatory bodies and using their networks to support the adoption and long-term integration of these recommendations.

The project's policy recommendations will also address their direct and indirect contribution to EU climate objectives, including the 2030 GHG reduction target and the broader goal of climate neutrality by 2050. These contributions are articulated across several areas:

 development of eco-designed products that reduce environmental footprint through improved durability, recyclability, and lower-impact materials enhanced efficiency and effectiveness of textile collection and sorting systems, enabling greater reuse and recycling and reducing landfill and incineration rates digitalised value chains that enable brands to exercise greater oversight of suppliers and unlock decision-making efficiencies EPR recommendations, including the establishment of systems for incentives and ecomodulated fees, expected to drive a broad market shift toward more sustainable practices.

These elements together represent some of the most impactful contributions TRUSTex can make toward supporting a circular, low-carbon textile system.

It is worth noting that the already submitted D4.1 "EPR policy benchmark" provides a first set of recommendations, as follow.

Furthermore, in the context of the present deliverable, a collection of stakeholders' positions is presented with the aim of showcasing the current year (2025) views of major stakeholders.

28 November 2025, European Branded Clothing Association calls for: - Harmonised PRO requirements to avoid fragmentation and ensure a level playing field. - Clear and consistent operational rules from registration to reporting and data formats. - Eco-modulation aligned with ESPR to support products designed with sustainability and durability in mind.

- Standardised funding criteria to boost research, innovation, and scalable technological

- Stronger coordination and cooperation among all actors.

18 November 2025, FESI welcomed the Commission's goal to propose a Circular Economy Act and addressed the need to harmonise and strengthen a unified EPR scheme across the EU through an EU-wide framework and a single digital reporting platform. Full harmonisation across national systems, including removing the inconsistencies in ecomodulation criteria and aligning differing registration and reporting requirements, will significantly reduce administrative burden for companies operating in multiple markets, allow for eco-modulation to fulfil its full incentive power for more sustainable solutions and safeguard EPR fees for their main objective, namely funding and driving circularity. These fees should form a key mechanism for driving the EU's transition towards a circular economy, aligning with the goals for reducing waste and increasing recycling rates. Without harmonisation at EU level, the very concept of EPR risks becoming unworkable in practice. Maintaining 27 parallel EPR schemes per waste stream or product group, each with its own definitions, data requirements, registration portals, and fee structures, would impose disproportionate administrative costs on companies, particularly SMEs, and make compliance across multiple markets nearly impossible. This fragmentation undermines the integrity of the Single Market and contradicts the EU's stated ambition to simplify and

 digitalise reporting obligations. A harmonised framework is therefore not only desirable but indispensable to ensure that EPR delivers on its environmental objectives without eroding Europe's competitiveness. In consequence, FESI calls for the development of a single EUwide EPR registration and reporting digital system to replace the obligation of differing registration requirements in separate national Member State systems. This will increase the transparency of the process and lead to a decrease in the administrative burden. Such a digital "One-Stop Shop" model would significantly reduce administrative costs, promote the best available EPR reporting technology, improve data consistency, and enhance market surveillance capabilities. Moreover, it is critically important to simplify the requirement to designate an authorised representative in each EU Member State by allowing companies to appoint a single representative for the entire EU market. This would ensure an expert point of contact for Member State authorities, prevent adding an additional market barrier to national Member State markets via EPR systems, decrease the likelihood of free-riders and ensure that EPR obligations are met without creating unnecessary administrative barriers and compliance costs. Furthermore, this set-up would be aligned with other EU legislation, including the General Product Safety Regulation and the New Legislative Framework. In the context of the EPR fees and modulation, FESI strongly calls for clear mechanisms for the effective implementation of fees due in instances where a product, initially placed on the market by an economic operator in one Member State complying with national requirements, is later transported and sold in another Member State. To avoid duplication of payments, EPR fees should be paid in the Member State where the product is made available and most likely to become waste. More than 90% of waste generated in the EU is treated in the country in which it was generated. The establishment of a harmonised adjustment mechanism for returned products will ensure this principle and prevent differing approaches across Member States, leading to inconsistencies, double payments, and indirect fees for the movement of goods in the internal market. Ensuring effective enforcement of Article 8 of the WFD which outlines EU-wide general minimum requirements for EPR is of equal importance to increase transparency and cost-efficiency. Additionally, the industry calls for full alignment of the eco-modulation of EPR fees at EU level, in line with the ecodesign requirements under the ESPR. Allowing the continuation of national, specific eco-modulation criteria in parallel as well as in the interim until ecodesign criteria are established for a product group, will minimise, if not remove, the incentive mechanism that eco-modulation can have on scaling sustainable solutions across the internal market. Furthermore, FESI wishes to stress that the EPR fees should be safeguarded and earmarked to finance further technical innovation and upgrades in the collection, sorting and recycling, transforming waste into a valuable resource and fostering a more circular economy. Specific provisions to ensure this should be introduced in the

6 November 2025, European Branded Clothing Association recommendations

- 1. Ensure harmonisation and consistency among legislation
- Prevent market fragmentation and administrative burden
- Harmonise requirements and enforcement defined under the WFD.
- 2. Enable access to economic instruments to scale up circular business models
- Foster investment in recycling capacity and circular business models
- Stimulate demand through recycled content requirements
- Enhance competitiveness and scaling supply of recycled fibres.
- 3. Update outdated definition with circularity in mind
- Accelerate the work by the JRC on common End-of-Waste (EoW) criteria, allowing for the uptake of a market for secondary raw materials.

8 October 2025, 38 organisations led by Zero Waste Europe co-signed a joint letter calling on the European Commission to ensure that existing and upcoming EPR schemes go beyond waste management and truly support circularity.

 To meet Europe's climate, circularity, and strategic autonomy goals, on top of optimising and harmonising EPR criteria, the Circular Economy Act must: ● Redefine EPR to include the full cost coverage of a product's end-of-life treatment, including littering/clean-up costs. ● Mandate EPR to finance the waste prevention, repair, and reuse stages of a product's life cycle, with the amount necessary to achieve the relevant policy goals and targets. ● Introduce binding targets for waste prevention and reuse (in addition to recycling) for the relevant product streams to be achieved with the funding of EPR schemes. ● Define the meaning of cost-coverage for non-waste-related activities (e.g, prevention, reuse, and repair) in order to delineate the limits of producer responsibility. ● Review the governance of EPR schemes not only to ensure harmonisation and oversight of PRO performance but also, in particular, where they address upstream measures, to ensure that municipalities, social enterprises, reuse organisations, and recyclers have a seat at the table in scheme design and decision-making. ● Ensure that EPR schemes include a 'fee transfer mechanism' to finance the end-of-life treatment of second-hand products when those are exported outside the EU.

5 October 2025, PRO Circularity Alliance Position Paper for the EU Circular

Economy Act Stakeholder Consultation

The position paper reflects the shared views of Producer Responsibility Organisations from across Europe and outlines how PROs can contribute to a coherent, effective and

implementable framework for advancing circularity at EU level.

Reaffirming producer-led PROs: Responsibility must remain with those placing products on the market, within flexible, innovation-driven and transparent governance frameworks. Harmonising reporting and data systems: A harmonised or centralised EU-wide register for PROs and producers would improve transparency, reduce duplication, and simplify

compliance.

Promoting fair competition and efficiency: Competitive system structures should be encouraged where they contribute to innovation, cost-efficiency and improved

performance.

Strengthening cross-border cooperation among PROs: Enabling mutual recognition and shared infrastructure can support regional circularity hubs and create economies of

Integrating new PRO responsibilities: PROs should play a role in R&D, eco-modulation,

consumer communication, and coordination with waste operators.

Anchoring EPR in the EU's strategic autonomy goals: Efficient PRO systems are vital for securing secondary raw materials and reducing Europe's reliance on virgin resources. Establish EU-wide minimum requirements for EPR schemes: Define baseline obligations for collection, treatment, and recycling across waste streams, and Member States. This will reduce fragmentation, ensure fair competition, and make all producers

Harmonise data systems and the reporting needs: Set up harmonised registers for PROs and producers and create an EU-wide system. This will improve transparency, cut

duplication, and enable performance benchmarking

Easier compliance for multi-country producers: Apply mutual recognition and more streamlined processes in order to avoid duplication of reporting needs. Simplification will make cross-border operations easier while maintaining strong environmental standards.

 Flexible governance structures for PROs: Enable governance models tailored to national or sectoral contexts, with accountability and transparency. Flexibility encourages innovation and responsiveness to change.

Strengthen performance monitoring: Define clear, comparable indicators for collection, recycling quality, and material recovery. Monitoring will guide policy, drive improvement, and build trust in PRO schemes.

Support innovation and investment: Provide stable conditions for long-term investment in sorting, recycling, and recovery infrastructure. Encourage eco-design and new recovery pathways to meet circularity targets and strengthen EU competitiveness.

13 March 2025, FEAD and EuRIC have argued that without urgent support, valuable reusable and recyclable materials will be lost, and a whole industrial ecosystem that is key to the circular transition will collapse before EPR takes effect. This conjuncture is challenging the financial sustainability of the industry and is potentially jeopardizing the resource autonomy of the EU. The existing operators wish to maintain and extend their important work, but they need support.

According to FEAD and EuRIC, action is urgently needed to support the sector until EPR schemes are operational. Without it, the textile recycling ecosystem risks collapse before EPR can take effect. They urge immediate support for the European post-consumer textile sector during EPR transition. They propose:

 Financial support to sustain collection and recycling operations Lower VAT for reuse, repair, and recycling Boost sorting capacity in the EU and globally Remove R&D barriers for textile recycling tech Optimise collection with public awareness Fair waste management costs for operators Facilitate cross-border textile waste transfers within the EU Protect European second-hand markets from unfair competition End ultra-fast fashion imports by removing the €150 duty exemption

In contrast, Resortecs' approach ensures high material recovery (<5% loss) with around 90% purity, at half the cost and up to 15 times the speed of manual disassembly. The process is fully automated, with no manual intervention required, and products exit the system completely dismantled. On the manufacturing side, the only design change needed is substituting conventional threads with Smart Stitch™ threads, which run seamlessly on standard industrial sewing machines. This level of automation and compatibility dramatically reduces pretreatment costs and could save the European recycling industry

## 10.2 Positioning and competitors of the project (business) partners

## RESORTECS

![](_page_235_Picture_6.jpeg)

Resortecs is an eco-design frontrunner advancing the circular transition in fashion through design for disassembly and full-service textile waste management. It commercialises two complementary design-for-disassembly technologies: Smart Stitch™ (heat-dissolvable sewing threads) and Smart Disassembly™ (thermal disassembly and sorting systems). These technologies are integrated into waste-management services that enable garments and other textile products to be dismantled efficiently and directed into high-quality recycling streams. By combining Smart Stitch™ and Smart Disassembly™, Resortecs removes the disassembly bottleneck that limits textile-to-textile recycling at scale.

The company focuses on fashion and apparel, workwear and bedding, offering both materials and services to brands and manufacturers. This positions Resortecs uniquely upstream in the value chain, enabling recyclability by design while simultaneously providing industrial thermal-disassembly capacity as a preprocessing step feeding recyclers. Importantly, recyclers are not competitors but key collaborators: Resortecs unlocks and supplies the level of material purity required for recyclers to run their processes efficiently, rather than competing with them for feedstock.

Regarding competition, Resortecs' solution offers significant advantages over existing disassembly approaches. Current manual disassembly and mechanical disassembly processes often result in high material losses (20 - 60%) and face limitations in scalability, speed, and suitability for multi-material or multi-layer products. Manual disassembly is time-consuming and not scalable, while mechanical processes struggle with multi-material or multi-layer items and with mono-layer articles featuring soft trims, which together represent roughly one-third of European textile products.

Several innovative disassembly concepts have emerged. Solutions using microwavesensitive thread enable semi-automated layer separation but still require manual handling and cannot withstand mechanical load due to microwave constraints. Clipping-based systems process garments pre-cut into small pieces; while compatible with standard fabrics, they generate high waste (up to 60%) and are not suitable for multilayer items. Water-soluble threads, initially for prototyping, dissolve during washing; however, their advanced variants require hot water above 100 °C under pressure, demand high energy input for heating, disassembly, and subsequent drying, and remain semi-automated. Robotic garment production using thermo-sensitive glues is also being explored. Although glue softening can enable disassembly, this is not the primary focus, and large-scale adoption would require a complete shift away from thread-based assembly.

an estimated €0.5 billion annually in feedstock costs if adopted across the EU market.

## SANKO TEKSTIL ISLETMELERI SANAYI VETICARET ANONIM SIRKETI

![](_page_236_Picture_7.jpeg)

Sanko Tekstil is a large, vertically integrated textile manufacturer that positions itself as a premium, sustainability-oriented supplier for apparel, athleisure, underwear and hometextile markets with growing investment in textile-to-textile recycling and circularity. Its core business lines and capabilities concern spinning (yarns), knitting/weaving, dyeing/printing and garment finishing, allowing control of quality and lead times. Also, home textiles and terry (towels, bath linens) plus fabrics for apparel (interlock, modal blends, cotton basics). Its strong export footprint and group scale via Sanko Holding, part of a broader industrial group with significant capex plans for textile expansion. As strategic differentiators can be named the vertical integration, sustainability / circularity push with public commitments to CradletoCradle principles, regenerated and organic cotton programmes and a new recycling tech venture, RE&UP Recycling Technologies, to convert textile waste back to re-usable material. Sanko has also attracted international financing and large group investment plans to expand spinning and recycling capacity. Sanko supplies successful globally and regionally its yarns and fabrics. A large branch of Sanko Holding is ISKO – one of the most innovative and also largest denim fabric producers in the world who exports to all major brands and regions its fabrics.

## RE&UP RECYCLING TECHNOLOGIES BV

![](_page_236_Picture_10.jpeg)

RE&UP is a circular-tech textile company that is reshaping the traditional textile-to-textile paradigm. Specifically, the company produces "Next-Gen Cotton fibers" and "Next-Gen Polyester Chips" from pre- and post-consumer and post-industrial textile waste, to match or approach the performance of virgin fibers. It is backed by a long textile heritage via SANKO Holding and leverages that legacy plus R&D/go-to-market capability. RE&UP has a feedstock-agnostic process already at scale with an annual capacity of 80.000tons that can handle mixed textile waste like polycotton with elastane, Polyester elastane. In May 2025, RE&UP became the first textile industry company to achieve Cradle to Cradle Certified® Circularity for all its products (Platinum level for pre- and postconsumer cotton, Silver level for recycled polyester chips). In March 2025, RE&UP announced a multi-year collaboration with major sports brand PUMA to scale fiber-to-fiber recycled solutions (using RE&UP's materials) in PUMA's supply chain. RE&UP is positioning itself as a technology-enabled, premium supplier of truly circular textile raw materials (fibers) to apparel, textile, and wider technical textile markets competing on sustainability, traceability, and performance. RE&UP and rivals such as Syre and Reju are together in the challenge to scale circular fiber production. RE&UP's differentiator is that it handles both cotton and polyester (and blends) in a single ecosystem, which is more

unusual. There may be smaller regional recyclers or sorting/management firms specialising in textile waste streams rather than full fiber-production (for example, sorting/collection, but not end-to-fiber). RE&UP is carving out a premium, technology-led niche in the textile raw materials industry focusing on converting textile waste (including blends) into high-performance, traceable textile-to-textile recycled fibers (cotton & polyester) that from a quality standpoint are similar to virgin materials. Its value proposition is built around circularity at scale, quality equivalence, and brand/retailer alignment with sustainability mandates. The company's competitive advantage lies in its patented process for blends, its heritage backing, and its early traction with brand partners. Its closest competitors are other textile-to-textile recyclers (mechanical or chemical) today mostly at lab- or pilot-size seeking industrial-scale circular fiber production.

## SCANTRUST SA

![](_page_237_Picture_5.jpeg)

Scantrust is a Swiss scale-up that sells a cloud platform for product identification, secure QR codes and connected-packaging solutions used for anti-counterfeiting, supply-chain traceability, consumer engagement, and digital product passports.

The core tech propositions include secure/serialized QR codes, analytics/business intelligence from scan events, tamper-evident labels, and integrations for enterprise workflows.

Their target customers include food and beverage, spirits, cosmetics, automotive components, pharma and luxury, to address their challenges with counterfeiting, traceability, consumer engagement and regulatory compliance.

To differentiate Scantrust uses secure QR codes as a low-cost, smartphone-first authentication and traceability layer. Furthermore, it emphasizes an end-to-end SaaS offering rather than only tags or inks positioning it as a connected-goods platform. Also, positioned both for addressing the needs of large enterprises (ERP connections, partner ecosystem integrators) as well as SMEs (e.g. for EU wine label and DPP support).

Regarding competitors below are peers that compete on anti-counterfeit, track-and-trace, or connected-packaging, grouped and noted by their typical strengths:

 Serialization & enterprise track-and-trace players Systech: serialization, enterprise track-and-trace for regulated sectors (strong on pharma/serialisation). Authena: blockchain and NFC/QR product authentication for luxury and pharma. Consumer-facing authentication / anti-counterfeit specialists Sproxil: consumer verification and anti-fraud (mobile-based) focused on emerging markets. VerifyMe / AuthenticVision / MarqVision: QR/NFC/image-based authentication and marketplace monitoring. Broader packaging or digital ID platforms Digimarc: digital watermarking at scale (broad media & packaging play). TrusTrace / Wherefour / other traceability platforms: more focused on supply-chain data and ESG/product-passport tooling. Security-ink / government-grade track-and-trace

SICPA / Tageos / NeuroTags: providers of secure physical technologies (inks, tags, NFC) and large government/regulatory contracts; compete where very high tamperresistance is required.

## MADE2FLOW GMBH

Made2Flow is a German software/data company founded in 2019. Its core offering is a SaaS platform aimed at the fashion sector for supply‑chain decarbonisation, environmental impact measurement, and sustainable product development. Their solution covers gathering supply-chain data, validating it and filling gaps, calculating environmental impact indicators (CO₂, water usage, land use etc) via LCA frameworks. Their vision is to "enable brands to measure the impact of all their products at all times, in a transparent, trustworthy and easy manner". They emphasise helping brands comply with regulations (e.g., product environmental footprint category rules, DPPs) and using data to drive internal decisionmaking and external transparency. Moreover, they have alliances with Infor (integration between Infor's PLM for fashion and Made2Flow's impact-measurement engine) to boost traceability and measurement. They serve brands, suppliers and sourcing companies and they are positioned in the intersection of fashion supply-chain, environmental measurement and SaaS. They focus on validated, credible environmental impact data and emphasise data credibility and gap-filling. Also, they appear to target brands that are under increasing regulatory and stakeholder pressure (ESG, product passport, consumer expectations) and need both internal decision tools and external reporting tools. Their approach is lean / digital (cloud/SaaS) and specialised in fashion.

Regarding competitors, a few of them are mentioned.

TrusTrace: A leading platform for capturing, digitizing, and sharing traceability and compliance data across high-volume textile and footwear supply chains.

Carbonfact: Offers product-level LCAs at scale for a brand's product catalogue, specializing in apparel and footwear and emphasizing data cleaning and intelligent gapfilling.

Carbon Trail: provides sustainability software for fashion and retail focusing on carbon accounting.

Retraced: Provides solutions for tracking products throughout their lifecycle, enabling consumer-facing transparency and compliance in the fashion supply chain.

Made2Flow is strong in data-validation, deep supply-chain environmental impact modelling (LCA based) and integration with PLM/sourcing workflows. Some competitors may focus more on traceability or transparency rather than full environmental impact calculations. Made2Flow is aiming to collaborate with larger enterprise software vendors (ERP/PLM providers). Existing partnerships exist with key players such as PTC and Infor, and thus instead of becoming competitors, the PLM/ERP suppliers can utilize the Made2Flow modules. Providers specialising in DPPs or blockchain traceability may also overlap in usecases for brands preparing for regulation.

## IRIS TECHNOLOGY SOLUTIONS, SOCIEDAD LIMITADA

IRIS is an Advanced Engineering company specialized in Real-Time Monitoring solutions based on Photonics-based Process Analytical Technology (PAT) systems and Industrial Artificial Intelligence (Machine Learning and Deep Learning) for data-driven process efficiencies and quality standardization in the Food, Chemical, Pharmaceutical, Bio-based and other process industries. They also develop Digital Twins, as well as AI-enabled Cloud Platforms for digitizing and connecting value chains. Their team of multidisciplinary experts combines experienced programmers, engineers (optical, mechatronic, electronic and process engineers), chemists, physicists, mathematicians and data scientists, biologists and technologists, converging the scientific and technological fields required for Industry 4.0 solutions development. The company has two divisions: IRIS Commercial that delivers B2B solutions and services to industry (serving a growing client base of blue chips and mid-caps from the farm to fork chain and the process industries); and, IRIS Innovation Lab that works in an Open Innovation context to cocreate and build new knowledge, knowhow and IP to fuel their innovation pipeline so that they can offer cutting edge solutions to industry via the Commercial Division.

In the B2B segment, the main alternatives to the NIR and Raman analyzers developed and commercialized by IRIS under the Visum® brand are Bruker, Büchi, FOSS, Thermo Fisher, and Viavi Solutions. These companies offer robust portfolios in both laboratory and process spectroscopy and have large installed bases across the food, pharmaceutical, chemical, and process industries.

IRIS differentiates itself through several competitive factors:

 Integrated chemometric development tools, including a built-in model builder that simplifies and accelerates calibration development. Excellent price–performance ratio, making Visum® analyzers a cost-effective choice without sacrificing analytical quality. High versatility and multiple integration options, enabling installation in pipelines, conveyor belts, blenders, reactors, and various at-line or in-line environments.

These strengths position Visum® solutions as flexible, accessible, and technologically advanced alternatives in the industrial spectroscopy market.

## TERRA MATTERS

Terra Matters is a Groupement d'Intérêt Économique (GIE) initiated in 2022 by the Ministry of the Economy and the Chamber of Commerce of Luxembourg to accelerate the adoption of the circular economy at company level. They are the official entity responsible for the deployment of the Product Circularity Data Sheet (PCDS), now formally published as ISO 59040 in February 2025, the first international standard dedicated to circularity data at product level. With the PCDS, businesses can measure and improve their product circularity, drive eco-design decisions and set measurable goals; be transparent and showcase circularity performance to clients, regulators, tendering bodies, or certification schemes; share and verify circular data securely across the value chain.

Terra Matters positions itself as the reference platform for generating, hosting and exchanging PCDS data. That makes them a standards-first, interoperability-focused player rather than a pure SaaS-for-marketing vendor. Active in textile-related EU projects and promoting PCDS adoption across industries, they are leaning into textiles as a priority vertical while keeping the PCDS generic. Terra Matters acts as a hub for the PCDS, intending to feed other tools (LCA, DPPs, procurement systems) rather than replacing them.

In terms of competitors, Toxnot is a U.S. platform that has integrated PCDS export and supports product transparency and declarations (PCDS hosting/creation on a broader product data platform). Circularise, is an enterprise traceability / data-sharing platform aimed at digital product passports and supply-chain traceability and can be chosen instead of a PCDS-specific hub for companies seeking broader DPP solutions. Lastly, circularity.ID / circular.fashion provides an open data standard for fashion DPPs and tooling.

## DPP Blockchain Integral Solution

Blockchain-based digital product passports represent an important technology for enhancing supply chain transparency, sustainability and compliance. By leveraging blockchain's immutable ledger, these solutions provide verifiable records of the product's lifecycle (from raw materials sourcing to end-of-life recycling), enabling circular economy practices. Driven by the EU regulations and the growing consumer demand for ethical products, the market is experiencing a significant growth. As of 2025, the broader DPP market (with blockchain as a core enabler) is valued at approximately USD 200-300 million, projected to reach USD 1-8 billion by 2030-2035 at compound annual growth rates (CAGRs) ranging from 22% to 45%. However, projections vary by scope (e.g., softwareonly vs. full platforms), but consensus points to hyper-growth fuelled by EU compliance deadlines. The table below summarizes such projections:

## Authority dashboard

The sector of authority dashboards that integrate DPP data with EPR information, represents a high-growth niche within the broader environmental compliance software market. These tools empower regulatory bodies to monitor product lifecycles, enforce sustainability standards, and support policy decisions through user-friendly interfaces and real-time analytics. Driven by the regulations like the ESPR and EPR mandates, the market is projected to expand. According to Verified Market Research, the global environmental compliance software market (encompassing dashboards) is valued at approximately USD 2.2 billion, projected to reach USD 4.5 billion by 2031 at a CAGR of 10-12%. Key opportunities lie in AI-enhanced predictive analytics and cross-border data integration, while challenges include legacy system interoperability and data privacy concerns. Several firms are pioneering authority-specific dashboards, emphasizing on the EPR/DPP integration. Recyda, offers interactive EPR compliance platforms with material/Stockkeeping unit breakdowns and forecasting. Empower.eco, specializes in DPP software for lifecycle verification and transparency. Earthsoft, provides web-based environmental dashboards for proactive compliance and data visualization. Texbase, focuses on unified EPR-DPP compliance with reporting analytics. Lastly, Avatier and Anthesis Group, deliver executive-friendly dashboards for regulatory enforcement.

### Eco-designed fibres

The eco-designed fibres sector, encompassing bio-based synthetics, recycled materials, and premium natural fibres like organic cotton and bamboo, is experiencing robust growth amid the global shift toward sustainable textiles. This aligns with rising consumer demand for ethical fashion, regulatory pressures to reduce textile waste and emissions and innovations that minimize environmental impacts compared to traditional cotton and polyester. DataM Intelligence Market Research reports that as of 2025, the market is valued at approximately USD 57-58 billion, with projections indicating expansion to USD 84-130 billion by 2030-2034 at a CAGR of 7.6-8.6%. Key players include established manufacturers like Lenzing AG and emerging innovators, fostering collaborations with fashion brands such as H&M and Adidas to integrate these materials into mainstream apparel. Challenges like competition from cheaper synthetics persist, but opportunities in circular economy models and niche applications promise sustained momentum.

| 2034 | 129.8                     | 8.6      | Long-term outlook; boosted by Asia-Pacific industrialization and EU circular mandates. |

Source: DataM Intelligence Market Research

Several interconnected factors are boosting the sector:

 Consumer demand for sustainable fashion: Ethical sourcing and transparency resonate with millennials and Gen Z, who prioritize low-impact materials. Regulatory pressures: The Waste Framework Directive mandates separate textile collection for reuse/recycling. Similar policies in the U.S. (e.g., California's textile producer responsibility laws) and Asia (e.g., India's eco-labeling schemes) penalize high-emission synthetics, accelerating adoption of bio-based alternatives. Innovations in materials: Bio-based synthetics (e.g., from sugarcane or algae) and regenerated fibres like Tencel (lyocell) offer 30-50% lower water use and carbon footprints than virgin polyester. Trends include antimicrobial and UV-resistant eco-fibres for activewear. Industry expansion: Growth is also expected in end-use sectors like automotive interiors and medical disposables, where eco-fibres reduce waste in high-volume production.

However, several hurdles are recorded:

 Cost premiums: Eco-fibres can cost 20-40% more than conventional options, limiting penetration in price-sensitive markets like fast fashion. Supply chain constraints: Scaling bio-based production faces raw material shortages (e.g., organic cotton yields only 1% of global supply) and competition from petroleumbased synthetics. Competition from synthetics: Polyester's dominance (over 50% of global fibre use) persists due to lower prices and versatility, potentially capping eco-fibre share at 15- 20% by 2030. Verification gaps: Greenwashing concerns erode trust, necessitating robust certifications.

## Eco-designed fabrics

There is a growing demand for fabrics utilizing eco-friendly coatings and finishings, such as bio-based, isocyanate-free, and fluorine-free options. Removable coatings and monomaterial coated fabrics are gaining traction, facilitating easier recycling and aligning with circular economy principles. Innovative textile companies are developing eco-designed fabrics, focusing on sustainability without compromising performance. These advancements cater to both regulatory requirements and consumer preferences for environmentally responsible products.

Recent market estimates cluster around USD 26–38 billion with multi-year projections that put the market between USD 27–115 billion by the 2030 depending on definition and forecast horizon. Typical CAGR ranges reported in 2024–2029 / 2024–2032 forecasts are 8% to 13%.

BCC Research projects a 8.1% CAGR (2024–2029) to reach about \$27.8B by 2029, while several market research companies project higher CAGRs (10–13%) when they include wider bio-based and recycled-textile segments or longer horizons.

Some examples from relevant reports provide the following figures:

 BCC Research: market expected to reach \$27.8 billion by 2029, CAGR 8.1% (2024–2029). Technavio: forecasts growth of the sustainable fabrics market at about CAGR 8.6% (2024–2029).

Sources that use broader definitions (bio-based or eco-fibres, or longer horizons) give higher starting values and stronger CAGRs (e.g., Market Research Future, ResearchNester and Verified Market Reports, report higher base values and CAGRs up to 12%).

Eco-designed fabrics can mean certified organic cotton and lyocell, bio-based fibres, recycled polyester/nylon, biodegradable textiles, or engineered eco-fibres, and the forecast differences arise from (a) which product types are included, (b) the geographic scope, and (c) the forecast period and method.

Top companies in the eco-designed fabrics ecosystem (cellulosic/regenerated fibres, recycled polyester/nylon, recyclers & circular tech, and integrated / diversified textile players) are presented below (Markets and Markets).

## Eco-designed garments for automatic disassembly and recycling

Designing garments for easy disassembly enhances recyclability, addressing the significant textile waste that is generated. Fashion brands are increasingly adopting design strategies that facilitate automatic disassembly, integrating features like detachable components and single-material compositions to streamline recycling processes.

Estimates for the "eco-designed garments / sustainable apparel" market clustered around USD 8–11 billion in 2024 with widely varying CAGR forecasts from 10% up to 23% depending on scope and timeframe (Custom Markets Insights).

Several reports place the global sustainable/apparel market at about USD 8–10.5 billion in 2024 (Custom Market Insights: USD 8.1B in 2024; Market Research Future: USD 10.5B in 2024). Forecasts vary with 9.9% (Coherent Market Insights) to 13% (Market Research Future) to 22.9% (Custom Market Insights).

The numbers differ because definitions differ. "Eco-designed garments" vs "sustainable fashion" vs "sustainable apparel" vs "sustainable clothing", some include second-hand, rental, repair and recycling and others only new garments using sustainable inputs. Some reports focus on North America/Europe while others are global. Also, forecast horizons differ.

Regarding the top companies actively positioned around eco-designed garments, the following brands are among the leading ones.

Patagonia, pioneering durable, repairable garments and recycled materials.

 Stella McCartney, luxury brand known for materials innovation and no-leather policy. Eileen Fisher, known for circular take-back, remanufacturing and simple eco-design. Reformation, brand focused on low-impact materials and transparent footprints. H&M, fast fashion player that runs sustainability lines and invests in recycled-fibre tech. Investors in eco-design: Adidas, Levi's, PVH (Tommy Hilfiger/Calvin Klein).

## Next-gen sorting technologies

Advanced sorting technologies, such as near-infrared (NIR) and visual spectroscopy (VIS), are revolutionizing textile recycling by accurately identifying and separating materials. These technologies enhance the efficiency of recycling operations, supporting the industry's move towards a circular economy. Companies specializing in recycling technologies are investing in NIR and VIS systems, offering solutions that improve sorting accuracy and processing speed, thereby increasing the quality of recycled fibres.

The market for textile-waste sorting / next-gen sorting tech is estimated at USD 1.0–1.3 billion in 2024 (DataIntelo reports about USD 1.24B specifically for a "Textile Waste Sorting AI" market in 2024).

DataIntelo projects 18.7% CAGR for Textile Waste Sorting AI, while broader enabling markets (hyperspectral imaging systems, sorting machines) show 10–19% CAGR ranges depending on the report. A reasonable blended CAGR assumption for next-gen sorting tech is 12–20% over the next 5–10 years (depending on geography and how broadly the segment is defined).

Given that the total textile-recycling market (all recycling processes and services) was estimated around USD 4.8–6.1 billion in the mid-2020s depending on the source and exact scope, this means advanced sorting tech is still partial slice of the overall value chain (Grand View Research, 2024).

Among the growth drivers are the new EU regulations and EPR rules, brand sustainability commitments, rising volumes of post-consumer textile waste, and the need for accurate feedstocks for mechanical and chemical recycling. As technology drivers are reported the falling sensor costs, advances in hyperspectral cameras, AI, robotics and automation.

Regarding the top companies, some of them are listed below:

 TOMRA is a global leader in sensor-based automated sorting and has commercial textile sorting solutions combining NIR and visual sensors for whole and shredded textiles. Pellenc ST is an optical sorting machines supplier. Refibred is a hyperspectral and AI start-up focused on textile material detection. NewRetex operates automated sorting lines (robots, NIR, cameras) and runs industrial recycling plants that produce yarns from sorted inputs.

PICVISA is an integrator that has built automated/hyperspectral sorting systems for industrial textile recyclers. trinamiX (BASF) is a NIR/mobile spectroscopy solutions provider for on-site and process identification. ANDRITZ is an industrial machinery supplier offering automated tearing and processing lines.

## Denmark

Leading the way in automated textile sorting NewRetex has developed the world's first fully automated textile sorting facility with a traceability system. They specialize in automatic textile sorting and mechanical recycling, transforming post-consumer fibre into valuable new resources for the textile industry. They aim to be the market leader in textile sorting technology and to create high-value materials through innovative processes. They design and sell textile sorting lines that integrate artificial intelligence, data collection systems, and advanced sensor technology. The systems empower companies to efficiently manage textile waste, stay ahead of EU regulations, and actively contribute to a more circular economy. NewRetex has accumulated extensive knowledge about post-consumer fibres and successfully developed yarns and products, proving that these fibres can be effectively recycled for the future of the textile industry. They have established a strong and scalable supply chain that provides industry with access to high-quality recycled fibres, yarns, and finished products, ready to be integrated directly into new collections.

### Austria

ANDRITZ is at the core of the movement to provide industrially and economically viable solutions that bring circularity to the world of natural and synthetic fibres. ANDRITZ has a diversity of solutions and cooperating partners covering the value chain from recovery of fibre to production of new yarn. They offer a full suite, from automated textile sorting, textile fibre preparation to mechanical and chemical recycling and combined processes.

## Dismantling lines

Automated dismantling processes, including heat-triggered systems, are being developed to efficiently disassemble textile products, facilitating material recovery and recycling. These innovations address the complexity of recycling blended fabrics and multicomponent garments. Engineering firms and recycling companies are collaborating to design and implement automated dismantling lines, aiming to scale up recycling capabilities and reduce manual labour requirements. Unfortunately, currently no specific reports for "textile products dismantling lines" were found.

argues that the equipment / dismantling lines are to grow at 6–7% CAGR over the next 5– 10 years.

Companies that either supply dismantling/sorting equipment or deliver the design-fordisassembly and thermal systems used in industrial lines are Resortecs for heatdissolvable threads and thermal disassembly systems and ANDRITZ for industrial shredders, separation and complete fibre-preparation lines. Resortecs has commercialized heat-dissolvable threads and an automated thermal disassembly system (to remove trims and speed disassembly versus manual methods) and ANDRITZ supplies shredders and separation lines to large recyclers.

## Thermo-mechanical recycling

Optimizing thermo-mechanical recycling processes is crucial for retaining material quality and enhancing the properties of recycled textiles. Advancements in this area contribute to the viability of recycled fibres as substitutes for virgin materials, supporting sustainability goals. Research institutions and industry players are focusing on improving thermomechanical recycling techniques, developing processes that maintain fibre integrity and expanding the applications of recycled materials.

Thermo-mechanical recycling is a subset of the broader textile-recycling / textile-wasteprocessing market and of the "mechanical" or "machine" segment in particular.

It refers to melting / heat-based reprocessing of thermoplastic textiles (mainly polyester/PET) to regranulate or directly produce fibres, i.e., melt-and-spin or thermomechanical reactor approaches that keep the polymer intact rather than chemically depolymerizing it. It is widely used for polyester and other thermoplastics and is the backbone of many textile-to-textile polyester recycling approaches.

An estimate for the 2024 global thermo-mechanical textile recycling market (equipment and processing services) ranges from USD 0.9 billion to USD 1.6 billion, while the expected CAGR is 4%–8% over the coming 5–8 years (Global Market Insights).

Some of the most prominent companies developing thermo-mechanical / melt-and-spin textile-to-textile processes or are major adjacent equipment and sorting suppliers are: Syre, H&M-backed startup focused on textile-to-textile polyester recycling; Earth Protex / Tex2Tex, developer of the Tex2Tex Thermo-Mechanical Reactor process that explicitly markets thermo-mechanical textile-to-textile polyester recycling; ANDRITZ, industrial supplier of textile-recycling equipment (tearing/tearing lines, recycling lines and full solutions).

# 11. SWOT ANALYSIS

The global and European textile industries are navigating a landscape characterized by rapid technological advancements, heightened sustainability demands and shifting consumer behaviours. The SWOT analysis that follows provides a comprehensive review by recording the relevant strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats.

### Strengths

 Recycling: Textile recycling reduces landfill waste, conserves natural resources, saves energy, and creates economic opportunities. Technological advancements: The integration of technologies such as IoT, AI digital textile printing etc., enhance manufacturing efficiency, enable predictive maintenance, and facilitate customization, thereby improving overall productivity. Particularly the European textile industry is embracing digitalization and automation, with advancements enhancing product offerings and competitiveness. Diverse applications: Textiles are utilized across various sectors, including fashion, home furnishings, automotive, healthcare, and agriculture, underscoring their versatility and essential role in multiple industries. Commitment to sustainability: Strong policy frameworks, such as the EU Green Deal, the Circular Economy Action Plan, the EU Strategy for Sustainable and Circular Textile and the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR), support systemic change. European initiatives aim to make products more environmentally friendly, reflecting a strong commitment to sustainability and circular economy principles. Strong brand image and reputation: European textiles are often associated with high quality, craftsmanship, and design. Focus on innovation: European companies are increasingly investing in research and development to create new technologies and materials. Skilled workforce: Many European countries have a long history of textile production and a well-trained workforce. Rise in sustainable consumer demand: Consumers, especially Gen Z, are showing increased preference for eco-friendly and ethical textiles, encouraging systemic shifts. Reuse practices: Textile reuse practices promote sustainable fashion reducing environmental impact. Second-hand market: The global second-hand market is experiencing growth driven by increasing consumer demand for affordable, sustainable and unique fashion options. EPR benefits: EPR creates transparency and traceability on global material flows. New EU rules will make companies responsible for their waste, encouraging better practices and sustainability in the fashion industry. Growing EPR adoption: The EU and countries like France are leading global efforts to embed EPR in legislation, promoting producer responsibility for post-consumer textile waste. Emerging circular business models: Models such as product-as-a-service, resale platforms, repair hubs, and textile-to-textile recycling present both environmental and economic advantages.

## Weaknesses

 Employment: Europe's textile and clothing industry continues to face challenging times. Compared to pre-pandemic levels, jobs in textiles are down. End-of-life textiles destination: Most end-of-life textiles are disposed into landfill or incinerated, instead of being reused or recycled. Also, textiles are traded to the Global South countries with limited infrastructure to sort, resell, refurbish or recycle the traded used textiles, leading to severe environmental, economic, and social impacts. Inappropriate feedstock: Despite the abundance of textile waste, recyclers face both scarcity and high costs of feedstock. There is insufficient post-consumer material suitable for recycling available due to low collection rates in most EU member states and a lack of incentive for sorters to provide feedstock quality needed for recycling. What exists often lacks the specifications needed for most current recycling technologies. Environmental impact: The industry is a significant contributor to environmental degradation, accounting for a notable percentage of global greenhouse gas emissions, water consumption, and waste generation. Scaling up innovative textile recycling: Very few recycling startups can scale into commercial ventures. Having a technology work at the lab and into mass production is not the same. Sorting solutions: Sorting efficiency is currently low. Manual sorting remains inefficient and costly. Complex supply chains: The complex supply chains can lead to challenges in transparency, ethical labour practices, and quality control, potentially resulting in reputational risks. Regulatory complexity: The rapid shift in legislation brings implementation challenges and uncertainty. While regulations promote transparency and sustainability, they can also create a compliance burden. Stringent environmental and consumer protection regulations, while promoting sustainability, impose administrative and financial burdens on companies, particularly SMEs. Shifting to circular models and complying with EPR regulations requires significant investment in infrastructure, training, and technology. Labour shortages: The industry faces workforce shortages, notably in specialized manufacturing positions, due to aging workforces and a lack of training programs for new workers. Consumer behaviour: Eco-friendly alternatives are often more expensive, and consumers choose cheaper, less sustainable products without incentives or motivation to make a change. Many consumers still prefer new items instead of second-hand clothes. Despite awareness, actual sustainable behaviours (reuse, recycling) are hindered by price perception, limited accessibility, convenience barriers and many other. Fast fashion model: Promotes overconsumption, short product life cycles, and significant waste, undermining sustainability efforts. The fast fashion model results in high volumes of low-quality textiles that are difficult to recycle. Online shopping: Global online marketplaces are enabling cheap garments to easily reach European consumers. The convenience of online shopping, amplified by targeted advertising, has reshaped consumer behaviour encouraging impulsive purchases based on desire rather than need.

 Illegal waste exports: Lack of standardized collection, sorting and recycling infrastructure, especially in developing countries, may lead to illegal waste exports, unsustainable practices, and labour exploitation. Labour rights violations: Persistent issues in sourcing countries, including low wages, unsafe conditions, and child labour, challenge the ethical legitimacy of global brands. Low EPR enforcement outside EU: Many countries still lack regulatory frameworks for EPR, limiting its global impact and effectiveness. Loss of revenues: While EPR presents an opportunity for the textile industry, the law against exporting textile waste threatens the current business model, as profit from exporting second-quality textiles to Asia, Eastern Europe, and Africa constitutes a crucial part of revenue. Consumers' deception: Many companies market themselves as sustainable to enhance their reputation, often appearing more environmentally friendly than they actually are. This discourages consumers from purchasing recycled products due to unreliable claims. Limited capacity: The obligation to separately collect textiles will further increase their volumes, while quality of the collected used textiles will keep declining, particularly due to the rise of ultra-fast fashion. However, managing capacity has not expanded, nor have the outlets for the processed used textiles. Handling of non-reusable textiles: The handling of non-reusable textiles (both for recycling and energy recovery) remains costly for sorting and reuse operators, on top of the lack of market uptake for the recycled textile fibres. Technical limits: many garments are hard or impossible to recycle at scale, e.g., blended fibres, coatings, trims and dyes mean recycling technologies are nοt yet able to meaningfully process large shares of waste, so EPR fees cannot by themselves solve end-of-life treatment.

### Opportunities

 EU leadership: EPR schemes represent a pivotal opportunity for the European Union to implement circular business models and solidify its position as a global sustainability leader within the textile industry. Sustainable practices: Adopting eco-friendly materials, implementing circular economy principles, and enhancing recycling initiatives highlight routes for innovation and market differentiation. Investments in biodegradable and organic textiles offer profitable opportunities. Smart textiles: Investments in research and development of smart textiles, such as those with embedded sensors or conductive fabrics, can open up opportunities in highgrowth segments like healthcare and activewear. Development of smart fabrics, antimicrobial textiles, and nanotechnology-driven materials can open new market segments. E-commerce expansion: The growth of online sales channels offers opportunities for brands to reach broader audiences, with investments in e-commerce platforms and digital marketing enhancing consumer engagement. Localized production: Reshoring and localized textile production are on the rise in Europe, driven by a desire for more control over supply chains and a reduction in reliance on global sourcing. Reshoring to Eastern Europe, North Africa, and

Mediterranean countries offers opportunities to reduce emissions and improve transparency.

 Emerging markets: Access developing regions with increasing disposable incomes and shifting consumer preferences. Growth in developing economies, particularly in Africa and Southeast Asia, showcase new consumer communities. European brands can explore new markets in Africa and Asia to increase exports. Collaboration and innovation: Partnerships with technology companies and material science firms can lead to breakthrough textile innovations. Leadership: Europe's commitment to sustainability brings growth opportunities in biodegradable fabrics, circular economy models, and low-impact dyeing techniques. Government support and regulation: EU policies encourage innovation and investments in green and digital textiles. Second-hand market: Second-hand market is expected to grow by 2030 especially in regions embracing low-cost sustainable fashion options. Platforms for resale, swapping, and renting are becoming mainstream, supported by consumer trends and economic incentives. Consumer behaviour: Growing consumer awareness of the social and environmental impact of fashion is driving demand for more transparent and traceable products, as consumers want to know where their clothes come from, how they were made, and their environmental footprint. Technological solutions: The use for instance of IoT for traceability, AI for sorting and DPPs can extremely optimize reuse and recycling systems, helping real-time data to all stakeholders during each phase of the garment lifecycle. EU and global regulations: Regulations hold producers accountable for the entire lifecycle of textile products, including financial contributions to support separate collection, sorting, reuse, and recycling, thereby encouraging sustainable practices within the textile industry. Circular economy transition: EPR schemes can catalyse circular business models by incentivizing product design for durability, recyclability, and reuse.

### Threats

Geopolitical instabilities: Trade tensions, tariffs, and regulatory changes can disrupt

global supply chains, leading to increased operational costs and uncertainties. Market competition: Emerging markets with lower production costs pose strong competition, challenging established players to innovate and maintain market share. European textile manufacturers face fierce competition from low-cost markets, particularly from Asia, challenging their ability to offer competitive prices while maintaining high quality and ethical production standards. Economic uncertainties: Fluctuations in raw material prices and economic instabilities can impact production costs and consumer purchasing power, affecting overall industry profitability. Third-world countries conditions: The massive flow of second-hand clothing into third-world countries triggers environmental, health and economic issues, primarily considering the inadequate waste management infrastructure, the improper disposal methods and unregulated landfilling. Fast-fashion: Overproduction of fast-fashion and poor-quality clothes that still dominate the market, discourage reselling and reuse.

 Resistance from incumbents: Established fast fashion business models may resist circular economy integration due to concerns over profitability and speed-to-market. Greenwashing and lack of trust: Unverified claims about circularity will bring consumer distrust and regulatory reaction. Implementation gaps in EPR: The implementation in member states risks resulting in different systems across the EU, complicating the transition. Even within Europe, EPR implementation varies widely, and producer resistance or inadequate monitoring may hinder impact. Poorly designed fee structures will fail to change producers' behaviour. Flat fees or weak eco-modulation will not sufficiently reward durable, repairable or recyclable design. Weak enforcement, monitoring and data gaps (free-riding), lack of harmonised reporting, traceability and enforcement will allow some producers to avoid payments or misreport volumes, undermining scheme's credibility. Furthermore, regulatory fragmentation with differing rules across countries will create compliance complexity and gaps. Compliance costs and administrative burden can become disproportionately hard for small SMEs.

What follows is a SWOT analysis concerning the technological solutions and products of TRUSTEX. Specifically, a comprehensive SWOT analysis of the specified technological solutions and products is presented considering current trends, regulatory frameworks, and technological advancements to assess each solution's strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats.

## 1. Digital Product Passport Blockchain Integral Solution

## Strengths

 Enhanced transparency: Blockchain integration ensures unchangeable records of product information, enhancing traceability and authenticity throughout the supply chain. Regulatory compliance: DPPs align with EU initiatives like the Eco-design for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR), facilitating adherence to environmental standards. Products' life: DPP is a useful tool for extending the garments' life by making relevant product data available throughout the entire lifecycle, especially during the post-consumer phase.

 Implementation complexity: Integrating blockchain with existing systems can be technically challenging and resource intensive. Data privacy concerns: Ensuring compliance with data protection regulations, such as GDPR, requires careful management of sensitive information.

## Opportunities

 Consumer engagement: DPPs provide consumers with detailed product data, fostering trust and informed purchasing decisions. Circular economy promotion: DPPs facilitate recycling and reuse by providing accurate material composition data.

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them. This draft deliverable has not yet been validated by the granting authorities

### Threats

 Technological barriers: Limited interoperability between different blockchain platforms may hinder widespread adoption. Market resistance: Stakeholders may be reluctant to adopt new technologies due to costs or lack of understanding.

## 2. Authority Dashboard

Strengths Informed decision-making: It aggregates data from DPPs and EPR schemes, enabling authorities to monitor compliance and environmental impact effectively. Policy development support: Provides empirical data to guide the formulation of environmental regulations and standards.

 Data integration challenges: Collecting and standardizing data from diverse sources can be complex. Resource intensive: Maintaining such dashboards require significant resources.

### Opportunities

 Enhanced compliance: It improves monitoring of producer responsibilities, leading to better enforcement of environmental policies. Public transparency: Can be used to inform the public about product sustainability, enhancing community engagement.

Threats Data security risks: Sensitive information may be vulnerable to cyber threats if not adequately protected. Resistance from industry: Companies may hesitate to share detailed data due to competitive concerns.

## 3. Eco-designed fibres

### Strengths

 Sustainability: Reduce reliance on traditional fibres like cotton and polyester, lowering environmental impact. Innovation: Encourage the development of new materials with improved properties.

 Performance variability: New fibres may not yet match the durability or comfort of conventional materials. Production costs: Initial manufacturing processes may be more expensive.

Opportunities Market differentiation: Brands can appeal to eco-conscious consumers by offering sustainable products.

 Regulatory incentives: Governments may provide support for sustainable material development.

 Consumer acceptance: Market adoption depends on consumer willingness to embrace new materials. Supply chain adjustments: Existing manufacturing processes may need significant changes.

## 4. Eco-designed fabrics

## Strengths

 Environmental benefits: Use of bio-based and non-toxic coatings reduces ecological footprint. Health advantages: Eliminating harmful chemicals benefits both workers and consumers.

 Technical limitations: Achieving desired fabric properties without traditional chemicals can be challenging. Cost implications: Eco-friendly materials may be more expensive to produce.

### Opportunities

 Regulatory alignment: Proactive compliance with upcoming environmental regulations. Consumer demand: Growing interest in sustainable products can drive sales.

 Market competition: Other companies may faster develop similar eco-friendly fabrics, increasing competition. Economic pressures: Higher costs may deter price-sensitive consumers.

## 5. Eco-designed garments

## Strengths

 Facilitate recycling: Designs that enable easy disassembly promote material recovery. Extended product life: Modular designs can allow for repairs and upgrades.

 Design constraints: Balancing aesthetics with functionality may be challenging. Consumer perception: Some consumers may associate modular designs with lower quality.

## Opportunities

 Circular business models: Support services like product take-back schemes. Educational impact: Educate consumers on sustainability and product care.

### Threats

 Market readiness: Consumers may not be prepared to adopt new garment designs. Manufacturing challenges: Existing production lines may need reconfiguration.

## 6. Next-gen sorting technologies

### Strengths

 Increased efficiency: Technologies like NIR/VIS spectroscopy enable rapid and accurate material sorting. Labour reduction: Automation reduces reliance on manual sorting, lowering labour costs.

 High initial investment: Advanced sorting systems require substantial capital expenditure. Technical limitations: Some materials may be challenging to identify accurately.

### Opportunities

 Scalability: Automated systems can handle large volumes, supporting industrialscale recycling.

Manual alternatives: Delayed adoption in price-sensitive markets.

## 7. Dismantling lines

Strengths Enhanced efficiency: Automated dismantling lines, particularly those utilizing heat-triggered processes, can significantly facilitate the disassembly of textile products, reducing manual labour and associated costs. Precision and consistency: Automation ensures uniformity in dismantling, leading to higher quality in the separation of materials for recycling purposes.

 High initial investment: The development and implementation of automated dismantling lines require substantial capital expenditure, which may be a barrier for small to medium-sized enterprises. Technical limitations: Heat-triggered processes may not be suitable for all types of textiles, particularly those sensitive to temperature, potentially limiting their applicability.

### Opportunities

 Advancements in technology: Ongoing research in automation and material science can lead to more versatile and efficient dismantling technologies, expanding their applicability across various textile types. Regulatory support: Increasing governmental emphasis on sustainable practices and waste reduction can result in incentives for adopting advanced dismantling technologies.

### Threats

 Technological obsolescence: Rapid advancements in technology may render existing dismantling systems obsolete, necessitating continuous investment in upgrades. Market acceptance: The textile industry's readiness to adopt automated dismantling technologies may vary, influenced by factors such as cost, perceived benefits, and compatibility with existing processes.

## 8. Thermo-mechanical recycling

Strengths Resource efficiency: Thermo-mechanical recycling enables the recovery of synthetic fibres, reducing reliance on virgin materials and conserving resources. Environmental benefits: By diverting textile waste from landfills, this process contributes to environmental sustainability and aligns with circular economy principles.

 Quality degradation: Recycled fibres may exhibit inferior mechanical properties compared to their virgin counterparts, limiting their application in high-quality textile products. Contamination sensitivity: The presence of contaminants in textile waste can adversely affect the quality of recycled materials, necessitating rigorous sorting and cleaning processes.

## Opportunities

 Technological innovations: Advancements in recycling technologies can enhance the quality of recycled fibres, expanding their applicability and acceptance in the market. Market demand: Growing consumer awareness and demand for sustainable products can drive the adoption of textiles made from recycled fibres.

## Threats

 Economic viability: Fluctuations in the cost of virgin materials can impact the competitiveness of recycled fibres, influencing the economic feasibility of thermomechanical recycling processes. Regulatory challenges: Compliance with evolving environmental regulations may require continuous adaptation of recycling processes, potentially increasing operational complexities.
