# 12. PESTLE ANALYSIS

## 12.1 Factors influencing the global and European textile industry

The global and European textile industry is navigating a complex landscape shaped by political trade tensions, economic fluctuations, social demands for ethical practices, technological advancements, strict legal regulations, and pressing environmental concerns. Companies that take measures and proactively address these factors through innovation, transparency and sustainability are better positioned to thrive in the industry. Recently, at the Brussels Textiles Forum of 2025, the industry took stock of the increasingly difficult geopolitical landscape. The EU industry is facing mounting trade challenges, e.g., overcapacity from China and rising tariffs from the United States are threatening the competitiveness of this highly trade-intensive sector. The European Commission is responding by prioritising market diversification to secure market access for European companies. This includes strong support for the ratification of the EU–Mercosur FTA; New negotiations with countries like the UAE, Malaysia, Thailand, and others; Acceleration of talks with India, while ensuring the agreement remains mutually beneficial and economically meaningful. These efforts aim to ensure that the European industry continues to benefit from global supply chains and a diversified export market. At the same time, companies must prepare to operate within a more uncertain trade framework, particularly in relation to the United States.

The analysis that follows illustrates Political, Economic, Social, Technological, Legal, and Environmental factors influencing the global and European textile industry.

### Political factors

 The Commission's 2030 Vision for Textiles is summarized to the following points: All textile products placed on the EU market are durable, repairable and recyclable to a great extent made of recycled fibres; free of hazardous substances; produced respecting social rights. "Fast fashion is out of fashion" and consumers benefit longer from high quality textiles. Profitable re-use and repair services are widely available. In a competitive, resilient and innovative textile sector producers take responsibility for their products along the value chain. The EU has addressed the Competitiveness Compass, the Clean Industrial Deal and the Textile Talent of Τomorrow. The EU Green Deal aims for net-zero emissions by 2050, pushing industries to prioritize sustainability. However, this has introduced significant bureaucratic challenges for businesses, especially SMEs, due to extensive data reporting requirements. Efforts are underway to streamline these regulations to balance growth and environmental goals. The textile industry faces challenges due to rising tariffs and trade tensions. The US has imposed tariffs on imports from countries like China and the EU, leading to operational and financial unpredictability for fashion companies. This volatility complicates long-term investments in sustainable practices.

### Economic factors

 The global fashion industry is projected to reach \$1.8 trillion by the end of 2025 (Uniform Market), driven by fast fashion's low prices and availability. However, this

overconsumption negatively impacts workers, the environment and consumers, leading to increased scrutiny and calls for more sustainable practices.

 The industry continues to face challenges such as price increases, poor product quality, lack of transparency, forced labour and geopolitical instability. Brands are reassessing inventory and improving efficiency through advanced demand forecasting and AI-driven solutions to adapt to these disruptions.

## Social factors

 Despite improvements, labour violations persist in several countries including underpayment and excessive working hours, particularly in subcontracting factories. This highlights the need for brands to improve transparency in their extended supply chains. There is a growing demand from consumers for brands to exhibit environmentally responsible practices. Approximately 80% of consumers are more likely to buy from a company committed to sustainability, prompting textile manufacturers to adopt and communicate their sustainable practices effectively (PwC).

## Technological factors

 Technologies like IoT and AI are revolutionizing the textile industry by improving efficiency and predictive maintenance. The adoption of smart manufacturing, direct-toconsumer models, and digital textile printing reduces waste and enables customization, enhancing operational efficiency and customer engagement. The industry is investing in sustainable technologies, such as eco-friendly materials and processes. The market for eco-friendly fabrics is projected to grow substantially driven by consumer demand and corporate commitments to sustainability.

## Legal factors

 Regulations like the EU's Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive aim to enforce environmental and social accountability in the fashion industry. These measures address issues such as poor working conditions, low wages, and labour exploitation, including child and forced labour.

## Environmental factors

 When textiles are discarded, they are extremely likely to become mismanaged and the environmental and social costs are significant. Around the world, the vast majority of textiles leak out of the system when they are discarded. They are incinerated, landfilled, or leak into the environment. To fix this leaky system, separate collection infrastructure for textiles needs to be scaled up dramatically and implemented in locations where it currently does not exist. The textile industry is significantly affected by climate change, impacting resource availability and demand patterns. Climate change for instance could substantially reduce global cotton production, posing challenges for raw material sourcing. The industry is increasingly embracing the principles of the circular economy, focusing on designing products for longevity, promoting garment recycling, and encouraging consumers to repair, reuse, or recycle clothing. Implementing take-back programs and using biodegradable materials are strategies to reduce textile waste.

## 12.1 Factors influencing partners' countries

As an introduction to the factors that are relevant to the countries represented by the consortium, the SDG performance per country provides an indication relevant to Political, Economic, Social, Technological, Legal and Environmental factors, since the SDGs call on all nations to combine economic prosperity, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability.

European countries offering the most opportunities for sustainable textiles Europe's top six apparel markets are Germany, France, Spain, Italy, the Netherlands and Poland. Together, they account for over 70% of the total value of EU imports. (Eurostat 2023).

## 12.1.1 Finland

After a contraction in 2023 and limited growth in 2024, Finland's economy is expected to almost stagnate in 2025. Economic activity is forecasted to resume expansion in 2026 and 2027, growing by approximately 1% in both years. The unemployment rate is forecasted at 9.5% in 2025, declining only slightly to 9.0% by 2027. Headline inflation is projected to slow down from 1.9% in 2025 to 1.6% in 2026 before reaching 2.0% in 2027. The accelerating economic activity and some fiscal consolidation measures are projected to reduce the general government deficit from 4.5% in 2025 to 3.9% by 2027. The public debtto-GDP ratio is set to increase from 82.5% in 2024 to 92.3% in 2027 (European Economic

## Forecast Autumn 2025).

Finland's textile sector operates in a high-cost but innovation-rich environment, with sustainability, circularity and advanced technology as key competitive levers. While domestic market size is limited, Finland's policy alignment, R&D strength, and ecoconscious reputation make it a leading force in Europe's green textile transition.

## Political factors

Finland benefits from political stability, strong institutions, and adherence to EU

trade, environmental, and labour regulations, providing a predictable framework for textile businesses.

 The Finnish government actively supports a circular economy, aiming for carbon neutrality by 2035. Initiatives such as funding for textile recycling enhance sector innovation. EU trade agreements ensure access to key export markets, but global geopolitical tensions (e.g., EU-Russia relations) have disrupted raw material sourcing and logistics.

## Economic Factors

 Finland's economy has shown resilience in the face of severe international economic and security turbulence. Finland ranks ninth in the European Union in terms of GDP per capita. High wages and energy costs challenge competitiveness versus low-cost textile producers. Investments in bio-based fibres (e.g., Spinnova, Infinited Fibre Company) are creating premium sustainable textile niches. Domestic demand is stable but limited. Growth depends on exports to Europe and Asia. EU funding and green transition financing continue to support R&D and circular textile solutions.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 108,74 Finnish consumers prioritize ethical production, transparency and durability, influencing brands to adopt slow fashion and local sourcing. Aging population and urbanization may shift demand toward functional and healthoriented textiles (e.g., smart fabrics, workwear). Finland's global reputation for design (e.g., Marimekko) supports brand differentiation through aesthetics and sustainability.

## Technological factors

 Finland is an Innovation Leader, performing at 125.3% of the EU average in 2025. Finnish companies pioneer cellulose-based and recycled fibres, positioning Finland as a global hub for sustainable textile technologies. Increasing use of AI, 3D design and digital fashion platforms enhances customization and efficiency. Recycling and waste management technologies are advancing rapidly, driven by EU directives on textile circularity.

## Legal factors

 Strict regulations on emissions, chemical use (REACH), and waste management shape production practices. Intellectual property protection remains notably strong, supporting design-led textile companies.

 Finland ranks highly in sustainable development. The Waste Law from 2021 requires municipalities and residential properties to have comprehensive recycling systems. Textile producers face expectations to minimize carbon footprints and water use. Finland is among the first EU countries to establish a nationwide textile waste collection system. Use of renewable forest resources for fibre production (e.g., wood-based cellulose) strengthens Finland's sustainable advantage.

## 12.1.2 Germany

After two years of contraction, the economy is set to broadly stagnate in 2025 and rebound with 1.2% GDP growth in 2026 and 2027. The positive effects of a ramp-up in public spending is partly counterbalanced by the negative impact of trade tensions, which are expected to impact exports. Expansionary fiscal policy and real wage growth are expected to boost private consumption. Investment is projected to contract in 2025 due to weak economic sentiment and high uncertainty, with a gradual recovery expected over time as consumption and public investment accelerate. The government deficit is forecasted to rise in 2026 and based on currently known policies remain elevated in 2027. The government debt ratio is expected to increase to 65.2% of GDP in 2026 and 67.0% in 2027 (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Germany's textile sector is technologically advanced, sustainability-oriented and innovation-driven with global leadership in technical and smart textiles. Although high costs and regulatory complexity pose challenges, strong R&D, digital transformation, and sustainability leadership position Germany as a European benchmark for circular and highvalue textile manufacturing.

Germany is the EU's largest apparel importer, sourcing 49.9% from within the EU and the rest from outside. Imports from European countries such as Poland and the Netherlands are on the rise. At the same time, Germany is also the EU's largest apparel exporter, dominating intra-EU trade. Major apparel exporters to Germany are Poland (12.7%), China (12.3%), Bangladesh (11.6%), the Netherlands (9%), Turkey (8.1%), and Italy (5.6%).

Germany's textiles and apparel industry is highly import-dependent for raw materials, with cotton largely sourced from India and the U.S. While raw materials are sourced from abroad, Germany's strength lies in value-added processing, technical textiles, and innovative fibres.

Germany is Europe's largest producer of technical textiles, supplying specialized fabrics for sectors like automotive, aerospace, and healthcare, supported by a strong focus on research and innovation. At the same time, Germany concentrates on producing high-end, capital-intensive apparel, while medium-priced, mass-market CMT (Cut-Make-Trim) manufacturing is largely outsourced to lower-cost Eastern European (Fashion for Good).

## Political factors

 Germany's strong governance and EU integration provide a predictable framework for textile and apparel businesses. Government commitment to sustainability, circular economy and low-carbon manufacturing influences the entire textile value chain.

 Programs under Industrie 4.0 and the High-Tech Strategy promote digital transformation, automation and innovation in textile manufacturing. As a major EU economy, Germany benefits from open trade within the EU but faces challenges from global supply chain disruptions and evolving trade tensions.

## Economic factors

 After a decade of strong export-led growth, the COVID-19 pandemic, Russia's war of aggression against Ukraine and rising trade tensions have hit the German economy. Germany ranks seventh in the European Union in terms of GDP per capita. The green economy is a key economic pillar, with millions employed in green sectors. Germany remains one of Europe's biggest textile producers, importers, and consumers. Rising wages and energy prices pressure competitiveness, particularly against low-cost producers in Asia and Eastern Europe. Despite global uncertainties, strong infrastructure, innovation funding and high export capacity sustain growth potential.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 116,20 German consumers prioritize eco-friendly, fair-trade, and durable textiles, pushing brands to enhance transparency and circularity. Government and NGO initiatives encourage recycling and sustainable fashion choices.

## Technological factors

 Germany is a Strong Innovator, performing at 111.1% of the EU average in 2025. Research institutes and industry clusters lead innovation in bio-based and recyclable textiles. Widespread adoption of automation, robotics, 3D knitting and digital design improves efficiency and customization. Innovations in textile recycling and eco-materials are expanding rapidly. Integration of IoT, AI and data analytics strengthen supply chain management and product traceability.

## Legal factors

 Supply Chain Due Diligence Act requires large companies to ensure human rights and environmental standards throughout global supply chains. Strong worker rights increase costs but promote safe and ethical working conditions. Legal frameworks demand clear sustainability and origin information for textile products.

## Environmental factors

 Germany leads in implementing circular textile initiatives. Significant investment in textile recycling infrastructure and closed-loop systems.

Textile waste remains key issue requiring continuous innovation and compliance.

Germany is the largest market for apparel in the EU, with an apparel import value of €41.1 billion in 2023 and an average annual growth rate of 4.3% between 2018 and 2023. According to Statista, Germans spent about €180.4 million on fair-trade textiles in 2022. Germany is host to some of the biggest and most influential trade fairs dedicated to materials, including Munich Fabric Start, Innatex, Performance Days and Sustainable Materials Expo. According to the Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS) database of certified companies, there are 57 German retailers that are GOTS-certified across all textiles product segments. This is the highest number of the top 6 European apparel importers. A 2023 IMF-Premiere vision study about consumer behaviour in sustainable fashion concluded that for German consumers the materials used is the number one criterion for determining a product's eco-responsibility. The percentage of German consumers who feel well informed about fashion sustainability issues has doubled since 2019, reaching 36%. 69% of German consumers say that environmental and social compatibility are fairly or very important to them when purchasing clothing or footwear. 65% of German consumers trust eco-labels and 50% specifically look for ecolabels before making a purchase. Germany has its own government-run social and environmental certification label, the 'Green Button', which is displayed on garments at the point of sale. Green Button recognises the following standards as proof of compliance in the auditing process: Fairtrade, GOTS, Oeko-Tex, Global Recycled Standard, Fair Wear, Bluesign and Cradle2Cradle. Certification is an important requirement for suppliers of German brands. Ethical German brands include Armedangels, LANIUS, Vatter, Infantium Victoria, Honest Basics, Anekdot, Jyoti, Jan 'N June, Dawn Denim, NIKIN, LangerChen, ThokkThokk, Sense Organics and Vaude.

## 12.1.3 France

Economic activity in France is set to decelerate to 0.7% in 2025 and to grow by +0.9% in 2026, as economic and policy uncertainty and the necessary fiscal adjustment weigh on domestic demand. Activity is expected to pick up slightly to 1.1% in 2027. Inflation is projected to gradually increase from 1.0% in 2025 to 1.3% in 2026 and 1.8% in 2027. The government deficit is forecasted to decline to 5.5% and 4.9% of GDP in 2025 and 2026, respectively, before edging up to 5.3% in 2027. Public debt is set to increase to 120% of GDP by 2027, from 113.2% in 2024, on the back of sizeable primary deficits (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

France's textile sector is evolving from traditional manufacturing toward a circular, innovation-driven, and design-led industry. With strong government backing, advanced technology adoption and sustainability regulations, France is positioned as a European leader in sustainable and high-value textile production, though high costs and compliance demands remain constraints.

## Political factors

 National initiatives provide funding for sustainable textiles, circular production and technological modernization. As an EU member, France benefits from free movement of goods and access to major export markets but is also subject to EU environmental and labour directives.

France is the second-largest market for apparel in the EU. It had an apparel import value of €23.9 billion in 2023 and an average annual growth rate of 2.5% between 2018 and 2023. While French luxury brands may have been slow to join the ethical fashion movement, smaller fashion labels are leading the way. France is also home to Premiere

 Government strategies emphasize reshoring of textile and apparel production to reduce import dependency and enhance domestic value chains.

## Economic factors

 Efforts to reduce public spending will be key to lower government debt. France ranks eleventh in the European Union in terms of GDP per capita. Domestic manufacturing remains expensive. Home to global brands, France's textile ecosystem thrives on high-value design, innovation and brand heritage. France is a leading exporter of luxury textiles. Inflation, energy prices, and global trade tensions influence input costs and competitiveness.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 113,45 French consumers increasingly prioritize eco-responsibility, transparency, and ethical sourcing, pressuring brands to adopt circular models. Paris is a global fashion capital, shaping trends and driving innovation across the textile value chain. The textile sector faces skill shortages in digital manufacturing, sustainable materials and advanced production technologies. Growth of second-hand and rental markets reflects shifting of social attitudes.

## Technological factors

 France is a Strong Innovator, performing at 108.6% of the EU average in 2025. Collaboration between research institutes, clusters, and universities supports innovation in smart fabrics, biobased materials, and automation. Digital transformation is accelerating.

### Legal factors

 France enforces the Anti-Waste and Circular Economy Law and EPR obligations. Strong union presence and strict labour laws can raise compliance costs but ensure social protection and quality standards. Strict labeling and traceability obligations enhance market transparency.

## Environmental factors

 France is a frontrunner in implementing circular economy principles, encouraging textile recycling and reduced carbon emissions. Legislation bans the destruction of unsold textiles, compelling companies to recycle or donate excess stock. Alignment with the EU Green Deal and France's national low-carbon strategy drives eco-design and renewable energy use.

 Vision, the world's largest trade fair for apparel and textile materials. France has introduced legislation that requires clothing companies to adhere to numerous sustainability requirements, including a ban on the destruction of unsold clothes; mandatory environmental score labels on all textile products sold in France, and notification (at point of sale) to customers about products that contain substances on the EU REACH Substances of Very High Concern list. According to the Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS) database of certified companies, there are 52 French retailers that are GOTS-certified across all textiles product segments. This is the second-highest number of the top six European apparel importers. A 2023 sustainable fashion consumer study revealed that there has been a significant increase in knowledge and understanding of fashion sustainability issues amongst the French public, particularly regarding sustainable materials. According to the study 44.9% of French respondents purchased eco-responsible clothing in 2022; only 33% of people surveyed felt they did not know enough about the issue, compared to 50.4% in 2019; 37.7% of respondents cite materials as the most important metric for eco-responsibility, up from 28.2% in 2019. According to another 2021 study however, 58% of French consumers that never or rarely bought sustainable fashion did not do so because of the higher price point. Ethical French brands include Hopaal, Veja, 1083, Bombón de algodón, caur, Marcia, Good Guys and émoi.

## 12.1.4 Switzerland

Private consumption is expected to remain the primary driver of growth, fuelled by a robust labour market and a solid increase in real disposable income. Investment growth is projected to remain subdued in view of the uncertain international environment. Exports are likely to be affected by tariff increases on exports to the US, which account for around 10% of total exports. In 2027, the projected recovery of global demand is expected to help raise GDP growth to about 1.5% (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Switzerland's textile sector is small but globally influential, characterized by innovation, quality and sustainability leadership. High costs and a strong currency limit volume manufacturing, but the country excels in technical textiles, advanced materials, and precision-driven design. With strong R&D ecosystems, environmental responsibility, and brand credibility, Switzerland positions itself as a premium innovation hub for the future of sustainable textiles.

## Political factors

 Switzerland's strong political stability, neutrality and efficient regulatory framework create a secure environment for textile investment and innovation. Through bilateral agreements, Switzerland aligns with many EU trade and environmental standards, ensuring smooth market access. Extensive global trade network and bilateral deals support export competitiveness for Swiss textile and apparel products.

## Economic factors

 Switzerland has proved resilient through the pandemic, geopolitical turmoil and reverberations in energy markets.

 Swiss textiles focus on technical, luxury, and precision-based segments. Manufacturing is capital-intensive and specialized, relying on automation and innovation rather than scale. The Swiss franc's strength can reduce export price competitiveness but reinforces the premium image of Swiss-made goods. Consistent government and private sector investment sustain productivity and global competitiveness.

## Social factors

 Swiss consumers and producers value craftsmanship, durability and precision, reinforcing a focus on high-end textile markets. Growing domestic and global demand for sustainable and ethically sourced textiles aligns with Switzerland's strong environmental values. High education levels and strong vocational training systems support innovation and quality assurance.

### Technological factors

 Switzerland is an Innovation Leader, performing at 139.8% of the EU average in 2025. Swiss firms are global leaders in advanced materials, smart textiles, and textile machinery. Strong integration of robotics, 3D knitting, and AI-driven production enhances precision and cost efficiency. Pioneering eco-friendly dyeing, recycling, and water-efficient textile technologies.

## Legal factors

 Swiss laws on environmental protection, labour safety and consumer transparency exceed many international norms. Although non-EU, Swiss manufacturers adhere to EPR and ISO certifications to maintain market access. Balanced framework ensures worker protection without excessive rigidity, maintaining productivity and ethical standards.

## Environmental factors

 National strategies emphasize circular economy, carbon neutrality, and responsible resource use across industries. Policies and partnerships encourage textile reuse, recycling, and reduced waste generation.

## 12.1.5 Belgium

Belgium's economic growth is expected to slow down in 2025, mainly due to high global uncertainty and decreased exports. GDP growth is projected to increase slightly in 2026 and in 2027, supported by improving external demand and investments. Inflation is forecasted to decrease in 2025 and in 2026, driven by lower price pressures for industrial goods and energy before rising in 2027 following an increase in energy prices. Based on currently known policies, the general government deficit is projected to increase driven by defence and interest expenditure. As a result, the government debt is expected to continue

## its upward trend (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Belgium's textile sector is transforming from traditional manufacturing toward innovationdriven, sustainable, and high-value production. While high costs and regulatory demands pose challenges, opportunities lie in technical textiles, digital transformation, and circular economy leadership.

Belgium's textile industry remains relatively small. The sector is highly export-oriented, with technical textiles making up the largest share of production. In 2023, exports to key European markets such as France, Germany, and the Netherlands declined, while Italy showed some growth. Despite lower production capacity utilization and contracting employment, the industry continued to invest in sustainability and digitalization.

Belgium sources most of its textile raw materials such as cotton, wool, and synthetics through imports, while its domestic fiber agriculture is centered on high-quality flax grown mainly in Flanders.

Belgium's textile and apparel industry is dominated by SMEs and is highly export-oriented, with 80% of output sold within the EU. It holds a leading global position in high-end home textiles, especially carpets, with home textiles making up 40% and industrial textiles 20% of output. Backed by a strong textile machinery sector, the industry focuses on high-value, specialized products

Belgium generates about 15.5 kg of textile waste per capita and has the largest share of separate textile collection in Europe, supported by multiple collection systems. However, with sorting dominated by manual processes, around 75% of sorted post-consumer textiles are exported mainly to Africa for reuse and Asia for recycling or landfill. Domestic recycling capacity remains very limited.

Major players in apparel manufacturing include De Paepe N.V., Avetex N.V., Gelitex B.V.B.A. and fibre producers like Balta Group, Brutex, Concordia Textiles, and Alsico (Fashion for Good).

## Political factors

 Belgium benefits from strong political stability and adherence to EU trade and environmental policies, providing a predictable business climate for textile firms. Free trade access within the EU and preferential trade deals facilitate textile exports and raw material imports. Belgian and EU programs provide subsidies and tax incentives for sustainable textile production, R&D, and circular economy initiatives. Divergent regulations and funding priorities between Flanders and Wallonia may complicate sector-wide strategies.

## Economic factors

 Belgium's economy has been relatively resilient to recent shocks and is expected to continue to grow steadily. Belgium ranks sixth in the European Union in terms of GDP per capita. Belgium's labour costs are among the highest in Europe, pressuring profit margins for traditional textile manufacturing. Strong logistics infrastructure and proximity to major markets (Germany, France, Netherlands) support trade efficiency.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 107,09 Belgian consumers and B2B buyers increasingly prefer ethically produced, traceable and eco-friendly textiles. Skills transfer and vocational training are essential as the traditional textile workforce ages. Belgium's global reputation in fashion design boosts innovation and brand value. There is a growing pressure for transparency in sourcing, labour practices and circular use of materials.

## Technological factors

 Belgium is a Strong Innovator, performing at 122.6% of the EU average in 2025. Strong collaborations between universities, textile clusters and manufacturers drive innovation. SMEs face high upfront costs in adopting Industry 4.0 technologies, potentially widening the gap between large and small players.

## Legal factors

 Compliance with EPR adds operational complexity. Any shifts in EU trade policy could impact raw material sourcing and export competitiveness.

## Environmental factors

 Achieving the green transition requires setting up binding targets and improving coordination of policy across federal and regional governments. EU Green Deal and Circular Economy Action Plan push Belgian firms to adopt recycling, bio-based fibres and waste-reduction strategies.

## 12.1.6 The Netherlands

The Netherlands' real GDP is projected to increase by 1.7% in 2025, due to strong domestic demand and despite global uncertainties such as US tariffs affecting trade. Higher wages are expected to boost private consumption, while government consumption growth stays high. Inflation continues to be elevated due to rising costs in services and processed food but is expected to gradually decrease in 2026. GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 1.3% as uncertainties persist, but to then recover to 1.7% in 2027. The government deficit is forecasted to increase to 1.9% in 2025 and to widen further to 2.7% in 2026 before decreasing to 2.0% in 2027. Public debt is set to reach 48.1% of GDP in 2027 (European Economic Forecast, Autumn 2025).

Netherland is the 3rd largest exporter and 5th largest importer of apparel within the EU. Despite lacking a significant domestic apparel manufacturing industry, its role as a major port has positioned it as a top apparel EU exporter. A large share of its imports (61.6%) come from outside the EU. The main supplier countries are China, Germany, Bangladesh, and Türkiye.

Netherlands is heavily dependent on imports for textiles, clothing, household textiles, cotton and synthetic fibres. Carpets and rugs dominate Dutch textile conversion and are the main export surplus category.

The Netherlands has very limited domestic clothing production, mostly restricted to samples and prototypes, with large-scale manufacturing outsourced abroad mainly Asia, Turkey, and North Africa. Specialized, high-end textile and carpet firms still partly produced in the Netherlands due to their capital-intensive nature. The country also acts as a reexport hub.

Netherlands is a major sorting and exporting hub, receiving large volumes of textiles from other EU Member States and re-exporting significant quantities outside the EU. It has a well-established collection and sorting ecosystem with a separate capture rate of 37%. Notably, almost the entire domestic sorting capacity is used to sort textiles from Germany, while 55 % of the collected textiles in the Netherlands are sorted abroad (Fashion for Good).

## Political factors

 The Netherlands offers a predictable political and regulatory environment, fostering confidence for textile investors and innovators. As an EU member, the Netherlands follows the European regulations and the EU Textile Strategy, driving sustainability and circularity. National initiatives like the Circular Economy by 2050 program promote recycling, textile waste reduction and innovation in eco-design. Strong diplomatic and trade ties within the EU and globally make the Netherlands a key logistics hub for textile imports and exports.

## Economic factors

 The Netherlands ranks third in the European Union in terms of GDP per capita. Rotterdam Port and Schiphol Airport position the country as a major gateway. Domestic textile manufacturing is limited. Competitiveness is achieved through innovation, design, and sustainable production rather than cost leadership. Stable GDP growth, strong consumer purchasing power and access to EU funding encourage R&D investment in sustainable textiles.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 112,39 Dutch consumers are among Europe's most environmentally conscious, accelerating demand for ethically produced, recyclable and durable clothing. The Netherlands' strong design education and creative industries foster innovation in fashion and materials. Growing social pressure for fair labour practices and traceable supply chains influences sourcing and production strategies. Circular fashion, repair, and resale platforms reflect changing consumption habits.

## Technological factors

 The Netherlands is an Innovation Leader, performing at 129.1% of the EU average in 2025. Research institutions collaborate with industry on bio-based fibres, textile recycling, and digital manufacturing. Dutch firms pioneer fibre-to-fibre recycling, biopolymer development, and closedloop production systems.

 Adoption of automation, 3D printing, AI-based design and supply chain digitalization enhances productivity and transparency. Start-ups benefit from public and private funding for material innovation and ecoefficiency.

## Legal factors

 Compliance with EU and Dutch sustainability legislation (EPR, eco-design, waste management) shapes industry practices. Strong IP enforcement supports innovation in textile design and material technology.

## Environmental factors

 The Netherlands is a European frontrunner in textile recycling, reuse and sustainable production. Tight environmental controls encourage adoption of cleaner dyeing and finishing processes. Collaboration between government, academia and industry supports ecoinnovation across the textile lifecycle.

The Netherlands is the fifth-largest market for apparel in the EU. It had an apparel import value of €17.2 billion in 2023 and an average annual growth rate of 2.8% between 2018 and 2023. By 2030, the Netherlands aims to have all textile products contain 50% sustainable materials (at least 30% of which being recycled content) and 50% of postconsumer material recycled. These targets provide incentives for Dutch brands and retailers to choose suppliers that have sustainable materials sourcing and development capabilities. The Netherlands is also home to several industry support organisations focused on sustainability, including Fashion for Good. This organisation connects companies that work on sustainable innovations with brands, retailers, manufacturers and funders. Of the ten innovators selected for its 2024 Innovation Programme, all directly related to sustainable materials. According to Statista, in 2024, 73% of the consumers in the Netherlands said they considered sustainability in their purchasing decisions and consumer behaviour. While this figure is relatively high, it indicates a decline compared to previous years. According to the Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS) database of certified companies, there are 17 Dutch retailers that are GOTS-certified across all textiles product segments. Ethical Dutch brands include MUD Jeans, Jackalo, Kuyichi, Kings of Indigo, Unrecorded, Saint Basics, A-dam, O My Bag, Goat Organic Apparel, RVDK and Noumenon. More brands can be found on thegreenlabels, which is an Amsterdam-based e-commerce platform that curates green labels.

## 12.1.7 Ireland

Ireland's GDP is forecasted to grow exceptionally by 10.7% in 2025, largely reflecting strong export activity in the first half of the year. As the frontloading effect unwinds, growth is expected to moderate to 0.2% in 2026, reflecting a technical base effect from 2025 dynamics, before stabilising at 2.9% in 2027. Inflation is projected to remain contained, while the labour market is expected to continue expanding. The outlook for public finances is positive but marked by significant risks to corporation tax revenues (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Ireland's textile sector is small but evolving, focusing on sustainability, innovation and design-led production. While high costs and limited industrial scale constrain mass manufacturing, Ireland's EU access, strong R&D ecosystem and growing consumer demand for ethical products position the country as a niche hub for sustainable and highvalue textile innovation.

## Political factors

 Ireland's strong governance, EU membership and transparent business policies create a predictable environment for textile operations and investment. Agencies like Enterprise Ireland and IDA Ireland promote R&D, sustainability and digital transformation in manufacturing, including textiles. As an EU member bordering the UK, Ireland benefits from being a key logistics and production gateway for textile trade between the EU and UK markets.

## Economic factors

 Ireland ranks second in the European Union in terms of GDP per capita. Despite GDP volatility, driven by some multinational-dominated sectors, the domestic economy remains robust. Ireland's capacity to service its debt is supported by large liquid asset buffers and a favourable debt structure. Traditional textile manufacturing has declined, but niche growth areas such as technical textiles, sustainable fashion, and design-led brands are emerging. Domestic manufacturing remains expensive, pushing firms toward high-value and low-volume production. Ireland's access to EU markets and trade agreements supports exports of specialty textiles and apparel.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 114,85 Irish consumers increasingly favour eco-friendly, ethically sourced and locally produced garments. Ireland's vibrant design, fashion, and craft sectors foster innovation and valueadded textile products. Strong tertiary education and training institutions support design, material science, and sustainable production skills.

## Technological factors

 Ireland is a Strong Innovator, performing at 123.1% of the EU average in 2025. Collaboration between universities and research centres supports textile engineering and sustainable production technologies. Adoption of automation, 3D designs and e-commerce platforms enables small producers to compete globally. There is growing investment in textile recycling, low-impact dyeing and biodegradable materials. Access to EU Horizon Europe funds and national innovation grants supports hightech and sustainable textile initiatives.

## Legal factors

 Ireland enforces EU textile regulations and eco-label standards. Strong labour laws and consumer rights frameworks ensure ethical production and fair-trade practices. Effective IP protection encourages design and brand innovation within the textile and fashion sectors.

## Environmental factors

 Ireland's roadmap toward net-zero emissions by 2050 encourages sustainable textile production and renewable energy use. Government support for circular economy models fosters textile recycling and waste reduction. Limited domestic recycling infrastructure for textiles and dependence on imported raw materials.

## 12.1.8 Spain

Real GDP growth is expected to remain strong in 2025, at 2.9%, moderating gradually afterwards. Economic activity is set to be driven by domestic demand, supported by continued strong labour market performance upholding private consumption and by the contribution of investment. Headline inflation is projected to ease from 2.5% in 2025 to 2.0% in 2026. The general government deficit is set to keep decreasing from 2.5% of GDP in 2025 to 2.1% in 2027, helped by the tax package adopted last year, the phase-out of energy measures and favourable economic developments. The debt-to-GDP ratio is set to decline further and move below 100% next year (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Spain's textile sector is dynamic, innovative and increasingly sustainable, combining fashion excellence, advanced technology, and circular economy practices. Despite challenges from high costs and global competition, Spain's strong design culture, digital transformation, and government-backed green transition, position it as among the leaders in sustainable fashion and textile manufacturing.

Textiles and Apparel is vital to the Spanish economy, contributing 2.8% of the national GDP and 8.7% of all exports. Though a net importer of apparel, it stands as one of the top 6 EU apparel exporters and importers with 63.6% of it coming from developing countries. Spain is the EU's second-largest cotton grower after Greece, producing just over 20% of the EU's cotton output, with cultivation almost entirely concentrated in Andalucía. The country's cotton textile processing industry however is primarily located in Catalonia, forming the main hub for yarn spinning, weaving and fabric production.

Spain's Textiles and Apparel sector is SME-driven and highly fragmented, relying on subcontracting, with vertical integration largely limited to large groups like Inditex. Most companies operate as small, specialized units within broader supply chains. The industry primarily focuses on mass-market and mid-value fashion.

Spain is considered the leading manufacturer of recycled fibre in Europe. Producing around 61,000 of which 50,000 tonnes derived from pre-consumer textile waste. With an estimated waste rate of 19 kg/person, Spain generates 890,244 tonnes of textile waste

annually, of which only 108,296 tonnes are sorted. Recycling in Spain is mainly mechanical and labour-intensive (Fashion for Good).

## Political factors

 In recent years, Spain has pursued an ambitious green transition agenda that is driving the modernisation of its economy, the creation of sustainable jobs, increased competitiveness and reduced reliance on external energy sources. Spain's political stability and EU membership ensure consistent trade, labour and environmental regulations. National and regional programs, such as Plan de Recuperación, Transformación y Resiliencia, provide funding for digitalization and sustainability in textile manufacturing. As part of the single market, Spain benefits from free trade with EU members and adherence to EU environmental and circular economy policies. Textile clusters in Catalonia, Valencia, and Galicia receive targeted government support for innovation and export growth.

## Economic factors

 Spain's GDP per capita ranks below the EU average. Spain's capacity to service its debt is underpinned by solid economic expansion and strong revenue growth. The country is set to exit post-programme surveillance by end-2025, being the first Member State to successfully conclude the postprogramme monitoring. Spain is one of the EU's largest textile and apparel producers, driven by strong domestic brands. Strong demand for Spanish fashion and technical textiles across the EU, Latin America and North Africa. Small and medium enterprises form the backbone of the sector, relying on innovation, flexibility and craftsmanship.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 103,94 Spanish consumers increasingly value eco-friendly, ethical and locally made textiles, influencing brand strategies. Spain's global influence in fashion and design (e.g., Barcelona and Madrid) drives textile innovation and brand prestige. Regarding the workforce challenges there is a need for digital and sustainability training to modernize traditional manufacturing skills. There is rising consumer and NGO pressure for transparency and fair labour standards in domestic and global supply chains.

## Technological factors

 Spain is a Moderate Innovator, performing at 92.7% of the EU average in 2025. Growth in smart textiles, biobased fabrics, and recycled fibres is supported by R&D centres like AITEX and Texfor. Advancements are recorded in textile recycling, eco-dyeing, and waterless finishing processes.

Digital sales and data-driven production models are reshaping textile value chains.

### Legal factors

 Compliance with EPR obligations for textile waste management. Strong labour laws ensure fair wages and working conditions as well as ethical standards. Legal requirements for labelling and origin disclosure reinforce trust in Spanish textile brands.

## Environmental factors

 Spain's Circular Economy Strategy 2030 promotes textile reuse, recycling and reduced resource consumption. The textile industry faces pressure to minimize water use and chemical pollution, especially in dyeing and finishing. Environmental responsibility enhances Spain's international reputation for quality and eco-conscious textiles.

Spain is the third-largest market for apparel in the EU. It had an apparel import value of €19.6 billion in 2023 and an average annual growth rate of 3.5% between 2018 and 2023. It is a world leader in fast fashion thanks to its flagship apparel groups Inditex (which owns Zara, Bershka, Massimo Dutti, Oysho, Pull&Bear, Stradivarius, Uterqüe and Lefties) and Mango. A 2021 study revealed that sustainable fashion represents a small segment (8.65%) of the Spanish fashion market. A lack of trust in fashion companies and their sustainability claims is the main reason that Spanish consumers do not buy sustainable products or do so less often. The second main reason is the perceived higher cost of sustainable clothing. According to the Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS) database of certified companies, there is only one GOTS-certified Spanish retailer across all textiles product segments. This is the lowest number amongst the top six European importers of apparel. Despite these issues, there are several smaller ethical Spanish brands that cater to the growing European market. Ethical Spanish brands include SKFK, allSisters, The Nude Label, Cus, Beyond Skin, Ecoology, Lefrik, Swim Against, amt., Ecoalf, TwoThirds and Thinking Mu.

## 12.1.9 Luxembourg

Real GDP growth in Luxembourg is projected to remain subdued in 2025, held back by weaker investment and net exports, but supported by private consumption. Growth is expected to accelerate in 2026 and 2027, benefitting from low interest rates which should stimulate investments and private spending while bolstering the exports of financial services. After remaining high in 2025, headline inflation is set to slow in 2026 following electricity tariff cuts before rising to just below 2% in 2027. Windfall revenues led to a surplus of the general government balance in 2024, which is expected to turn to a deficit over 2025-27 (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Luxembourg's textile sector is small but innovation-driven, focusing on high-value, sustainable and technical textiles rather than mass production. With strong R&D capabilities, green finance and EU policy alignment, Luxembourg positions itself as a niche hub for advanced materials and circular textile innovation, though high costs and limited

manufacturing scale constrain expansion.

## Political factors

 Luxembourg's strong political stability, transparent governance and deep integration within the EU ensure a predictable policy environment for businesses. The government promotes green technologies, digitalization, and circular economy initiatives indirectly benefiting textile firms focused on sustainability or advanced materials. As an EU member, Luxembourg benefits from free trade access to European and global textile markets.

## Economic factors

 As Luxembourg increasingly faces capacity constraints, transitioning from labour force expansion to a growth model centred on skills and innovation is essential. Luxembourg has the highest GDP per capita in the European Union. Manufacturing in Luxembourg is expensive, discouraging large-scale textile production but encouraging specialization in high-value niches. A stable economy, high purchasing power and access to EU funding make Luxembourg attractive for R&D and design-oriented textile ventures. Most consumer textiles are imported with domestic firms focusing mainly on innovation and specialized B2B applications.

## Social factors

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 113,10 Luxembourg's population values sustainability, quality and ethical sourcing, influencing demand for eco-friendly textile products. A diverse, multilingual labour pool supports international collaboration and innovation in design and research. Luxembourg lacks a large traditional textile base, focusing instead on advanced materials and niche applications. Growing interest in sustainable fashion and circular consumption models aligns with national sustainability goals.

## Technological factors

 Luxembourg is a Strong Innovator, performing at 114.5% of the EU average in 2025. Widespread adoption of automation, data analytics, and digital manufacturing tools enhances productivity and traceability in niche textile segments. Collaboration between textiles, materials science and ICT sectors fosters innovation in smart and functional fabrics. High-tech R&D presence contrasts with small-scale domestic production capacity.

## Legal factors

 Strong legal protection for intellectual property benefits R&D and design-oriented textile firms. High labour standards and social protections ensure workforce quality but increase production costs.

 Growing legal emphasis on ESG and corporate sustainability disclosures affect textile-related companies and retailers.

## Environmental factors

 Although significant progress has been made in the treatment of waste, its production remains stagnant and therefore problematic. Luxembourg's Circular Economy Strategy encourages recycling, material reuse and low-carbon production. National aim for carbon neutrality by 2050 promotes eco-friendly textile technologies.

## 12.1.10 Greece

Greece's economy is expected to continue to grow at a strong pace with a projected expansion of 2.1% in 2025 and 2.2% in 2026, driven by steady consumption and investments supported by EU funds. GDP growth is set to moderate to 1.7% in 2027 as the Recovery and Resilience Funds comes to an end. Inflation is expected to decline gradually, to 2.4% by 2027, as solid demand and the projected increase in energy prices will put an upward pressure on consumer prices. Unemployment is decreasing to levels not seen in more than a decade, but structural challenges remain. Greece's fiscal outlook remains favourable over 2025-27, with broadly stable primary surpluses, despite the tax cuts and social measures. Strong nominal GDP growth and budget surpluses are set to keep the debt-to-GDP ratio on a downward trend, moving below 140% by 2027 (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

The Greek textile sector retains structural strengths, heritage, natural resources (cotton), geographic position, and the potential to move into higher‑value, technologically sophisticated and sustainable segments. However, it faces serious headwinds as cost competitiveness (energy cost), shrinking domestic demand, increased imports, skill gaps and the need for modernization investments.

## Political

 EU membership and regulatory alignment binds firms to the EU rules. Labour relations and periodic strikes can disrupt production operations. Political focus on raising wages affects operating costs.

## Economic

 Greece's economy has been outpacing the euro area average since 2021. Greece's GDP per capita ranks below the EU average. Greece's capacity to service its debt is helped by a declining public debt-to-GDP ratio, large government cash reserves and low short and long-term debt sustainability risks. Growing disbursements of the Recovery and Resilience Funds, continuous employment gains and disinflation, and recent structural reforms are all expected to support growth. Greece's textile exporters face tight margins versus low-cost countries. Minimum wage increases in recent years have pushed base labour costs higher, affecting competitiveness for low-value garment production.

## Social

 2024 Disposable household income (per capita, Index 2008): 84,13 Consumer sustainability expectations are increasing, driving demand for ethical and durable products. Tourism supports domestic retail in summer months (lifestyle textiles), while demographics and urban consumption patterns shape year-round demand.

## Technological

 Greece is a Moderate Innovator, performing at 75.8% of the EU average in 2025. Innovation in sustainable fibres (bio-based, recycled) and increasing adoption of digital design/production tools create differentiation and higher-margin product lines. Circular-economy technologies (sorting, textile recycling, chemical recycling) are becoming commercially relevant as regulation and supply-chain buyers demand traceability.

### Legal

 EU rules (WFD, EPR) push producers toward take-back, traceability and ecodesign and compliance will require investment and new business models.

## Environmental

 Greece is accelerating its green transition, aiming to improve its environmental performance. Challenges persist across SDG performance indices. Greece revised its waste management framework and adopted the circular economy action plan (2021–2025) as a national strategy. The national programme to promote waste prevention (2021–2030) was also enacted. Greece is rolling out initiatives and regional projects to improve textile collection/recycling and overall waste management.

## 12.1.11 Turkey

Economic growth is expected to remain resilient at 3.4% in 2026 and to pick up to 4.0% in 2027. Despite a tight monetary policy stance, domestic demand remained robust in the first half of 2025. In view of services price stickiness and elevated inflation expectations, inflation is expected to come down only slowly in the next two years. Fiscal consolidation is expected to advance slowly and government indebtedness to remain moderate (European Economic Forecast. Autumn 2025).

Turkey's textile sector remains a global manufacturing powerhouse, balancing cost competitiveness, innovation and sustainability transition. Despite macroeconomic volatility and regulatory pressures, the industry's strategic location, skilled workforce and strong export base position it as a key supplier to Europe and an emerging leader in technical and eco-friendly textiles.

Turkey is the world's 7th largest textile exporter and the 3rd largest supplier to the EU, serving key markets like Germany, Spain, UK, and the Netherlands. It serves as a key

nearshoring hub for EU brands seeking speed, responsiveness, and lower inventory risk. It exports 60% of its apparel production. Major brands sourcing from Turkey include Boss, Inditex, Mango, Bestseller and Primark.

Turkey is a large cotton producer but remains heavily reliant on imports of both cotton and man-made fibres, with particularly high dependence on synthetic raw materials. Main cotton exporting countries to Turkey are the U.S., Uzbekistan, Brazil, Greece and India. In 2021, it exported \$18.3 billion worth of apparel, about 60% of which was cotton-based. The country also contributes 15% of Western Europe's synthetic fibre output. Turkey's fabric industry is strongly export-oriented, dominated by woven fabrics (62% of production value) and followed by knitted fabrics (38%), while it ranks as the world's 5th largest denim exporter.

Turkey's Textiles and Apparel industry operates on a vertically integrated model, which reduces lead times, ensures raw material security, and supports broad product diversification. This integration positions Turkey as a reliable sourcing alternative for global brands. The sector is dominated by SMEs, enabling both small-batch and large-scale production without the need for long pre-orders. Over time, the industry has shifted from low-value to high-value goods reflecting skilled labour, strong design and tech investment. Turkey's textile recycling sector is centered in the Usak and Izmir regions, primarily operating mechanical downcycling units that process pre-consumer waste. While postindustrial waste is efficiently managed, post-consumer waste remains underutilized due to weak collection and recycling systems. Most of the recycled yarn produced is exported.

Major apparel manufacturers include Akcakaya Textile, Konsey Textile, Zeria Textile and fibre producers like Kipas Textile, Gurteks and Kaynak Group (Fashion for Good).

## Political factors

 Turkey's customs union with the EU and proximity to European, Asian and Middle Eastern markets make the country a vital textile hub. State incentives for exports, investment in organized industrial zones and support for digitalization and green transition strengthen competitiveness. Regional instability and political relations with the EU and U.S. can affect trade flows, investor confidence, and access to funding.

## Economic factors

 Turkey has been one of the fastest-growing economies in the OECD over the past decade. However, the income gap with OECD countries remains significant. Textiles and apparel account for around 15–20% of Turkey's exports, with the EU as the largest market. The Turkish lira's depreciation boosts export competitiveness but raises costs for imported raw materials and machinery. Inflation and energy prices press margins despite relatively low labour costs compared to Western Europe.

## Social factors

 Turkey benefits from an experienced workforce in textile manufacturing and design, with strong vocational education programs. Rich textile tradition combined with growing fashion and home textile brands enhances global appeal.

 Global buyers demand higher transparency and social compliance, pressuring firms to meet international labour and safety standards.

## Technological factors

 Turkey is an Emerging Innovator, performing at 58.0% of the EU average in 2025. Turkish textile firms increasingly adopt Industry 4.0, digital printing, and AI-based production planning to improve efficiency. Textile Technology Centres and university collaborations foster innovation in ecofriendly and smart textiles. Smaller firms struggle with financing high-tech upgrades amid economic uncertainty.

## Legal factors

 Compliance with EU customs union rules and standards is essential for maintaining access to key markets. Government efforts to align labour regulations with international norms continue, though enforcement inconsistencies remain a concern. Turkey is gradually adopting EU-style environmental and waste management regulations, especially for textile dyeing and finishing operations.

## Environmental factors

 Growing demand from EU buyers for low-carbon and circular textile production drives green investment. Textile dyeing and wastewater remain major environmental issues, prompting stricter controls and treatment investments. Manufacturers increasingly use solar and natural gas to reduce emissions and energy costs.

# 13. CHANGES INFLUENCING THE COMMERCIAL

# PROSPECTS IN POST-PROJECT PERIOD

The textile industry is undergoing significant changes influenced by technological advancements, sustainability initiatives, shifting of production strategies, and evolving consumer preferences. Yet, the shift from a linear to a circular model of global supply chain opens opportunities for growth that extend into areas such as recycling, repair, resale and innovative material development.

The importance of online sales for textile production and consumption is growing since online trade has increased accessibility and convenience for consumers, enabling lower prices, and greater product variety. The integration of AI, IoT and blockchain is revolutionizing various facets of the industry. IoT devices enable real-time monitoring of production processes. Blockchain facilitates transparency allowing consumers to trace products from raw materials to retail. Digital traceability using blockchain and IoT will transform the tracking of textile flows, bringing transparency to a complex value chain. AIdriven predictive analytics will enhance demand forecasting, reducing overproduction and inventory costs. DPPs will create new opportunities for brands to monetize services. AI is expected to play a significant role in the whole DPP endeavour. AI tools will improve the second-hand fashion market by offering personalized shopping experiences, such as outfit suggestions thereby increasing consumer engagement and sales. Additionally, AI will optimise supply chain management, from inventory control to personalized marketing strategies, leading to increased operational efficiency. Indeed, digital technologies provide opportunities to enhance the efficiency, productivity and competitiveness of the sector.

![](_page_282_Diagram_7.jpeg)

Technologies like IoT, AI, big data analytics and automation have the potential to transform the textiles industry, as well as how we consume its products (EEA).

quicker responses to trends and provides valuable consumer insights, facilitating targeted product development and marketing. Implementing augmented reality and virtual try-on technologies can further enhance the online shopping experience.

Investing in fair wages, safe working conditions, and skill development programs enhances brands' image and compliance with ethical standards. As automation becomes more prevalent, reskilling the workforce to adapt to new technologies is an opportunity to maintain employment levels and improve productivity.

There is also a growing emphasis on sustainable and circular practices within the industry. Brands are adopting circular business models, focusing on reducing waste, utilizing recyclable materials, and implementing efficient resource usage. Collaborations between brands and recycling firms are expected to optimize post-consumer waste management. The industry is also shifting towards eco-friendly materials, aligning with consumer demand for environmentally responsible products. As consumers become more conscious of the environmental impact of their purchasing decisions, there is a growing demand for sustainable and eco-friendly products. This shift is encouraging brands to adopt recycling practices and incorporate recycled materials into their offerings. Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) policies are also drivers for businesses aiming to ensure long-term viability and meet evolving consumer expectations.

Yet, fashion companies are interested in sustainability and circularity, but as soon as it's clear that it will be more expensive, they quickly lose interest. Producing more sustainably in the fashion industry is still largely a marketing term. Clothing brands continue to prioritize the lowest possible production costs to offer their products to a large consumer base.

Geopolitical tensions and supply chain disruptions have prompted brands to explore nearshoring and reshoring production strategies. Producing goods closer to home markets reduces times, enhances reliability and allows greater control over production processes. Regions like Eastern Europe and North Africa are becoming significant for the European market, while Mexico and Central America are pivotal for North America. However, challenges such as limited domestic manufacturing capacity and higher labour costs persist, making a large-scale shift to domestic production unlikely in the immediate future. Trade policies, particularly tariffs, significantly impact the industry. Increases of U.S. tariffs lead to higher clothing prices, affecting consumer purchasing behaviour and prompting a shift towards second-hand markets. The era of cheap clothing, sustained over the past decades, may be coming to an end due to these trade dynamics.

Consumer behaviour is shifting towards sustainability and cost-effectiveness. However, still a large part of consumers is not environmental conscious. Despite ongoing discussions about sustainability, the willingness to pay more for sustainable products remains limited, discouraging producers from fully committing to circularity. Consumers tend to follow trends and buy what is easily accessible, often without considering the environmental impact. Lack of awareness leads to lower participation in buying sustainable clothing. On the other hand, the second-hand fashion market is experiencing growth, driven by financial constraints and imitation. Global sales of second-hand clothing are growing, surpassing overall fashion market growth, with projections indicating further increase. But also, second-hand shopping still faces challenges, as many people worry about

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

hygiene, finding the right size, or the time it takes to search for good items.

Furthermore, as repairing clothes is often seen as expensive or too much effort, it is not very popular.

By June 2027, Member States must transpose textile EPR into national legislation. By April 2028 operational textile EPR systems must be in place. At the moment there is a mismatch between ambitious EU legislation and implementation realities in Member States. Exact fees are unknown. How much producers will be charged is unknown, but it is likely to depend on social and environmental criteria. Fast fashion brands may face "penalties". There is much to be decided and there are concerns whether the time will be enough. Countries must finalize what it means within their country, and reuse and recycling targets are still to be defined. In any case, EPR is fast becoming a cornerstone of sustainable business practice and there is indeed a transformative potential of welldesigned EPR systems in achieving a circular textile economy.

Yet, an EPR approach by itself will be insufficient to address all environmental impacts of garments and additional policies are likely needed to complement EPR.

Harmonized EPR would increase the effectiveness of the fee modulation and the effectiveness of the R&D for new and existing recycling and preparation for recycling technologies across Europe due to economies of scale.

Strong networks are needed to connect brands with innovators and create more opportunities to scale these recycling processes. Enhancing industry collaboration is crucial to bridge data gaps and improve transparency, especially for new materials. Partnerships with brands and suppliers are vital for collecting real-time data, driving innovation, and increasing transparency, all crucial for advancing sustainability goals. Success will depend on strong collaboration across supply chains, with brands, retailers, and recyclers working together to make EPR effective. Coordination of actions across science, technology, policy, and finance, as well as collective action are necessary. Collaboration across the supply chain is crucial for addressing circularity challenges, but building partnerships can be difficult when goals or technological systems are not aligned. Multi-level governance involving environment, trade, and ministries can ensure collaborative solutions. Indeed, there is significant potential for cross-sector and crossborder collaboration among producers, recyclers, and policymakers to share best practices and optimize waste management.

"EPR is a tool, not the silver bullet. It can only work if it's well designed, inclusive, and fair. If France's experience teaches us anything, it's that governance, eco-modulation, and the right incentives matter" (Recycling Europe).

"The limited available experience suggests that EPR improves rates of separate collection of garments. In addition to improving collection and sorting, there is the possibility that EPR can help to reduce environmental impacts generated by the production, use, and disposal of garments. This is primarily by changing the economic incentives for producers, consumers, and waste managers. However, an EPR approach by itself will be insufficient to address all environmental impacts of garments and additional policies are likely needed to complement EPR" (OECD).

To divert textiles from landfill, incineration, or leakage into the environment, it is crucial to build collection infrastructure at scale. To establish separate collection systems at scale, funding is needed to cover the cost associated with managing all discarded textiles. While the market potential for textile-to-textile recycling in Europe is clear, realising a viable business case remains challenging due to limited access to quality feedstock and lack of infrastructure at scale. Investments in improved sorting, recycling, and collection infrastructure, especially in regions with emerging EPR frameworks, can boost overall system efficiency. Significant investments are required to create separate collection, reuse, and recycling infrastructure at scale. Infrastructure gaps, such as inadequate sorting technology and limited domestic recycling capacity hinder progress. Investment in advanced recycling technologies capable of handling complex textile blends will increase the feasibility of circular textile practices. But also, it should be noted that although many solutions are being developed and tested, they are not yet market ready. The industry requires large volumes of new machines that still face significant restrictions due to being relatively new.

The European Circular Textile Coalition is calling for urgent EU action to match regulatory ambition with investment in recycling and manufacturing systems, because without system readiness, even the most forward-looking regulations risk falling short.

FEAD and EuRIC have argued that without urgent support, valuable reusable and recyclable materials will be lost, and a whole industrial ecosystem that is key to the circular transition will collapse before EPR takes effect.

Although promising new technologies like near-infrared light scanning are helping streamline the recycling process, it remains largely a tedious, manual task. Manual sorting, which is still the primary method, remains inefficient and costly and automation could improve the quality and purity of feed stock available for recyclers. The majority of recycling facilities today process one or a few specific material types and before processing can even begin, non-textile components such as zippers and buttons, and treatments like dyes and waxes, must often be removed.

Pre-processing is critical and harmonised mechanical pre-processing for post-consumer textile waste, ideally co-located with sorting facilities for improving yields and efficiency, is vital to improve feedstock consistency.

Brands and retailers face significant challenges due to the limited availability and scale of textile recycling infrastructure. Recycling processes also need improvement, namely by being more energy efficient and ensuring that recycled fibres are of high enough quality to replace virgin sources. The brands that will lead the race on scaling recycling will be far better positioned to navigate long-term resource scarcity and regulatory change.

A major barrier is also the ongoing lack of consistent demand for recycled fibres. Without strong and sustained purchasing commitments from brands, textile recyclers struggle to secure funding, cover operational costs, or justify investment in expanding capacity.

Virgin textile materials often remain cheaper and more readily available, creating a price gap that undermines the business case for using recycled content. Brands also face commercial pressure to keep product prices low. Furthermore, alternative end markets, such as construction, automotive, and furniture sectors, often lack established links with

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

textile recyclers.

Innovations in recycling technologies, such as chemical and mechanical recycling methods, have improved the efficiency and quality of recycled textiles. These advancements make recycling a more viable option for manufacturers and contribute to the industry's growth. Chemical recycling processes show the most promise, yielding truly circular materials. Given that most clothing today is made from a blend of organic and synthetic fibres, with polyester accounting for more than half of global production, it is no surprise that many innovative programs focus on perfecting the recycling process for polyester or poly blends, often using proprietary solutions featuring chemical solvents and catalysts to deconstruct materials.

Fibre-to-fibre textile recycling is gaining momentum as a key driver for transitioning to a circular fashion industry. This shift will likely increase demand for post-consumer textile collection, sorting, and recycling infrastructure in the EU, necessitating significant investment (Fashion for Good).

Retailers view resale as a new revenue stream that helps them stay competitive and acquire new customers. Therefore, a reliable EPR system, with DPP will be a major contributor to the growth of the textile resale market.

As long as circular offerings like second-hand, rental and repair remain expensive or inconvenient, fast fashion will continue to dominate.

Creating a strong internal market for reuse and recycling in Europe will be a key to achieving textiles circularity by 2030.

So, the European textile ecosystem is in a period of profound change, where sustainability, technology, and shifting consumer expectations converge to create a fertile landscape for growth. At the policy level, the EU's commitment to environmental leadership is shaping the industry's trajectory. EPR schemes and broader sustainability regulations are pushing companies to redesign products for durability, recyclability, and reuse. This regulatory momentum not only strengthens the EU's position as a global frontrunner in sustainable textiles but also unlocks new avenues for circular business models, supported by public incentives for green and digital innovation.

Within this framework, companies are increasingly exploring sustainable practices that differentiate them in a competitive marketplace. Investments in eco-friendly materials, biodegradable fabrics, and low-impact dyeing techniques are gaining traction, while circularity principles, from enhanced recycling systems to the adoption of organic and renewable fibres, are becoming central to value creation. At the same time, rapid advancements in technology are opening the door to entirely new product categories.

Digitalization is reshaping the commercial landscape as well. The expansion of ecommerce offers brands unprecedented access to diverse consumer groups, with digital marketing and online platforms strengthening customer engagement and enabling more personalized experiences. Parallel to this shift, technological solutions such as IoTenabled traceability, AI-powered sorting systems, and Digital Product Passports are enhancing transparency and operational efficiency across the supply chain, ultimately
