supporting more robust reuse and recycling infrastructures.

Geopolitical and supply chain considerations are also influencing strategic decisions. European manufacturers are increasingly turning toward reshoring and localized production, particularly in Eastern Europe, North Africa, and the Mediterranean, to reduce emissions, enhance supply chain control, and improve transparency. These moves are complemented by opportunities in emerging markets, especially in Africa and Southeast Asia where rising disposable incomes and evolving consumer preferences create new demand for European textile products.

Meanwhile, consumer behaviour is becoming a transformative force. Growing awareness of the social and environmental impacts of fashion is driving demand for transparency, ethical production, and products with minimal ecological footprints. This shift is contributing to the rapid expansion of the second-hand and resale market, where platforms for renting, swapping, and repurposing garments are moving into the mainstream as cost-effective and sustainable alternatives.

Crucially, many of these opportunities depend on collaboration across sectors. Partnerships with technology firms, material scientists, and innovators in digital infrastructure are accelerating breakthroughs in sustainable materials and recycling systems. Together, these developments position Europe to not only meet regulatory expectations but also to lead the global transition toward a more circular, technologically enabled, and ethically driven textile industry.

With respect to opportunities for improvement in the European textiles sector, design, manufacturing, recycling and governance can accelerate the transition to a circular, competitive, and low-impact textiles ecosystem. More specifically: - Integrate circular design principles into product development to extend lifespan, enable

recycling and reduce waste. - Scale the use of bio-based, regenerative and recycled fibres to reduce dependency on virgin cotton and polyester.

- Deploy digital and low-emission production technologies and expand textile-to-textile

recycling infrastructure. - Build trust and efficiency through transparent data-driven supply chains.

- Strengthen policy framework and human capital.

- Shift consumer behaviour towards sustainable purchasing and longer product use.

Regarding the market fit and market acceptance of TRUSTex KERs, under the current circumstances the following arguments can be addressed.

## KER1. DPP Blockchain Integral Solution

The DPP blockchain solution has strong market potential due to the EU's Digital Product Passport requirements, which create a clear regulatory push for end-to-end traceability. Blockchain directly addresses industry concerns around data accuracy and supply chain opacity, making it appealing to brands seeking compliance and enhanced transparency. Market acceptance is expected to be highest among large brands and manufacturers with existing digital infrastructure, while SMEs may adopt more slowly due to integration costs

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

and technical capacity, though they will ultimately need solutions that support regulatory compliance.

## KER2. Authority Dashboard

The authority dashboard aligns strongly with regulatory needs as national bodies prepare for mandatory monitoring of textile flows, EPR performance, and compliance reporting. Its market fit is high because public authorities lack unified digital interfaces for data oversight. Early acceptance is likely to be strong since regulators, PROs, and waste agencies urgently require reliable digital tools, provided the platform integrates smoothly with existing national systems and demonstrates ease of use and data security.

## KER3. Textiles Collection Systems

Textile collection systems show strong market fit due to the EU's obligation for separate collection by 2025 and the increasing demand for efficient, traceable collection logistics. Acceptance is expected to be high among municipalities, waste operators, and PROs seeking scalable solutions that reduce contamination and improve recovery rates. Their adoption will rely on cost efficiency, adaptability to different local conditions, and the ability to interface with sorting and recycling systems.

## KER4. Eco-Designed Fibres

Eco-designed fibres respond directly to rising consumer expectations and regulatory pressure for sustainable inputs, giving them a solid market fit. However, their commercial acceptance depends on their ability to match conventional fibres on durability, performance, and cost. Premium and sustainability-driven brands are likely to adopt them first, while price-sensitive mass-market players may wait for improved scalability and competitive pricing before wider integration.

## KER5. Eco-Designed Fabrics

Eco-designed fabrics have a strong market fit as brands increasingly seek materials that are recyclable, low-impact, and compliant with eco-design regulations. Their acceptance is boosted by the fact that sustainable fabrics do not typically compromise aesthetics or performance when engineered well. Brands focused on transparency and circularity are likely to integrate these fabrics quickly, and acceptance will broaden as supply volumes increase and costs decrease.

## KER6. Eco-Designed Garments

Eco-designed garments respond directly to regulatory developments such as the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation and rising consumer demand for longerlasting, lower-impact clothing. Their market acceptance is growing, especially among brands aiming to demonstrate leadership in sustainability. However, cost sensitivity and consumer pricing expectations may slow adoption in fast-fashion segments. Mainstream acceptance will strengthen as design guidelines standardise and production costs stabilize.

## KER7. Semi-Automated Sorting for Reusing

Semi-automated sorting technologies enjoy very high market fit due to the rapid expansion of the second-hand and reuse markets and the growing need for more efficient, accurate

This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon Research and Innovation program under grant agreement N° 101181901 and from the Swiss State Secretariat for Education, Research and Innovation (SERI). Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

sorting of collected textiles. Acceptance is expected to be strong among sorting centres, charities, and resale platforms that face increasing volumes and require scalable solutions to improve throughput and classification quality. Implementation will be favoured by facilities seeking better efficiency without full automation.

## KER8. Dismantling Pilot Lines

The dismantling pilot lines address the industry's need for pre-processing solutions that enable recycling of complex garments with trims, mixed materials, or hardware, giving it clear market relevance. Acceptance is likely among recyclers and pre-treatment facilities, though it may remain moderate until the technology is proven cost-effective and capable of handling diverse product types at speed. Brands producing technical or complex garments may also see value in enabling end-of-life dismantling pathways.

## KER9. Thermomechanical Recycling

Thermomechanical recycling technologies show strong market fit due to their scalability and compatibility with common fibres like polyester and polyamide, aligning well with brands' commitments to increase recycled content. Market acceptance is already building among recyclers and fibre producers, but it depends on receiving high-quality feedstock and achieving consistent output properties suitable for high-value applications. As supply chains improve in sorting and pre-processing, acceptance is expected to grow rapidly.

## KER10. Chemical Recycling

Chemical recycling has very high strategic market fit because it can process blended and contaminated textiles that mechanical methods cannot, making it essential for achieving true circularity. Acceptance among brands, recyclers, and material producers is growing quickly, driven by sustainability targets and regulatory pressure. However, widespread commercial adoption will depend on reducing costs, ensuring regulatory compliance, and demonstrating environmental benefits at industrial scale.

With respect to the potential early adopters of the project results, the current market conditions provide the following evidence.

## Digital and traceability solutions (KER1–2)

Early adopters of digital and traceability solutions such as the DPP blockchain system and the authority dashboard are likely to be large fashion brands, vertically integrated manufacturers, and producer responsibility organizations that already rely on sophisticated digital systems and can readily integrate new traceability tools. These actors face strong regulatory pressure to meet EU requirements for DPPs and lifecycle reporting, making them highly motivated to adopt robust and interoperable traceability solutions. Public authorities and waste management agencies can also be early adopters, as they require reliable data infrastructures to oversee compliance, track textile flows, and enforce EPR obligations. In parallel, second-hand platforms, rental services, and certification bodies are expected to integrate such solutions because they depend on transparent product information and authentication systems.

## Collection, sorting and pre-processing (KER3, KER7, KER8)

For solutions related to textile collection, semi-automated sorting, and dismantling, early adopters can include municipalities, national waste agencies, and textile collection NGOs that urgently need scalable systems to meet the EU's mandatory separate collection requirements. These actors are motivated by regulatory deadlines and increasing textile waste volumes and seek efficient and traceable approaches. Industrial sorting facilities, social enterprises, and commercial resale platforms are also ready to adopt these

Posts and shares reflect only the views of all the involved partners. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

innovations as they face growing demand for high-quality reuse streams and improved operational efficiency. Additionally, logistics companies and PROs are likely to integrate these systems as they seek to build more coherent national and regional infrastructures that connect collection, sorting, dismantling, and recycling into a seamless ecosystem.

## Eco-designed materials and products (KER4–6)

Eco-designed fibres, fabrics, and garments will likely attract early adopters among sustainability-driven fashion brands, outdoor and performance wear companies, and manufacturers with strong ESG commitments seeking materials that align with circularity principles. These innovators tend to invest in sustainable sourcing and are already familiar with industrial-scale transitions in fibre and fabric development, making them wellpositioned to test and adopt new eco-designed materials. Designers and brands focused on modular, recyclable, or biodegradable products can also be early adopters, especially those participating in pilot schemes or preparing for compliance with the EU's eco-design regulations. Workwear and uniform producers, which prioritize durability and performance, may adopt these solutions as well, given their interest in long-lasting and sustainable material innovations.

## Recycling technologies (KER9–10)

Thermomechanical and chemical recycling technologies will attract adopters among textile recyclers, polymer processors, and fibre manufacturers seeking new sources of highquality recycled feedstock. These industrial actors face growing pressure to supply recycled content to major brands, positioning them to test and implement advanced recycling solutions early. Large apparel companies with strong circularity commitments can also be adopters, as these technologies enable them to meet recycled content targets, particularly for blended or complex materials that traditional recycling methods cannot process. Waste management companies and industrial investors focused on scaling circular infrastructure are expected to adopt these technologies, recognizing their potential to unlock new value chains and meet regulatory expectations for textile-to-textile recycling.

# 14. CONCLUSIONS

The European textile industry stands at a critical turning point where significant strengths and emerging opportunities are counterbalanced by structural weaknesses and growing external threats.

The sector benefits from a strong tradition of craftsmanship, a skilled workforce, and a reputation for quality, all reinforced by major technological advances, from AI-enabled sorting and IoT-driven traceability to innovations in smart textiles. Paired with a robust regulatory push, these strengths position the industry to lead global sustainability transitions, supported by rising consumer demand for ethical, durable, and circular products.

However, persistent weaknesses, such as declining employment, complex supply chains, limited recycling capacity, and the dominance of fast fashion, continue to hinder progress. The industry's environmental footprint and technical barriers to large-scale textile-to-textile recycling also present substantial challenges, as do high compliance costs and behavioural gaps among consumers.

At the same time opportunities for circular business models illustrate pathways to resilience and competitiveness.

Unfortunately, these gains could be undermined by geopolitical instability, fierce price competition from low-cost producers, greenwashing, and uneven EPR implementation, all of which threaten both the industry credibility and long-term sustainability goals.

Overall, the sector's future will depend on its ability to accelerate innovation, harmonise regulatory frameworks, scale circular infrastructures and align economic incentives with sustainable design, transforming current challenges into drivers of systemic change.

In the middle of December 2025, in its "EU Textile & Clothing Industry Outlook 2024–2025", EURATEX argued that "Europe's textile and clothing industry continues to face significant headwinds. After a weak performance in 2024, the first half of 2025 brought further declines in production, turnover, exports and employment. Industrial capacity utilisation fell below 80%, while rising energy prices and a challenging global environment continue to erode competitiveness. Key developments include a. Persistent pressure from high energy costs and geopolitical uncertainty; b. Increasing imports from non-EU countries, widening the EU trade deficit; c. Falling exports, driven by weaker global demand; d. Growth of low-value online platforms bypassing compliance rules and harming EU manufacturers; e. Ongoing regulatory uncertainty, coupled with major investment needs for digital and sustainable transitions. Despite these challenges, there are pockets of resilience like defence textiles, medical applications, and textile recycling that continue to grow, supported by strong innovation in bio-based fibres, CO₂ reduction, resource efficiency and chemical alternatives. The message is clear. The EU risks de-industrialising its textile and compliance, and ensures a true level playing field".

The analysis of the TRUSTex exploitable solutions, DPP–blockchain integration, authority dashboard, eco-designed materials, advanced sorting and dismantling systems, and thermo-mechanical recycling, address that the textile sector is undergoing a technological and sustainability change.

The strengths of these innovations lie in their ability to enhance transparency, traceability, resource efficiency, and environmental performance, while enabling new circular practices such as disassembly, repair, and high-quality recycling. Collectively, they form an interconnected ecosystem in which reliable data flows, smart design, and automated processes can drastically reduce waste and extend product lifecycles. Opportunities are

substantial, ranging from heightened consumer trust through transparency tools, to regulatory incentives, market differentiation, expanding sustainable product demand, and technological breakthroughs that can scale circular solutions.

However, threats remain equally significant, including industry resistance to change, fluctuating economic conditions, and varying levels of market acceptance.

In conclusion, the successful deployment of these technologies will require coordinated action among industry and policymakers, ensuring investment in infrastructure, harmonized standards, robust data governance, and continuous education. If these conditions are met, the textile sector can transition from a linear, resource-intensive model to an integrated circular system capable of delivering long-term environmental, economic, and social value.
